- **[HaltNav: Reactive Visual Halting over Lightweight Topological Priors for Robust Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12696)**  `arXiv:2603.12696`  `cs.RO` `cs.CV`  
  _Zihui Yu, Pingcong Li, Bichi Zhang, Sören Schwertfeger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) is shifting from rigid, step-by-step instruction following toward open-vocabulary, goal-oriented autonomy. Achieving this transition without exhaustive routing prompts requires agents to leverage structural priors. While prior work often assumes computationally heavy 2D/3D metric maps, we instead exploit a lightweight, text-based osmAG (OpenStreetMap Area Graph), a floorplan-level topological representation that is easy to obtain and maintain. However, global planning over a prior map alone is brittle in real-world deployments, where local connectivity can change (e.g., closed doors or crowded passages), leading to execution-time failures. To address this gap, we propose a hierarchical navigation framework HaltNav that couples the robust global planning of osmAG with the local exploration and instruction-grounding capability of VLN. Our approach features an MLLM-based brain module, which is capable of high-level task grounding and obstruction awareness. Conditioned on osmAG, the brain converts the global route into a sequence of localized execution snippets, providing the VLN executor with prior-grounded, goal-centric sub-instructions. Meanwhile, it detects local anomalies via a mechanism we term Reactive Visual Halting (RVH), which interrupts the local control loop, updates osmAG by invalidating the corresponding topology, and triggers replanning to orchestrate a viable detour. To train this halting capability efficiently, we introduce a data synthesis pipeline that leverages generative models to inject realistic obstacles into otherwise navigable scenes, substantially enriching hard negative samples. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our hierarchical framework outperforms several baseline methods without tedious language instructions, and significantly improves robustness for long-horizon vision-language navigation under environmental changes.
  </details>

- **[Teach, Abstract, Apply: A Framework for Dialogue-Driven Task Learning with Collaborative Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00297)**  `arXiv:2611.00297`  `cs.RO` `cs.CL`  
  _Chalamalasetti Kranti, Sherzod Hakimov, David Schlangen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collaborative robots (cobots) are designed to be safer and more interactive than traditional industrial robots, share the same workspace as humans, and assist them in a range of shop-floor tasks such as palletization, joint assembly, and part inspection. Many of these tasks involve repetitive workflows that may evolve with changes in production environments and task specifications. These requirements motivate the need for intuitive interfaces that allow workers to modify workflows on the shop floor and generalise these routines for future use. This work introduces the Teach-Abstract-Apply (TAA) framework, which enables human operators to (a) teach new workflows to cobots in natural language, (b) abstract the taught workflows into skills for later use, and (c) apply these skills in different settings. As a case study, we apply the TAA framework to a collaborative structure-building task and evaluate it with both open-weight (Qwen3-Coder) and closed (GPT5.2-Codex) models in self-play and cross-model configurations. Our results show that skill abstraction improves task success rates and reduces interaction cost. Beyond automated evaluation, a preliminary human-in-the-loop study suggests that the pipeline also works with human instructions.
  </details>

- **[SCOUT: Planning under Occlusion via Object-Centric World Model Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01600)**  `arXiv:2611.01600`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Amir Hossain Raj, Xuesu Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots manipulating in cluttered spaces must often plan from partial views where foreground objects occlude the target, surrounding geometry, and usable free space. Reactive policies often miss these hidden constraints, while exhaustive search becomes computationally prohibitive in dense clutter. We present SCOUT, an occlusion-aware planning framework that bridges high-level deliberation with grounded physical rollouts. SCOUT leverages an object-centric scene representation to reconstruct hidden geometry and derive a relational planning state capturing blocker hierarchies and accessibility corridors. To navigate the vast action space in clutter, an LLM proposes structured action candidates which are then rigorously evaluated via Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) using a learned action-conditioned world model. Evaluations on 'Reveal' and 'Placement' tasks show that SCOUT outperforms Vision-Language-Action (VLA) policies, embodied agents, and 3D world models, with significant gains in long-horizon tasks requiring reasoning over occluded geometry. We further validate the framework's robustness through real-world robotic manipulation in a cluttered shelf environment.
  </details>

- **[Late Autumn: LLM Agents Specialize a General Multi-Robot Planner for One Warehouse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02391)**  `arXiv:2611.02391`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Yimin Tang, Han Zhang, Shao-Hung Chan, Erdem Bıyık, Sven Koenig, Jingkai Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  No algorithm is best on all problems, but a real warehouse is a narrow problem: its layout and processes rarely change. Operators still run one general multi-robot planner at many sites, because experts cannot tune it for each one. In this work, which we call Late Autumn, we explore whether LLM coding agents running autonomous research can specialize a general planner for one site, trading generality for throughput, much as post-training adapts a pretrained LLM to one task. We study six campaigns on a lifelong planner that combines task assignment with multi-agent path finding, on a floor modeled on an existing automated warehouse. We find that (1) the agents raise throughput by 22--60%, and the gains hold on unseen seeds; (2) they rediscover known MAPF mechanisms, such as corridor-exit priorities, local guidance, and rollouts, and independent campaigns find the same bottleneck; (3) the gains largely carry over to another layout, a larger fleet, and other service times, shrink with a smaller fleet, and mostly vanish under an outbound-heavy demand mix; and (4) each final patch (0.7k--2.4k lines) is a chain of scored commits with a median step of 27 lines, so experts can review it, and review finds problems that the score misses; some patches multiply planner compute by over 100x. LLM agents can therefore turn each site's bias into extra throughput at little engineering cost, with reviewable patches; we expect experts to shift toward designing insightful metrics and fast simulators that mirror the real problem.
  </details>

- **[Auriga Technical Report: A Task-Agnostic Agent Harness for Zero-Shot Embodied Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01112)**  `arXiv:2611.01112`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Chengcheng Wang, Yuheng Shi, Siyu Xu, Yutong Wang, Jinjing Zhao, Chenghao Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models can interpret instructions and plan manipulation tasks, yet struggle when acting directly as robot policies. On RoboDojo, a large planning-execution gap separates high-level task decisions from physical execution: removing arm execution raises GPT-6 Astra's success rate from 22.5% under the reference control interface to 76.2%. We present Auriga, a task-agnostic agent harness that closes 68.3% of this gap in zero-shot manipulation without target-task trials or resets. In Auriga, the model makes all task decisions over an explicit task belief and acts through either direct tool calls or synthesized multi-step Python programs, while the harness supplies calibrated 3D measurements, finger-referenced dual-arm control, and a single admission gateway to the robot. Before motion, the model inspects a visual trajectory preview while simulation is paused; during execution, the harness checks declared stage tolerances and grasp bounds, stopping the robot on divergence and returning the measured discrepancy for in-place recovery. Developed across 13 cross-task capability iterations and frozen for evaluation, Auriga ranks first on the RoboDojo leaderboard with a 57.7% success rate (64.0 Score), more than doubling the reference harness on the same model, and remains first under scene randomization while learned policies drop by up to 33.9%. On RoboCasa365, the same frozen harness reaches a 73.2% success rate, outperforming VLA-assisted agents by up to 23.1% on composite tasks.Project page:this https URLCode:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Spotter: Let the Embodied Model Lead, and the VLM Reflect for It](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.36808)**  `arXiv:2609.36808`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Long Li, Qichao Zhao, Yue Yang, Fan Xu, Zhe Wang, Alan Wee-Chung Liew, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current embodied models do not respond to their own failures, although what just went wrong could inform a small adjustment on the next attempt, the kind of reflection behind the gains of thinking in language models. We test whether they can repair a known error, which requires producing a correction and judging whether it is right. Stopped at a failure and allowed to retry, they seldom repair it through their own randomness or from a language description of the error, and best-of-N selection cannot pick the successful candidate after a failure. We attribute this to training only on successful demonstrations and to inputs too narrow to show what went wrong, and conclude that reflection must come from a vision-language model (VLM), which takes in far more information, such as the episode history and text, and is more general. Prior VLM-led work has the VLM plan every step and invoke the embodied model as a tool, placing the VLM on the critical path. We propose Spotter, which reverses the roles: the embodied model leads and executes continuously, while the VLM runs in parallel, monitors through a lightweight local screener, intervenes only when an error is detected, reflects on and corrects it, and returns control. We run Spotter with Qwen and with GPT as the VLM, and both improve the embodied models; with GPT, Spotter improves Cosmos Policy and $\pi_{0.5}$ by 5.6 and 7.5 percentage points on RoboCasa, and raises $\pi_{0.5}$ from 47.2% to 57.0% on the Hard setting of RoboTwin 2.0 and from 53% to 83% on a real robot. Because the VLM steps in only when an error is confirmed, a successful episode with Qwen takes only 13 to 16 s longer than with the embodied model alone and about 70% less time than with a VLM-led baseline using the same model. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Self-Improvement of Exoskeleton Assistance Policies through Human-in-the-Loop Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01581)**  `arXiv:2611.01581`  `cs.RO`  
  _Muyuan Ma, Shuo Jiang, Lijun Han, Xinpan Meng, Zhenghua Ma, Xiuze Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To reduce reliance on manual design and extensive parameter tuning, a framework for the autonomous evolution of exoskeleton assistance policies through human feedback is proposed. Assistance policies are autonomously generated as executable control code by an intelligent agent and evaluated in experiments with human participants. Subjective user feedback, movement performance, and muscle activation measurements are incorporated into policy assessment. Based on these assessments, policy deficiencies are autonomously identified and the control code is revised by the agent for subsequent trials. A closed loop of policy generation, human feedback, evaluation, and autonomous refinement is thereby established. The framework is evaluated using GPT, DeepSeek, GLM, and Kimi, and initial assistance performance and refinement outcomes are compared across models. Despite differences in initial performance, effective assistance is achieved with all four models after a single autonomous refinement iteration under the tested conditions. Across three tasks, biceps and triceps muscle activation levels are reduced by 29.4% and 35.2%, respectively, compared with those measured without an exoskeleton. However, limited adaptability to unexpected situations is observed, indicating that further improvements in robustness and safety are required.
  </details>

- **[Democratizing Robot-Use Agents: A Unified Harness with Open Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01560)**  `arXiv:2611.01560`  `cs.RO`  
  _Jiarui Yuan, Yuxin Zuo, Xiaoyu Huang, Wenbo Zhang, Kwanyoung Park, Junlin Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot-use Agents (RUAs) have shown promising zero-shot manipulation capabilities, but reliance on frontier, proprietary models limits research into further training. To democratize RUA research, we introduce uni-robot-agent, a unified harness for competitive open-weight robot use that supports further training. Without privileged inputs, an out-of-the-box Qwen3.8-Flash-Next achieves a RoboDojo Score of 31.39, surpassing the published GPT-6 Astra-RoboProbe result of 28.97 and many VLA/WAM baselines, and performs competitively on 19 real-world tasks. Our analysis across model generations, families, and sizes links effective robot use to four general capabilities, suggesting that future RUA training should develop them jointly.
  </details>

- **[Knowing Where to Look: Interactive Object Search via Spatial-Semantic Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00499)**  `arXiv:2611.00499`  `cs.RO`  
  _Aditya Agarwal, Lucy Cai, Kelly Lu, Travis Manderson, Nicholas Roy, Tomás Lozano-Pérez, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When searching for an object, humans naturally combine semantic intuition - reasoning about where the object is likely to be stored - with geometric awareness of what parts of the space are visible, occluded, or unexplored. Effective robot object search requires the same joint reasoning, yet existing methods rely on only one: geometric methods maintain principled spatial beliefs but lack deep semantic understanding, while language-model based methods leverage commonsense knowledge but maintain no explicit belief over space. We argue that jointly reasoning over semantic and geometric evidence in an explicit 3D belief enables human-like interactive object search that outperforms either modality alone. To test this claim, we build a system that accumulates per-object VLM plausibility scores and geometric occupancy evidence via Bayesian log-odds filtering into a unified 3D voxel belief field. High-belief regions in the combined belief field drive action selection at each step: taking a closer look from a different viewpoint, navigating toward an unexplored region of the workspace, or physically interacting with a closed cabinet. We deploy this system end-to-end on a bimanual mobile humanoid and find that it outperforms geometry-only search and LLM-based planning methods. Project website -this https URL
  </details>

- **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252)**  `arXiv:2610.35252`  `cs.RO`  
  _Shitong Liu, Xu Yang, Jingwei Zhang, Sheng Lian, Xinyu Zhuang, Kaiwen Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.
  </details>

- **[LLM4Robot-Bench: How Far Can GPT-6 Astra Go in Robotic Manipulation? A Hierarchical, Matched Sim-Real Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34527)**  `arXiv:2610.34527`  `cs.RO`  
  _Haibao Yu, Jiaqi Meng, Zijian Zhang, Bowen Jing, Lei Yang, Yue Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce LLM4Robot-Bench, a hierarchical, matched Sim-Real benchmark for evaluating physical interaction capabilities in contact-rich manipulation. Its 16 household tasks span three cumulative levels: contact selection and geometric alignment (L1), force regulation within sustained contact (L2), and coordinated contact reconfiguration and force regulation (L3). Each task is instantiated in physically and visually matched simulated and real-world environments, with shared task definitions and success criteria. We evaluate GPT-6 Astra zero-shot with three trials per task in each environment. Real-world success rates decline from 80.0% at L1 to 47.6% at L2 and 0.0% at L3, with corresponding simulation rates of 66.7%, 47.6%, and 0.0%. Ten of the 16 tasks have identical success counts across environments; the remaining six differ by one success out of three trials. Across both environments, recurring difficulties in spatial reasoning, contact selection, force regulation, and coordination often prevent local physical progress from accumulating into task completion. These findings characterize Astra's observed capability boundary across increasing interaction demands and, for this model, support matched simulation as a repeatable, scalable testbed for studying the sustained, coordinated physical interactions that limit reliable manipulation.
  </details>

- **[MAC-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Collaboration via Interconnected Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01728)**  `arXiv:2611.01728`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zerui Chen, Qinggang Zhang, Qichuan Liu, Zhishang Xiang, Zhimin Wei, Yunbo Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) tackle challenging tasks by dividing work among agents and coordinating their contributions toward shared objectives. Evaluating these systems therefore requires benchmarks that assess not only task-solving performance but also the ability to handle interdependent work. However, existing benchmarks often emphasize task difficulty without substantial collaboration requirements or evaluate only a limited range of predefined collaboration patterns. In this paper, we introduce MAC-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaboration through interconnected tasks. Rather than imposing predefined agent roles or communication protocols, MAC-Bench embeds collaboration requirements directly into task dependencies and shared-resource constraints. Specifically, we mine reusable collaboration structures from selected benchmark tasks at two levels. Local collaboration patterns capture recurring interactions among subtasks, while global task structures characterize how these interactions contribute to complete workflows. Through coarse-to-fine composition, these structures are recombined into task graphs combining different collaboration requirements. Tasks are instantiated in executable environments with automated verification of partial progress and complete task resolution.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Context Sensitivity of Collaboration Behavior in Multi Party Simulation Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00089)**  `arXiv:2611.00089`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Anvesh Rao Vijjini, Sheshera Mysore, Pegah Jandaghi, Ashish Sharma, Snigdha Chaturvedi, Bahareh Sarrafzadeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group Social Behavior is increasingly used as a lens to study Large language model (LLM) simulations to aid model training and evaluation. However, little prior work has examined how contextual factors such as team size and goal type manifest in long running simulations. To overcome this gap, we conduct a controlled, full-factorial study examining how contextual factors affect collaboration behavior across multi-party simulation frameworks. Using well established social science taxonomies, we quantify context sensitivity of collaboration behavior in simulated teams. Our analyses reveal significant effects of contextual factors such as simulators consistently favor smooth collaboration over disagreement, negotiation pressure, and coordination breakdown. These findings provide the first systematic evidence of how contextual factors influence emergent collaboration in LLM based multi-party simulation frameworks, offering both guidance and empirical results for the use of such simulations.
  </details>

- **[Cloudra: Geometric Reasoning and Scientific Validation for Autonomous Reaction Mechanism Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02909)**  `arXiv:2611.02909`  `cs.MA`  
  _Chengyi Yang, Qianxin Zhou, Yang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous reaction-mechanism exploration requires proposing reaction steps, preparing three-dimensional structures, and determining which reactant and product a computed saddle point connects. Large language model (LLM) agents can misjudge spatial relationships and confuse a computed structure with the species or step they intended to study.We present Cloudra, a multi-agent framework that constrains mechanistic output through a Delivery DAG of chemical objects. Species nodes carry geometries and bonding topology; elementary-step edges carry transition states and reaction connections between species. Cheminformatics algorithms establish molecular identities and atom correspondences. Deterministic routines measure and transform structures to prepare candidate geometries. Frequency and IRC calculations determine the stationary points found and the species they connect. Agents use these objects to test proposed steps, compare pathways, and revise the mechanism.CloudraBench evaluates the framework's end-to-end mechanism-calculation capability across 50 tasks covering elementary reactions, multistep mechanisms, stereochemical competition, and computational method limitations. Across 200 runs under four workflow configurations, the primary Guided workflow achieves a mean composite score of 0.805, compared with 0.584 for the same-model Standard baseline. On Hard-tier tasks, the Guided mean increases from 0.689 under Standard to 0.958. Under Guided, the self-consistency component reaches its maximum score in 24 of the 40 non-Impossible tasks, compared with seven under Standard. Behavioral analyses examine how the agents use bond and dihedral measurements, optimized structures, and IRC endpoints to revise local mechanistic assignments.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Modal Multi-Agent System for Bridge Operation and Maintenance based on Large Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02738)**  `arXiv:2611.02738`  `cs.MA`  
  _Xiangyun Luo, Yunkai Zhang, Yong Huang, Stephen Wu, Hui Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bridges are critical transportation infrastructure, and reliable operation and maintenance (O&M) is essential for traffic safety and long-term structural performance. However, bridge O&M faces key bottlenecks: no unified architecture coordinates heterogeneous resources; model inferences lack clear links to physical-mechanical laws; and experts' tacit knowledge remains difficult to formalize. This study proposes a knowledge-enhanced autonomy formalization methodology and apply it to construct the Bridge O&M Multi-Modal Multi-Agent System (BOMMA), which integrates heterogeneous diagnostic actions, physics-mechanics-informed representations, long-term memory, and structured experiential knowledge within a coordinated multi-agent workflow, enabling interpretable and traceable scheduling, analysis, and decision-making. On 3,492 multi-source instructions, the proposed method achieves scheduling recalls of 85.37% for simple and 99.40% for composite tasks, with 95.35% BERT F1-score for O&M action reasoning. Ablation studies confirm the contributions of the components in the autonomy. This study bridges general LLM reasoning and specialized bridge O&M requirements.
  </details>

- **[MAServeBench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent LLM Serving Systems with Hierarchical Collaboration Workloads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40293)**  `arXiv:2610.40293`  `cs.MA`  
  _Shengxuan Qiu, Haochen Huang, Shuzhang Zhong, Zizhuo Fu, Zebin Yang, Meng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) coordinate specialized agents to solve complex tasks, turning each user request into interdependent model and tool operations. Unlike independent requests, these operations become ready at different times and consume evolving context as agents coordinate and exchange results. This creates changing patterns of concurrency, computation, and memory demand that can expose different bottlenecks in LLM serving hardware. Yet benchmarking these effects requires capturing the collaboration rules that generate serving workloads, beyond the requests observed during execution. We present MAServeBench, a benchmark that treats multi-agent collaboration as a configurable dimension of serving workloads for LLM serving and hardware evaluation. Its composable stages capture how agents coordinate and exchange context in documented MAS workflows, enabling native executions to generate different workloads even when task inputs and tools remain unchanged. Hierarchical Semantic Traces preserve realized operations and dependencies for reproducible fixed-work replay and as inputs to architecture simulators. Task-linked measurements connect these workloads to serving execution and GPU/NPU hardware behavior, revealing why serving efficiency does not always translate into timely task completion. The benchmark code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Scalar Smoothness Is One Point on a Besov Spectrum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02037)**  `arXiv:2611.02037`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Bum Jun Kim, Anastasis Kratsios_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Studies of neural network generalization and robustness often summarize smoothness with a single number, such as a Lipschitz constant or a Hessian curvature statistic. However, Lipschitz constants summarize global worst-case sensitivity and Hessian statistics summarize local curvature; neither captures how model responses vary with the size, direction, and location of perturbations encountered in real-world deployment. To measure smoothness under these perturbations, we introduce a finite-difference roughness spectrum that profiles model responses across these conditions. Our roughness spectrum generalizes scalar smoothness measures, bringing Lipschitz sensitivity and directional Hessian curvature into a common framework across perturbation scales. We connect this spectrum to classical Besov theory, which describes how a function's variation changes with scale. Our theoretical analysis also proves that a larger output shift can cause less task damage than a smaller one, and that local curvature alone can miss the blocks most vulnerable to finite perturbations. In image classification, Roughness Source Control (RSC) suppresses margin roughness along noise directions and improves noise robustness in a comparison of 80 checkpoints across four model--dataset combinations. In post-training quantization, we identify blocks that need higher precision by measuring task-loss increases from perturbations matched to the size and direction of each block's quantization error. Our mixed-precision allocation achieves lower perplexity than the Hessian-based HAWQ-V2 baseline under the same GPTQ weights and bit budgets in all 14 settings across seven held-out large language models.
  </details>

- **[Replay for LLM Continual Learning with Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02746)**  `arXiv:2611.02746`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yiqiao Qiu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) distills domain experts into one student but needs every expert at once. When domains arrive one at a time and earlier experts are gone, the previous-stage student can teach replayed prompts of earlier domains inside every stage. Some pipelines instead store every stage snapshot for a final multi-teacher recovery stage. On five domains with a 1.5B student and five public experts, this paper compares both with joint MOPD. It varies the replay budget and the replay signal. The signal is token-level distillation alone or with alignment of hidden states, attention and value relations. Sequential distillation without replay or recovery is 6.2 points below 208-step joint MOPD on a 14-benchmark average and 3.5 points below on the 11 benchmarks outside safety. Without snapshots, replay toward the previous student closes 3.8 of the 6.2 points when the earlier domains share the replay budget of a stage. With a fixed share of seven replay steps per earlier domain and stage, it closes 5.8 over two training runs. This is level with the 5.9 of recovery from all snapshots, both at its default length and when given as many training steps as the fixed share. Alignment terms on replay steps change the average by about one point or less. If refusal and compliance count equally in safety, shared-budget replay ranks first among the trained configurations, and the fixed share stays at or above recovery. Against the shared budget, the fixed share lowers the score on the newest domain. Recovery lowers this score as well, in five of six cases. The replay steps of the fixed share grow quadratically with the number of stages.
  </details>

- **[Train the Sampler You Run: Few-Step Masked Diffusion Language Models via Mixture-Likelihood Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02666)**  `arXiv:2611.02666`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Amin Najafgholizadeh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a masked diffusion language model is sampled in a few parallel steps in a random order, the model actually run is a mixture over reveal plans, while cross-entropy training fits token conditionals rather than this mixture. We introduce mixture-likelihood fine-tuning (Mix-FT), which fine-tunes a pretrained model on an importance-weighted bound of its own sampler's likelihood, with no teacher and no change to the architecture or the sampler. On LLaDA-8B, a LoRA fine-tune for 0.002% of the pretraining compute writes better OpenWebText continuations than continued cross-entropy and Alpha diffusion given the same compute, at every tested step count. With half the model evaluations, 16 rather than 32 steps per block, its continuations score better than the original model's in every seed under three judges, with confidence-based decoding as well as random order, and under confidence-based decoding two blinded LLM raters from different model families prefer them in 69 and 71% of pairs. On a public 110M-parameter block diffusion model, Mix-FT takes under four T4-hours and beats continued cross-entropy, Alpha diffusion and SDTT distillation at 4, 8 and 16 steps per block, under two judges in every seed. Exact likelihoods on text8, with a control that weights every plan equally, attribute about three quarters of the likelihood gain over continued cross-entropy to weighting each plan by how well it explains the sequence.
  </details>

- **[TVP: TV is Almost All You Need for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00588)**  `arXiv:2611.00588`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Aaryam Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates LLM inference with a drafter whose proposed tokens are verified by the target model in parallel. Under lossless speculative sampling, a draft token is accepted with probability one minus the total variation (TV) distance between the target and draft distributions, yet drafters are often trained with cross-entropy (CE) or KL. We first show that under matched and well-tuned training, TV matches or surpasses several published draft-training objectives when fine-tuning, and a fixed TV and KL mixture does the same when training from scratch. We identify two remaining problems, TV's vanishing gradients on undercovered tokens and the prefix products that starve later positions in acceptance-length objectives. To address both, we derive TVP, a loss family based on expected acceptance length, whose power parameter interpolates between coverage and acceptance oriented objectives. Annealing the power from zero to one gives the highest or tied-highest acceptance length across three drafter architectures and three target models, including longer drafts and block verification. TVP improves acceptance length over CE by 9.8% when fine-tuning, and over the public DFlash2 training recipes by 6.2 to 12.9%.
  </details>

- **[Unlearning by Extrapolating a Shrink-and-Heal Lookahead](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00374)**  `arXiv:2611.00374`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Hoang-Chau Luong, Lingwei Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlearning aims to make a large language model behave like one retrained without its forget set. Prevailing likelihood-suppression objectives, however, lack an explicit forgetting target: they continually reward lower forget-set likelihood, making forgetting sensitive to the optimization budget. We address this limitation by estimating the direction toward the oracle without training it. Our key idea is to shrink the fine-tuned model toward its pre-fine-tuning weights and briefly re-learn the retain set, thereby removing the forget-specific contribution; in the linearized regime, this shrink-and-heal lookahead moves the model a fixed fraction of the way to the retrained model, its unique fixed point. Our method, SHIFT, extrapolates the lookahead's per-token changes into distillation targets and iteratively refines them. Evaluated against eleven baselines under a unified OpenUnlearning protocol, SHIFT achieves the highest aggregate score across all TOFU settings and the closest oracle alignment, 3.7x closer on average than the next-best method; on MUSE, it preserves the most retain knowledge. Under larger optimization budgets, baselines overshoot the oracle, while SHIFT remains near it, even under relearning attacks.
  </details>

- **[Two is better than one: A Collapse-free Multi-Reward RLIF Training Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22620)**  `arXiv:2605.22620`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Shourov Joarder, Diganta Sikdar, Ahsan Habib Akash, Binod Bhattarai, Prashnna Gyawali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved the reasoning ability of LLMs, but often depends on external supervision from human annotations or gold-standard solutions. Reinforcement learning from internal feedback (RLIF) has recently emerged as a scalable unsupervised alternative, using signals extracted from the model itself. However, existing RLIF methods typically rely on a single internal reward, which can lead to reward hacking, entropy collapse, and degraded reasoning structure. We propose a multi-reward RLIF framework that decomposes the training signal into two complementary components: an answer-level reward based on cluster voting and a completion-level reward based on token-wise self-certainty. To combine these signals robustly, we apply GDPO-based normalization to reduce reward-scale imbalance. We further introduce KL-Cov regularization, which targets low-entropy token distributions responsible for disproportionate entropy reduction, preserving exploration and preventing late-stage collapse. Across mathematical reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, our method improves stability and robustness over prior unsupervised RL approaches, while achieving performance close to supervised RLVR methods. These results show that complementary internal rewards, combined with targeted regularization, can support stable long-horizon reasoning without relying on external ground-truth supervision. Code will be released soon.
  </details>

- **[PrismServe: Efficient LLM Serving on Heterogeneous Accelerator Clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09204)**  `arXiv:2508.09204`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Jinhao Zhang, Zicheng Yan, Boyang Zhang, Zeyu Liu, Daning Cheng, Yunquan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Serving large language models across heterogeneous accelerators is challenging because partitioning a replica across device types introduces communication overhead during autoregressive decoding, while deploying complete quantized replicas can degrade model quality. We present PrismServe, a serving system that combines request-level collaboration across heterogeneous hardware with a mixture of quantized experts derived from the same foundation model. Each expert executes within a group of same-type accelerators in a single node, while a two-level router selects an expert based on predicted response quality and then selects a replica according to load and prefix-cache locality. Implemented on unmodified vLLM, PrismServe achieves 2.0--2.6$\times$ the throughput of cross-node or cross-vendor full-precision pipelined deployments and reduces P99 time per output token by 55--65\% when serving Qwen3-14B on 48 accelerators from three vendors. Across Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-14B, and Qwen3-32B on five downstream tasks, PrismServe outperforms the best single quantization method by 2.0--4.3 points while remaining within 1.4 points of full-precision accuracy. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Can Large Language Model Agents Learn by Watching Tutorial Videos?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01460)**  `arXiv:2611.01460`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Haoyu Zhao, Zafeirios Fountas, Jun Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans continuously acquire knowledge from carefully crafted materials such as books and tutorial videos, carrying that understanding into future tasks. Millions of such instructional videos are uploaded online every day, yet LLM agents remain unable to exploit this ever-expanding wealth of human teaching. Their capabilities are frozen after training, while parameter updates at scale remain prohibitively expensive. This raises the central question: can LLM agents learn from this vast instructional resource without parameter updates? We introduce Tutorial Agentic Learning (TAL), a nonparametric continual-learning paradigm that consolidates tutorial videos into a persistent, structured tutorialbook memory through a two-stage adversarial process that harvests hard, video-grounded questions and uses them to refine the memory. At inference time, TAL retrieves through a Table-of-Contents (TOC) tree, tying retrieval directly to the model's reasoning rather than to embedding similarity. To study this setting, we introduce VidLearn, a multimodal QA benchmark built from 72 chess tutorial videos spanning 12 openings. On VidLearn, TAL outperforms all evaluated baselines and improves monotonically as more videos are consumed, achieving up to 113% and 91% relative gains over the corresponding vanilla MLLMs on the training and test splits, respectively. In actual gameplay, TAL reaches a stronger opening-phase position than its vanilla counterpart in 69% of head-to-head games. Evaluation in a second domain further demonstrates the generality of TAL. Together, these results highlight instructional videos as a scalable resource for parameter-update-free agent continual learning.
  </details>

- **[Quantizing Recurrent Language Models Is Easier Than You Think](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01864)**  `arXiv:2611.01864`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Philipp Nazari, Neehal Tumma, Daniela Rus, T. Konstantin Rusch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear recurrences are becoming an increasingly common building block in large language models, replacing the growing KV cache of softmax attention with a fixed-size recurrent state that enables efficient decoding. However, the quantization stack has largely been developed around softmax Transformers, whose numerical properties and inference state differ from those of recurrent models. Our goal is to understand these differences and study the design space of quantization methods for recurrent models. In our analysis, we differentiate between two targets. First, extra-state quantization concerns weights and activations outside the recurrent state, whose distributions are shaped indirectly by the sequence mixer during training. In controlled 1.3B parameter models, we find that decayed recurrences develop weaker residual outlier channels than softmax attention and are therefore easier to quantize without incoherence processing. Second, intra-state quantization concerns the recurrent state itself during decode, where repeated updates create a different numerical problem from KV cache quantization. We instantiate our findings as LoReS, a state quantization pipeline combining value-axis scale grouping to match output channel magnitude, decay-based mixed-precision to protect slowly decaying heads or channels, and stochastic rounding to limit error accumulation under repeated requantization. Across Qwen-3.5 9B, Nemotron-H 8B, and Kimi-Linear 48B-A3B, LoReS supports robust 8-bit state quantization and lower effective precision on shorter decode horizons. We implement LoReS in vLLM and demonstrate end-to-end decode speedups.
  </details>

- **[Evolving Memory, Learning to Meta-Control: Test-Time Adaptation for Experience-augmented LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01747)**  `arXiv:2611.01747`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zeyuan Zhao, Yuanyuan Shi, Peng Yu, Lingyue Fu, Shao Zhang, Ying Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-evolving agents accumulate experience from task streams, but existing frameworks typically rely on fixed pipelines to retrieve and utilize this experience, neglecting when and how to deploy it. We introduce Online Meta-Control of Experience (OMCE), a framework that updates experience memory and learns a meta-control policy from task feedback during test-time adaptation. OMCE employs a lightweight contextual-bandit meta-controller that uses compact retrieval signals to select among four cognitive actions: DIRECT, EPISODE, GUIDE, and DISTILL. This routing mechanism operationalizes three coupled decisions: whether to invoke experience, which memory format to utilize, and when to consolidate new guidance. OMCE evolves online, leveraging task feedback to update the memory bank and refine the routing policy via contextual bandit updates. Experiments on four online adaptation benchmarks across four backbones show that OMCE improves first-attempt success by up to 20.5 percentage points over the strongest compared baseline, while reducing per-task token use by a factor of 5.7 to 62.5 relative to ACE with GPT-5.2. Controlled ablations further support the benefits of learned routing and continued routing policy adaptation.
  </details>

- **[Sweep Less, Steer More: Using Activation Steering Geometry for Reduced Hyperparameter Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01496)**  `arXiv:2611.01496`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Nishkal Hundia, Swastik Agrawal, Navita Goyal, Sarah Wiegreffe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Representation steering methods like difference-in-means (DIM) offer a lightweight approach to controlling LLM behavior, but their effectiveness varies widely across concepts, datasets, and layers, making principled deployment costly without exhaustive hyperparameter sweeps. We build on the discriminability index $d'$, a compute-cheap geometric measure of how well contrastive training activations separate along the steering direction. Across five behaviors on 3 models, we demonstrate uses beyond its established role as a dataset-level steerability predictor. First, in the multiple-choice question answering (MCQA) setting, we show that $d'$ reliably identifies performant layers at which to steer, eliminating the need for a layer-wise sweep. Second, we extend this analysis to open-ended multi-token generation, and find that $d'$ remains successful for layer selection across different settings. Lastly, we test whether the position of a generated token's representation on the DIM line predicts steering success on that individual example. In the MCQA setting, this works for high-$d'$ datasets but is inconsistent across behaviors, models, and training-token choices in open-ended generation. This highlights that methods developed and conclusions drawn in single-token MCQA settings do not necessarily transfer to open-ended generation.
  </details>

- **[MoBiQuant: Mixture-of-Bits Quantization for Token-Adaptive Any-Precision LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.20191)**  `arXiv:2602.20191`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Dongwei Wang, Jinhee Kim, Seokho Han, Denis Gudovskiy, Yohei Nakata, Tomoyuki Okuno, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic runtime latency and memory constraints necessitate flexible large language model (LLM) deployment, where an LLM can be inferred with various quantization precisions based on available computational resources. Recent work on such any-precision quantization either relies on hardware-inefficient vector quantization or induces additional scaling factors when switching between bit-widths. Meanwhile, existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods calibrated for a fixed low precision show poor generalizability under runtime precision change. In this work, we attribute the source of poor generalization across bit-widths to a precision-dependent outlier migration phenomenon where the distribution of PTQ-sensitive tokens changes across precisions. Motivated by this observation, we propose MoBiQuant, a novel any-precision Mixture-of-Bits quantization framework that adjusts weight precision for flexible LLM inference based on token sensitivity. Specifically, we propose a many-in-one recursive residual quantization that can iteratively reconstruct higher-precision weights at runtime and mitigates outlier migration with a token-aware router to dynamically select the optimal inference precision of eachthis http URLexperiments show that MoBiQuant matches or surpasses frontier single-precision PTQ while exhibiting strong elasticity, achieving significant memory savings and throughput gains of up to $1.34\times$ over state-of-the-art any-precision methods. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Hindsight Credit Assignment over Trees for Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02706)**  `arXiv:2611.02706`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Penghong Zhao, Yihao Chen, Maoheng Yuan, Yunjian Zhang, Abudukelimu Wuerkaixi, Haoran Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for long-horizon LLM agents faces a fundamental dilemma: successful experiences are essential for policy improvement, yet limited policy capabilities make such experiences difficult to discover. Sparse outcome rewards compound this challenge, leaving sampled trajectories with little relative feedback for learning. In this paper, we propose TriGRPO, a tree-based reinforcement learning framework that turns scarce successes into exploration and learning signals through hindsight credit assignment within and across rollout trees. TriGRPO constructs rollout trees through hindsight-guided branching: when an ongoing trajectory reaches a matched decision state, a successful action discovered during rollout initiates a sibling branch that continues autonomously, enabling further exploration and parent-sibling comparisons. Three complementary groups organize policy optimization over these trees: root groups capture within-prompt relative returns, node groups assign credit from parent-sibling outcome differences, and cross groups supplement sparse feedback with historical performance on related tasks. Experimental results demonstrate consistent gains in task performance and rollout efficiency across model scales, highlighting structured hindsight credit assignment as an effective approach to long-horizon agent learning.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating LLM Pretraining with Zeroth-Order Curvature on Idle Serving Compute](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02688)**  `arXiv:2611.02688`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Mingchuan Ma, Dongqi Cai, Daqian Yang, Zhenyan Lu, Chen Chen, Teresa Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM pretraining is increasingly compute-intensive, while substantial inference serving compute remains idle during off-peak periods. However, such serving hardware is difficult to use for conventional first-order training due to its memory, networking and other system requirements. Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization offers a natural interface to this forward-only compute resource, but its gradient estimates break down in pretraining. Unlike fine-tuning, which can confine updates to a small parameter subspace, pretraining must update every parameter, and the alignment of a ZO gradient estimate with the true gradient shrinks as the number of updated parameters grows. We make a different observation: noisy ZO estimates can still provide useful curvature information, even when they are too inaccurate to serve as gradients. Based on this insight, we propose ZO-Curv, which leverages idle forward-only devices to estimate curvature and uses it to adapt and refine the update used during first-order training, thereby accelerating the process. We provide convergence guarantees that characterize the effect of finite-query curvature estimation. Experiments on LLMs from 125M to 1.5B parameters show that ZO-Curv consistently accelerates pretraining. At 770M parameters, ZO-Curv reaches AdamW's 20K-step validation loss in 3.9 hours instead of 7.5 hours, while achieving a lower final loss of 2.70 versus 2.84 under the same step budget. These results show that idle model serving compute can accelerate LLM pretraining when zeroth-order queries are used to estimate curvature rather than gradients directly.
  </details>

- **[FactorSpec: Serving Exact Relational Constraints as Speculative Drafts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02297)**  `arXiv:2611.02297`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jianchang Su, Dawei Xiang, Wei Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding speeds up LLM serving by drafting tokens that the target model verifies in one pass, and its gain grows with the drafts the target accepts. Structured outputs should be an ideal case, because much of a record follows from relations among its fields: an itinerary's days follow from its cities' stays and flights. Serving engines, however, draft such text by proposing it from the prompt or a learned head, and their grammar masks enforce only syntax, so the model must satisfy the relations, which it violates: on NATURAL PLAN's trip problems, Qwen3-8B writes a valid itinerary for 10%, and retries that name the violated relation raise this to 14%. We present FactorSpec, which serves exact relational constraints as speculative drafts. FactorSpec compiles a JSON Schema and its relations into a factor graph with an exact feasibility check, drafts every token the constraint determines as certain, verifies each request at a width chosen for it, and computes the masks in worker processes outside the scheduler while the GPU runs. In SGLang, its drafts yield up to 10.5 tokens per verify step, up to 1.9x those of the fastest n-gram drafter, and it decodes up to 1.43x faster than speculators tuned per load, at a grammar mask's cost; it also leads in vLLM. Because every record is also valid, it serves up to 13.9x the valid records per GPU-second of syntax-only baselines.
  </details>

- **[Value Forcing: Aligning Return Distributions at All Noise Levels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02160)**  `arXiv:2611.02160`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Chongyi Zheng, Zhiyuan Zhou, Qiyang Li, Benjamin Eysenbach, Sergey Levine_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning accurate value functions is central to reinforcement learning (RL), yet remains challenging because supervision is limited and indirect: ground-truth values are unavailable, and each transition in the data only provides an immediate reward that can be used to construct bootstrapped targets as supervision. This challenge is particularly pronounced when data is limited or reward signals are sparse, as is common in real-world applications such as robotics. We argue that the key to accurate value learning in this limited data regime is extracting richer learning signals from the available data. In this paper, we introduce Value Forcing, a new approach that uses flow matching to estimate return distributions and enables accurate value learning from limited data. Our key insight is that we can extract richer learning signals by enforcing Bellman consistency at every noise level. Empirically, Value Forcing improves the accuracy of estimating the return distribution, and prevents the distribution from collapsing to a single value, as occurs with prior methods. We instantiate Value Forcing with two practical value-learning algorithms: VF, an actor-critic method, and IVF, an implicit value-learning method. We evaluate both algorithms across $26$ long-horizon tasks in the offline RL setting and find that they outperform prior value-learning methods, with particularly large gains when data is limited. Our project website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[MatMoE: Matryoshka Mixture-of-Experts with Dynamic Mixed-Precision Quantization for Efficient Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01704)**  `arXiv:2611.01704`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Renjie Wei, Haochen Huang, Chuyu Qiu, Jinqi Wen, Dehao Xu, Meng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture has been widely adopted in large language models (LLMs) since it reduces the computational cost through sparse expert activation. However, the massive parameter scale of MoE models hinders their deployment on resource-constrained edge devices. Although existing mixed-precision quantization reduces the model size, they are inherently static, fixing the bit-width of each expert across decoding steps. Such static schemes yield suboptimal performance as they overlook the dynamic variation in expert importance during decoding. Furthermore, they fail to adapt to the dynamic latency constraint driven by the fluctuating system utilization. To address these challenges, we propose MatMoE, a dynamic mixed-precision quantization framework for efficient MoE inference. MatMoE features: (1) a Matryoshka MoE PTQ algorithm that enables experts to operate flexibly at multiple precisions within a stringent memory budget; (2) a floor-and-compensation quantization method that eliminates redundant memory accesses in the Matryoshka representation; and (3) a dynamic bit-width allocation strategy that determines the precision for each expert based on their routing scores to satisfy the dynamic latency requirement. We implement our MatMoE system atopthis http URLfor both CPU and GPU platforms. Extensive evaluations across three modern MoE models and challenging benchmarks demonstrate that MatMoE achieves $4.0\% \sim 18.3\%$ higher accuracy than state-of-the-art static mixed-precision method. Compared tothis http URL, MatMoE delivers $1.3\times \sim 3.7\times$ speedup on Raspberry Pi 5, and $1.2\times \sim 1.3\times$ speedup on NVIDIA RTX 4090 and Jetson AGX Orin.
  </details>

- **[World Models Need Not Be Right to Rank Policies Right: Relative Bounds and Audit Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01139)**  `arXiv:2611.01139`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ahanaf Hasan Ariq, Hasaan Ahmad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting a reinforcement-learning (RL) policy usually requires additional environment interaction, while learned world models offer cheap but potentially biased evaluations. This raises two distinct questions: can shared model errors preserve pairwise rankings despite poor absolute values, and do they make a small number of real audits more informative? We derive an exact pairwise simulation identity and a relative bound separating common-mode Bellman residuals from policy-specific error. The analysis motivates RAVEL (Relative Auditing of Value Errors in Learned models), a selective policy tournament whose eliminations use held-out real outcomes. We test 45 SAC, TD3, and PPO policies per task in DMC cheetah-run and walker-walk. At rollout horizon 500, model rankings remain strong (Kendall tau = 0.962 and 0.800), and same-run differential-error ratios are 0.674 and 0.106. Yet mean paired audit-error correlations are only 0.007 and 0.090, and pairing changes standard deviation by -2.5 percent and +1.9 percent. Simultaneous intervals attain 99.98 percent and 99.75 percent coverage, but certify only 13.2 percent and 0 percent of decisions at eight audits. RAVEL improves low-budget walker-walk regret, whereas real-only successive halving is better at the largest budget, rejecting the precommitted twofold efficiency target. Frozen-model optimization lowers real return without increasing predicted return on average. Thus ranking fidelity, useful audit covariance, and upward model exploitation are distinct properties, separated in part by closed-loop trajectory divergence.
  </details>

- **[Selecting LLM-Generated Hypotheses Raises Truth Beyond What an LLM Panel Expects: A Pre-registered Study on NAS-Bench-201 and HPOBench](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00643)**  `arXiv:2611.00643`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Shanglin Yang, Zhan Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM research-ideation systems aim for hypotheses that are both true and non-obvious, yet neither property can normally be checked. We build a testbed in which both can. Generators propose conditional interaction claims about two fully tabulated benchmarks, NAS-Bench-201 and HPOBench; every claim is verified exactly against the tables, and its non-obviousness is scored against a calibrated blind prior elicited from other LLMs. Excess truth measures how much more often a run's claims hold than this panel expects. Across a series of pre-registered stages with Claude and GPT generators, our main positive result is a simple two-stage step found by a harness optimizer: draft a large pool of claims, then select a subset in a separate call. For Sonnet on NAS-Bench-201, this step raised excess truth in every pre-registered test, including against a same-pool ablation that keeps only likely-true claims, without lowering precision. The gain comes both from credit for true claims and from avoiding near-miss false ones. In post hoc checks it held under every calibration, panel and truth rule we tried, and it exceeded random and type-filter selection from the same pools. Because kept claims recur across runs, the evidence weakens when runs and claims are resampled jointly, and it is not significant against Sonnet's own prior. Two secondary findings are also pre-registered. GPT makes real use of the results of cheap probes, beating shuffled and withheld controls, though not more efficiently than asking for more claims. Optimizing the harness for an LLM judge raised judge scores while sharply cutting precision for every generator.
  </details>

- **[AIGB-R1: Self-Evolving Generative Auto-Bidding via Hierarchical Planner-Executor Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.17281)**  `arXiv:2607.17281`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuejia Dou, Hesong Wang, Xinyu Zhang, Yuchao Ma, Tianyu Wang, Zhilin Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auto-bidding plays an essential role in online advertising, automatically adjusting bids for advertisers to optimize their commercial goals. The emerging AI-Generated Bidding (AIGB) paradigm widely adopts generative modeling to optimize bidding strategies, yet suffers from the limited mode coverage of offline datasets and inadequate task-state understanding, hindering effective exploration of optimal strategies. Large Language Models (LLMs), with prior world knowledge and reasoning capabilities, offer a promising approach to overcome these limitations. However, directly applying LLMs to auto-bidding tasks faces inherent challenges in limited numerical precision, hallucinations, and inference latency. To address these limitations, we propose AIGB-R1, a hierarchical self-evolving auto-bidding framework aiming to enhance AI-Generated Bidding via LLMs' Reasoning capabilities, comprising a high-level Planner module for macro-level strategy planning and a low-level Executor module for fine-grained decision-making. Building upon this, we design an experience-driven self-evolving loop, enabling autonomous strategy exploration and optimization from accumulated experience. We adopt a two-stage pipeline of offline pre-training and post-training alignment, and build an interactive bidding simulation environment for strategy rollout. Furthermore, we propose Decoupled Group Relative Policy Optimization (D-GRPO) to achieve end-to-end optimization via advantage decoupling. Experimental results on a large-scale public dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of AIGB-R1.
  </details>

- **[Towards Continual Motion-Language Agents: LoRA Variants for Incremental Motion Understanding and Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30266)**  `arXiv:2606.30266`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Bertram Taetz, Hugo Albuquerque Cosme da Silva, Gabriele Bleser-Taetz_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Motion-language agents must possess the bidirectional capability to both understand human movement (motion-to-text, M2T) and generate it from natural language (text-to-motion, T2M). While foundational models have achieved strong performance in static settings, autonomous agents operating in dynamic environments must continuously incorporate new motion concepts -- such as novel athletic styles or specialized gestures -- without catastrophic forgetting of previously acquired skills. We investigate the stability-plasticity trade-off in bidirectional motion-language learning under sequential task exposure. Building on a frozen large language model backbone, we introduce low-rank adaptation (LoRA) variants designed to mitigate inter-task interference. We specifically propose mixture-of-experts architectures that utilize an autoencoder-based router to select task-specific experts at inference time, so that no task-label is needed. To evaluate these methods, we establish a reproducible five-task benchmark derived from HumanML3D through semantic clustering of motion descriptions. Our experimental results demonstrate near-zero forgetting across both M2T and T2M directions while maintaining high generation and captioning quality. Furthermore, we show that hard expert selection via routing significantly outperforms soft expert blending in quality metrics, indicating that preserving expert isolation is critical for maintaining performance in our continual learning setting. Finally, we observe that a divergence between token-level accuracy and downstream generation quality may occur, highlighting the need for more comprehensive evaluation protocols in future research on lifelong motion-language agents.
  </details>

- **[Reaching Hidden Populations: Generative Frontier Planning for Peer-Referral Recruitment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08360)**  `arXiv:2606.08360`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Lingkai Kong, Hezi Jiang, Andrew Ma, Keyu Wang, Akseli Kangaslahti, Davin Choo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Peer-referral programs help connect populations at high risk for HIV and other infectious diseases with testing and prevention services when conventional outreach is ineffective. However, referral vouchers and incentives are limited, and standard operational guidelines give each participant the same fixed number of vouchers, ignoring differences in referral capacity. Motivated by our collaboration with public health practitioners, we study how to allocate a fixed voucher budget adaptively across participants and rounds to maximize total recruitment. Prior work assumes future recruits are i.i.d. from a population-level distribution and summarizes the recruitment frontier only by its size, ignoring covariate-dependent recruitment potential. We propose Generative Frontier Planning (GFP), which learns referral capacity with a censored count model and referrer-conditioned recruit covariates with a conditional diffusion model. Its structured value function yields a deterministic Bellman backup shared by fitted value iteration and planning, while inducing diminishing returns that give intra-round greedy allocation a (1-1/e)-approximation guarantee for each fixed round budget. Cross-round selection then determines how much budget to spend. We evaluate GFP on two data-calibrated recruitment environments constructed from de-identified recruiter-recruit records: a public ICPSR respondent-driven sampling dataset and a dataset provided by FHI 360, an international global health nonprofit. GFP recruits up to 21.5% more individuals than the strongest baseline under the same budget, showing its potential for resource-limited peer-referral programs.
  </details>

- **[Adversarial Robustness of Activation Steering in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07696)**  `arXiv:2606.07696`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Kien Le, Thai Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering has become a popular training-free method to control LLMs' behavior by injecting precomputed direction vectors into the model's residual stream at inference time. Yet, its robustness to realistic input variation remains under-explored. We present the first systematic evaluation of activation steering robustness under adversarial text perturbations on the inputs, covering four extraction methods, three attack strategies, six personas from the Anthropic Model-Written Evaluations dataset, and five models ranging from 1.5B to 30B parameters. Attacks succeed broadly across all settings: directional robustness drops by up to 64\%, post-attack confidence collapses near or below 0.25 across all methods and models, and steering strength degrades on nearly every steerable input. Layer selection is equally fragile, with the optimal layer identified by an automated method on clean inputs shifting by up to 17 positions under perturbation, a failure that compounds the vector-level breakdown. Extracting vectors from adversarially perturbed inputs partially recovers steerability for PCA and MD on mid-to-large models, but they consistently fail to locate the improved optimal layer, limiting the practical benefit of this mitigation. Together, these findings reveal that the brittleness of activation steering is structural rather than method-specific, and that current layer selection strategies are not robust enough for real-world deployment. Our code is available at \href{this https URL}{this http URL}.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Auto-Harness: Sustained Self-Improvement for Agentic System Deployment on Open-Ended Task Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01770)**  `arXiv:2606.01770`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zewen Liu, Zhan Shi, Yisi Sang, Bing He, Minhua Lin, Tianxin Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auto-harness systems improve LLM agents by adapting prompts, skills, tools, and supporting infrastructure from execution feedback. Sustaining these gains on open-ended task streams requires handling growing histories, heterogeneous tasks, and shifting distributions. We introduce Adaptive Auto-Harness, a unified framework that separates persistent evolution workspaces from deployable harnesses and supports interchangeable evolution procedures. We compare configurations adapted from five prior approaches on recorded streams of prediction markets, security challenges, and event forecasts. With the same Sonnet solver, our full system reaches 80.9\% market all-task accuracy and 50.2\% security Pass@1, versus 22.2\% and 37.2\% without evolution; market accuracy also exceeds a market-following control's 72.0\%. Our analyses show that artifact accumulation does not guarantee sustained gains, and knowledge that transfers across tasks can still lose value as the task distribution changes. Continued evolution can therefore help preserve earlier gains through revision of accumulated knowledge. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Curriculum Learning for Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.26315)**  `arXiv:2605.26315`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sandeep Kumar, Virginia Smith, Chhavi Yadav_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a widely used approach for safety alignment that aims to reduce harmful behaviors in large language models. However, prior work shows that it can be brittle and exhibits poor out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization. In this paper we investigate whether Curriculum Learning can improve the robustness of DPO-based safety alignment. We propose Staged-Competence, a curriculum-based framework that organizes preference data by difficulty, employs competence-based sampling, and progressively updates the reference model during training. Averaged across three model families and three training seeds, Staged-Competence reduces OOD harmful response rates by 13.6 percentage points and attack success rates across five jailbreak attacks by 16.5 percentage points, while preserving general capabilities and maintaining near-zero over-refusal. We further show that Staged-Competence: (1) matches baseline safety performance with only 75% of the training data, demonstrating improved data efficiency and (2) yields better separation between safe and unsafe responses. Staged-Competence is agnostic to the underlying policy optimization loss and can extend to other DPO variants and alignment domains beyond safety. Additionally, we find multiple inconsistencies in standard RLHF datasets and create a cleaner combined version, named PiKacHHU. Our code is available atthis https URLand the dataset atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LEAD: Length-Efficient Adaptive and Dynamic Reasoning for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09806)**  `arXiv:2605.09806`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Songtao Wei, Yi Li, Zhikai Li, Xu Hu, Yuede Ji, Guanpeng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large reasoning models, such as OpenAI o1 and DeepSeek-R1, tend to become increasingly verbose as their reasoning capabilities improve. These inflated Chain-of-Thought (CoT) trajectories often exceed what the underlying problems require, wasting compute, latency, and context budgets. While introducing length-based efficiency rewards during reinforcement learning offers a natural remedy, existing methods struggle with two fundamental challenges: the optimal balance between correctness and efficiency is non-stationary throughout training, and intrinsic reasoning budgets vary drastically across problems. Relying on static reward weights and global length constraints inevitably forces a compromise between degraded accuracy and unrealized compression. To overcome these limitations, we propose LEAD (Length-Efficient Adaptive and Dynamic reasoning), a method that replaces static heuristics with online, self-adaptive mechanisms. LEAD dynamically calibrates the correctness--efficiency trade-off at each step using a Potential-Scaled Instability, directing optimization capacity to the most informative learning signal. Furthermore, it estimates an adaptive per-problem target length online based on the model's own correct rollouts, applying a symmetric efficiency reward that penalizes both overthinking and over-compression. Evaluated on five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, LEAD achieves the highest accuracy and Accuracy-Efficiency Score among RL-trained efficient-reasoning methods in our matched comparisons while producing substantially shorter outputs than the base model.
  </details>

- **[Understanding, Predicting, and Steering LLM Behavior via Representational Effective Theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09294)**  `arXiv:2605.09294`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Muhammed Ustaomeroglu, Guannan Qu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) assist with writing, teaching, programming and decision-making, yet the computations underlying their behaviour remain largely opaque. Full access to every weight and activation does not by itself yield understanding: a microscopic description need not reveal how computation is organized at the level that governs behaviour. Complex systems are often understood instead through a few macroscopic variables whose dynamics capture the behaviour of interest, as temperature and pressure do for molecular motion in thermodynamics. Here we show that such an effective description of LLM computation can be learned directly from data. We introduce Representational Effective Theory (RET), a framework that learns low-dimensional macrostates and their dynamics from a model's hidden-state trajectories. These macrostates exhibit approximately closed dynamics and trace interpretable trajectories through reasoning. They also predict sycophancy and alignment faking early in generation, while steering the model towards selected macrostates produces corresponding changes in its reasoning. Together, these results show that LLM computation admits useful macroscopic descriptions that support interpretation, prediction and control.
  </details>

- **[Fooling Reward Models with Gibberish: Adversarial Examples Beyond the Semantic Regime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02686)**  `arXiv:2604.02686`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuheng Zhang, Mingyue Huo, Minghao Zhu, Mengxue Zhang, Nan Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reward models (RMs) are widely used as optimization targets in reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF). We show that leading RMs can assign higher scores to gibberish than to meaningful reference answers. To systematically discover such inputs, we introduce Token Mapping Perturbation Attack (TOMPA), a framework that uses a perturbed token mapping to extend an attack LLM's search beyond natural language. The mapping changes the linguistic meaning of the attack LLM's natural language responses, producing non-linguistic text for the target RM to score. We then use these scalar scores as rewards to train the attack LLM through reinforcement learning to discover non-linguistic adversarial inputs. Experiments against Llama and Qwen RMs demonstrate such attacks using two instantiations: identity token mapping across different tokenizers and random permutation with a shared tokenizer. Against Skywork-Reward-V2-Llama-3.1-8B, ranked first on RewardBench 2, the two mappings achieve mean rewards of +33.64 and +43.78, respectively, compared with +17.48 for GPT-5 reference answers. Their attack success rates are 97.6% and 100%, measured as the percentages of individual responses scoring above their corresponding GPT-5 references. These results show that strong benchmark performance does not ensure robustness to non-linguistic adversarial inputs, exposing a fundamental vulnerability in using RMs as optimization targets.
  </details>

- **[Pretraining Data Scale Reverses the Muon-AdamW Fine-Tuning Gap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03633)**  `arXiv:2611.03633`  `cs.LG`  
  _Catherine Li, Jingchu Gai, Andrej Risteski, Jacob Mitchell Springer, Aditi Raghunathan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Muon has recently emerged as a promising optimizer for pretraining large language models, offering a speedup over AdamW in minimizing pretraining loss. However, it remains unclear whether Muon's advantage persists after fine-tuning. In this paper, we study the downstream behavior of Muon and AdamW and uncover a surprising trend: beyond a sufficiently large pretraining data budget, AdamW outperforms Muon after fine-tuning. Moreover, as model size increases, AdamW overtakes Muon at progressively smaller pretraining token-parameter ratios. To explain this reversal, we view fine-tuning as a perturbation of the outputs and introduce the margin-to-spread ratio, which measures how far the correct logit lies above the wrong ones relative to how widely the wrong logits are spread. In language models, Muon has the larger margin-to-spread ratio at small data budgets and AdamW at large ones, mirroring the fine-tuning trend. In a linear associative memory, we prove that after one update the margin-to-spread ratio of gradient descent gains on that of Muon as the data budget grows, and simulations show that this trend persists after extended training.
  </details>

- **[Not All Reasoning Generalizes: A Case Study on Learning Generalizable Reasoning Strategies in RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03627)**  `arXiv:2611.03627`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziqi Pang, Yu-Xiong Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) can enable generalizable improvement for large language model (LLM) reasoning. This paper studies how to incentivize such generalization via reasoning strategies. We study this question with a controllable equation-solving framework: finding multiple inputs x for equations f(x)=y, where f is a non-bijective string function. This setting makes reasoning strategies observable while supporting a clear definition of easy-to-hard out-of-distribution (OOD) shifts, e.g., more solutions, longer y, etc. We uncover a subtle RLVR failure: it teaches the model to solve in-distribution tasks with high accuracy and structured step-by-step derivation, yet under OOD shifts, the same model abandons derivation and collapses to ineffective brute-force enumeration, causing sharp accuracy drops. This observation reveals the deeper challenges of RLVR generalization as it is neither incapable base model nor reward hacking, since the model succeeds in-distribution with the correct reasoning strategy. The key to mitigating this failure is an indirect intervention that surprisingly helps: co-training with bijective inversion tasks. Although these bijective tasks are actually not uniformly harder and even further from the non-bijective OOD evaluation, they make derivation more certain and substantially restore the correct strategy and accuracy under OOD shifts.
