This effect further motivates our theoretical analysis: outcome-only RLVR can shape reasoning processes through implicitly crediting the effective reasoning strategies more, even without process supervision. To conclude, our analysis framework and findings systematically reveal the crucial role and incentivization of reasoning strategies in RLVR generalization under OOD shifts. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Ask, Don't Equip: Question-Driven Coding Agents for Deep Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03517)**  `arXiv:2611.03517`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gabriele Oliaro, Jaeseong Lee, Zhaoyuan Su, Sreeram Vennam, Zhihao Jia, Samyam Rajbhandari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents for GPU kernel optimization sit at two ends of a structure-freedom tradeoff. Scripted agents fix what the model does at each step, via a learned policy, an optimization menu, or fixed roles, and are sample-efficient where the recipe applies and stall where it does not. Open-ended agents let a self-directed model mutate a population of kernels and pay for the freedom in trials: thousands of evaluations, repeated edits, and wrong candidates. Both respond to hard targets by equipping the agent with more: parsed profiler output, disassembly analysis, retrieval over documentation and libraries, and stores of lessons. We argue that both lack direction, a hypothesis about where the remaining gap is, and that a short question asked at the right moment supplies it without narrowing what the agent may do next. We present KernelSeek, a question-driven agent that makes an existing CUDA kernel faster with no operator-specific knowledge and no faster implementation to imitate: a general-purpose coding agent, a minimal harness that enforces a verifiable goal, and questions at decision points, first from a human, then from a bank the agent applies to itself. On the same task, evaluator, hardware, and week, a tool-rich autonomous agent built in the prevailing style plateaued at 0.50x FlashAttention-3 after 133 model-driven steps; KernelSeek reached 1.13x. It then produced dependency-free FlashAttention forward kernels for Hopper and Blackwell, single CUDA files with no CUTLASS, CuTe, cuBLAS, or cuDNN, correct on all 182 scenarios per architecture and 1.16x FlashAttention-3 and 1.46x FlashAttention-4 (geometric mean). Across 162 human messages and 11.5K agent tool calls, every large gain followed a question that moved the agent to a different layer of the system or rejected a claimed limit, never one that supplied information. We release the kernels, harness, question bank, and all traces.
  </details>

- **[A Living Leaderboard for Graph Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03433)**  `arXiv:2611.03433`  `cs.LG`  
  _Martin Ritzert_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Graph machine learning benchmarking results are scattered across papers that use quite different sets of datasets and baseline methods, making it difficult to tell whether a reported gain reflects architectural progress or merely an outdated comparison or weakly tuned baseline. We introduce a living, graph-learning-specific leaderboard that organizes reported results by dataset, putting new results into context. As of 15 October 2026 our LLM-based extraction pipeline extracted 27,723 eligible results across 111 datasets from 885 peer-reviewed papers. Our analysis of these results quantifies concerns about graph-learning benchmarking: legacy datasets are still actively used and many result tables contain baseline results that are drastically lower than those already available in the literature, e.g. 45% Amazon-ratings tables and 37% for Peptides-struct. The leaderboard allows to quickly compare new results against compatible prior results, aiming to improve the overall quality of graph learning benchmarking.
  </details>

- **[ENGINE: Endogenous Variational MultiScale Optimization for Zeroth-Order LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03293)**  `arXiv:2611.03293`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhuoli Ouyang, Changxi Chi, Siyuan Li, Tailin Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization enables memory-efficient large language model (LLM) fine-tuning, and recent methods incorporate curvature-aware strategies. However, in the high-dimensional parameter space of LLMs, these methods inherently suffer from exploding gradient variance and prohibitive costs. Conversely, confining updates to a low-dimensional subspace reduces variance but discards critical high-dimensional information, degrading performance. This creates a fundamental dilemma: full-space optimization is informative but has high variance, while subspace optimization has low variance but loses information. To break this trade-off, we propose ENGINE (Endogenous Variational MultiScale). Inspired by computational mechanics, we adapt static Variational MultiScale (VMS) theory to dynamic optimization, treating low- and high-dimensional spaces as resolved coarse and unresolved fine scales. Through a multigrid-style V-cycle, ENGINE alternates between spaces, dynamically probing fine-grained information in the high-dimensional space and injecting it to correct the efficient low-dimensional sampling process. Under appropriate assumptions, statistical analysis characterizes variance reduction and correction of curvature-induced bias, and convergence analysis establishes a stationarity bound for the corrected coarse updates. Evaluations on standard LLM benchmarks show that ENGINE achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence than existing ZO methods, achieving up to a $2.61\times$ speed-up in forward passes with zero additional peak memory overhead. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Cost of Mismatch: Noise Amplification in Zeroth-Order Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03260)**  `arXiv:2611.03260`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lianmin Chen, Junbin Qiu, Chenxing Wei, Yao Shu, Kun He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zeroth-order (ZO) optimization has emerged as a promising approach for memory-efficient large language model (LLM) post-training, yet its reliable application to reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) remains largely unexplored. Motivated by the distinct data regimes of on-policy RL and fixed-data optimization, we develop a generic variance-theoretic framework to investigate what limits the reliability of ZO gradient estimation. Our analysis reveals that estimation reliability is structurally determined by whether the two finite-difference evaluations are performed on matched data. On-policy reinforcement learning (RL) under the mismatched-data regime produces a $1/\mu^2$ oracle-noise amplification that sets an irreducible noise floor as the perturbation scale $\mu$ decreases, whereas Boltzmann Targeted SFT (BOLT) under the matched-data regime eliminates this amplification via noise cancellation. We further derive convergence guarantees with explicit constants that cleanly separate the two regimes. These results naturally lead to ZO-BOLT, which applies ZO optimization to BOLT's RLVR-aligned weighted-SFT objective. Controlled perturbation-scale sweeps and matched-versus-mismatched end-to-end comparisons validate the predicted effect of data matching. Experiments across three model variants and two mathematical reasoning benchmarks further show that ZO-BOLT consistently improves over the pretrained baseline while retaining a near-inference memory footprint. The code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CISA: Retrofitting Dense Attention Models with Chunk-Indexed Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03200)**  `arXiv:2611.03200`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammed Dabbah, Izhak Golan, Oren Tropp, Daniel Afrimi, Vladimir Anisimov, Tomer Asida, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context LLM inference is costly because attention repeatedly reads a growing key-value (KV) history. We introduce Chunk-Indexed Sparse Attention (CISA), which retrofits pretrained dense attention models without gradient updates. CISA selects fixed-size KV chunks using compact summaries of existing keys, avoiding token-level indexing and a separate full-length index cache. It uses one indexing query per KV group and shares selected chunks across queries in that group. During prefill, QShare reuses selections across adjacent queries to reduce indexing cost and improve KV reuse and GPU utilization. We evaluate CISA on NVIDIA Nemotron 3 Nano and Ultra, and Inkling-Small. In one-layer BF16 benchmarks at batch size 32 using Ultra's per-device attention dimensions, core attention is 11.7x faster for prefill queries near the end of a 1M-token prompt and 13.0x faster during decode. Including chunk scoring, selection, and all other measured GPU work, speedups are 8.6x and 3.0x. On Ultra NVFP4 with a 4,096-chunk budget, aggregate scores stay within 1.5 percentage points of dense attention on MRCR, LongBench v2, and GraphWalks. RULER sweeps on Nano and Ultra BF16 up to one million tokens show scores within 2.05 percentage points of dense attention with 8-16x fewer KV reads. Serving million-token prompts with 32 concurrent requests and 4K output tokens per request, CISA achieves 2.29x the end-to-end throughput of dense attention using Nemotron 3 Ultra NVFP4 on four NVIDIA B300 GPUs. Selecting fewer chunks increases throughput to 2.61x; in separate GraphWalks evaluations, the smaller budget widens the aggregate score gap from 1.49 to 3.59 percentage points. These results expose a practical quality-speed tradeoff without sparse training.
  </details>

- **[Controlling Sharpening in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03146)**  `arXiv:2611.03146`  `cs.LG`  
  _Simon Matrenok, Nicolas Menet, Nicolas Flammarion, Andreas Krause_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM post-training via reinforcement learning has shown consistent improvements in accuracy at the cost of reducing behavioral coverage - a phenomenon known as sharpening that can stall progress on difficult tasks. Repeated sampling objectives such as $\mathrm{pass@k}$ and $\mathrm{max@k}$ are known to mitigate sharpening, but the mechanism behind it remains poorly understood. Moreover, existing approaches for $\mathrm{@k}$ optimization use dedicated algorithms, and no general framework exists for adapting established policy optimization methods from $\mathrm{@}1$ to $\mathrm{@k}$. We derive the optimal policies for KL-regularized $\mathrm{pass@k}$ and $\mathrm{max@k}$ objectives in closed form. Our explicit solutions reveal that $\mathrm{@k}$ optimization naturally redistributes learning pressure based on prompt difficulty, directing it toward harder problems. Crucially, we show that standard $\mathrm{@}1$ objectives achieve these exact same optimal policies through prompt-adaptive regularization or reward transformation. Ultimately, this yields an optimizer-independent explanation for why $\mathrm{@k}$ objectives reduce sharpening on easy tasks while prioritizing success on harder ones. Building on these insights, we introduce $\mathrm{@k}$ Adaptive Pressure for Policy Optimization (KAPPO). Following a one-time reference calibration, KAPPO seamlessly adapts existing $\mathrm{@}1$ methods to $\mathrm{@k}$ via prompt-adaptive regularization or reward transformation, without otherwise altering their training procedures. Experiments with several RL methods on mathematical reasoning and code generation demonstrate that KAPPO provides direct control over sharpening. Crucially, we demonstrate that $\mathrm{@k}$ pressure can be fully realized with single completion training, matching the performance of multi-completion $\mathrm{@k}$ optimizers at a fraction of the cost.
  </details>

- **[SHq: Learnable Grids for LLM Weight Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02966)**  `arXiv:2611.02966`  `cs.LG`  
  _Michael Helcig, Earl Killian, Dan Alistarh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block-scaled lookup-table (LUT) formats, which represent weights as indices into a small value set (the grid), have become standard for low-bit LLM quantization, ranging from NF4 and BOF4 to the 3-bit LUT instruction in NVIDIA Rubin. While these formats leave the grid definition flexible, existing implementations rely on fixed grids derived either from numerical data types (INT, FP) or assumed weight distributions (NF, BOF). We demonstrate that no single fixed grid is universally optimal; the best fixed grid varies with bit-width: BOFS at 2 bits, NF at 4 and 5 bits, and INT at 6 bits. We introduce SHq, a closed-form family of grids parameterized by two critical degrees of freedom: a companding strength {\alpha} that interpolates level spacing from uniform to floating-point-like, and an offset c that introduces asymmetry. This offset allows the sign bit of the FP8 block scale, otherwise unused, to select between the grid and its mirror image, up to doubling the number of distinct values available per block. SHq modifies only the lookup table contents and serves as a drop-in replacement for the grid in any such format. Across five models ranging from 3B to 35B parameters, including a mixture-of-experts architecture, evaluated at 2-6 bits using RTN and GPTQ, SHq achieves the lowest KL divergence in every setting at 3 and 4 bits and in most settings at 2, 5 and 6 bits. It outperforms the best fixed grid by up to 34% and remains superior under mixed-precision configurations. At 4.5 bits per weight, the SHq format reduces the KL divergence of NVFP4 by 41-67% while improving downstream accuracy. Grid families incorporating both spacing and offset parameters perform comparably, whereas those lacking an offset exhibit clearly higher error, confirming that these two degrees of freedom drive the observed gains. We therefore propose SHq as the standard grid for block-scaled lookup-table formats.
  </details>

- **[Evolving Explanations: Complexity-Adaptive Refinement of Faithful Explanations for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02634)**  `arXiv:2611.02634`  `cs.LG`  
  _Elisabeth Mayrhuber, Ting Hu, Stephan Winkler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language explanations generated by large language models (LLMs) for machine learning models often seem fluent but are not necessarily faithful to the underlying model. We formalize this mismatch as a faithfulness gap and propose a complexity-adaptive refinement framework for symbolic regression that iteratively generates and validates explanations until numerical agreement with the target expression is achieved. The approach follows a two-stage pipeline: an explainer LLM produces textual descriptions at increasing levels of detail, while an independent validator LLM extracts a symbolic expression that is compared against the original model. This process is framed as a discrete search over explanation space, where complexity is minimized subject to a faithfulness constraint. We evaluate the method on real-world and synthetic datasets, with a total of 250 runs across varying model complexities. The explanation refinement loop achieves an overall pass rate of 84.0%, with most explanations converging at low complexity levels and only a small fraction requiring detailed descriptions. Our results demonstrate that faithful explanations can be obtained with minimal necessary complexity by explicitly closing the explanation loop through symbolic validation.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Localization of Knowledge Negation for Continual LLM Unlearning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02620)**  `arXiv:2611.02620`  `cs.LG`  
  _Abudukelimu Wuerkaixi, Qizhou Wang, Sen Cui, Wutong Xu, Bo Han, Gang Niu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the growing deployment of large language models (LLMs) across diverse domains, concerns regarding their safety have grown substantially. LLM unlearning has emerged as a pivotal approach to removing harmful or unlawful content while maintaining utility. Despite increasing interest, the challenges of continual unlearning, which is common in real-world scenarios, remain underexplored. Successive unlearning tasks often lead to intensified utility degradation. To effectively unlearn targeted knowledge while preserving LLM utility, it is essential to minimize changes in model parameters by selectively updating those linked to the target knowledge, thereby ensuring other knowledge remains unaffected. Building on the task vector framework, we propose a new method named ALKN (Adaptive Localization of Knowledge Negation), which uses dynamic masking to sparsify training gradients and adaptively adjusts unlearning intensity based on inter-task relationships. Comprehensive experiments across three well-established LLM unlearning datasets demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms baseline methods in both unlearning effectiveness and utility retention under continual unlearning settings.
  </details>

- **[SpecPilot: Autonomous Algorithm Discovery and Systems Optimization for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02501)**  `arXiv:2611.02501`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kunjun Li, Wenhao Chai, Yihao Liang, Yuezhou Hu, Shuyao Xu, Yi Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding accelerates memory-bound LLM decoding with a lightweight drafter, and parallel drafters extend this speedup by drafting a whole block in one forward pass. However, it remains unclear whether their released systems (stock) attain the full speedup that parallel drafting permits. We identify three challenges. First, with one request, each step accepts few tokens but reads every target weight. Second, with batching, per-request verification work roughly halves the speedup over autoregressive (AR) decoding. Third, speculative decoding is deployment-specific, and no universal optimization fits every serving regime, drafter, target model and GPU. To address these challenges, we present SpecPilot, an autonomous agentic loop that discovers algorithms and systems optimizations for each deployment. In each round, a cost model locates the largest gaps between the current system and its ceiling. Parallel agents explore directions and integrate the successful ones. Token forcing checks every output token against stock to detect reward hacking. A structured memory carries validated mechanisms to new rounds and deployments. SpecPilot discovered a general lossless 12-bit weight format, tree verification for hybrid recurrent targets, two-level verification and three batching mechanisms. We evaluate it across four target models, three drafters, two GPU platforms and five benchmarks. Without training or quality loss, it raises the speedup over AR from stock's 4.46$\times$ to 10.87$\times$ with one request, and from 1.94$\times$ to 3.93$\times$ with batching.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Caches for LLM Web Services Leave Their Error Budget Unspent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02498)**  `arXiv:2611.02498`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hanshuai Cui, Qianli Ma, Zhiqing Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web services built on large language models (LLMs) receive many paraphrases of earlier queries, which a semantic cache answers from stored responses. A reused answer can be wrong, so wrong answers must stay below a share $\delta$ of the queries. Existing caches reuse above a fixed similarity threshold or, like vCache, learn a per-entry rule that bounds each query's error by $\delta$. However, fixed thresholds break the budget on new streams, and per-entry rules need labels on every entry before reusing it. We find an unspent error budget, as vCache realizes only 12-55% of $\delta$ on three public query streams. Surplus spends this budget where reuse is most likely correct. It ranks queries by a risk score shared across paraphrase clusters and opens risk bins as far as an audited error estimate allows. Free labels from every LLM call and a ledger of realized errors keep this threshold safe under drift. On five streams, including ORCAS search queries, Surplus holds $\delta$ in every test cell, which no baseline does. On the shuffled streams it serves 40% more hits than vCache, and its pooled hit rate beats the best baseline holding $\delta$ in each cell by 4.10 points. In a vLLM testbed it cuts the mean latency by 31.59% at the highest load, against 23.67% for vCache.
  </details>

- **[Fragility Is Not Reducible to Hardness: Conditional Causal Difficulty for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02065)**  `arXiv:2611.02065`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad Reza Rezaei, Dingxian Wang, Nikhil Verma, Sajjad Pakdamansavoji, Andrew Rabinovich, Zhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models show large and seemingly arbitrary performance drops when a task is rewritten in ways that leave its requirements untouched. We recast task difficulty as a causal quantity: the treatment effect of meaning-preserving perturbations on a model. A meaning-preserving rewrite can only be observed to hurt a task the model can already solve, so we place the estimand on the principal stratum where the degradation occurs: the conditional causal difficulty (CCD), the probability that a randomly drawn twin breaks a solvable task. Because harder tasks tend to be more phrasing-sensitive, complexity enters as an effect modifier rather than a confounder, and we residualize it out to obtain complexity-residualized difficulty (CRD); zero for a task exactly as fragile as its complexity predicts, positive for anomalously fragile ones. Current benchmarks do not supply the data CCD requires, so we introduce GlassGSM and GlassMATH, augmenting GSM8K and MATH with twins built from perturbation families, raising fragility on GlassGSM by $\approx 12\times$ over GSM-IC-style distractors on the same problems ($3.7\%\to44.9\%$) while keeping the answer key unchanged. Our predictor Frail learns the per-perturbation break probability and reaches $0.613{\pm}0.023$ of the suite reliability ceiling on GlassGSM and $0.557{\pm}0.023$ on GlassMATH, against $0.171$/$0.401$ for a surface-feature baseline and $0.444$/$0.463$ for supervised baselines such as VAP3, and, at a $5\%$ curation budget on GlassGSM, doubles random recall of fragile items, matching an oracle ranking. Additionally, cross-fitted residualization leaves CRD uncorrelated with the declared proxy in practice.Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Same Function, Different Gradients: Evaluating Gradient Reliability under Reparameterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02052)**  `arXiv:2611.02052`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bum Jun Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Gradients are no longer used only to train models: Gradient magnitudes are now measurement instruments, used to rank attention heads for pruning, to decide per-example clipping in private training, to allocate adapter rank budgets, and to debug models. Across these workflows, larger gradient magnitudes are taken to mean greater importance. Even for a fixed input--output function, these gradients are computed in one of many possible parameter coordinate systems. For example, attention scores depend on the query and key projections only through the paired product $W_QW_K^\top$. Scaling a head's query projection by $c$ and the corresponding key projection by $1/c$ therefore leaves every output logit unchanged. This change in parameter coordinates rescales the corresponding query and key gradient norms by $1/c$ and $c$, respectively. To test whether gradient-based decisions remain unchanged under these coordinate changes, we introduce Gradient Reliability under Reparameterization (GRR). GRR rewrites a trained model in new parameter coordinates that compute exactly the same function, verifies that predictions are unchanged, and then tests whether the decisions derived from the gradients are unchanged too. Our audits span GLUE classifiers, a ViT on CIFAR-10, GPT-2-medium and GPT-2-large, a 1.1B-parameter Llama-style model on WikiText-2, and six trained LoRA adapters. Empirically, predictions are preserved throughout, but both the gradients and the gradient-based decisions change. Across 66 GLUE attention audits, the median top-3 overlap of raw head-gradient rankings is only $1/3$, and rankings and pruning masks change even under the mildest rescalings we test. In the largest of 14 global-pruning audits, the shifted ranking selects head masks that differ in four of 22 pruned heads and reach $50.92\%$ and $94.84\%$ validation accuracy on one ELECTRA SST-2 model.
  </details>

- **[Sensitivity Inflation from Cross-Record Precision Routing in Private Quantized Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01411)**  `arXiv:2611.01411`  `cs.LG`  
  _Annan Zhang, Shengjie Song, Wenshuai Yao, Lihao Zhang, Tianmiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Private fine-tuning of large language models increasingly pairs DP-SGD with low-rank adapters on frozen, quantized backbones, and every such stack inherits one unstated premise: frozen layers keep each record's clipped gradient a function of that record alone. We show that LLM.int8 breaks this premise. It routes outlier activation columns to a higher-precision path using a union over the whole batch, so one record decides how every other record's activations are computed, even though the weights are frozen and the quantization scales are per row. We bound the resulting sensitivity inflation, state a record-isolation contract that restores the standard bound, and define record-owned precision dispatch that meets it with the same GEMMs as batched LLM.int8, so isolation needs no extra arithmetic. We then audit the native CUDA stack (bitsandbytes, PEFT, Opacus) against four matched controls. A constructed LoRA-Opacus witness reaches $15.0\times$ the add/remove bound. On pretrained OPT-125M with natural text, other records alter 20% of record-layer precision splits at batch size 16. An exploratory re-clipping analysis finds 10 of 64 candidate neighbors above the bound once every gradient is clipped; the top pair, revalidated natively, reaches $1.065C$ while every control stays within $C$, and forcing one layer's outlier mask removes the excess. A two-million-draw audit of the released adapters yields $\varepsilon$ lower bounds ($3.25$ at $\delta=10^{-5}$) below the advertised guarantee ($4.38$). Resolving an excess this small at the release level would take about 200 times more draws, whereas checking the sensitivity contract directly exposes it in a few native runs, which makes contract checking the practical audit for private quantized training.
  </details>

- **[Unified dynamics of training, learning, inference, and reinforcement in neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01345)**  `arXiv:2611.01345`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mian Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite their remarkable success, neural networks remain largely black boxes, and the mechanisms underlying their behavior are still poorly understood. Nine nanoGPT experiments established training-learning-inference relations: realized updates elicit state-dependent finite-amplitude responses; learning persistently reorganizes distributed functional support, with target-specific readout-boundary crossings determining capability transitions; inference recruits learned support without changing the learned state. Factors identified in the fifth experiment yielded a direct predictor of post-update capability transitions, achieving 91.43% accuracy and 91.49% four-way macro recall across 5,088 targets. We tested these relations and prediction across five additional neural architectures. In a seventh system, concentrated correct feedback eroded previously mastered skills; rebalanced feedback restored their accuracy from 70.31% to 99.48%. Linking inference consequences to subsequent training closes these relations into unified training-learning-inference-reinforcement dynamics.
  </details>

- **[AgentCBO: Causal Belief Routing for Bayesian Optimization under Unknown Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01317)**  `arXiv:2611.01317`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qiyu Wei, Xin Zhang, Richard Allmendinger, Mauricio A Álvarez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Causal Bayesian optimization (CBO) typically relies on the unrealistic assumption of a perfectly known causal graph. Recent methods relax this constraint by coupling optimization with structure learning, but they often waste sample budgets on structural uncertainties that are irrelevant to the optimizer. In this paper, we formalize routing-equivalence to shift the objective from full causal-graph recovery to causal belief routing, where structural differences only matter when they alter optimizer-facing objects such as admissible exploration sets and do-priors. Furthermore, we propose agentcbo, a graph-belief routing framework that incorporates LLM causal priors through guarded proposal and commit mechanisms. Specifically, a Language-Prior Agent combines LLM beliefs with constraint-based discovery to extract a protected causal backbone alongside residual uncertain edges. A Calibration Agent and a Residual Repair Agent then refine only these uncertain structures through score-gated bounded local edits. Finally, a deterministic commit gate evaluates candidate updates using routing-proxy checks before allowing them to influence intervention selection. Across ten benchmarks, agentcbo performs comparably to known-graph CBO, demonstrating that guarded LLM priors can improve fixed-budget CBO performance.
  </details>

- **[TRIAGE: Reliability- and Load-Conditioned Escalation for Two-Tier LLM Serving with a Semantic Evidence Judge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01174)**  `arXiv:2611.01174`  `cs.LG`  
  _Basavaraj R M, Aryan Bharat Kumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Two-tier LLM serving escalates selected requests from a small model to a large one, but prompt-based routers cannot see whether retrieved evidence supports an answer, and they treat the GPU cost of an escalation as constant. We study TRIAGE, which conditions escalation on a post-generation reliability state and on a load-dependent GPU price, and we report both what held and what did not on one A40 serving Qwen2.5-7B and 32B (AWQ) under VLLM. Escalation cost rose 21.9x from 1 to 10 requests/s but showed no monotone dependence on KV-cache headroom, and a pre-registered claim that the reliability state detects corrupted evidence (AUROC>=0.70) was falsified at 0.571, although any prompt-only router scores exactly 0.500 by construction. Post hoc, a semantic evidence judge raised detection to 0.846 (0.926 on never-served items) and predicted wrong small-model answers at AUROC 0.90-0.96 on RGB and three RouterBench model pairs; the resulting cascade stayed within about 3 pp of GPT-4 at 79.5% lower API cost for GPT-3.5->GPT-4, yet fell below random routing for open-model pairs with 2-3x price ratios. End to end, the 7B->32B pair showed only a +2.88 pp held-out gap, so the allocator rightly declined to escalate and the headline is a null that a small pre-launch gate failed to expose.
  </details>

- **[Scute: Exact Batched Serving for PEFT Adapters Beyond LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01089)**  `arXiv:2611.01089`  `cs.LG`  
  _Thomas Walker, Richard Baraniuk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing serving systems for large language models batch requests for many different LoRA adapters on one GPU. However, these systems reject adapters from other parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as DoRA, (IA), VeRA and OFT. One solution to this is to convert these adapters to LoRA, but this induces a significant memory and accuracy cost. Instead, we show that, under some modifications, a batched LoRA kernel can serve all of these methods exactly and efficiently. We achieve this by writing each method in one normal form, a LoRA computation with six slots for an adapter's own vectors and matrices. Some slots can merge into an adapter's weights at load time; the others remain live and induce a cost. We give ways to efficiently pay this cost and predict it from a zero-parameter byte model. We package this system, Scute, and build it on top of vLLM. Scute serves all five methods, coming as checkpoints from the Hugging Face PEFT library, in one batch, and it matches that library with a mean error of at most 1.5% of each adapter's effect. Under a burst of requests on an RTX A6000, a mix of these methods takes 5-17% more decode time than the same number of LoRA adapters. Furthermore, using Scute, the same GPU can serve 64 to 1024 (IA) and VeRA adapters at 1.5x to at least 2.5x the request rate of their LoRA conversions.
  </details>

- **[Jet-QSparse: Accelerating Agentic LLM Inference with 4-Bit Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01022)**  `arXiv:2611.01022`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haozhan Tang, Hao Kang, Meng Lu, Yecheng Wu, Zerui Wang, Seokho Han, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon reasoning and agentic workloads strain KV-cache capacity and decode attention throughput. We present Jet-QSparse, a training-free method that combines compact NVFP4 cache storage with native FP4 query-key computation on Blackwell tensor cores. A dense scan scores the full key history and selects 128-token blocks for sparse FP16 value aggregation with FP32 accumulation, preserving higher precision in the output-forming step while enabling contiguous tile loads. Calibrated key centering and at most two BF16 pages per sequence and layer address quantization error; FP8 prefill uses a temporary arena while the persistent cache is packed directly from BF16 activations. Across four hybrid LLMs, a budget of 128 blocks per KV head shows no detected accuracy loss on the evaluated mathematical reasoning, coding, and agentic benchmarks, although some smaller budgets incur losses. With 64 blocks, Qwen3.8-27B achieves a $1.94\times$ end-to-end decode speedup over dense BF16 at 128K context and batch size 16 on one GB300, and the measured Qwen KV pools provide $2.58$-$2.76\times$ BF16's token capacity.
  </details>

- **[GateTrim: Adaptive-Rank Quantization for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00654)**  `arXiv:2611.00654`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hongwei Ren, Jincheng Ren, Yuchuan Zhou, Jingchun Ling_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce GateTrim, an adaptive-rank post-training quantization method for large language models that learns matrix-specific ranks through gate optimization and threshold-based rank selection. GateTrim standardizes the low-rank factors of a quantized low-rank--residual representation, learns a separate gate vector for each target weight matrix, and uses a shared threshold to automatically determine its retained rank. The resulting ranks can differ across matrices, and the compact quantized representations are subsequently reconstructed. We evaluate GateTrim under W4A8 on Qwen3-4B/8B/14B, Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. Across all five models, we observe a counterintuitive finding: increasing the threshold from $\tau=0.3$ to $\tau=0.7$ reduces the mean retained rank by 46.60%--67.89% while simultaneously lowering WikiText-2 perplexity by 0.59%--5.17%. Threshold 0.7 achieves the lowest mean retained rank and final perplexity among the three tested thresholds, 0.3, 0.5, and 0.7, and reduces measured target-export size by 1.50%--4.25% relative to 0.3. GateTrim uses an $8\times512$-token fitting pool and a separate development pool of the same size. Its Qwen3-4B/8B/14B perplexities at $\tau=0.7$ are numerically below TwinQuant's published W4A8 values, reported with a $128\times2048$-token calibration pool, in a cross-study comparison. On Qwen3-8B, the threshold-0.7 configuration already has the lowest perplexity before reconstruction under both tested initialization seeds; reconstruction improves it further without increasing retained rank or export size.
  </details>

- **[Inference First, Training Underneath: Layer-Granular LoRA Co-Serving with LayerLoom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00652)**  `arXiv:2611.00652`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zongpu Zhang, Yishu Yin, Xuehai Qian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Serving and fine-tuning a large language model on the same GPUs can turn the idle capacity between inference bursts into training progress, and low-rank adaptation (LoRA) makes this co-location cheap because both workloads read a single frozen copy of the base weights. The difficulty lies in the fine-tuning backward pass, which has no counterpart in inference. Current co-serving systems schedule backward work per serving iteration: each iteration carries a fixed backward budget, and requests that arrive in the meantime wait for it to finish, so fine-tuning dictates inference latency whenever load spikes. We observe that the backward pass of a LoRA microbatch decomposes into per-layer tasks over the same weights that the inference forward pass visits, which allows backward work to be scheduled at layer rather than iteration granularity. Building on this observation, LayerLoom attaches same-layer backward tasks to the inference forward pass and, at each layer, runs only as many of them as the latency slack of in-flight requests permits. To keep a ready task at every layer, LayerLoom maintains a wavefront of fine-tuning microbatches staggered across layers, and it bounds the wavefront's activation memory by offloading microbatch state to host memory and prefetching it ahead of the layer where the scheduler plans to use it. A fused kernel serves the paired forward and backward GEMMs from a single read of each base weight. Because all backward work of an iteration finishes within its forward pass, new requests join the next iteration as in continuous batching. On an RTX 4090 GPU running Llama-3.1-8B under a bursty production trace, LayerLoom raises joint SLO attainment by 13.5-21.8 points over prior works with fine-tuning throughput that is comparable or up to 12% higher, and it reaches the same training losses.
  </details>

- **[Do Orthogonality Constraints Help Language-Model Training Beyond Sphere Optimization?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00593)**  `arXiv:2611.00593`  `cs.LG`  
  _Kai Hu, Li Zhang, Weichen Yu, Matt Fredrikson, Haoqi Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A growing body of work reports benefits from orthogonality constraints in fine-tuning large language models (LLMs). These results raise an open question: do orthogonality constraints also help LLM pre-training? We conduct a systematic empirical study of decoder-only LLM pre-training with models ranging from 360M to 3.1B parameters, trained on nearly 200B tokens. We examine fourteen variants of orthogonality training: some constrain the weight matrix itself, while others use orthogonal factors to reparameterize it. Because a strictly orthogonal square matrix loses half of its degrees of freedom, we also study block-wise orthogonality, where every $d$ rows form a row-orthonormal (Stiefel) matrix and the block size $d$ is a hyperparameter. Among block sizes ranging from 1 to the shorter side of the matrix, no $d>1$ used throughout the network reliably outperforms $d=1$, and large blocks are clearly worse; the one placement that helps, symmetric positive-definite factors on the query and key projections, loses its small gain as models grow. At $d=1$, the constraint reduces to sphere optimization, with each row direction on its own unit sphere, which by itself already improves on tuned Muon. The same two findings hold for a diffusion transformer trained on ImageNet, and after fine-tuning, downstream accuracy does not separate the parameterizations. Our conclusion is that, across the realizations we tried, orthogonality between rows does not help language-model pre-training beyond sphere optimization. Since this appears to conflict with several prior results, we re-examine five prior methods in pre-training and fine-tuning that report orthogonality improvements. In each case the reported gain is attributable to numerical precision, training horizon, optimizer realization, or per-row norm control rather than to the orthogonality the methods impose.
  </details>

- **[From Weeks to Hours: Fast and Principled SFT Data Generation for Reasoning LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00554)**  `arXiv:2611.00554`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hongyi Henry Jin, Wenhan Yang, Meysam Ghaffari, Carlos Morato, Baharan Mirzasoleiman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning-oriented post-training enables large language models (LLMs) to solve complex tasks via multi-step inference. Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on high-quality reasoning traces is a particularly efficient approach, but its effectiveness depends critically on careful data curation. Existing pipelines rely on a costly generate-then-filter paradigm: large pools of long reasoning traces are produced by multiple teacher models and subsequently filtered for difficulty and diversity using additional LLMs, often requiring weeks of computation. In this work, we propose SFTProbe, a fundamentally different approach that bypasses this pipeline by directly predicting the quality of reasoning data.We show that a brief LoRA-based adaptation, combined with evaluating loss on only 5% of each reasoning trace, suffices to estimate both difficulty and diversity. SFTProbe is grounded in a geometric view of post-training, where pretrained LLMs lie in a low-rank, anisotropic loss basin. By probing this basin via low-rank perturbations, we construct compact loss signatures whose clustering captures diversity, while loss at curvature inflection points provides a robust proxy for difficulty. We also provide a simple criterion for teacher selection. Empirically, our approach reduces data curation time from weeks to hours while maintaining or improving performance. Remarkably, on mathematical reasoning, 5k generated examples match the highly filtered 89k-example in OpenThoughts-Math dataset, and 7.5k generated examples outperforms it by 2.5% in accuracy. We further introduce a high-quality physics reasoning dataset, and use it to train a model that outperforms an IPhO 2025 silver-medal-winning model. The code and the dataset are released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[VoirDire: Counterfactual Calibration for Reliable LLM-as-a-Judge Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00491)**  `arXiv:2611.00491`  `cs.LG`  
  _Changho Shin, Frederic Sala_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-judge systems often aggregate multiple judges to improve evaluation reliability. However, judge agreement can reflect shared biases toward verbosity, formatting, or fluency rather than the intended evaluation target. Addressing these shared biases is challenging because observational judge outputs alone cannot distinguish agreement driven by target quality from agreement driven by nuisance attributes. We introduce VoirDire, an intervention-calibrated aggregation method for LLM-judge panels. VoirDire applies controlled interventions that vary specified surface attributes while aiming to preserve the evaluation target; it then uses paired changes in judge scores to identify nuisance-sensitive directions. It attenuates these directions and selects aggregation weights that retain variation across items while penalizing sensitivity to the interventions, making the final consensus less dependent on superficial features. We show theoretically that observational judge outputs alone cannot identify which shared direction is target-relevant, while paired interventions provide the contrast needed to identify nuisance-sensitive variation. We evaluate VoirDire on 12 datasets spanning continuous scoring, binary classification, and pairwise preference. Compared with the existing LLM-judge aggregation baselines, VoirDire reduces MAE by up to 26.8% on continuous-score tasks and improves accuracy by up to 5.3 percentage points on binary and preference tasks. These findings suggest that counterfactual interventions provide an additional means of improving the reliability and controllability of LLM-based evaluation beyond merely relying on observed judge agreement.
  </details>

- **[CLiS: Plug-and-Play Attention Acceleration for Video Diffusion on TPUs via Certified Stabilization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00444)**  `arXiv:2611.00444`  `cs.LG`  
  _Elisa Tsai, Shao-yu Huang, Daniel Mandragona, Wei-Yu Lin, WenXin Dong, Jevin Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  High-resolution video diffusion relies on attention over long token sequences, making attention efficiency critical to generation latency. Our analysis identifies repeated maximum reductions and accumulator rescaling in online softmax as a bottleneck in long-context TPU attention. We propose CLiS (Certified Loop-invariant Stabilization), a training-free, plug-and-play optimization for video diffusion inference. CLiS fixes per-row stabilizers before the KV loop and certifies numerical safety per head, falling back to online softmax when certification fails. It integrates into existing fused-attention kernels without changing model weights or architecture. On Wan 2.2 and LTX-2 across TPU v6e and TPU7x, CLiS reduces diffusion transformer (DiT) step latency by up to 19.6% relative to tuned online softmax without measurable quality loss. Controlled TPU adaptations of prior GPU stabilization methods show performance or numerical-safety limitations. Preliminary causal LLM experiments also show up to 13.5% higher training throughput in continued pretraining and 7.7% in supervised fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[TileWeave: Heterogeneous Tiling for Sparse Prefill on Mobile SoCs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00408)**  `arXiv:2611.00408`  `cs.LG`  
  _Wentao Ni, Hong Cai, Mingu Lee, Chihao Yu, Zhongming Yu, Hejia Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Smartphone applications increasingly require large language models to process long contexts, whereas high prefill latency and memory demands constrain local inference. Block-sparse attention reduces computation and memory traffic, but its irregular selections conflict with mobile NPUs' preference for large, regular tiles. Efficient execution must exploit cross-query reuse while avoiding redundant computation and preserving selections needed for accuracy. In this paper, we present TileWeave, which partitions sparse attention by this cross-query reuse: highly shared key blocks run in coarse NPU tiles, while low-reuse blocks run on the GPU only for the query blocks that selected them. In addition, we design an NPU-native pooled-key selector that keeps selection inexpensive, and a runtime that publishes GPU partials in the order the NPU consumes them and merges them in its existing output store. On RULER, HeteroSparse matches XAttention's accuracy with Qwen3-1.7B and improves it with Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct; its mean LongBench scores match XAttention as well. On Snapdragon SM8750 and SM8850 phones, it achieves 1.35--3.23$\times$ faster full-model prefill than XAttention over 1k--16k tokens and up to 1.58$\times$ faster than dense attention.
  </details>

- **[MXCode: Vector Quantization for MX-Native Inference at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00403)**  `arXiv:2611.00403`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gaurav Kolhe, Cansu Demirkiran, Shubhi Tyagi, Sulaiman Sadiq, Boyao Wang, Richard Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory capacity and bandwidth are major bottlenecks in LLM inference, motivating weight compression beyond conventional 4-bit quantization. Vector quantization (VQ) enables approximately 2-bit weight storage but often relies on codebooks incompatible with native FP4 computation. We introduce MXCode (MX-Native Codebook Quantization), a framework bridging ultra-low-bit VQ compression with MicroXcaling (MX)-native FP4 computation. MXCode proposes three strategies: PostScale, which quantizes learned codebooks to MXFP4; PreScale, which extracts MX scales before codebook learning; and PreScale+, which further compresses these scales using VQ. These strategies explore tradeoffs among quantization quality, scale overhead, and codebook granularity. All reconstruct MX-native W4A4 operands without materializing intermediate high-precision weights. We evaluate MXCode on Qwen3 (235B), DeepSeek-R1 (671B), and GLM-5.2 (753B) using the Microsoft MX Emulation Library. MXCode achieves approximately 2 bits per weight with competitive, workload-dependent model quality. On GB300 hardware, experiments with Qwen3 (235B) show approximately 50% lower model-load memory than Native FP4 while maintaining near-Native-FP4 serving throughput. Under memory-constrained prefix reuse, P90 TTFT improves by up to 94.5%. These results demonstrate software-based reconstruction on existing FP4 hardware and inform the development of future MX-native inference systems.
  </details>

- **[Quantization of Large Models with Weight Outliers and Sparsity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00286)**  `arXiv:2611.00286`  `cs.LG`  
  _Leonidas Tsigkounakis, Abraham Jaeger Mountain, Yilang Zhang, Georgios B. Giannakis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces the memory burden of large model deployment by casting the high precision trained weights to fewer bits. While this promotes memory-efficient inference, it also incurs quantization error that impairs model performance. To compensate for quantization error, the present contribution decomposes the original trained weights into a quantized matrix, a low-rank component, and a sparse matrix that accounts for outlier rows in the weights. Due to analytical intractability and the discrete structure of the decomposition, a simulated annealing search is employed to explore candidate decompositions while avoiding poor local minima. Numerical tests demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach across a broad suite of large language models, including Llama 3.2 1B, Llama 3.2 3B, Llama 3.1 8B, and Qwen2.5 7B, evaluated on natural language generation benchmarks using 4-bit NF4 and FP4 quantizers. The novel method can markedly outperform direct quantization while incurring minimal memory overhead.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Design SR Solvers: Harness Optimization for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16966)**  `arXiv:2610.16966`  `cs.LG`  
  _Qian Li, Yuxiao Hu, Qihao Duan, Jinyue Yan, Yuntian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression (SR) seeks explicit equations that explain observed data, and recent methods increasingly employ large language models (LLMs) and agents for equation discovery. However, these systems typically rely on manually designed workflows and prompts. We argue that the SR agent harness should itself be an optimization target: its design shapes search priors and how numerical feedback guides subsequent equation refinement. We therefore propose HarnessSR, a framework for jointly optimizing prompts and workflows to construct reusable SR agents. During both harness optimization and agent execution, HarnessSR supports either PySR or an LLM-based equation generator through tool-specific interfaces, allowing the framework to optimize search guidance for different equation-discovery mechanisms. HarnessSR first optimizes role prompts through equation-discovery feedback in basic workflows, then jointly searches prompt assignments and workflows with brief adaptation to align the two. Finally, it refines role instructions through reflection on residual patterns, candidate equation structures, and search outcomes, improving how these signals guide subsequent equation search. On LSR-Synth in LLM-SRBench, HarnessSR achieves a mean ID $\mathrm{Acc}_{0.01}$ of 61.33% across three backbones, a 45.0% relative improvement over the next-best baseline.
  </details>

- **[DASS: A Solver-Adaptable Dynamic Auxiliary Search Strategy for Symbolic Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16812)**  `arXiv:2610.16812`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxiao Hu, Qian Li, Ding Wang, Yekun Zheng, Jinyue Yan, Yuntian Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Symbolic regression (SR) aims to discover interpretable mathematical expressions from data and plays a key role in scientific discovery. However, existing methods face a common bottleneck: the enormous search space and severe combinatorial explosion make it difficult to achieve a favorable trade-off between accuracy and algorithmic efficiency. We argue that, in addition to designing increasingly complex solver-specific SR strategies, an equally promising direction is to equip them with a shareable auxiliary strategy that can provide reliable search guidance. To this end, we propose DASS, a solver-adaptable Dynamic Auxiliary Search Strategy that improves SR by constructing and refining a high-potential auxiliary search subspace. DASS represents the auxiliary search space as a functional subspace spanned by a set of high-potential basis function terms. It estimates term utility through multi-environment evaluation, filters unstable guidance, and updates term potential scores with a Gibbs posterior, thereby calibrating the subspace to provide more reliable guidance for downstream solvers. The high-quality solutions produced by the solver are used to update the set of basis terms. This forms a virtuous closed-loop optimization process: the subspace guides the downstream solver, and the solver feedback reshapes the subspace for subsequent exploration. DASS can be easily integrated with various types of SR solvers. Extensive experiments on LLM-SRBench show that DASS significantly improves the accuracy of original solvers with a manageable increase in runtime.
  </details>

- **[SPIN: Shadow Predictive Indexer for Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09025)**  `arXiv:2610.09025`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yao Fu, Jiahan Chang, Yueying Li, Ritchie Zhao, Bryce Long, Mohammad Mahdi Kamani, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indexer-based sparse attention reduces the cost of core attention by passing only a fixed, small number of important tokens to it. However, the indexer must still score the entire KV cache at every decoding step. This scoring overhead becomes a major bottleneck as the context length grows. We propose SPIN (Shadow Predictive Indexer) to reduce this indexer overhead. SPIN uses lightweight, history-based prediction to identify important KV blocks, avoiding the need to score the full KV cache at every decoding step. SPIN keeps the score history fresh with random exploration and allocates the block budget across layers by their measured miss rates. SPIN treats KV blocks and speculative decoding as first-class design and implementation considerations. Across extensive evaluations on long-context and agentic benchmarks, SPIN achieves 30-40% sparsity while preserving task quality. With larger exploration shares and per-layer block budgets, SPIN keeps task quality on the especially difficult MRCRv2 benchmark close to baseline at 50-57% effective block sparsity. In end-to-end vLLM serving, SPIN improves output throughput by up to 13.9% and reduces median inter-token latency by up to 13.6%.
  </details>

- **[ORCA: The Annealed Spectral Conditioning Optimizer for Faster, Better LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06116)**  `arXiv:2610.06116`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuanshi Liu, Boyuan Jiang, Liang Hou, Xin Tao, Pengfei Wan, Zhouchen Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern LLM optimizers such as Muon often produce weight matrices with higher effective rank than Adam, yet further spectral control has delivered only modest gains. We identify a tension behind this result: concentrated spectra can suppress gradient directions in coupled weight matrices and slow optimization, while constraints maintained throughout training can limit task-specific adaptation and raise the attainable loss floor. We introduce ORCA (Orthogonal Regularization, Cooled After), a minimal optimizer intervention that applies strong but temporary soft orthogonality regularization early in training, then removes it. This allows the weights to benefit from a broader spectrum early on and adapt freely afterward. Across LLaMA, Qwen3, and fine-grained mixture-of-experts models ranging from 130M to 8B parameters, ORCA achieves lower final validation loss than Muon. Its loss reduction relative to Muon matches or exceeds Muon's reduction relative to Adam. Ablations support the early-shaping, later-release design. Further, ORCA requires no architectural changes and adds minimal overhead. An implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[On the Indistinguishability of Human v/s AI Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26797)**  `arXiv:2608.26797`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jaee Ponde, Aritra Das, Mihir More, Debayan Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid improvement of LLMs has made distinguishing AI-generated text from human writing a pressing problem. This challenge is further amplified by paraphrasing tools designed to make machine-generated text appear more ``human''. We study how access to human writing samples can be used to strategically paraphrase machine-generated responses toward the human distribution. Under a multi-sample setting with human and machine responses to the same prompts, we show that repeated paraphrasing moves the machine distribution toward a human target under mixing and stability conditions. We derive an explicit convergence rate, examine how semantic-preservation and quality constraints affect this rate, and extend the analysis to finite samples to characterize how the required human data and paraphrasing rounds scale with the desired error. We extend the analysis beyond the shared-parameter setting by learning a human-preserving rewriting kernel.
  </details>

- **[GRIMIP: A General Framework for Instance-Specific Configuration of MIP Solvers Using LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.23299)**  `arXiv:2606.23299`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yidong Luo, Chenguang Wang, Fangzhou Zhu, Tao Zhong, Tianshu Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Configuring the hyperparameters of Mixed-integer programming (MIP) solvers is a high-dimensional, instance-dependent optimization problem where suboptimal settings can degrade solving time by orders of magnitude. Default configurations are often suboptimal, while traditional tuning methods either suffer from the ``cold-start'' problem and inefficient search or heavily rely on expert experience. This paper introduces \textbf{GRIMIP} (\textbf{\underline{G}}eneral \textbf{\underline{R}}easoning for \textbf{\underline{I}}nstance-specific \textbf{\underline{MIP}} configuration), a novel hybrid intelligence framework that synergistically integrates the semantic reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) with the sample-efficient search of Bayesian Optimization (BO). GRIMIP enables the LLM to function as a complete probabilistic surrogate within the BO loop, significantly improving performance and reducing sampling and evaluation costs. On seven benchmarks including MIPLIB, GRIMIP achieves over 40\% reduction in Primal-Dual Integral on hard instances, outperforming SMAC and other LLM-assisted BO methods. By granting LLMs sufficient autonomy, GRIMIP combines the expert-level reasoning of LLMs with the efficient search of BO, achieving state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[HERALD: High-Throughput Block Diffusion LLM Inference via CPU-GPU Cooperative KV Cache Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21633)**  `arXiv:2606.21633`  `cs.LG`  
  _Omin Kwon, Doyeon Kim, Jongseok Park, Seung Yul Lee, Sehoon Kim, Ion Stoica, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Block diffusion LLMs (block dLLMs) improve decode throughput by generating tokens in parallel, and are emerging as a next-generation alternative to autoregressive LLMs. At long contexts, however, the KV cache remains a major throughput bottleneck in both memory bandwidth and capacity. For autoregressive LLMs, dynamic sparse attention is often combined with KV offloading to alleviate both bottlenecks, keeping the full KV cache in CPU DRAM and fetching only a critical subset to the GPU. However, KV subset selection and retrieval over bandwidth-limited PCIe incur substantial offloading overheads that often stall the GPU. We present HERALD, the first dynamic sparse attention method for block dLLMs that supports KV offloading. HERALD exploits a property of block dLLMs: a critical KV subset selected once per block is reused across all of its denoising steps, so the next block's selection can be hidden behind the current block's denoising. HERALD realizes this with a two-stage selection algorithm co-designed with its execution system. While the GPU denoises the current block, the CPU speculatively searches the full KV cache for the next block with a single query merged from the current block's B queries, cutting the selection compute by Bx; the GPU then refines the candidates into the final top-K subset with the next block's real queries. At K=1024, HERALD achieves near-lossless accuracy relative to Dense on LongBench and reasoning tasks across four block dLLMs, and improves decode throughput by up to 3.70x over Dense GPU-only inference, 2.59x over MAGE, a state-of-the-art block-dLLM sparse attention method, and 8.38x over MAGE-Offload, its direct KV-offloading extension.
  </details>

- **[FuRA: Full-Rank Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning with Spectral Preconditioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22869)**  `arXiv:2605.22869`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yequan Zhao, Ruijie Zhang, Liyan Tan, Niall Moran, Tong Qin, Zheng Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Both full fine-tuning (Full FT) and parameter-efficient fine-tuning methods such as LoRA introduce weight updates without accounting for the spectral structure established during pretraining. As a result, noisy gradients from limited fine-tuning data can perturb robust pretrained features. We identify spectral preconditioning as the missing ingredient: reparameterizing each weight matrix through its full-rank singular value decomposition (SVD) and freezing one singular basis constrains updates to the pretrained column space, yielding a preconditioned optimization scheme that outperforms unconstrained Full FT at the same trainable parameter count. Building on this insight, we propose FuRA (Full-Rank Adaptation), an efficient full-rank adaptation framework based on a block tensor-train factorization W = LSR, where the large core L is fixed to the pretrained block-wise SVD basis, while only the compact core R and the block-wise singular values S are optimized. This design simultaneously provides full-rank spectral preconditioning, preserves full-rank update expressivity, and achieves parameter, memory, and step-time efficiency comparable to LoRA. FuRA consistently outperforms Full FT across multiple settings, including LLM fine-tuning (+1.37 on LLaMA-3-8B commonsense reasoning), LLM reinforcement learning for mathematical reasoning, and visual instruction tuning for VLMs. Furthermore, the 4-bit quantized variant, QFuRA, also surpasses QLoRA. Code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Selective Safety Steering via Value-Filtered Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14746)**  `arXiv:2605.14746`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bat-Sheva Einbinder, Hen Davidov, Yee Whye Teh, Yarin Gal, Yaniv Romano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While large language models (LLMs) are trained to align with human values, their generations may still violate safety constraints. A growing line of work addresses this problem by modifying the model's sampling policy at decoding time using a safety score. However, existing decoding-time steering methods often intervene unnecessarily, modifying generations that would have been safe under the base model. Such unnecessary interventions are undesirable, as they can distort key properties of the base model such as helpfulness, fluency, style, and coherence. We propose a new test-time steering method designed to reduce such unnecessary interventions while improving the safety of responses. Our approach filters tokens using a value-based safety criterion and provides an explicit bound on the probability of false interventions. A single threshold hyperparameter controls this bound, allowing practitioners to trade off higher rates of unnecessary intervention for better output safety. Across multiple datasets and experiments, we show that our value-filtered decoding method outperforms existing baselines, achieving better trade-offs between safety, helpfulness, and similarity to the base model.
  </details>

- **[Benchmarking Generalist and Specialist Time-Series Foundation Models for PPG-Based Health Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.14254)**  `arXiv:2510.14254`  `cs.LG`  
  _Saurabh Kataria, Yi Wu, Zhaoliang Chen, Hyunjung Gloria Kwak, Yuhao Xu, Lovely Yeswanth Panchumarthi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time-series Foundation Models (FMs) are pretrained on either heterogeneous corpora or specific signal domains. Using Photoplethysmography (PPG) as a representative modality, we compare generalist time-series FMs, PPG-specialist FMs, and traditional machine learning (ML) baselines for PPG-based health assessment. We conduct a two-stage benchmark. First, we evaluate multiple FM families across 23 downstream evaluations (e.g., atrial fibrillation detection, vital-sign estimation). Second, we conduct a focused four-FM comparison (MOMENT, Chronos-2, GPT-PPG, MOMENT-PPG) at multiple model sizes on an expanded 51-task suite, under both frozen-backbone and full-model tuning, to study tuning strategy, model scaling, and cross-dataset transfer. The multi-model benchmark shows that PPG-specialist FMs achieve the strongest aggregate performance, but their advantage is not uniform: generalist FMs remain competitive on several tasks, and traditional ML baselines outperform FMs on selected clinical regression tasks. The focused analysis shows that the advantage of PPG-specific pretraining appears mainly with frozen backbones and on targets that the waveform directly reflects, such as atrial fibrillation and vital signs. Full-model tuning narrows this advantage, model rankings change with the tuning strategy, and the largest model variant is the best choice in only about one-third of cases. Additionally, controlled experiments with two generalist models show that initializing PPG pretraining from generalist checkpoints provides an early advantage over training from scratch, even when the pretrained features offer little downstream benefit. Together, these findings suggest that PPG-specific pretraining is useful but task-dependent, and that FMs should be evaluated against simpler statistical, traditional ML, and clinical history-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[Improving Sampling Efficiency in RLVR through Adaptive Rollout and Response Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25808)**  `arXiv:2509.25808`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuheng Zhang, Wenlin Yao, Changlong Yu, Yao Liu, Qingyu Yin, Bing Yin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Group relative policy optimization (GRPO) trains large language models with verifiable rewards by normalizing rewards within response groups. When every response in a group receives the same reward, the advantages vanish and the group provides no policy gradient signal. We propose Adaptive Rollout and Response Reuse Policy Optimization (AR3PO) to obtain informative groups with fewer generations. Adaptive rollout samples responses to each prompt in stages and stops sampling once the model generates a correct response. If all sampled responses are incorrect, response reuse replaces one of them with a past correct response to the same prompt, when available, and recomputes the group advantages. Policy-recomputed reuse trains on the updated groups using probabilities recomputed under the rollout policy; advantage-only reuse uses historical responses only to compute advantages. Across four mathematical benchmarks with 7B and 8B models, AR3PO consistently improves over GRPO and achieves performance comparable to DAPO while requiring up to $4.2\times$ fewer generated responses. Experiments with a 32B model also show comparable performance to DAPO at a similar training stage with substantially fewer generated responses.
  </details>

- **[Disen-Forcing: Disentangling Semantic Anchoring from Motion for Autoregressive Video Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02822)**  `arXiv:2611.02822`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhengyang Yu, Akio Hayakawa, Masato Ishii, Qingtao Yu, Xuesong Li, Takashi Shibuya, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive video diffusion models (AR-VDMs) enable real-time, long-horizon video generation but degrade rapidly once the inference horizon outruns the training horizon, due to compounding exposure bias. To stabilize long rollouts, recent methods heuristically adopt frame sink, keeping initial frames as a persistent model context, inspired by attention sinks in LLMs. We revisit this design and find that the mechanism by which initial frames stabilize long-range consistency in AR-VDMs is not an LLM-style attention sink; instead, long-range consistency is anchored through a sparse subset of attention heads, while the majority of heads predominantly capture local temporal dynamics. This observation reveals an over-conditioning pathology in existing frame-sink strategies: by universally exposing every head to the pinned initial-frame context, the model becomes prone to collapsing motion toward the low-dynamic modes of the teacher during model distillation. We propose Disen-Forcing, a distillation-time plug-in for AR-VDMs that disentangles and reinforces this head specialization through disentangled context routing and motion-disentangling regularization. On long-horizon video generation benchmarks, Disen-Forcing simultaneously improves long-range consistency and motion dynamics of base AR-VDMs, a trade-off that sink-based methods fail to balance.
  </details>

- **[Lie Routing Vision Language Models for View-Robust 3D Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02306)**  `arXiv:2611.02306`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Jingjun Yi, Chen Hu, Qi Bi, Hao Zheng, Huimin Huang, Yongkang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial progress in 3D scene understanding from multi-view RGB observations. Yet a fixed object and query can elicit inconsistent target-related responses across camera viewpoints, a phenomenon we characterize as viewpoint-induced semantic drift. We introduce LieVLM, a Lie routing framework for view-robust 3D understanding. Motivated by the rotational component of viewpoint change, Lie routing adapts visual representations by applying local $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ transformations to projected features within learned three-dimensional subspaces. Geometry tokens extracted by VGGT predict local rotation parameters and channel-wise routing coefficients. A closed-form Padé--Cayley map transforms projected visual features, whose identity-relative corrections are projected back and gated into the original visual and geometry features. This design retains the pretrained semantic and direct spatial context while learning view-dependent feature adjustments. Experiments on 3D captioning, grounding, detection, and spatial reasoning show consistent improvements over additive fusion across training scales and backbones. Controlled ablations examine the roles of geometric conditioning and transformation structure. Viewpoint analyses further reveal systematic variation in the predicted routing states alongside more localized target responses and stronger task performance across viewpoint changes.
  </details>

- **[An Agentic AI Workflow for Liver Lesion Segmentation and Structured Radiology Reporting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34599)**  `arXiv:2610.34599`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Fatma Ahmed Mansour, Rahatara Ferdousi, Zyad Medhat Mahmoud Ashor, Manar Fathy Elshenawy, Ibrahim Shehaby, Salma Elmongi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite advances in automated liver lesion segmentation, a gap remains between image-derived predictions and clinically interpretable radiology reports. Bridging this gap requires quantitative measurements that are both reproducible and traceable. We present an evidence-grounded agentic AI framework that connects liver and lesion segmentation with deterministic quantification and structured reporting. TotalSegmentator-based nnU-Net models delineate the liver, Couinaud segments, and lesions, while computational tools extract volumes, dimensions, counts, and anatomical locations. A large language model (LLM) organizes these measurements into a predefined reporting schema, with numerical consistency checks against source measurements. On 51 LiTS cases, the segmentation stage achieved a liver Dice score of 0.947, lesion sensitivity of 75.6%, and positive predictive value of 68.9%. Exploratory screening of 25,494 MERLIN reports identified 10,208 (40.0%) with liver-related abnormalities. By connecting quantitative imaging evidence with agentic report generation, this work takes a step toward human-centered medical AI that prioritizes traceability and clinical relevance.
  </details>

- **[Mixture of Probes: Learning from Privileged Modalities in Multimodal LLMs Through Probing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08839)**  `arXiv:2607.08839`  `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Dominick Reilly, Qiyu Wu, Hiromi Wakaki, Srijan Das, Yuki Mitsufuji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are typically designed under the assumption that all modalities available during training will also be accessible at inference. However, many real-world settings violate this assumption, requiring models to operate under a privileged modality setting, where auxiliary modalities are available only during training. While these modalities contain valuable information, existing MLLMs largely fail to leverage them effectively, as they treat modalities as interchangeable inputs rather than sources of complementary supervision. We propose Mixture of Probes (MoP), a novel framework that disentangles modality-specific and modality-general signals within the MLLM, allowing the model to preserve modality-dependent structure while learning transferable representations across modalities. At its core, MoP achieves this through a structured probing mechanism that extracts and organizes information from intermediate representations of a shared modality encoder, rather than relying only on final-layer alignment as done in existing MLLMs. To support this disentanglement, we further introduce MoP Cross-modal Training (MoP-X), a training strategy for MoP centered around a probe disentanglement loss that prevents probe collapse and encourages cross-modal learning. We evaluate MoP across two domains spanning eight tasks and four modalities under a comprehensive evaluation protocol tailored to the privileged modality setting, where each modality is independently treated as the sole input at inference time. MoP consistently outperforms strong MLLM baselines, achieving up to 65% relative improvement, demonstrating that auxiliary modalities, even when unavailable at inference, can provide substantial gains when effectively leveraged during training. Code, model checkpoints, and evaluation protocols will be made available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PixelBench: Can LLMs Understand and Generate Images Pixel by Pixel?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02540)**  `arXiv:2611.02540`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Haoquan Zhang, Feiyang Liu, Juanxi Tian, Wanhua Li, Haodong Duan, Weiyang Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate visual assets through text-based formats such as SVG and CAD, but these symbolic representations describe images using high-level primitives rather than explicit raster values. We ask whether LLMs can instead generate pixel images directly through their existing vocabulary, without a learned image tokenizer or decoder. We introduce PixelBench, a benchmark for text-to-pixel (T2P) and image-to-pixel (I2P) generation across multiple resolutions, together with a complementary pixel-understanding evaluation. Models generate an RGB color palette followed by a grid of indices specifying the palette color of each output pixel. In T2P, the strongest models produce recognizable images directly from text. In I2P, the strongest models outperform palette-matched downsampling and specialized pixelization algorithms at low resolutions. Pixel-understanding performance correlates strongly with generation performance. A resolution-scaling study shows that pixel-generation performance often improves initially before declining at high resolutions. Finally, we introduce Pixel Reconstruction Tuning (PRT), which augments visual instruction tuning with caption-palette-grid trajectories. We post-train Molmo2-4B from its pretraining checkpoint by mixing general instruction data with our curated PRT data, improving performance across 16 visual-centric benchmarks, indicating that explicit pixel reconstruction can provide useful auxiliary supervision.
  </details>

- **[A Systematic Analysis of Live Intraoperative Surgical Expert Narrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00028)**  `arXiv:2611.00028`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Arda Mamur, Matthias Seibold, Christoph Laux, Florian Wanivenhaus, Philipp Fürnstahl_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Manual annotation remains the principal bottleneck in surgical video understanding. Live intraoperative narration by the operating surgeon is a scalable alternative that requires no manual annotation, but the information it contains has not been characterized. We analyzed 13 endoscopic lumbar spine decompression procedures (13.2 h) by two senior spine surgeons, using time-synchronized close-mic German audio. An annotation-free pipeline (word-level speech recognition, deterministic correction of surgical-term errors, utterance fusion, LLM-based translation to English, and relevance filtering) converted speech into time-stamped procedural cues labelled with the surgical entities they reference and their speech act. Of 3,171 utterances, 1,201 (37.9%) were retained as procedural cues, while 57.6% were non-procedural and removed. Retained cues referenced instruments (60.7%), anatomy (48.0%) and surgical actions (57.1%), and 98.5% carried at least one of these. 39% of cues (472 of 1,201) were directives, mainly instrument requests and commands. All 467 manually verified instrument requests preceded the instrument's appearance in the field, by a median of 8.2 s. Domain-term transcription corruption affected 18.0% of retained cues. Live narration is thus a dense, annotation-free supervisory signal describing instruments, anatomy, actions and team coordination, but it is noisy and temporally displaced from the events it names, and this displacement must be modelled before safe downstream use.
  </details>

- **[Disentangling Perception and Reasoning in Multimodal LLMs via Reward Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.00215)**  `arXiv:2601.00215`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL`  
  _Omar Sharif, Eftekhar Hossain, Nikhil Singh, Patrick Ng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has driven major gains in LLM reasoning, and it is intuitive to assume this recipe will transfer well to multimodal models. However, multimodal models do two things: first, perceive what is in an image, then reason about what it implies. Because these stages are graded jointly, it is hard to tell how much room reasoning alone has to grow.
