We study this on algorithmic visual puzzles, where both components are necessary and show that perception, not reasoning, is the binding constraint. Replacing images with simple textual descriptions raises performance by over 20 points on average for Claude models. We then evaluate six reward designs aimed at inducing visual grounding during reasoning without chain-of-thought supervision. Training Qwen-2.5-VL-7B with GRPO, reward design induces long, structured reasoning with self-reflection and visual references, yielding a 5.56-point gain over the base model. These gains are, however, uneven; no single reward improves all categories, and rewards with verifiable accuracy signals trade cross-domain transfer for in-domain accuracy. These results point to perception-aware reward design as a path forward, so that signals correct perception at its source rather than the reasoning that inherits its errors.
  </details>

- **[NeuroPilot: An Agent-Driven Smart Pipeline for Processing, Quality Control, and Managing Neuroimages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07541)**  `arXiv:2608.07541`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yiyao Chen, Yucheng Li, Jungong Tong, Shaoqi Wang, Kunhao Zhou, Ziquan Wei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transforming raw neuroimage archives into analysis-ready derivatives relies on three brittle stages: data standardization, modality-specific preprocessing, and quality control (QC). While individual neuroimaging tools are well developed, their orchestration requires project-specific scripts, environment-adaptive tuning, and labor-intensive manual QC. To address this, we introduce NeuroPilot, a multi-agent system that digitalizes the expertise of neuroimage processing, QC, and data management into three LLM-invocable skills: dcm2bids-skill, neuroimage-pre-skill, and qc-agent-skill. The LLM-driven agent autonomously orchestrates workflows, generalizing various infrastructure settings into a single configuration to achieve the highest scalability. Demonstrating the system's generalizability, we deployed NeuroPilot across 17 cohorts (>123,000 subjects) spanning infant to aging populations and multiple MRI modalities (structural, diffusion, functional). In practice, after standardizing data via the dcm2bids-skill, the agent dynamically routes datasets to the optimal neuroimage-pre-skill based on available modalities and cohort traits (e.g., dispatching T1w and fMRI data to fMRIPrep, or selecting specialized pipelines for infant cohorts). The qc-agent-skill then drives an evidence-based, semi-automated QC via a 3-D browser dashboard, utilizing a multi-tiered verification system to optimize failed cases and escalate complex issues for supervisor inspection. Quantitatively, our QC agent screened 558 production subjects, validating its automated flags against FreeSurfer's topology-defect metrics. The infant processing pipeline achieved a 100% (201/201) completion rate on QC-validated inputs. Importantly, NeuroPilot compresses the traditional 2--3 month timeline for training staff and processing complete datasets into a single week. NeuroPilot is deployed inthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[What Does a Safety Label Measure? Auditing Situational-Safety Benchmarks for Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01125)**  `arXiv:2611.01125`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Wayner Barrios, Kenneth Barrios_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Situational-safety benchmarks label each scene SAFE or UNSAFE and score a multimodal large language model (MLLM) against that label, usually by judging its free-form answer, and these scores guide deployment. We ask what such a label measures. A label score can come from the scene, the request or the benchmark's own files. We propose the Benchmark Card, seven measured fields that tell them apart, and fill it for six benchmarks. Only MSSBench chat leaks the label through its image files: image size alone reaches 82.3% accuracy, matching or beating six of seven commercial models. Elsewhere the signal outside the scene comes from design. In MOSSBench the item partition fixes the label, and on HoliSafe the request alone ranks pairs about as well as the scene. On our corrected chat split, the size rule falls to chance. We then introduce the Spot-Stop decomposition, which compares ranking with labelling on pairs of a safe and an unsafe scene. It splits the gap above a label-swap null into three parts: a threshold part, overlap between safe and unsafe scores, and order that holds only within a pair. Most of fourteen open MLLMs rank better than they label, beyond what their AUROC predicts. On chat, one shared threshold repairs a third to a half of the excess, and label-free recentering recovers it on balanced batches. Recentering on unlabelled scenes of the same situation improves ranking on VSCBench for 13 of 14 models.
  </details>

- **[Concept-Grounded Reasoning with Prompt-Driven Localization for Interpretable Structured Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.15334)**  `arXiv:2609.15334`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xinyue Xu, Hongbin Lin, Juangui Xu, Hualiang Wang, Lehan Wang, Lijie Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical imaging modalities such as ultrasound and X-ray are widely used in clinical practice, where diagnosis follows a structured, evidence-driven workflow aligned with standardized criteria. While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) show promise for automated medical report generation, most existing systems rely on end-to-end multimodal fusion without modeling clinically defined intermediate attributes, leading to limited grounding and interpretability. To address this issue, we propose CORAL (COncept-grounded ReAsoning with Localization), a multimodal framework that integrates spatial grounding and concept-level supervision into a unified reasoning process. CORAL employs a prompt-driven medical segmentation model to localize lesions and predicts multi-class clinical attributes through a Concept Bottleneck module. The resulting textual concept tokens are combined with mask-modulated visual features within an MLLM to enable structured report generation and diagnostic prediction. Experiments on BUS-CoT and IU X-ray datasets demonstrate consistent improvements in diagnostic accuracy, concept consistency, and report quality over strong general-purpose and medical MLLMs, indicating that concept-grounded reasoning better aligns generation with clinical decision processes.
  </details>

- **[LF-VideoQA: Evaluating Long-Video Understanding with Long-Form Theory-of-Mind Questions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00383)**  `arXiv:2611.00383`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shaden Shaar, Bradon Thymes, Majd Aldaye, Claire Cardie, Bharath Hariharan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The hardest part of understanding a long video is not pinpointing any one moment but synthesizing many, e.g., answering a question that requires tracking what develops across hours of content and articulating how the pieces fit together. To date, most long-video QA datasets require only a short answer or a multiple-choice selection, oversimplifying the long-video understanding task to retrieval. We introduce LF-VideoQA, a dataset for long-form, open-ended question answering over long narrative videos built on theory-of-mind questions: a character's belief depends on everything they have seen or been told, so each question demands assembling and reasoning from evidence distributed across the video. The dataset contains more than 6,300 questions on 94 recent films; each requires reasoning over scenes spanning an average of 23 minutes and comes with a question-specific rubric for recall and precision scoring. The dataset is created entirely automatically by annotating screenplays with scene events and each character's first-order beliefs and meta-beliefs, then generating (question, answer, rubric) triples through LLM-based question generation and verification; human studies validate their quality. We evaluate four families of state-of-the-art open-source VLMs across three settings and find the task highly challenging: (i) even an oracle with access to the screenplay falls well short of complete answers; (ii) the best non-oracle model attains barely half of the recall level of the oracle; (iii) unlike on prior long-video benchmarks, captions and subtitles underperform the video itself, indicating that the questions need visual evidence; and (iv) recall is the predominant bottleneck, with answers omitting many required rubric claims.
  </details>

- **[ClinFusion: A Vision-Centric Multimodal LLM System for Holistic Medical Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24743)**  `arXiv:2607.24743`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hangjie Yuan, Yichen Qian, Zhiwei Tang, Xianzhe Xu, Sicheng Yang, Lirong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) hold immense potential to revolutionize clinical practice, yet deploying them in the medical domain is fundamentally a vision-centric challenge: models must absorb knowledge from heterogeneous 2D and 3D medical images, and evaluation protocols must align with radiologists' clinical practice and provide an accurate, fine-grained and factualness-driven assessment. In this paper, we introduce ClinFusion, a vision-centric MLLM designed for holistic medical understanding that systematically addresses these limitations. We propose a compositional and cascaded vision encoder architecture featuring a Cascade Spatial-Aware Locality Fusion operator that unifies diverse 2D and native 3D medical image understanding within a fused encoder. We further introduce a vision-grounded evaluation framework, including MedIF-Bench for instruction-following assessment and a region-of-interest-grounded method for clinically aligned and factualness-driven report generation evaluation. We show that ClinFusion sets a new state-of-the-art across a comprehensive suite of 2D and 3D multimodal medical benchmarks---spanning visual question answering, report generation, and instruction following---as well as textual medical tasks, outperforming leading open-source medical MLLMs (\textit{e.g.}, Hulu-Med, Lingshu) on 20 out of 24 benchmarks and demonstrating multimodal capabilities better than powerful proprietary models such as GPT-5.2 and Gemini-3-Flash on 13 out of 16 benchmarks, and can be further augmented with agentic tool use for retrieval-augmented and tool-assisted clinical workflows. A blinded evaluation by board-certified radiologists confirms that ClinFusion produces the highest-ranked reports, and validates our RoI-grounded metric as achieving the strongest correlation with expert judgment among all automatic evaluation metrics examined.
  </details>

- **[Hierarchy-Aware Multimodal Unlearning for Medical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09867)**  `arXiv:2512.09867`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fengli Wu, Vaidehi Patil, Jaehong Yoon, Yue Zhang, Mohit Bansal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used in sensitive domains such as medical AI, where privacy regulations, including HIPAA and GDPR, require the removal of specific individuals' or institutions' data. This motivates machine unlearning, which aims to remove the influence of target data from a trained model. However, existing unlearning benchmarks fail to reflect the hierarchical and multimodal structure of real-world medical data, limiting their ability to properly evaluate unlearning in practice. Therefore, we introduce MedForget, a hierarchy-aware multimodal unlearning benchmark that models data from medical institutions as a nested structure, enabling fine-grained evaluation across retain and forget splits. Experiments show that current unlearning methods struggle to achieve effective hierarchy-aware forgetting without degrading retained utility, measured by performance on clinically relevant prediction tasks. To address this limitation, we propose Cross-modal Hierarchy-Informed Projection for unlearning (CHIP), a training-free, hierarchy-aware multimodal unlearning method that deletes information by selectively removing target-specific weight subspaces while preserving sibling-shared information. Our results show that CHIP achieves the highest forget-retain performance gap across all hierarchy levels while maintaining competitive downstream utility compared to existing methods. Overall, MedForget provides a practical benchmark for evaluating hierarchy-aware multimodal unlearning for medical data, while CHIP offers an effective solution that generalizes across architectures and balances deletion with utility.
  </details>

- **[Q-CamTuning: A Multimodal Large Language Model for Fine-Grained Quality Reasoning in Livestreaming Camera Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03007)**  `arXiv:2611.03007`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yanchao Bi, Pengfei Chen, Yulong Yan, Qian Zhang, Biao Wang, Leida Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the popularization of livestreaming applications, automatic camera tuning has become indispensable for ordinary users to enjoy high Quality of Experience (QoE). However, the existing Image Quality Assessment (IQA) methods face two challenges in camera tuning. First, they struggle to identify subtle quality difference between images, which is common in camera tuning. Second, the ability to explain fine-grained differences and to provide suggestions for camera tuning are even more lacking. To address these challenges, we first construct FGLive-Comments, an instruction tuning dataset with rich annotations of both preferences for fine-grained image pairs and quality reasoning from global and local visual evidence, which supports selective adjustment recommendations of camera parameters. Based on FGLive-Comments, we develop Q-CamTuning, a multimodal large language model for livestreaming camera tuning. Its progressive perception-evidence-guidance framework learns quality-aware visual representations, enhances fine-grained reasoning with global-local evidence, and maps quality issues to camera-parameter adjustment directions. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that Q-CamTuning outperforms existing IQA models in both quality scoring and fine-grained image pair ranking. User study also validates its ability to recommend camera tuning suggestions.
  </details>

- **[Reconstruction as Spatial Evaluation: Diagnosing Cross-View Spatial Intelligence in Large Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02026)**  `arXiv:2611.02026`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Junfei Wu, Qiuyue Wang, Xuhong Huang, Qihang Peng, Mingsheng Li, Jian Guan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The spatial intelligence of large vision-language models (LVLMs) requires the ability to combine local visual observations into a geometrically consistent representation of the physical world. Many static benchmarks assess isolated spatial judgments, such as relative direction or distance, while interactive embodied evaluations combine perception with exploration, planning, control, and simulator-specific execution. To bridge this gap, we introduce RECONSPATIAL, a benchmark centered on multi-view 3D scene reconstruction across indoor, tabletop, and outdoor environments. Given real multi-view images and target-object queries, LVLMs are required to jointly predict oriented 3D bounding boxes in a shared, camera-anchored metric coordinate frame. Reconstructions are evaluated along two complementary axes: Structure for relative layout and Metric for physical scale and camera-anchored placement. Nine atomic spatial QA tasks, grouped into Geometric Perception and Spatial Relations, probe spatial capabilities involved in joint reconstruction. Offline navigation extends evaluation to path planning. Evaluation of 18 frontier LVLMs reveals a consistent gap between relative scene structure and metric reconstruction; even GPT-6-Astra scores 81.4% on Structure but only 58.7% on Metric. Tracing this gap, diagnostic analyses link atomic spatial capabilities to reconstruction quality and show that relational consistency alone does not ensure metric accuracy. Experiments with 3D map injection further separate scene construction from path planning, revealing how these geometric errors limit navigation success. Finally, spatial fine-tuning yields broad transfer across both spatial and general vision-language benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Zero-shot evaluation of open vision-language models as System 1 decision models on radiology tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01631)**  `arXiv:2611.01631`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Bradley J Erickson, Sara Salehi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: We compared two multimodal System 1 decision models, Clef 27B and d1 3B, with a traditional LLM (Qwen 3.8-27B) on seven radiology tasks covering text, radiographs, CT and MRI. Methods: Three models were compared: Clef, Qwen3.8-27B and d1. Three tasks were interpretation of text and 4 were detection of finding in images. The tasks were: 1. finding presence of finding in CT-RATE chest CT reports; 2. Presence of finding in PadChest chest radiograph reports; 3. the same 18 findings on CT-RATE chest CT but 3D volumes; 4. MR safety status of devices from AccessGUDID device records; 5. line and tube position on RANZCR CLiP chest radiographs; 6. motion-artifact severity on MR-ART T1-weighted brain MRI and 7. sequence identification on BraTS 2021 brain MRI. The primary endpoint was AUROC. There was no fine-tuning of the models on the tasks. Results: All models completed all tests without a failed request. All retrieved findings from reports almost perfectly (AUROC >0.95). Clef ranked higher than its Qwen backbone on CT-RATE volumes and RANZCR lower on MR-ART. The two did not differ on BraTS, GUDID or PadChest, and differed by 0.002 on CT-RATE reports. On BraTS, Clef correctly identified 90 of 100 FLAIR volumes while Qwen never chose it. d1 ranked lower than Clef on six of seven tasks. No model performed well on motion severity. Clef was faster per request than Qwen on tasks with many questions per input. d1 used 3 to 9 times less energy per request than Clef. Conclusions: System 1 models can perform important tasks in radiology with high accuracy, at lower compute cost than LLMs used in the usual way. When an LLM is constrained to a single answer token, its cost and performance is similar to System 1 models unless multiple questions are asked. A 3B System 1 model was close to the 27B models on report text but not on images. No model performed well on motion severity.
  </details>

- **[SAVIER: Source-Anchored Visual Inspection and Evidence-Guided Repair for Vietnamese Cultural Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00722)**  `arXiv:2611.00722`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Gia-Huy Thai, Duc-Tien Khong, Trung-Nghia Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present SAVIER, a training-free, one-pass framework that repairs the depiction of a named Vietnamese cultural entity in a generated image while preserving the scene details requested by the user. The problem is hard because a plausible scene can miss the cues that identify the entity, and prompt-level refinement never inspects the image it produces. SAVIER starts from a Culture-TRIP expanded prompt; a Curator separates source-linked cultural cues from prompt-preservation requirements, a prompt-blind Observer describes the first image, and a Refiner turns the discrepancies into at most three positive instructions before one regeneration with the same seed and a fixed bank of reference photographs. On the 50 bilingual prompts of ViCuP, evaluated with SDXL and FLUX.1-dev against a frozen, human-verified visual contract, SAVIER raises contract fulfillment from 55.6 to 69.6 and from 59.1 to 67.3 over Culture-TRIP and raises CAIRE cultural relevance on FLUX.1-dev. A reference-only ablation that removes the agent repair shows that SAVIER's photographs account for most of the contract gain; the agent roles add country-level relevance on FLUX.1-dev without lowering adherence to the original prompt under the official VQAScore. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ReactionBench: A Human-Anchored Benchmark for Modeling Reactions to Video](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00050)**  `arXiv:2611.00050`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yibo Zhao, Ao Qu, Xuan Jiang, Jiahao Xu, Keane Ong, Hang Jiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding what happens in a video is not sufficient for socially responsive multimodal systems: such systems also aim to relate what a person observes to how that person responds. Existing video benchmarks largely evaluate events, temporal reasoning, or people depicted in the video, while affective and viewer-response datasets often focus on emotion labels, aggregate responses, or prediction from the stimulus alone. This leaves an underexplored question: can current multimodal models ground an observed viewer reaction in the stimulus context associated with it? We introduce ReactionBench, a human-anchored benchmark for reaction-specific video understanding. Built from a candidate pool of 31,834 synchronized stimulus-reaction clips spanning 433 hours and eight domains, ReactionBench evaluates three complementary abilities: ranking the intensity of a viewer's reactions within a video, matching a reaction to its temporally aligned stimulus, and explaining the concrete stimulus cues linked to that reaction. Human annotation provides 14,894 ratings over 3,257 moments, and the frozen evaluation comprises 250 intensity profiles, 112 matching items, and 200 rationale items with 447 blind-written human references. Across seven multimodal models, reaction-specific grounding remains challenging: the best model reaches 50.9% accuracy on four-way stimulus-reaction matching, while GPT-5 shows no detectable loss under reaction substitution. Performance is also sensitive to evaluation protocol. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Mixture of Layers: Dynamic Layer Routing for Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09440)**  `arXiv:2610.09440`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jeonghwan Kim, Sofia Stoica, Jiwan Chung, Ansel Blume, Hyeonjeong Ha, Zhenhailong Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-trained vision encoders contain layer-wise visual representations that differ in spatial granularity, semantic abstraction, and sensitivity to local details. However, most Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) rely on only the final or penultimate vision encoder representations or fixed aggregation rules, making visual abstraction largely query-agnostic and limiting access to fine-grained cues such as small objects, spatial details, text, and subtle visual attributes. In this work, we propose Mixture of Layers (MoL), an instruction-conditioned layer routing approach at the visual patch level that dynamically aggregates query-relevant latent representations from intermediate vision encoder layers. Given a text query, MoL predicts routing probabilities over vision encoder layers and performs a top-k sparse aggregation over selected hidden states at either the image level, patch level, or through a hybrid routing mechanism. In doing so, MoL enables query-adaptive access to layer-specific visual features for fine-grained visual reasoning. Our experiments across 7 fine-grained visual reasoning tasks demonstrate substantial performance improvements, especially across fine-grained visual grounding and understanding tasks such as +18.9% improvement on V* in overall accuracy, +4.5% on HRBench4K, and +16.3% on CharXiv compared to the baseline MLLMs, without resorting to multi-resolution inputs, simple interleaving of multiple vision encoders, or increasing the number of patch tokens. We study vision encoders' receptive field scales across different layers and their sampling behaviors to provide an in-depth analysis of why layer-wise sampling is helpful, demonstrating that conditional visual representations are a key step towards better visual perception and reasoning in MLLMs. Our project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DiffCap-Bench: A Comprehensive, Challenging, Robust Benchmark for Image Difference Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04503)**  `arXiv:2605.04503`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuancheng Wei, Haojie Zhang, Linli Yao, Lei Li, Jiali Chen, Tao Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Image Difference Captioning (IDC) generates natural language descriptions that precisely identify differences between two images, serving as a key benchmark for fine-grained change perception, cross-modal reasoning, and image editing data construction. However, existing benchmarks lack diversity and compositional complexity, and standard lexical-overlap metrics (e.g., BLEU, METEOR) fail to capture semantic consistency or penalize hallucinations, which together prevent a comprehensive and robust evaluation of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on IDC. To address these gaps, we introduce DiffCap-Bench, a comprehensive IDC benchmark covering ten distinct difference categories to ensure diversity and compositional complexity. Furthermore, we propose an LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation protocol grounded in human-validated Difference Lists, enabling a robust assessment of models' ability to both capture and describe visual changes. Through extensive evaluation of state-of-the-art MLLMs, we reveal significant performance gaps between proprietary and open-source models, highlight the critical importance of reasoning capability, and identify clear limitations in model scaling. Our framework also demonstrates strong alignment with human expert judgments and strong correlation with downstream image editing data construction quality. These findings establish DiffCap-Bench as both a reliable IDC evaluation framework and a practical predictor of downstream utility. The benchmark and code will be made publicly available to support further research.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models for Crash Detection in Video: A Survey of Methods, Datasets, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.02074)**  `arXiv:2507.02074`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Sanjeda Akter, Ibne Farabi Shihab, Anuj Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Crash detection from video feeds is a critical problem in intelligent transportation systems. Recent developments in large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) have transformed how we process, reason about, and summarize multimodal information. This paper surveys recent methods leveraging LLMs for crash detection from video data. We present a structured taxonomy of fusion strategies, summarize key datasets, analyze model architectures, compare performance benchmarks, and discuss ongoing challenges and opportunities. Our review provides a foundation for future research in this fast-growing intersection of video understanding and foundation models.
  </details>

- **[Reviso: Not All Visual Evidence Deserves Equal Attention in Video Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03602)**  `arXiv:2611.03602`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tengfei Liu, Yang Shi, Yuran Wang, Yuqi Tang, Yizhuo Jia, Zhuoran Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can generate detailed video captions, yet they may overemphasize minor details while overlooking essential context and key events. We argue that not all visual evidence deserves equal attention, as the importance of visual information depends on the content of each video. Motivated by this observation, we propose Reviso, an agentic video captioning framework that learns what to observe and how to gather relevant visual evidence. From a low-frame-rate global preview, Reviso constructs a hierarchical Context--Focus--Points (CFP) observation plan that identifies the video context, prioritizes key aspects, and defines concrete observation targets and corresponding tools. Guided by this plan, Reviso performs goal-directed active perception by selectively revisiting temporal segments and combining tools for fine-grained inspection. It interprets the acquired evidence, updates its plan as needed, and integrates global and local information to generate the final caption. To learn this observation strategy, we develop a verification-guided pipeline that constructs high-quality training trajectories through factual verification and teacher-guided refinement. We further train Reviso using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), incorporating a key-fact reward for caption quality, a Plan Alignment Reward (PAR) for identifying important observation targets, and an Unproductive Tool-Call Penalty (UTCP) that adjusts observation-cost penalties based on caption quality. Experiments across four video captioning benchmarks show that Reviso outperforms the evaluated open-source baselines, while its generated captions better support downstream video question answering. These findings highlight the effectiveness and potential of priority-guided active observation for video captioning.
  </details>

- **[ReVA-HPE: Relative 6DoF Head Pose Estimation with Synthetic Pose Supervision and Personalized Virtual Anchors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03283)**  `arXiv:2611.03283`  `cs.CV`  
  _Vasiliki Vasileiou, Panagiotis P. Filntisis, Petros Maragos, Kostas Daniilidis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Monocular head pose estimation is usually formulated as absolute regression from a single face crop, yet many applications observe the same person over time and can supply an explicit anchor image. Recently, relative head pose estimation was proposed, which casts the problem as estimation of the relative geometric change between an anchor and a query image. However, existing methods still require the anchor image pose to be known at inference, significantly limiting their practical use. We first extend relative head pose estimation to full 6DoF by additionally predicting a depth-normalized relative translation, which composes with the anchor to recover metric query pose in the camera frame. To eliminate the need for a ground-truth anchor pose, we reconstruct a personalized avatar from a single unlabeled enrollment image and render a bank of virtual anchors with known poses. The bank provides robust rotation hypotheses through multi-anchor consensus, while the real enrollment image serves as the real anchor in the translation branch via landmark PnP. We also introduce an efficient variant that amortizes the bank cost once at enrollment, after which each query requires a single real-to-real relative prediction, with limited accuracy sacrifice. The relative pose estimator is trained exclusively on synthetic face renderings, without real pose supervision, pseudo-labels, domain adaptation, or fine-tuning on the evaluation sets. On real 6DoF benchmarks, ReVA-HPE achieves state-of-the-art rotation accuracy on BIWI and, given a near-frontal enrollment image, on FaMoS, while remaining competitive in metric translation despite relying solely on synthetic pose supervision. Project page and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[R$^2$-Omni: Reducing Structural and Precision Redundancy in Omni-LLMs for Long-Form Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03247)**  `arXiv:2611.03247`  `cs.CV`  
  _Bolong Feng, Pengfei Zhou, Zhenglin Wan, Wei Wang, Wangbo Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni-modal large language models (Omni-LLMs) typically process large numbers of multimodal tokens from audio-visual inputs, incurring substantial computational costs and a large memory footprint for the key-value (KV) cache. Aggressive token pruning can alleviate this problem while maintaining competitive performance on multiple-choice tasks, which require only a single option as output. However, we observe substantial quality degradation when generating long-form, open-ended responses. We attribute this gap to query-guided selection, which can retain sufficient evidence for answer selection while discarding details needed for comprehensive generation. This motivates reducing overlapping information across tokens, termed structural redundancy, while preserving diverse content. However, the retained representations may still contain precision redundancy due to unnecessarily high numerical precision in their cached values. To address these two levels of redundancy in token representations, we introduce a training-free framework named R$^2$-Omni. First, Structure-Aware Token Consolidation (STC) consolidates redundant representations using spatiotemporal similarity in video and local continuity in audio. Then, Semantic-Sensitivity Guided Precision Allocation (SSPA) allocates value precision to the retained representations according to the estimated reduction in attention-output error under quantized keys, with query attention and audio-visual alignment providing a semantic prior. Extensive experiments on long-form generation benchmarks demonstrate an average 5.0$\times$ reduction in the memory footprint while preserving 97.98% of baseline generation quality and achieving a 1.48$\times$ end-to-end speedup, highlighting the benefits of jointly reducing structural and precision redundancy.
  </details>

- **[SurgPace: Complexity-Aware Progressive Distillation for Surgical Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03125)**  `arXiv:2611.03125`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhicheng He, Xiaohan Xing, Hongqiu Wang, Kun Yuan, Guankun Wang, Yanheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown strong potential in robotic surgery for question answering, scene understanding, and grounded assistance, but their high computational cost limits real clinical deployment. Knowledge distillation provides a practical way to build compact surgical MLLMs, yet direct distillation is not well suited to surgical data because surgical samples vary greatly in difficulty, visual ambiguity, and required clinical context, making uniform teacher-student transfer unstable. In addition, the role of supervision changes during training: early stages require stronger teacher guidance for multimodal alignment, while later stages rely more on task supervision for effective optimization. To address these challenges, we propose SurgPace, a complexity-aware continuous curriculum framework for progressive distillation of surgical vision-language models. SurgPace estimates sample complexity using predictive confidence and Lorentz geometric consistency, and uses the fused score to guide a continuous easy-to-hard training process. We further introduce hierarchical representation alignment, which aligns the student with the teacher at the feature, logit, and probability levels, while gradually shifting the training focus from teacher imitation to task supervision. This design separates sample-level curriculum from objective-level scheduling, leading to more stable and effective distillation. We evaluate SurgPace on Surg-396K, a benchmark built from four public surgical datasets and covering five dialogue paradigms. SurgPace consistently outperforms strong MLLM distillation baselines, demonstrating its effectiveness for building compact, memory-efficient surgical MLLMs with faster decoding under controlled benchmarks. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PhotoGuider: Composition-Aware Cropping and 360$^{\circ}$ View Framing with Explainable Photographic Assessments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02897)**  `arXiv:2611.02897`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuan-Kang Lee, Kuan-Lin Chen, Chia-Che Chang, Yu-Lun Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional image cropping assumes the photograph already contains the composition worth keeping, and asks only where to crop. A full 360$^{\circ}$ scene offers no such guarantee: the viewing direction in a scene has to be discovered before anything can be framed. We present PhotoGuider, a measure-then-frame method that answers both questions with compact learned scorers over one shared set of composition-aware features. The designed features are extracted using foundation and task-specific vision models and structured into four photographic tokens: Subject, Composition, Aesthetics, and Integrity. Our MLLM explainer is fine-tuned to generate composition-related explanations conditioned on images and measured token assessments. To our knowledge, PhotoGuider is the first method to combine explicit composition-based direction selection in 360$^{\circ}$ panoramas with refined framing, camera-parameter output for native re-rendering, and measurement-conditioned explanations. On two standard 2D cropping benchmarks, PhotoGuider achieves the highest average mIoU performance against ground-truth crops. In a blind ranking study, 120 participants ranked three cropping methods over 100 panoramic scenes from the public Poly Haven HDRI library, placing our framings first in 53.2% of cases, against 27.3% and 19.5% for two strong alternatives. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Lookahead Prediction-Guided Adaptive Visual Refocusing for Interleaved Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02780)**  `arXiv:2611.02780`  `cs.CV`  
  _Mengzhao Wang, Yanli Ji, Peng Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interleaved multimodal chain-of-thought reasoning improves the visual reasoning capabilities of multimodal large language models by dynamically incorporating visual evidence during reasoning. However, existing methods primarily select evidence from historical context. When the reasoning focus shifts, history-guided evidence may remain focused on prior regions and fail to cover the visual information required by the next step. We refer to this lag between visual evidence and subsequent reasoning demands as Temporal Evidence Myopia. To address this issue, we propose Lookahead Prediction-guided Adaptive Visual Refocusing (LPAVR), a training-free test-time framework. LPAVR transforms Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) from a decoding acceleration mechanism into a lookahead signal for anticipating upcoming reasoning intent. During MTP decoding, the draft head generates multiple future tokens, while the target model verifies them in parallel and retains the longest prefix consistent with its decoding trajectory. This target-verified prefix contains near-future semantics, which LPAVR uses to construct lookahead-guided visual evidence and assess whether history-guided evidence covers the indicated regions. If the coverage is sufficient, the history-guided evidence is retained; otherwise, LPAVR switches to the lookahead-guided evidence. This better aligns the visual context with upcoming reasoning and improves consistency between draft predictions and target-model decoding. Experiments on multiple multimodal reasoning benchmarks show that LPAVR improves both reasoning accuracy and the acceptance rates of MTP speculative tokens, jointly enhancing visual reasoning and speculative decoding efficiency.
  </details>

- **[MedHEB: Benchmarking Medical Embeddings Across Heterogeneous Clinical Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02519)**  `arXiv:2611.02519`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yingshu Li, Shaoyang Zhou, Zhanyu Wang, Yunyi Liu, Xinyu Liang, Xi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical evidence is inherently relational: medical images are interpreted with prior studies, cross-modal examinations, reports, and localized findings, and retrieving relevant evidence is therefore central to clinical interpretation. Recent foundation models increasingly encode these heterogeneous sources into shared embedding spaces, yet existing embedding evaluation often focuses on isolated matches, leaving unclear whether current models retrieve relevant evidence across changes in modality, appearance, and granularity. We introduce MedHEB, a unified benchmark with 107 retrieval tasks from 54 datasets, including both in-distribution and held-out-source evaluation, across three axes: evidence form (2D images, 3D volumes, text), pairing type (image--image, image--text, text--text, and modality-bridging visual matching), and relevance granularity (global, question-conditioned, region-level). Under a single ranking protocol, MedHEB varies clinically relevant evidence definitions to test whether embedding proximity remains meaningful across heterogeneous evidence, including modality-bridging visual retrieval and region-level text-to-region retrieval. Across representative models, performance is uneven across retrieval families: models strong on conventional image--text or text--text retrieval are less reliable on modality-bridging and region-level tasks. Current embedding spaces capture some clinical relationships well but do not yet provide balanced retrieval across heterogeneous evidence. We further provide MedEmb, an MLLM-based reference embedding model trained on the MedHEB ID split, as a reproducible baseline for unified 2D--3D--text medical retrieval. We release MedHEB with preprocessing scripts, evaluation code, access manifests, and MedEmb to support research on clinically meaningful medical embeddings.
  </details>

- **[Advances in Agentic Computer Graphics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01860)**  `arXiv:2611.01860`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hengyu Liu, Jihyeon Je, Ruiqi Wang, Mingxuan Wu, Haven Feng, Qixing Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Computer graphics is built on code. Large language model (LLM) agents can now generate graphics code and iteratively refine it through various feedback. This capability opens the door to a potentially new paradigm of computer graphics, with graphics algorithms increasingly built around agentic systems to automate progressively complex tasks. Over the past years, research on agentic graphics has expanded rapidly across a diverse range of tasks. This rapid growth makes it increasingly difficult for manual literature reviews to keep pace with new developments. At the same time, similar ideas are often described using different terminology across these tasks, further complicating efforts to develop a systematic understanding of the field. In this report, we address these challenges through two contributions. First, we introduce a unified taxonomy for characterizing agentic systems across diverse computer graphics tasks. Second, we develop an agentic literature system that can assist human researchers in analyzing existing and newly published papers at scale. Building on these contributions, we analyze emerging research trends and discuss open challenges in coordinating agents and tools across graphics tasks, designing interfaces and representations for human-agent collaboration, sustaining long-horizon planning and refinement, and reliably evaluating interactive graphics systems.
  </details>

- **[MMCoT$^3$: Empowering 3D Scene Understanding with 3D-Text Interleaved Multi-Modal Chain-of-Thought Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01676)**  `arXiv:2611.01676`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yongqi Shan, Yifan Wang, Yunzhi Zhuge, Lijun Wang, Huchuan Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding and reasoning over 3D scenes from point clouds is essential for a broad range of AI applications. Recent 3D large language models have shown promising results, yet they still face two fundamental limitations: (i) insufficient capability for grounded multi-step reasoning in complex 3D scenes, and (ii) a text-centric inference paradigm that lacks effective interaction between textual reasoning and visual evidence. As a result, these models often fail to verify intermediate inferences, disambiguate similar object instances, and avoid hallucinations. To address these limitations, we propose MMCoT$^3$, the first 3D-Text Interleaved Chain-of-Thought (CoT) framework for 3D scene understanding. The core idea is to explicitly interleave textual reasoning with task-relevant 3D evidence, enabling the model to consult scene observations throughout the reasoning process. In addition, since a textual mention in 3D scenes may correspond to multiple candidate objects, naive object-mention alignment can inject incorrect evidence and mislead subsequent reasoning. To mitigate this multi-instance ambiguity, we design an ambiguity-aware evidence aggregation mechanism that uses query-guided cross-attention to fuse candidate object tokens and inject both evidence and ambiguity cues into the reasoning process. To better equip the model with multi-step reasoning capability, we further develop PointCoT, a large-scale 3D-specific CoT instruction tuning dataset with approximately 176.2K instances spanning captioning, question answering, dialogue, and planning tasks. Extensive experiments on six benchmarks show that our method consistently improves performance and achieves state-of-the-art results, validating the effectiveness of our proposed ambiguity-aware 3D-text interleaved reasoning for 3D scene understanding.
  </details>

- **[AR-RRG: Anatomy-Guided Retrieval-Driven Radiology Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01529)**  `arXiv:2611.01529`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiang Zhang, Shijie Song, Wanqing Zhao, Wei Zhou, Rui Cao, Dekui Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Radiology report generation (RRG) streamlines clinical workflows and alleviates the burden of radiologists' documentation. Standard vision-language models capture global visual cues and lack fine-grained alignment between anatomical regions and diagnostic text. Most retrieval-augmented methods retrieve complete historical reports, introducing redundant noise and severe vision-text mismatches that trigger clinically risky hallucinations and missed lesions. In this paper, we introduce AR-RRG, an anatomy-guided retrieval-driven method for the generation of radiology reports. AR-RRG consists of two core modules: the anatomy-aware self-training lesion detection network (ASLDNet) and the retrieval-driven radiology report generation (RRRG). ASLDNet leverages anatomy-aware spatial verification (ASV) and class-adaptive dynamic thresholding (CDT) via self-training, delivering accurate lesion localization to support subsequent generation. As the generative branch of AR-RRG, RRRG eliminates clinical hallucinations through dual-granularity retrieval (DR) and complements missing lesions with a neighborhood consensus (NC) mechanism. A parallel continuous visual perception (PCVP) stream converts radiographs into visual tokens through fixed medical encoding and resampling. Multi-scale text and visual features are fused, followed by LoRA-efficient large language model tuning to generate clinically consistent, visually grounded radiology reports. Extensive evaluations on MS-CXR, MIMIC-CXR, and IU X-Ray demonstrate that AR-RRG consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods across mainstream metrics. Our code will be made publicly available.
  </details>

- **[StreamDuo: A Live Commentator and a Research Agent for Unseen Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01120)**  `arXiv:2611.01120`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zixian Gao, Penghao Luo, Xiangcheng Cao, Lixin Xiu, Ziqi Cai, Zhen Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Streaming video LLMs can now commentate live gameplay, but they are trained and tested on the same games. We study cross-game streaming commentary, in which a model trained on one game commentates games it has never seen, and find that current commentators transfer their timing but not their knowledge of the game world. We propose StreamDuo, which pairs a trainable commentator with a frozen research agent that reads the wiki of the game being played. When the commentator sees something it cannot name, it sends the current frames, and the research agent returns a few wiki entries two seconds later while the stream continues. We train only the commentator, on a single game, in two stages. Information-gain labels teach it to ask only where an answer would help it say what the human caster said, and on-policy self-distillation from a copy of itself that sees the ideal answer teaches it to use noisy answers. Trained on Baldur's Gate 3 and tested on ten unseen games, StreamDuo raises entity recall from 0.082 to 0.116 over Proact-VL trained on the same data, cuts source-game leakage from 9.9% to 1.0%, and achieves the best timing and the highest judged liveliness and overall quality among streaming models.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Group and Order: Cross-Instance Self-Supervised RL for Vision-Centric MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00892)**  `arXiv:2611.00892`  `cs.CV`  
  _Songming Yang, Hong-Tao Yu, Xiu-Shen Wei_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) post-training has become an important paradigm for improving multimodal large language models (MLLMs), especially with automatically verifiable rewards. Self-supervised visual pretext tasks provide such rewards without human annotations. However, existing methods mainly exploit within-instance structure, offering limited supervision for cross-instance discrimination and fine-grained comparison. We introduce Group-and-Order Self-Supervised Reinforcement Learning (GO-SSL), a cross-instance jigsaw framework for vision-centric MLLM post-training. Given two visual instances, GO-SSL mixes their local elements and trains the model to group them by instance of origin while recovering the order within each group, coupling instance-level comparison with spatial, temporal, or geometric structure recovery. This paired formulation turns the same unlabeled data into richer verifiable supervision through diverse pairings, shuffles, and a random-to-hard curriculum. We instantiate GO-SSL mainly on cross-image jigsaw tasks and further extend it to 3D depth and video temporal ordering. Across diverse benchmarks, GO-SSL consistently improves over the base MLLM and prior self-supervised post-training baselines, with clear gains in fine-grained perception and spatial understanding. Further evaluations across model scales and families support its generality. These results suggest that cross-instance comparison provides a useful self-supervised RL signal for improving transferable visual understanding in MLLMs. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DramaVQA: Probing Detailed Understanding of Ultra-Long TV Dramas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00769)**  `arXiv:2611.00769`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinyue Huo, Pengfei Chen, Jiannan Ge, Kaiwen Duan, Jiacheng Shao, Yunning Cao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite recent progress in long-form video understanding, current benchmarks mostly focus on short clips or hour-scale videos. This leaves a major gap in evaluating ultra-long video understanding, where videos often last tens of hours. To bridge this gap, we introduce DramaVQA, a large-scale benchmark built upon long-form TV dramas. Containing approximately 20K question-answering (QA) pairs, the scale of DramaVQA exceeds previous long-video datasets, such as Video-MME and LVBench, by an order of magnitude. Our benchmark has two main features that make it stand out from existing datasets. First, we establish a scalable, LLM-assisted annotation pipeline that uses multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to generate complex questions, followed by strict human checking to ensure high data quality. Second, DramaVQA introduces a multi-level time structure; queries are designed across different video lengths, with the hardest questions requiring overall understanding across the entire drama. Extensive evaluations show that state-of-the-art MLLMs face major challenges when tested on these very long videos, highlighting the need for agentic AI algorithms capable of managing long-term memory. The project is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Density-Aware Calibration for Resource-Efficient Medical Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00684)**  `arXiv:2611.00684`  `cs.CV`  
  _Tien-Dat Dam, Huu-Thuan Huynh, Minh-Triet Tran, Trung-Nghia Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical vision-language models (VLMs) show promise for clinical image understanding, but their computational and memory requirements hinder deployment on resource-constrained clinical hardware. Post-training quantization (PTQ) offers a practical compression strategy without retraining, yet its effectiveness depends strongly on the calibration data. We propose CoreCalib, a training-free and quantizer-agnostic framework for constructing compact calibration sets that preserve the target medical VQA distribution. CoreCalib stratifies data by clinical question class, identifies representative visual modes using frozen DINOv2 features with UMAP and HDBSCAN, and expands selected images into class-balanced question-answer pairs. On Kvasir-VQA-x1, we evaluate CoreCalib with MASQuant, GPTQ, and QIG using a fixed 128-pair budget. CoreCalib achieves the best METEOR with all three quantizers and the best ROUGE-L with MASQuant and GPTQ. With W4A8 MASQuant, it improves METEOR from 0.5672 with COCO calibration to 0.6234, recovering approximately 41% of the degradation relative to BF16. These results support CoreCalib as a data-centric component for resource-efficient medical VLM deployment. Project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[OCRia-1 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00092)**  `arXiv:2611.00092`  `cs.CV`  
  _Aman Rangapur, Antonio Jimeno Yepes, Daniel Bustamante, Nutchapol Dendumrongsup, Sebastián G. Acosta, Pierre-Loïc Doulcet, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce OCRia-1, a 4B vision-language model (VLM) for document parsing that excels at preserving table structure, extracting chart data and retaining text formatting. OCRia-1 handles content that has traditionally been challenging in document parsing: merged cells and spanning headers, tables with thousands of cells, chart data, and meaningful text formatting such as bold, italics, subscripts and superscripts, preserving details needed for business-critical document analysis. At the time of this report, it achieves the highest scores on tables, charts and text formatting among the systems evaluated, including a 35B model and Mistral OCR 4.1. Its mean ParseBench score over the four dimensions we evaluate is 0.87, against 0.75 for the next-best system.We emphasize data quality and reward design in training OCRia-1. Synthetic pages provide exact ground truth for long tables and rich formatting, while real pages are transcribed using a pipeline grounded in the PDF text layer. We attribute OCRia-1's performance primarily to careful data curation that prioritizes quality and diversity over quantity. Dimension-specific rewards evaluate table reconstruction, chart-data extraction and text-formatting fidelity. We train the model on the curated data using supervised fine-tuning (SFT), then refine it through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR).To our knowledge, OCRia-1 is the fastest published document-parsing VLM in its class, decoding at about 4,700 tokens per second with a single request on one B200 GPU, about nine times faster than the base model on stock vLLM. This is achieved through a block-diffusion speculator, quantization and custom kernels.
  </details>

- **[BreastXAgent: A lightweight agentic explainability framework for breast density classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00065)**  `arXiv:2611.00065`  `cs.CV`  
  _Muhammad Turab, Raja Vavekanand, Knooz Fatima_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep learning models for breast density classification typically return only a label and confidence score. This paper presents BreastXAgent, a lightweight agentic framework that combines a pretrained classifier, Grad-CAM visual explanation, LangGraph-based routing, LLM report generation, and rule-based guardrails to produce controlled, faithful, non-diagnostic reports. On 160 mammography images, uncontrolled LLM reporting produced 75% Grad-CAM overclaims and 62.5% clinical overclaims. Strict prompting and LangGraph routing improved safety but did not eliminate violations. The full BreastXAgent achieved 100% safety pass rate, zero overclaims, and highest utility score. Results demonstrate that agentic guardrails enable safe, structured explanation generation without modifying the underlying classifier.
  </details>

- **[Keyframes First, Motion Second: Revisiting Compressed-Domain Video Captioning under Limited Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00048)**  `arXiv:2611.00048`  `cs.CV`  
  _Asok B K, Ashim Nepal, Loknath Regmi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compressed-domain video captioners such as CoCap read the H.264 bitstream directly: a pretrained encoder reads the keyframes (I-frames), while motion and residual encoders, trained from scratch, turn the codec's motion vectors and residuals into action tokens. This motion branch costs decoding time and supervision, both scarce on edge devices. We ask when it pays off under limited supervision. In a controlled study on 2,500 VATEX training clips, we fix a frozen SigLIP 2 keyframe encoder, a GPT-2 caption decoder and the training schedule, vary only the visual tokens the decoder receives (34 configurations), and report held-out test CIDEr with paired bootstrap intervals and three seeds. Motion encoders trained from scratch bring no measurable benefit: CoCap-style action tokens change CIDEr by -0.55 (95% CI [-2.3, +1.2]), and six other uses of motion do not help. Warm-started from a model trained on 48x the caption samples, they add +1.3 CIDEr on average, though not significantly across seeds, and only while the keyframe tokens stay frozen. Reading the keyframes better matters more: fine-tuning SigLIP 2's own attention-pooling head adds 5.4 CIDEr, and one to eight such tokens beat the 16 CoCap-style tokens on every seed while decoding only I-frames (7.3 ms instead of 44-114 ms per clip). The same holds on MSVD's full split in CoCap's own video encoding, where the readout leads by 14.2 CIDEr and action tokens bring no reliable gain, also with clean single-reference motion vectors. Under limited supervision, keyframes come first and motion second.
  </details>

- **[VideoMemBench: How Well Do MLLMs Remember in Video Conversations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40131)**  `arXiv:2610.40131`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhengdong Hu, Chao Wang, Kaituo Feng, Huanjin Yao, Mingfei Lu, Hehe Fan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can now process hour-long videos, making extended multi-turn dialogue over a single video a natural mode of use. Sustaining such a dialogue requires more than answering each question in isolation: the model must retrieve and reuse information established earlier in the conversation, both what it has seen and what it has said, an ability we term in-context video memory. Existing video QA benchmarks leave this ability unmeasured: their questions are mutually independent, never reference one another, and each is answerable from the video alone, so nothing ever requires carrying information across turns. We introduce VideoMemBench, in which each sample pairs a single video with a long multi-turn dialogue, whose turns are connected by typed cross-turn dependencies: an Episodic Dependency refers back to a visual episode described in an earlier turn, probing memory of what the model has seen, while an Answer Dependency builds on the model's own earlier answer, probing memory of what the model has said. Every dependency is verified considering two properties: 1) necessity, meaning the question cannot be answered without its earlier turns, and 2) non-leakage, meaning the question does not restate the information from its earlier turns. To enforce these properties, we design a construction pipeline with three LLM roles: a planner, a generator, and a judger, with each dialogue reviewed by human experts. VideoMemBench comprises 326 videos with 2,476 turns, of which 1,387 depend on earlier turns, including multi-parent dependencies and long-range call-back. Among over 20 evaluated open- and closed-source MLLMs, Claude Opus 5 performs the best with an overall score of only 45.7%, highlighting the challenge of in-context video memory. Our dataset and evaluation are released.
  </details>

- **[From Label Priors to Task Evidence: Efficient Long-Video Frame Selection via Bayesian GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25418)**  `arXiv:2610.25418`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuochen Chang, Bingjie Gao, Qingyang Liu, Qianli Ma, Yibo Miao, Haonan Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) face significant scalability challenges in long-video understanding due to quadratic attention complexity and context window constraints. Existing retrieval-based approaches typically rely on supervised learning from static keyframe annotations. However, these label priors often fail to align with the actual information required by downstream MLLMs for complex reasoning, creating a misalignment between the training target and inference needs. To bridge this gap, we propose a probabilistic framework that reformulates frame selection as latent-variable inference. We first initialize a lightweight policy using supervised learning to capture general visual saliency. Then, we treat a frozen MLLM as a task environment and use its answer likelihood as dense task evidence to refine the policy via Bayesian Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Within this formulation, the negative log-likelihood (NLL) over ground-truth answer tokens provides a principled dense reward, while a prior-matching KL term preserves semantic consistency with the elicited label prior. This process transitions the selection principle from static label priors to task evidence. Extensive experiments on Video-MME, MLVU, and LongVideoBench demonstrate that our selector achieves competitive accuracy with MLLM-based scorers while retaining the inference efficiency of lightweight methods. The selector is trained once with a frozen MLLM environment and transfers directly to multiple downstream MLLMs without retraining. Code is available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[Matching in Context: Temporal Fusion and Query Adaptation for Training-Free Video Temporal Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08850)**  `arXiv:2609.08850`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhuo Cao, Bingqing Zhang, Sen Wang, Xue Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-guided Video Temporal Grounding (VTG) localizes moments in an untrimmed video that correspond to a text query. Supervised approaches require costly temporal annotations and task-specific training, yet can generalize poorly under distribution shift. To reduce this dependence on task-specific supervision, training-free approaches use pretrained vision-language models to localize moments by measuring the similarity between video and query features. However, independently encoded frames provide limited temporal context, while a fixed query embedding can assign similar scores to different candidate moments within the video. To address these limitations, we propose DSE-VTG, a \underline{D}ual-\underline{S}ide \underline{E}nhancement framework that combines visual evidence across temporal granularities with video-specific query adaptation, without task-specific training. On the visual side, Multi-scale Similarity Fusion (MSF) combines frame- and clip-level similarities into a unified, temporally aware similarity profile. On the query side, Query-level Test-Time Adaptation (Q-TTA) uses pseudo-labels to optimize a lightweight additive offset for more discriminative matching within the video, without fine-tuning the backbone or calling external large language models. Experiments on three standard and two out-of-distribution (OOD) benchmarks show gains over the prior training-free methods. On Charades-STA, DSE-VTG improves mIoU by 5.89 percentage points over the strongest compared baseline under the same backbone. Under distribution shift, DSE-VTG reaches 50.86 mIoU on Charades-CG Novel-Word, surpassing the strongest supervised baseline by 2.76 mIoU. Our code will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[UAV-DualCog: A Dual-Cognition Benchmark for Grounded UAV Agent-World Reasoning with MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.16193)**  `arXiv:2607.16193`  `cs.CV`  
  _Like Liu, Zhengzheng Xu, Hongzhe Li, Lin Wang, Dian Shao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) perform strongly across vision-language tasks, yet remain insufficiently explored in UAV scenarios. Recent UAV-oriented benchmarks have begun to evaluate MLLMs in aerial scenarios, but typically focus on scene understanding, event recognition, or navigation completion, rather than jointly assessing the dual-cognition capability required for UAV agents: reasoning about the UAV's own position, behavior, and observation state alongside external landmark states under continuously changing viewpoints, supported by explicit spatio-temporal grounding. To address this gap, we present UAV-DualCog, the first benchmark to formulate UAV dual cognition as the coordinated evaluation of Agent-State Reasoning and World-State Reasoning across multi-view images and egocentric videos, with explicit spatial or temporal grounding. The State-aware UAV Simulation Platform (SUSP) and the DualCog-aware Capture System (DCCS) construct 4,096 image and 2,048 video samples from 12 scenes, pairing complementary state questions within shared Agent-Landmark contexts. Extensive evaluations on 45 models show that current MLLMs remain far from reliable in UAV dual cognition: Agent-State Reasoning is generally weaker than World-State Reasoning, semantic answers do not reliably coincide with spatial or temporal grounding, and coordinated abilities remain limited. Thinking-mode comparisons, open-ended analyses, and sim-to-real validation show that these limitations persist across inference settings, formats, and environments. We further construct UAV-DualCog-Train and improve UAV dual-cognition performance through a lightweight optimization probe. Supplementary website, code, and dataset:this https URL
  </details>

- **[SHIFT: Steering Hidden Intermediates in Flow Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.09213)**  `arXiv:2604.09213`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nina Konovalova, Ibragim Idrisov, Aibek Alanov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models have become the leading approach for high-fidelity image generation, with recent DiT-based models achieving strong prompt adherence and high visual quality. However, removing unwanted concepts from these models typically requires expensive gradient-based fine-tuning, which is particularly prohibitive for multi-billion-parameter Diffusion Transformers and incompatible with distilled variants. We propose SHIFT, a simple model-weight-update-free inference-time framework for concept suppression in DiT models via targeted manipulation of intermediate activations at inference time, inspired by activation steering in LLMs. SHIFT learns steering vectors from contrastive prompts and applies them dynamically. We demonstrate that a single time-independent steering vector generalizes across all denoising timesteps and that vectors computed on a distilled model transfer effectively to its non-distilled counterpart without recomputation. SHIFT achieves strong concept suppression while preserving prompt fidelity and image quality. The same mechanism also enables flexible control -- adding, removing, or changing objects and styles without retraining. SHIFT targets hosted inference settings: it does not remove the concept from the model weights and is therefore complementary to weight-level unlearning.
  </details>

- **[Can MLLMs Perceive and Describe Medical Image Quality Beyond Scores?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.01691)**  `arXiv:2510.01691`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jiyao Liu, Jinjie Wei, Wanying Qu, Chenglong Ma, Junzhi Ning, Yunheng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical Image Quality Assessment (IQA) serves as a critical safety gate for clinical AI, yet existing approaches rely on scalar metrics that fail to capture the descriptive, expert-like evaluation process central to clinical practice. We introduce MedQ-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for language-based medical IQA evaluation with MLLMs, establishing a perception--description paradigm across 5 imaging modalities and over 40 quality attributes. The benchmark comprises two complementary tasks: MedQ-Perception probes low-level visual attribute discrimination via 2,600 expert-annotated perceptual queries; MedQ-Description covers both no-reference and comparison description sub-tasks with 708 expert-annotated samples, evaluated through a four-dimensional judging protocol. Comprehensive evaluation of 23 state-of-the-art MLLMs reveals that current models exhibit only preliminary and unstable capabilities, and highlights the urgent need for targeted optimization of MLLMs in medical IQA. Project & Dataset:this https URL
  </details>

- **[EviCast: Forecasting Retrieval Gains Through Shared Response Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02387)**  `arXiv:2611.02387`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yibo Wang, Jisheng Dang, Bimei Wang, Jiongpei Hou, Jingwen Zhao, Wenjun Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) extends large language models with external evidence, yet relevant evidence does not ensure that a generator can use it. Indiscriminate retrieval can degrade task performance while adding retrieval and long-context inference costs. Existing methods neither determine before large-scale execution whether a generator, task distribution, and retriever configuration is worth deploying nor diagnose multiple candidate retrievers under a shared budget. We introduce EviCast, a framework that forecasts retrieval value through interventional evidence-state decomposition. EviCast measures a generator's response to controlled evidence states once and combines this shared profile with the offline evidence-state distribution of each candidate retriever, yielding the end-to-end net utility and its 95% lower confidence bound without executing every RAG configuration. A state-refinement analysis characterizes when its binary and topology-aware instantiations are exact and when shared calibration is statistically more efficient than per-retriever calibration. In frozen evaluations, EviCast correctly decides all 14 HotpotQA retrieval configurations with a forecast error of 2.75 F1 points while reducing diagnostic generation calls by 80%, and it attains the lowest forecast error among seven predictors in the low-budget regime. Its topology-aware instantiation, EviCast-T, captures the complementarity between terminal and bridge evidence, correctly deciding all 16 configurations on MuSiQue and HotpotQA and 23 of 24 after prospective transfer to a third dataset. These results show that retrieval deployment depends on both evidence availability and the generator's ability to convert evidence structure into stable net utility. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TopoSteer: Topological Layer Selection for Activation Steering in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01493)**  `arXiv:2611.01493`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Tuc Nguyen, Marcos Gutiérrez-del-Pozo, Eduardo Paluzo-Hidalgo, Yifan Hu, Adaku Uchendu, Thai Le_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation steering controls large language models at inference time by adding a vector to their internal representations. Most work asks how to build that vector, and far less attention has gone to where to add it, even though steering at a poorly chosen layer can be worse than not steering at all. Prior selectors choose a layer without ever steering the model to test it: they score how separable the target behavior is at that layer, and how consistently the individual examples point along the steering direction. We argue that what matters is not the separability of the two activation clouds, nor the alignment of individual displacements with their mean, but the extent of the cloud of per-example displacements that mean-difference steering averages. We propose a topology-based layer selection method, called TopoSteer, which scores a layer by the total H_0 persistence of its displacement cloud, computed as the length of the cloud's Euclidean minimum spanning tree. We show that the score focuses on the displacement cloud's relative geometry, while class separation affects it only through standardization. We also provide a theoretical explanation for our method by proving that, as example-level variation increases, TopoSteer and discriminability move in opposite directions, so the two criteria can rank layers in opposite ways. Across multiple language models and steering tasks, TopoSteer provides a principled alternative to discriminability- and separability-based layer selection and yields more effective steering without requiring held-out validation.
  </details>

- **[Towards Fine-grained and Robust Detection of Human-AI Co-authorship](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01427)**  `arXiv:2611.01427`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ziruo Zhao, Jie Fu, Wendy Hui Wang, Zining Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The widespread adoption of large language models (LLMs) is reshaping how textual content is produced, complicating text provenance, and raising concerns for misinformation, plagiarism, and intellectual property. While prior work on AI-generated text detection has progressed, one-dimensional human-AI co-authorship scores lack signals directly relevant to ``AI involvement''. It remains unclear how adversarial paraphrasing distorts detection signals. In this work, we formulate a discrete-class text provenance detection framework. This framework allows an abstraction of realistic co-authorship scenarios into distinct degrees of AI involvement, differentiating the AI polishing on the surface level from the AI updating the discourse structures. This framework reveals the mechanisms of how adversarial paraphrasing blurs the decision boundaries for co-authorship detectors. To improve robustness of the detectors, we augment training data with adversarial paraphrases generated by the state-of-the-art paraphrasing attack model, exposing detectors to challenging meaning-preserving rewrites. Extensive experiments across multiple datasets and generation models demonstrate that adversarial paraphrasing substantially degrades detectors trained only on benign data, while adversarial data augmentation leads to consistent robustness gains and good cross-generator transferability. Overall, our findings highlight the value of discrete-class formulation and principled data augmentation for building reliable detectors of human-AI co-authorship.
  </details>

- **[FlyWheel: Attention Kernels Built for the TPU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01426)**  `arXiv:2611.01426`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zishuo Bao, Zeshen Zhang, Linkun Chen, Yucheng Lu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attention kernels on Tensor Processing Units (TPUs) often underutilize their matrix units (MXUs) because of repeated matrix operand loads, vector-intensive computation, and dependencies that leave MXUs idle. We present FlyWheel, a set of fast TPU kernels for both softmax and linear attention. FlyWheel reformulates softmax and triangular inversion to reduce vector work, while coordinating data layouts to improve matrix operand reuse. It then overlaps independent computation to hide execution latency. We apply these techniques to both softmax attention and Gated DeltaNet on TPU v6e and v7x. FlyWheel speeds up softmax attention by up to $4.14\times$ over the state-of-the-art RPA kernel and $1.80\times$ over Splash Attention, reaching 81% of peak BF16 throughput on v6e. It also speeds up Gated DeltaNet prefill by up to $3.61\times$. Integrated into vLLM serving Qwen3.8-27B, FlyWheel improves prefill throughput by up to $1.56\times$ while maintaining comparable accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Information-Theoretic Design of Sparse Autoencoder-Based Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01378)**  `arXiv:2611.01378`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hirofumi Morise_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse autoencoder (SAE)-based activation steering is a lightweight approach for modifying the behavior of large language models (LLMs) by adding small vectors to hidden states during inference. However, designing effective steering interventions remains challenging. In this work, we develop an information-theoretic framework for analyzing SAE activations through empirical feature-firing distributions. The central object of the framework is the Jensen--Shannon divergence (JSD) between such firing distributions, which quantifies how far a generated or intervened state deviates from a dataset-induced target distribution. Based on an analytic divergence response for virtually shifted states, we construct steering vectors toward the target firing distribution. The same divergence can also be used to monitor deviation from another empirical distribution, such as a general valid-language distribution. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the steering in a question-mode generation task study. We further use teacher-forced pulse experiments to show that local interventions induce structured downstream JSD responses in later layers, and apply the framework to a safety-related target--contrast response setting for feature analysis.
  </details>

- **[Do Earnings Calls Help Predict Financial Restatements? Evaluating Text Beyond Public Firm Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01123)**  `arXiv:2611.01123`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ahmet Efe, Shivank Sapra, Bilal Ahmed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Financial restatements correct errors in previously published financial reports. Could managers' language in earnings calls warn of them in advance? Comparing 109 calls that precede restatements with 218 matched control calls, we find that the text predicts restatements but adds no detectable ranking gain beyond public firm information. A text classifier beats chance, largely because of restatements tied to special-purpose acquisition companies, but a model using only public information scores slightly higher. With refitting, the 95% interval for the gain from adding text (-0.05 to 0.11 AUROC) still leaves room for a meaningful benefit. A large language model scores higher, but we show that it names the exact ticker in 66% of masked transcripts, and it was released after the restatements occurred. Our results suggest that finding new warning signs in call text requires a public-information baseline and a check of what a language model already knows.
  </details>

- **[What Breaks and What Comes Back: Capability Damage and Recovery in Depth-Pruned Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00877)**  `arXiv:2611.00877`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Piyush Dhoka, Vedant Pandhare, Varun Inamdar, Aadarsh Pathre, Pavitha Nooji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Depth pruning removes whole Transformer blocks to make a language model smaller and faster, but the damage is not spread evenly across what the model can do, and a short fine-tuning run, called healing, decides what comes back. We measure this for small models used as agents. Four instruction-tuned models of 1.2-1.7B parameters (Llama-3.2-1B, Qwen2.5-1.5B, SmolLM2-1.7B and OLMo-2-1B) are pruned and healed, and six capabilities are tracked: structured output, function calling, instruction following, mathematical reasoning, safety refusal and knowledge. Three results hold across the families.
