First, function calling and math collapse in every model (retention 0.00-0.07 of the unpruned score), and the order in which capabilities break agrees closely across the four (Kendall's W = 0.92; 0.62 after correcting for chance floors). Second, which parameters are trained matters as much as which data: fully tuning the last three blocks instead of adding low-rank adapters to all blocks costs up to 0.18 in capped mean retention on three families, and one 300k-token pilot per scope detects this. Third, we test a planner that allocates a 1M-token healing budget from 21 short pilot heals. At a fixed budget it raises the weakest capability on two families, although only one of these gains survives a test that also resamples training seeds. Once its 3.48M pilot tokens are counted, uniform healing with the same total number of training tokens matches or beats every planned variant on every family and retains math significantly better on three of them in an item-level test (on one when seeds are also resampled). Healing longer is a strong and simple baseline that planned recovery must beat. Code, per-item predictions and every result are public.
  </details>

- **[Li$^{2}$SAO: Lifting Light Single-Rollout Asynchronous RL Optimization via Group-Relative Actor-Critic Bootstrapping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40726)**  `arXiv:2610.40726`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zichen Bai, Yanli Wang, Yang Liu, Junhao Shi, Xueqin Liu, Jinguo Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) is becoming a mainstream paradigm for post-training large language models (LLMs), as it improves training efficiency by updating the model as rollouts arrive. Recent work argues that group-wise sampling in GRPO does not naturally fit asynchronous RL training because rollout lengths follow a long-tailed distribution, and proposes Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization (SAO) to improve stability and off-policy behavior, especially on agentic tasks. However, SAO relies on a closed-source value model obtained from scaling value pretraining. In this paper, we analyze GRPO and LiSAO, our lightweight reproduction of SAO without the pretrained value model, on single-turn reasoning and multi-turn agentic tasks. We find that GRPO outperforms LiSAO on reasoning tasks, while the reverse holds on agentic tasks. Since the value model of LiSAO is not yet well trained , LiSAO lags behind GRPO substantially in early training steps. To address this issue, we propose Li$^{2}$SAO, which Lifts Light Single-rollout Asynchronous Optimization with group-relative actor-critic bootstrapping: while the critic is cold, a group-relative advantage trains the actor and a group-centered return trains the critic, after which training switches to single-rollout GAE. Experiments show that Li$^{2}$SAO catches up with or surpasses GRPO in early training and further raises the later performance ceiling. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Consilience for Verifier-Free Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09898)**  `arXiv:2608.09898`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Lecheng Kong, Like Hui, Haitao Mao, Jun Huan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling often uses an external verifier, such as compilers and test cases in coding or trained value functions in robotics applications, to obtain high-quality rollouts. Verifier-free test-time scaling (or VF-TTS) is gaining extensive attention as a mechanism to enhance Large Language Model (LLM) reasoning, primarily because we do not have access to such high-quality verifiers in many real-world applications. Among existing VF-TTS methods, confidence-based VF-TTS methods, which compute and rank rollouts solely by confidence, are particularly promising. Such methods introduce near-zero overhead for sample evaluation and require minimal access to internal model states, making the methods highly flexible across models and tasks.In this paper, we demonstrate a critical limitation of existing confidence-based VF-TTS methods by showing that such methods catastrophically break down on complex tasks. We observe a very interesting phenomenon: uniformly high confidence frequently indicates a failure to explore, favoring confidently wrong answers. To address this, our core insight is that robust cognitive search requires a specific confidence trajectory pattern: such methods perform exploratory branching at the beginning, as manifested by low initial confidence, and converge to a high final confidence solution. To implement this insight, we introduce consilience, a novel selection framework that explicitly evaluates the temporal asymmetry of confidence in reasoning. We operationalize this via a combinatorial metric that actively penalizes high initial confidence while strictly demanding final certainty. Extensive experiments covering both graduate-level mathematics problems and free-form code generation demonstrate that consilience effectively outperforms existing baselines, validating our novel perspective on completion confidence.
  </details>

- **[Activation-Based Active Learning for In-Context Learning: Challenges and Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.05134)**  `arXiv:2606.05134`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Yaseen M. Osman, Geoff V. Merrett, Stuart E. Middleton_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep active learning has previously been explored for LLM in-context sample selection, but not with methods that utilise recent advances in understanding of transformer activations. In this paper, we test the hypothesis that model activations could provide a fine-grained signal to optimise the selection of in-context examples. We present a comprehensive analysis of MLP activation-based deep active learning methods applied to in-context learning, including how different attention masking strategies impact active learning across diverse classification and generative datasets, using both Llama-3.2-3B and Qwen2.5-3B base models. However, we find a negative result: MLP and embedding layer outputs, viewed through the lenses of massive activations or the first four moments, do not correlate with example quality or task performance. Specifically, the absolute Spearman correlation coefficient is at most 0.33 for all tasks and models we tested, showing that such activation-based sampling should not be used for in-context learning. We hypothesise that this may be due to superposition, whereby models represent more features than they have dimensionality, suggesting that methods like Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) may be a promising future direction.
  </details>

- **[Probing the Limits of the Lie Detector Approach to LLM Deception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.10003)**  `arXiv:2603.10003`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Tom-Felix Thormann_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A strand of mechanistic approaches to deception in large language models (LLMs) relies on "lie detectors" - truth probes aimed to identify model outputs that are internally represented as false. This lie detector approach to LLM deception implicitly assumes that deception is coextensive with lying. This paper challenges that assumption by experimentally investigating whether LLMs can produce deceptive statements that are not false and whether truth probes (if naively applied as deception detectors) fail to detect such behavior. In doing so, we make four core contributions: First, we provide a dataset of manually written examples of deceptive non-falsities grounded in linguistic theory. Second, we show across six open-source LLMs of different sizes and families that some models can select these deceptive non-falsities in a forced-choice task. Third, we demonstrate that while truth probes trained on a standard true-false dataset perform well at detecting false outputs, they perform significantly worse at detecting deceptive non-falsities. We view this as substantiating the worry that naive applications of truth probes as deception detectors suffer from a critical blind spot. Fourth, we explore whether training deception probes in dialogical contexts or including misleading non-false statements in the training improves the deception detection rate.
  </details>

- **[CheXPoT: Post-Hoc Visual Verification and Correction of VLM-Generated Radiology Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00785)**  `arXiv:2611.00785`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Joey Guo, Rayan Ansari, Alaa Youssef_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown promise for chest radiograph report generation, but adoption remains limited because of hallucinated findings that are not grounded in the image. Correcting these hallucinations and improving overall report quality remains a significant challenge. Prior work has addressed this problem by refining models with reinforcement learning, preference optimization, or chain-of-thought supervision, often at substantial compute cost. To address this gap, we introduce CheXPoT, a program-of-thought (PoT) framework that verifies each generated finding against image features and removes those it cannot ground. CheXPoT decomposes generated reports into predicates with RadGraph and an LLM, grounds these predicates against a DenseNet-121 and Grad-CAM visual evidence table, executes PoT verification code, and rewrites reports after removing unsupported predicates. On a 4,145-image held-out split, CheXPoT yields statistically significant precision gains on 8 of 14 CheXpert classes and F1 gains on 5, with no significant decreases in either metric. CheXPoT lifts micro-average precision from 19.2% (MedGemma-4B) to 46.8% (MedGemma-4B + CheXPoT) and micro-average F1 from 23.0% to 49.4%, absolute gains of 27.6% and 26.4% respectively. We also tested CheXPoT on other VLM report generators. On LLaVA-Med, CheXPoT lifts micro-average precision from 4.5% to 17.7% and micro-average F1 from 6.3% to 19.4%. On MAIRA-2, CheXPoT lifts micro-average precision from 15.6% to 42.1% and micro-average F1 from 21.0% to 45.3%. Our results suggest that inexpensive post-hoc visual verification may improve VLM trustworthiness by correcting unsupported findings from generated reports. CheXPoT lays a foundation for tools that help validate VLM-generated radiology reports and reduce unsupported findings. These innovations may subsequently help increase trust and adoption of VLM tools in Medicine.
  </details>

- **[A Multi-Instance LLM Framework for Rating the Quality of Surgical Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25440)**  `arXiv:2605.25440`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Rafal Kocielnik, J. Everett Knudsen, Steven Y. Cen, Jasmine Lin, Cherine H. Yang, Atharva Deo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verbal feedback delivered by attending surgeons in the operating room plays a critical formative role in resident trainee skill acquisition. Yet, assessing the quality of trainer feedback and its effectiveness in influencing trainee behavior during live surgery remains a challenge. Prior studies assessed feedback content relying on extensive manual annotation by expert human raters and focused on developing broad taxonomies that overlook the qualitative aspects of feedback delivery such as clarity or urgency. Limited existing automated methods, including keyword analysis and topic modeling, also fail to capture these nuanced aspects. We introduce a two-stage LLM-based framework that discovers interpretable feedback quality criteria grounded in the context of surgical training. Our method uses multi-instance prompting via repeated independent sampling of candidate criteria followed by consolidation, and surgical domain knowledge injection to discover a small set of human interpretable scoring criteria (e.g., Encouragement, Urgency, Clarity). These criteria are then used to automatically score live surgical feedback via an LLM-as-a-judge approach. Evaluation on 4.2k trainer feedback instances demonstrates that our AI-discovered criteria outperform prior content-based frameworks in predicting feedback effectiveness, including observed trainee behavioral adjustments and trainer approval. This work advances scalable, human-aligned assessment of communication quality in the operating room and provides a foundation for improving surgical teaching practices.
  </details>

- **[RCR-Router: Efficient Role-Aware Context Routing for Multi-Agent LLM Systems with Structured Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.04903)**  `arXiv:2508.04903`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Jun Liu, Zhenglun Kong, Changdi Yang, Fan Yang, Tianqi Li, Peiyan Dong, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly organized into multi-agent systems for complex reasoning and decision-making tasks. However, scaling such systems can substantially increase inference cost, especially when multiple agents repeatedly process overlapping or role-irrelevant context. Many existing multi-agent frameworks focus on orchestration and role specialization, but still rely on full-context sharing or static context routing, leading to redundant token consumption, increased runtime, and inefficient memory utilization. We present \emph{Role-Conditioned Context Routing (RCR)}, an execution-time layer that allocates role-relevant shared-memory items to agents under token budgets. Given a structured shared memory and a set of role-specialized agents, RCR selectively routes task-relevant memory items to each agent according to its role, optional task-stage metadata, and token budget. RCR integrates role-conditioned relevance scoring, token-budgeted memory selection, and iterative memory updates to reduce redundant context exposure during multi-agent execution. Experiments on multi-hop QA, embodied reasoning (ALFWorld), and web-based decision-making (WebShop) show that RCR reduces token usage by 35\% on average and runtime by 38\% on average relative to Full-Context, while maintaining or improving task performance.
  </details>

- **[VIAS: Value-Isolating Activation Steering for Multi-Value Alignment and Compliance for Organisations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03518)**  `arXiv:2611.03518`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Avni Mittal, Avinash Anand, Dikshant Kukreja, Erik Cambria, Rajiv Ratn Shah, Sandipan Dandapat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Organisations increasingly deploy large language model agents that must follow organisational values. We check whether activation steering, which adds vectors to a model's internal activations during generation without changing its weights, can improve compliance beyond prompting alone. Since most scenarios involve several values at once, we focus on multi-value steering. A simple way to build these vectors is to contrast responses that follow all values relevant to a scenario with responses that violate them. However, this gives nearly identical vectors for different values: they capture a general compliance signal rather than individual values, which limits their use when combining several values. We propose Value-Isolating Activation Steering (VIAS), which contrasts responses to the same scenario under two prompts, one listing all relevant values and one omitting a single target value. We keep a pair only if the target value changes from compliant to non-compliant and both responses still meet a minimum compliance level on the other values. We evaluate VIAS on PROTECT, a workplace compliance dataset where each scenario is tagged with relevant values from a 21-value taxonomy. VIAS reduces overlap between value vectors, making them better suited for multi-value steering, although this alone does not lead to independent control over each value. Combined with prompting, VIAS complies with every tagged value on 69.6% of scenarios for Qwen2.5-7B, 75.2% for Mistral-7B, and 72.6% for Phi-3.5-mini, outperforming prompting alone for all three models.
  </details>

- **[An Experimental Study of Hybrid Retrieval, NLI and Open-source LLMs for Scientific Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03093)**  `arXiv:2611.03093`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lakshmi Narayanan P, Narenkarthik K, Naren Kumar C_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Claim verification is the task of deciding whether a source text supports a claim, contradicts it, or lacks sufficient evidence. Natural Language Inference (NLI) models can judge whether a single sentence supports or contradicts a claim, but they struggle when the evidence is spread across several sentences. Conversely, Large Language Models (LLMs) can reason across sentences, but may draw conclusions the text does not support. In this experimental study, we test whether a conservative fusion rule, labeling a claim Support or Contradict only when both models agree and defaulting to Not Enough Evidence otherwise, combines their strengths. We construct a benchmark of 2,035 claims from SciFact and CliniFact (858 Support, 152 Contradict, 1,025 Not Enough Evidence). We retrieve the top five most relevant sentences per claim using a hybrid of semantic and lexical (BM25) retrieval, which slightly outperforms either method alone. These retrieved sentences are then provided to every verification model. We then compare a DeBERTa-v3-base-mnli-fever-anli NLI baseline against Llama 3.1 8B, Qwen3 8B, and Mistral 7B. Qwen3 achieves the highest Macro-F1 (0.7937), followed by Llama 3.1 (0.7204) and Mistral (0.6388), while the NLI baseline reaches only 0.3157. Contrary to our hypothesis, fusion degrades performance, dropping Macro-F1 to 0.7392, 0.6730, and 0.5991, respectively. To understand this decline, we analyze agreements, disagreements, and common error patterns between the NLI and LLM verifiers. Our error analysis shows that NLI and LLM disagreements often result in correct LLM predictions being overturned.
  </details>

- **[MemShift: Separating Detection from Repair in LLM-Derived Personal Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02338)**  `arXiv:2611.02338`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zichuan Xu, Haochen Zhang, Yuyang Dong, Makoto Onizuka, Chuan Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI assistants store values that a language model derived from a person's records under the person's own definitions, such as their important contacts. Keeping such a value correct takes two steps: detection, noticing that an input changed, and repair, producing the correct new value. Benchmarks that score only the final answer cannot tell which step failed, and they state every change as new text. Seeing this limitation, we present MemShift, a benchmark of five shifts: source revision, deletion, redefinition, writer switch, and export. The first three fork a synthetic world into branches that differ only in the shift. Gold values and dependencies are fixed before any text, and a deterministic scorer replaces an LLM judge. For a memory that reports its invalidations, MemShift shows for each changed item whether the memory noticed the change and whether its repair was right. Moreover, we build a reference memory with known detection and repair to validate the measure. By evaluating four open-weight LLMs, we find that detection helps only as much as repair allows: the LLMs re-derive few values computed by the person's definitions correctly, and programs that the LLM writes over typed records close most of the gap to exact recomputation. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[From Lookups to Lessons: Turning Retrieved Knowledge into Parametric Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00450)**  `arXiv:2611.00450`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Changjiang Han, Bowei He, Ye Yuan, Weixu Zhang, Haolun Wu, Xue Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) helps large language models (LLMs) answer factual questions beyond their training data, but it adds storage and inference costs. Distilling previously retrieved information into parametric memories enables reuse without online retrieval. However, training the memory to mimic a retriever rewards accurate independent predictions, which do not necessarily improve the final output quality when combined with the LLM's existing predictions. To address this, we introduce ResMem, in which the LLM reads retrieved text offline to generate teacher targets. This design avoids storing massive token representations, unlike existing parametric memory approaches such as MLP Memory, which relies on k-nearest neighbor (kNN) datastores. Furthermore, by using the LLM as an offline teacher that reads privileged retrieved evidence alongside the input, we train a parametric memory to learn an additive logit residual. This residual aligns the predictions of a student sharing the same backbone with the teacher distribution, while self-distillation updates remain confined to the parametric memory. On a Wikipedia subset, ResMem reduces the storage retained for teacher construction by 94.7% compared with the evaluated kNN pipeline. Across five question answering datasets, ResMem raises mean token F1 from 32.83% for the frozen LLM to 34.48%. It also breaks 21% fewer of the LLM's correct answers than a kNN-trained memory. These findings validate our motivation of treating retrieval as a learning resource, with parametric memory shaped by how retrieved knowledge is used to inform the model's future predictions. Code and model weights are available atthis https URLandthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[T2D: Target-to-Draft Model Pruning & Adaptation for Efficient Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34103)**  `arXiv:2610.34103`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shahriar Kabir Nahin, Xiao Ye, Muhao Chen, Ben Zhou, Anshuman Chhabra_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding significantly accelerates autoregressive text generation in Large Language Models (LLMs), but its efficacy depends largely on two key factors: the distributional alignment between the draft and the target model, and the draft model's latency during inference. However, existing approaches typically utilize independently pretrained small draft models or seek to prune the target model to a manually chosen architecture, thereby failing to explicitly balance the trade-off between draft model alignment and token generation efficiency. To address this gap, we formulate draft model design as an optimization problem and propose T2D, a framework that constructs draft models directly from the target model using latency-aware structured pruning and knowledge distillation. In this manner, T2D obtains draft models that are jointly optimized for both measured inference latency and cross-entropy loss on the target model's responses, resulting in superlative speculative decoding performance. Through extensive experiments on multi-domain speculative decoding benchmarks and across several target model families of differing scale, we demonstrate how T2D prunes up to 92\% of the target model to obtain highly performant draft models compared to competitive baselines. Moreover, draft models obtained via T2D can also be utilized by other speculative decoding token-alignment and knowledge distillation methods in a plug-and-play fashion, leading to further gains. Our findings underscore the importance of jointly navigating the draft model architecture-alignment frontier to unlock the full potential of speculative decoding for LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Reasoning Consensus: Structural Ensembling of LLM Reasoning via Weighted DAG Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27783)**  `arXiv:2607.27783`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Amruta Parulekar, Jinu Lee, Dilek Hakkani-Tür, Hari Sundaram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) reason in free-form chain-of-thought (CoT) without revealing which steps were well supported and which alternatives were rejected. In high-stakes domains, users then cannot judge if an answer rests on sound reasoning. Majority voting and multi-agent debate improve answers using model agreement, but do not expose agreement on individual steps. Reasoning Consensus (RC) extracts a typed reasoning graph from each CoT, merges equivalent steps across graphs from diverse models, and weights every step by how many chains attest to it. RC reveals this step-wise support and the varied justifications behind each answer. On 7 benchmarks across law, science, medicine, narrative reasoning and logic, RC matches majority voting and debate in accuracy (69.4% vs. 69.0-70.3%). An LLM judge prefers RC's best-supported graph to other graphs for the same answer. In a user study, participants read RC graphs faster than debate transcripts or vote tallies, and 14 of 18 preferred them for future use.
  </details>

- **[What Transfers from Text to Vision? Capability Scaling Laws and Transfer Dynamics for VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00013)**  `arXiv:2608.00013`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Ziran Li, Qiang Wang, Zhengyu Chen, Shanglin Lei, Borun Chen, Jingang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Choosing the right large language model (LLM) backbone is the most consequential decision when building a vision-language model (VLM), yet it remains fundamentally unprincipled: compute-based scaling laws fail to generalize across model families, and no framework exists for directly predicting VLM performance before training begins. We propose the Capability-Driven Multimodal Scaling Law, the first cross-family framework that predicts VLM benchmark accuracy from directly observable textual capability. Given a low-dimensional capability score $S$ extracted from LLM textual benchmarks via PCA, we model VLM performance as a function of $S$, with a per-backbone transfer rate and an absorption rate that quantifies data-scaling efficiency. To fit and validate the framework, we train over 150 VLMs on 34 LLMs spanning 7 model families under a strictly controlled recipe. Evaluations on more than 200 textual and 50 multimodal benchmarks show that the law accurately extrapolates transfer rate from models up to 8B parameters to 72B-scale backbones, predicts full VLM training trajectories with high fidelity, and generalizes to entirely held-out model families. Beyond the scaling law, our analysis surfaces actionable insights: certain textual benchmarks negatively correlate with multimodal performance, exposing latent benchmark-gaming behavior; base LLMs outperform instruction-tuned counterparts as VLM backbones due to higher absorption rates and lower data-scaling decay; and different model families occupy distinct positions in the transfer--absorption space. The framework turns backbone selection from costly empirical sweeps into a principled, quantitative decision. Project website:this https URL; Code:this https URL; Model weights:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Tracing the Scientific Discovery Capacity of LLMs to Their Training Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03617)**  `arXiv:2611.03617`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Arda Uzunoglu, Mahler Revsine, Michael Schatz, Benjamin Van Durme, Mehrdad Farajtabar, Daniel Khashabi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) show growing promise for scientific discovery, yet basic questions remain about what enables reliable discovery. How can we trace the scientific discovery capacity of LLMs to their training data? We study this question as one of missing probability mass and develop a theoretical account in which monofacts-the facts observed once during training-set the discovery capacity. In materials science and regulatory genomics, we directly manipulate monofact rates in training sets and find that more monofacts consistently yield more discoveries across two open-weight model families, sampling strategies, and reasoning budgets. Further experiments show the relationship cannot be explained by training-set diversity alone and can transfer across scientific domains when paired with in-domain data. Together, our results identify monofact rate in training data as a key factor governing discoveries in language models.
  </details>

- **[Can LLM Agents Be Forward-Deployed Engineers? Requirement Discovery and Delivery for Enterprise Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03551)**  `arXiv:2611.03551`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Marianne Menglin Liu, Rongguang Wang, Zheng Wang, Yewen Wang, Leonid Boytsov, Tao Sheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM coding agents can increasingly automate model post-training, but whether they can act as forward-deployed engineer (FDE) agents, discovering customer requirements and turning them into deployment decisions, remains unclear. We study this on a real enterprise text-to-SQL engagement: a simulated customer, grounded in the real use case's requirements, starts with an ambiguous request. An agent must discover acceptance criteria, data permissions, and hardware constraints in the discovery phase, then plan and execute a post-training recipe in the delivery phase. Across 15 models, the strongest discover over 80% of withheld requirements and open-weight models discover 40-50%; in the delivery phase, only 25% of runs across eight coding-agent configurations pass all acceptance criteria. We identify four bottlenecks in this engagement: (1) incomplete discovery, with 40% of withheld requirements missing or misrepresented; (2) unreliable execution; (3) underused compute budgets and a lack of systematic comparisons across base models and post-training methods; and (4) benchmark accuracy does not guarantee production readiness. We suggest human review of discovered requirements, monitoring of the agent's plan during execution, and an explicit experiment-environment specification covering hardware, budget scope, and production checks.
  </details>

- **[Unlocking Criminal Court Decisions: Flexible Information Extraction for Empirical Legal Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03051)**  `arXiv:2611.03051`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tomáš Knap, Ivana Kvapilíková, Jan Černý, Klára Bendová, Jaromír Šavelka, Vojtěch Pour, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Criminal verdicts contain descriptions of criminal behavior that can support quantitative legal research, but structuring them requires attributes and extraction rules that cannot always be fully specified in advance. We present an LLM-based pipeline that builds a structured dataset through attribute exploration, text span extraction, and value normalization, guided by annotation instructions that legal experts can refine. We evaluate it on two traffic offenses: driving under the influence (DUI) and driving despite a ban (DDB). For DUI, revising the instructions was associated with higher extraction accuracy (from 52% to 70% of correctly extracted values) and higher agreement between human annotators. Repeated runs of the same model agreed with each other more than with the gold annotations, showing that consistent output is not necessarily correct, while disagreement between annotators and between models helped reveal unclear instructions. For DDB, where the instructions defined fixed categories and output formats, agreement was high for most attributes.
  </details>

- **[Tug of Watts: A study on the Energy - Accuracy Trade-off of Large Language Models on Medical Question Answering Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02889)**  `arXiv:2611.02889`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Viju Sudhi, Giulio Billi, Andreea Scrob, Regina Stodden, Philipp Cimiano_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in medical question answering (QA) systems. While recent works aim at incrementally improving the downstream task accuracy, an empirical estimation of the models' energy consumption and the associated carbon footprints is often overlooked. In this study, we demonstrate how practitioners can estimate energy consumption and arrive at reliable emission estimates for medical QA tasks available in the OpenMedical-LLM leaderboard by investigating 13 LLMs from 3 model families ranging from 270M to 14B parameters. We discuss how one can critically interpret energy versus accuracy trade-offs for a set of models for a given task. With this work, we underline that reporting disjoint energy and accuracy metrics alone does not suffice for careful trade-off assessments, but it also demands systematic construction and interpretation of joint trade-off metrics. We envisage such studies aiding practitioners in adopting better and more efficient models for their task and helping them become active participants in building sustainable LLM pipelines.
  </details>

- **[They Know Where It Is, But Not What It Says: A Bilingual Hallucination Benchmark of Hadith Recall in Open LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02587)**  `arXiv:2611.02587`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Dayam Nadeem, Safdar Mustafa, Ameen Ur Rehman, Shahab Saquib Sohail, Mohammad Nadeem, Usman Naseem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly consulted for religious knowledge, including the recall of specific Hadith (recorded sayings and actions of the Prophet Muhammad, peace be upon him). Unlike open-ended generation, Hadith recall is a high-stakes, verifiable task: a reference (collection, chapter, and Hadith number) has exactly one correct text, and any deviation from it constitutes a fabrication that can be mistaken for authentic scripture. This study introduces a bilingual (English/Arabic) benchmark that probes five open-weight, locally-deployable language models (Mistral-7B, Gemma3-4B, Jais-7B, ALLaM-7B, and Phi4-mini-3.8B) across all six canonical Sunni Hadith collections (Sahih al-Bukhari, Sahih Muslim, Sunan Abi Da'ud, Jami' al-Tirmidhi, Sunan an-Nasa'i, Sunan Ibn Majah), sampling 500 Hadith per collection (3,000 per model per language, 30,000 responses total). Each model is given only the bibliographic reference and asked to reproduce the exact Hadith text, with no retrieval augmentation, and is scored on lexical fidelity (BLEU) and semantic fidelity (multilingual cosine similarity), with refusals flagged separately. Across all five models and both languages, 0% of 30,000 responses achieve both high lexical and high semantic fidelity, i.e., an exact, verifiable match to the source text. Arabic responses fare uniformly worse than English. These findings suggest that current open-weight LLMs, used without retrieval grounding, are unreliable and potentially dangerous sources for exact religious-text recall, and that deployment for Islamic scholarship should mandate retrieval-augmented generation over a verified corpus rather than parametric memory. The complete code, prompts, evaluation pipeline, and scored outputs are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Solver-Like Behavior in Large Language Models as Formalizers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02395)**  `arXiv:2611.02395`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Rikhil Amonkar, Stuti Mohan, Kevin Nguyen, Ceyhun Efe Kayan, Ronan Le Bras, Li Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In the LLM-as-formalizer paradigm, a large language model translates a problem into a formal program and leaves the search to a solver, gaining the solver's guarantees. Formalizers are now often reasoning models that write a long reasoning trace before the program, yet they are judged only by the program's output. We find that the trace routinely does the solver's job, assigning, testing and ruling out values until it often reaches the full answer; we call this solver-like behavior (SLB). We define an ontology of SLB over the trace, the program and its output, and use LLM judges, checked against human labels, to label 2,000 generations from three reasoning models on four constraint satisfaction datasets, each formalized in a declarative and an imperative formal language. SLB appears in 85% of traces and takes 24% of reasoning tokens, yet the program usually recomputes the answer, so the effort is redundant. The 9% of programs that inherit the trace's answer also inherit its errors, and pass output-based evaluation whenever that answer is correct. The amount of SLB is not associated with accuracy, and SLB is more common in imperative Python than in declarative Z3 or PDDL. Cautionary prompts reduce it by only a quarter, but hiding the problem's values behind placeholders, which the program receives only at run time, removes 97% of it without loss of accuracy. Formalizers should therefore be evaluated by how their programs are reached, not only by what they output.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Accuracy: How Quantization Affects Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02368)**  `arXiv:2611.02368`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Areej Mehboob, Muhammad Ali Shafique, Muhammad Usman Qadeer, Hamza Farooq_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agents are increasingly used for coding tasks, but the inference cost of large language models (LLMs) limits where they can run. Quantization reduces model size, but its effects on coding agents that execute and revise programs remain underexplored. We compare BF16 with 4-bit AWQ and GPTQ for Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct (7B, 14B, and 32B) and Devstral-Small-2507 (24B) on OJBench coding problems across three seeds. We detect no statistically significant accuracy loss for any of the coding agents. However, final accuracy can conceal substantial behavioral changes: AWQ reduces Qwen-7B one-shot solves from 17 to 0 while increasing loop-based solves from 10 to 25. All Qwen-32B successes involve tool use, whereas Devstral rarely makes a valid executor call. Failure profiles also differ: Qwen-32B produces far more compilation errors than the smaller Qwen models, rising slightly under quantization, while Devstral's failures shift significantly from runtime errors toward wrong answers and timeouts. These findings indicate that quantization robustness in coding agents should be assessed through solution trajectories, tool use, and failure modes, in addition to final accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Consolidating Reasoning with Test-Time Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02104)**  `arXiv:2611.02104`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Wu, Zeming Chen, Hao Zhao, Antoine Bosselut_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Effective multi-agent collaboration requires finalizing solutions from reasoning that is distributed across the individual attempts of many agents. However, useful intermediate reasoning can remain unexploited when collaboration is limited to voting on conclusions reached among individual agents, or aggregating long, noisy collections of reasoning trajectories in context. In this work, we propose TTT-Consolidate, a framework that uses multi-agent reasoning trajectories as adaptation data to improve a model's subsequent reasoning. TTT-Consolidate jointly consolidates trajectories into parametric representations through test-time training, then generates a new solution by conditioning on the consolidated information. We meta-learn how to consolidate using bi-level optimization at training time, where an inner loop encodes reasoning trajectories into a lightweight LoRA adapter, and an outer loop learns meta-parameters for the inner loop such that the consolidation is helpful for the model to synthesize a final solution.As no large-scale corpus of diverse reasoning trajectories exists for training aggregation methods, we construct the OpenParallelThinking corpus composed of 12,810 math problems paired with a mixture of trajectories from six LLMs. Evaluated on challenging math and STEM benchmarks, TTT-Consolidate outperforms the best aggregation baselines by up to 20 percentage points, even under matched compute and runtime settings, with the gap widening at larger model scales. TTT-Consolidate also recovers from unsuccessful individual trajectories more robustly, an ability that improves as the number of available (even incorrect) trajectories increases. Finally, we demonstrate that TTT-Consolidate benefits from both SFT and RL-based outer loop objectives. We will release TTT-Consolidate and OpenParallelThinking for use in future work on reasoning aggregation.
  </details>

- **[TrackMem: Tracking Evolving Beliefs in Long-Horizon Financial Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02001)**  `arXiv:2611.02001`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Austin M.Y. Cheung, Harsh Vardhan Gupta, Go Nam Lui, Lok Kan Chan, Ginny Y. Wong, Simon See_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems for LLM agents are evaluated mostly on social dialogue, where facts accumulate and rarely change, so remembering reduces largely to finding the turn in which something was said. Financial dialogue breaks this assumption: figures are revised, stances shift without being restated, rules stated once govern later questions, and a single company is discussed under several analytical lenses. Memory systems that retrieve the passages most similar to a question, or the most recent ones, therefore return superseded values, miss rules that share no wording with the question, and cannot recover a stance spread across sessions. We present TrackMem, a per-speaker graph memory that tracks the history of figures, separates perspectives by entity and lens, and preserves each speaker's actions in chronological order. To evaluate it, we introduce FinLoCoMo, a benchmark of 15 long-horizon investment conversations with 765 questions in two splits: one following the LoCoMo categories and one targeting four failure modes. With two readers, TrackMem outperforms existing memory systems on both splits. On the failure-mode split it reaches 73.9% macro accuracy against 69.4% for the strongest baseline, with the largest gains on questions about revised figures (86.5% against 73.4%).
  </details>

- **[LegoEnv: Self-Evolving Agentic Environments via Modular Recombination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01834)**  `arXiv:2611.01834`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuosong Jiang, Chengxu Wu, Junqi Liu, Yujie Yi, Siqin Wang, Zhixuan Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents learn by interacting with environments, yet existing environments are designed for specific tasks and fail to generalize to new ones. Recent methods automate environment construction with LLMs. However, they generate each environment from scratch, neither reusing parts shared across environments nor carrying a fix from one environment to the rest. To address this, we propose a new paradigm of evolving environment atoms, which composes environments from reusable atoms (intent, rule, data and server) and evolves the atoms when an environment fails to give reliable feedback (e.g., an application lacks a tool the task requires). To instantiate this paradigm, we introduce LegoEnv, which builds 5,465 verified environments over 223 applications, and one round of evolution raises the share of solvable environments from 71.6% to 88.3%. On AutomationBench, SFT on these environments lifts Qwen3.6-27B from 16.0% to 32.0%, and RL with their verifiers lifts Qwen3.8-27B from 37.6% to 40.3%. Further analyses show steady gains with corpus size, transfer to unseen applications, and failures concentrated in a few shared root causes.
  </details>

- **[MemRole: From Memories to Decisions for Long-Horizon Reasoning in Role-Playing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01778)**  `arXiv:2611.01778`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Mengting Li, Xilong Cheng, Zhihao Xiao, Zhenhua Xu, Jingyuan Zhou, Linfeng Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based role-playing agents are fluent at imitating a character's style, yet struggle to make role-consistent decisions over long narrative horizons. Such decisions are shaped by experiences spanning many prior scenes, which are often partial and may point in different directions. Existing methods inject cross-scene memories as context, but do not reason about how past events shape a character's current motivations and behavior. We propose MemRole, a framework that makes this memory-to-decision process explicit. It introduces Long-Horizon Memory Thinking (LHMT), a reasoning schema that decomposes hidden reasoning into context understanding, memory activation, and response strategy planning, trained on a novel-derived dataset whose character-grounded, timeline-constrained memory pools limit each character to what it could plausibly know at the current narrative point. Beyond supervised fine-tuning, we propose Self-Refining On-Policy Self-Distillation (SR-OPSD): the model critiques its own rollouts against the reference under a data-driven rubric, and a critique-conditioned teacher view of the same model distills token-level guidance back into the policy. MemRole-32B improves over its backbone by 15.2 points on memory-grounded role-playing, performs on par with frontier LLMs such as GPT-5.4, and achieves the best character-motivation recognition accuracy on CroSS-MR.
  </details>

- **[Revisiting LLM Homogeneity Through a Multidimensional Lens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01549)**  `arXiv:2611.01549`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziliang Qiu, Renfen Hu, Yi Ding, Ted Underwood_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Concerns about LLM homogeneity are typically quantified with embedding similarity, which reduces two texts to a single score. We revisit homogeneity with 25 interpretable metrics spanning five dimensions: semantic, lexical, syntactic, structural, and stylistic & presentational. A human study shows that perceived similarity depends on both content (what responses say) and form (how they say it), yet embedding similarity correlates less with human meaning judgments than sentence-level matching ($\rho=0.417$ vs. $0.606$) and not significantly with form judgments ($\rho=0.140$). Across 18 contemporary LLMs, models say similar things in different words: they share global semantics and broad linguistic profiles but remain distinct in lexical characteristics, structural patterns, and interactional conventions. Across releases from 2024 to 2026, models become more like themselves but not more like one another: among significantly changing metrics, intra-model distances decrease for 17 of 22, and inter-model distances increase for 14 of 21. Across two post-training pipelines, model-specific conventions emerge during SFT and are selectively reshaped by preference optimization. These results suggest that homogeneity is multidimensional and evolving, and that embedding similarity alone can overstate cross-model homogeneity while concealing diverging intra- and inter-model trends.
  </details>

- **[Step-dLLM: Adaptive Step-aware Sparse Attention for Efficient Diffusion LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01320)**  `arXiv:2611.01320`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhichen Zeng, Xichong Zhang, Junpan Wu, Yifei Zuo, Chi-Chih Chang, Jiayi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) generate tokens in parallel via iterative denoising, but their bidirectional attention prevents the KV caching used by autoregressive models and recomputes the full attention map at every step, incurring quadratic per-step cost. Existing sparse attention methods for dLLMs construct a sparse mask after an initial full-attention warm-up and reuse it for the remaining steps, while allocating the sparsity budget uniformly across heads. We argue both choices ignore key structural properties of attention in dLLMs: empirically, attention patterns are only locally consistent across steps and shift abruptly at certain transitions, and different heads concentrate their attention mass to very different extents. To this end, we design Step-dLLM, which periodically refreshes a block-sparse mask at anchor steps along the denoising trajectory and reuses it within each locally consistent interval, while employing a head-adaptive global budget that more aggressively compresses heads with concentrated attention. Backed by a fused IO-aware Triton kernel for block-sparse attention, Step-dLLM applies to pre-trained LLaDA-1.5 and Dream-7B-Instruct without retraining, achieving a 2.78x end-to-end speedup over dense FlashAttention on LLaDA-1.5 at 8K context with 256 denoising steps and 90% sparsity, while improving the accuracy-latency trade-off over prior sparse-attention methods.
  </details>

- **[CuriousTogether: Implications of Curiosity and Cultural Grounding in Multi-Agent LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01301)**  `arXiv:2611.01301`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Angana Borah, Zoha Hayat Bhatti, Zhijing Jin, Rada Mihalcea_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in multi-agent settings, exhibiting emergent collective behaviors, yet the role of curiosity as a driver of such dynamics remains understudied. We introduce CuriousTogether, a framework evaluating curiosity-driven multi-agent LLM interactions across three complementary dimensions: novelty-seeking, epistemic and social curiosity. Across eight tasks spanning reasoning, creative generation, and social bias mitigation, curiosity-guided multi-agent settings yield improvements over non-curious ones on seven out of eight tasks, with gains of up to 20.6% in story generation (semantic diversity) and a 14.9 pp reduction in stereotype rates. Furthermore, country-conditioned cultural grounding show benefits for story generation and social tasks, but can hurt precision-oriented reasoning. Finally, we show that inference-time multi-agent curiosity-based approach offers favorable quality-token trade-offs, compared with single-agent baselines. Together, these findings support curiosity-guided interaction as a practical mechanism for improving multi-agent collaboration, with downstream benefits for reasoning, creative, and societal tasks.
  </details>

- **[Character-Name Concentration in LLM Fiction: Measuring Displacement and Mitigating Repetition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01284)**  `arXiv:2611.01284`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Alejandro Mozo Quesada, Sergio Gálvez Rojas, Francisco López Valverde_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Character names in large language model (LLM) fiction concentrate on a handful of recurrent names such as 'Elara' and 'Elias', a measurable face of AI slop. Existing fixes either require logit access or retraining, or operate per request and cannot control concentration across a corpus. How much prompt-level strategies reduce concentration, and at what cost to name naturalness, has not been systematically measured. We present an empirical study of name-concentration control across eight strategies and introduce speculative naming, a fine-tuning-free method that adapts the draft-verify-reject paradigm of speculative decoding from tokens to names. The method requests candidate name-sets through verbalized sampling, scores them against a SlopTracker that combines a per-model fingerprint with a runtime counter, and selects through a temperature-controlled rule or its strict variant (HARD). We use 20 LLMs to generate 44,782 stories in four languages from text and visual stimuli (88,919 named characters), and measure concentration with a list-free collision metric (Slop Ratio, SR) decomposed into known and emergent components. List-based strategies do not remove slop: static banlists displace concentration to emergent names and more than halve the share of attested real names. Speculative naming removes it: the sampling scaffold alone cuts SR by 89% on average, the dynamic tracker brings the total reduction to 95%, mean collision probability falls from 7.3% to 0.4%, and known-name collisions hold at zero while naturalness stays close to baseline (42-69% attested by language) and within-story collisions drop from 4.3% to 0.2%. The strategy ranking is stable across languages, model families, and name-counting conventions. Name concentration is thus controllable at the corpus level without logit access or retraining; broader creative diversity beyond naming remains open.
  </details>

- **[A Token Is Not a Unit of Difficulty: Segmentation Controls Where Adaptive Computation Goes Inside a Word](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00983)**  `arXiv:2611.00983`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Dipto Sumit, Mahima Mahzabin Sandria, Farig Sadeque, Md. Golam Rabiul Alam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early-exit language models decide for each token how much computation to spend, usually by stopping once the prediction is confident. Yet a token is not a fixed unit: tokenizers cut words into different numbers of pieces in different languages. We ask where inside a word such a policy spends its budget. In Qwen3, along two adaptive axes (the timesteps of a spiking conversion and the layers of the original Transformer), confidence stopping gives the continuation pieces of a word less computation than its first piece in English, German, and Turkish, but more in Bangla. Segmentation drives this. Re-segmenting the same parallel passages with BPE-dropout shifts the gap by up to eight exits on both axes, small models trained under two tokenizers shift Bangla's gap by six exits, and BLOOM and mGPT, which split Bangla less finely, give it the English pattern. Characters per token do not predict the gap across languages; within-word surprisal explains 75% of its variance. A learned stopping head beats confidence stopping by 7 to 8 points, but gains less than one point over the best mix of fixed exits at equal cost, and sometimes loses. All results come from offline analysis, not measured speedups.
  </details>

- **[Labadain-MedQA: A Tetun Medical QA Dataset for Fine-Tuning and Evaluating Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00935)**  `arXiv:2611.00935`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Gabriel de Jesus, Sérgio Nunes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Advances in large language models (LLMs) have created opportunities for cross-lingual transfer of medical knowledge from high-resource to low-resource languages to support access to healthcare information. However, medical question-answering (QA) datasets for fine-tuning and evaluating LLMs remain scarce for Tetun. To address this gap, we translated MedAlpaca's Medical Flashcards and WikiDoc Patient Information collections into Tetun using Gemini 2.5 Flash, producing 39,077 medical QA pairs. Translation quality was assessed on 50 randomly sampled QA pairs (100 documents) using automatic metrics against human reference translations and evaluations by two native Tetun-speaking healthcare professionals, focusing on medical accuracy and Tetun fluency. Human translations achieved higher mean ratings than LLM-generated translations for both criteria (4.43/5 vs. 4.02/5), with statistically significant differences (Wilcoxon signed-rank test, p < 0.001). To assess downstream utility, we fine-tuned Gemma and Llama variants on the translated MedAlpaca dataset using parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) and evaluated them on 325 Tetun-translated United States Medical Licensing Examination multiple-choice questions. Gemma 4 12B fine-tuned using QLoRA achieved relative improvements in mean accuracy of 10.79% with 4-bit NormalFloat (NF4) inference and 10.42% after merging the LoRA adapters into the BF16 base model, compared with the corresponding base models. These results highlight the viability of automatic dataset translation for Tetun medical QA and the effectiveness of PEFT in enhancing model performance. The datasets, models, and evaluation resources are accessible atthis https URLto support reproducible research on Tetun medical QA and facilitate machine translation, cross lingual, and multilingual research.
  </details>

- **[ShiftRAG: Bypassing the Textual Bottleneck via Decoupled Learning and Continuous Soft Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00871)**  `arXiv:2611.00871`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Youmin Ko, Jihong Jeong, Hyunjoon Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite advances in retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), suppressing hallucinations during complex reasoning remains a persistent challenge. We identify that existing RAG pipelines suffer from a textual bottleneck: by passing only raw text to the generator, they discard discriminative signals of the retriever. Furthermore, recent attempts to unify retrieval and generation within a single LLM induce severe objective conflict and exacerbate the rank collapse of decoder-only architectures, degrading performance on both tasks. To address these limitations, we introduce ShiftRAG, a decoupled RAG framework that employs a single LLM backbone but isolates discriminative retrieval learning from autoregressive generation. To bridge these decoupled modes, we project retrieval alignments into continuous soft tokens, establishing a high-bandwidth interface that infuses the generator with retrieval-side signals. Concurrently, our soft orthogonality objective mitigates rank collapse, while layer-wise relevance dynamics preserve the fine-grained latent space required for highly discriminative evidence separation. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that ShiftRAG significantly improves retrieval and question answering performance over state-of-the-art baselines. ShiftRAG preserves foundational capabilities of the backbone while maintaining a favorable efficiency-performance trade-off on general text encoding and generative tasks.
  </details>

- **[Precedent Labels and Facts Shift LLM Approval Decisions in Opposite Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00672)**  `arXiv:2611.00672`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junwei Yao, Zike Yang, Yongxi Zhou, Yanxian Chen, Jianan Liu, Jiaxi Wen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a language model decides refund or claim requests under written policies, the system can show it similar past cases. Each past case has two parts: its facts and the decision recorded for it. We separate the two. A model sees a policy, a request and three past cases, and we change either the recorded decisions or the facts, keeping the request fixed. We select requests that are close calls: deciding without past cases, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash approved each in some of four tries and denied it in others. When the past cases are near-copies of the request (its facts with a few details changed), switching their recorded decisions from DENY to APPROVE raises approval by about 30 to 55 percentage points in DeepSeek V4.1 Flash, GPT-5.6 Luna and Claude Haiku 5.5 on policies written for the study, and DeepSeek and Luna show the same on nine public policies paraphrased from real ones. The facts push the other way: shown without their recorded decisions, past cases whose facts support approval make these two models approve less often than past cases whose facts support denial. One reading is that the request looks weaker next to them. This push is not just a correction toward the right answer: in an additional DeepSeek analysis, it was of similar estimated size on requests that human annotators judged approvable and on those they judged deniable. In a separate DeepSeek run on the public policies, when the past cases are near-copies of a different request, switching their decisions moves approval by 9 points instead of 43, while facts that support approval still lower it by 22 points against facts that support denial. Systems that retrieve past decisions should test the two parts separately.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Estimate Elicitation Does Not Uniformly Debias Language Model Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00597)**  `arXiv:2611.00597`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _An-Sheng Jhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does eliciting five weighted estimates within a single response change how a language model responds to information? Across anchoring, hindsight, base-rate, conjunction, and status-quo/default tasks, we compare direct numerical answers with a multi-estimate response format, contrasting source framings, explicitly random cues, and probability-consistency constraints. We evaluate three completed configurations--gpt-6.1-sol, claude-sonnet-5.5, and Qwen3-8B (reasoning disabled, analyzed descriptively). In the first two configurations, direct elicitation yielded near-zero irrelevant-anchor indices and status-quo effects, while remaining responsive to informative sources and informed advice. Of 23 preregistered confirmatory tests, six rejected after Holm adjustment; however, all six absolute point estimates fell below their prespecified smallest effect sizes of interest without establishing equivalence. Moreover, while separate versus joint elicitation shifted conjunction judgments, interpretation is constrained by between-call noise. Ultimately, multi-estimate elicitation failed to produce uniform debiasing or response attenuation across tasks and models, though post hoc correlations between candidate spread and anchoring error motivate independent predictive validation. Materials:this https URL
  </details>

- **[AStockArena: Beyond Returns, Can We Entrust Real Capital to the Decisions of Financial Agents?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00515)**  `arXiv:2611.00515`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zeyu Liu, Sing Kwong Lai, Xuanzhi Feng, HaoYang Shang, Yang Li, Zihao Fang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in dynamic financial trading, yet real-time trading benchmarks mainly evaluate agents by cumulative returns and risk-adjusted performance. Such outcome metrics cannot show whether trades are faithfully reported, supported by available evidence, and consistent with account states. We introduce AStockArena, a real-time decision-auditing benchmark for continuous trading in China's A-share market. Under shared market conditions and execution constraints, we evaluate 13 open- and closed-source models over 3,198 sequential decisions. AStockArena reconstructsthe actions actually posted to accounts by aligning reports, trade requests, execution responses, and account states, and audits them with eight checks spanning execution traceability (O), evidence sufficiency (RK), and financial meta-awareness (B), combining record verification with consensus expert annotation. Of 1,070 trading points, 72.99% exhibit at least one audit gap; model-level rates range from52.2% to 91.2%, with evidence gaps most prevalent. Historical trade-filtering replay further shows thatportfolio outcomes are sensitive to these flagged trades. Decision-process auditing thus offers a verifiable complement to return-based evaluation of financial agents.
  </details>

- **[Spotting the Artificial: Dialogic Humor Improvisation in Humans and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00492)**  `arXiv:2611.00492`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Lizeth Palacios Patiño, Ximena Gutierrez-Vasques, Daniel Rodriguez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a methodology for investigating the differences between natural and synthetic language in a challenging setting: humorous improvisational dialogue. Our experimental setting compares human-produced comedy improvisations with the dialogues generated by LLMs in a multi-agent environment that we designed. The analysis combines automated linguistic measures with manual annotation grounded in Systemic Functional Linguistics (SFL).The results show that, although LLMs can create coherent and creative scenes, they often fail to reproduce key characteristics of spontaneous spoken interaction. In addition, LLM-generated interactions display a strong tendency toward politeness and positive evaluations, contrasting with the broader range of attitudes found in human humorous improvisation. Our evidence highlights both the strengths and limitations of current LLMs in modeling dialogic humor and demonstrate the value of integrating linguistic theory with NLP methods for the analysis of machine-generated language
  </details>

- **[Fidelity Collapses with Table Size, Not Turn Count: Delivery Architecture in a Deployed Tool-Using Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00442)**  `arXiv:2611.00442`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tung Thanh Hoang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a multi-turn agent delivers a tool's structured records into the model's context and re-emits them each turn, every turn can corrupt data the tool computed correctly. We measure this in a production award-travel agent: two architectures over identical frozen payloads, lineage-matched prompts and sampling, differing only in delivery architecture. SYNTHESIS feeds full record sets into the planner's context and answers follow-ups in-head; BYPASS, the deployed system, keeps records in session state, translates follow-ups into arguments for a deterministic filter, and delivers rows over a typed side channel. Across 24 dialogues (339 scored turns per condition), BYPASS scores 92.3% of turns fully correct, SYNTHESIS 45.7% (paired gap +46.6pp [+35.7, +57.1]). Varying delivered-table size by capture date, SYNTHESIS collapses from near-perfect at 10 rows or fewer to 8% or less beyond about 74 rows. On a content-controlled prefix ladder the collapse threshold orders by model capability, from under 10 rows (GPT-4o-mini) to about 198 or more (Gemini 2.5 Pro), while BYPASS stays size-flat for all four models. The collapse is expensive: beyond 100 rows, where almost no SYNTHESIS answer is correct, each correct one costs roughly 200x BYPASS's flat \$0.012. Practitioners converged on typed side-channel delivery for rendering, not correctness; we show it was, unknowingly, also a correctness decision.
  </details>

- **[Subquadratic Attention with a Hierarchical Tree-Structured Indexer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.42112)**  `arXiv:2610.42112`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Leon Lufkin, Cutter Dawes, Alexander Speicher, Kamesh Krishnamurthy, Tomás Figliolia, Beren Millidge_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The quadratic scaling of attention is a fundamental bottleneck limiting the long-context capabilities of large language models (LLMs). A variety of approaches have attempted to circumvent this, but they either fail to match attention's expressiveness (e.g., sliding-window or linear attention variants with fixed-size state) or only achieve constant-factor speedups (e.g., sparse attention methods whose indexers themselves scale quadratically). In this paper, we derive an asymptotically subquadratic sparse attention method based on a hierarchical, tree-structured indexer, which we call Zyphra Hierarchical Attention (ZHA). ZHA constructs a tree of recursively compressed key summaries over the KV cache, which future queries traverse from the top down to retrieve a sparse subset of keys. Crucially, by iteratively growing the tree's depth, the total cost including routing and final attention grows sublinearly in sequence length, resulting in overall subquadratic (but superlinear) scaling for attention. We demonstrate that ZHA can be cheaply applied to existing pretrained models with only a short KL distillation training stage of a billion tokens, and maintains performance on long-context retrieval and challenging reasoning, mathematical, and agentic coding evaluation tasks. Moreover, we show that our method is highly robust to tree geometry including very deep trees of up to 6 levels. Finally, we develop a detailed performance model and show that our approach scales subquadratically, dramatically outperforms competing methods beyond 1 million tokens of context, and ultimately has the potential to extend effective context windows to the hundreds of millions or billions of tokens.
  </details>

- **[Rank-Aware Differentially Private Release of Listwise Preferences for LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37682)**  `arXiv:2610.37682`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Junwei Chen, Manjiang Yu, Pengpeng Qiao, Yang Cao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Listwise preference feedback offers richer supervision for large language model (LLMs) alignment than pairwise comparisons, but a full ranking also reveals more sensitive user preferences. Existing privacy-preserving alignment methods focus mainly on pairwise feedback, while listwise alignment under local differential privacy (LDP) remains unexplored. Extending LDP to this listwise domain raises a critical challenge: the high sensitivity of the full ranking space demands excessive noise, neutralizing the benefits of listwise supervision. To address this challenge, we analyze the interaction between supervision granularity and privacy noise in downstream training, and introduce a rank-aware exponential mechanism that privatizes listwise preference data into a low-sensitivity granularity suitable for downstream alignment. The mechanism leverages ranking information to sample a fixed-size binary partition, concentrating the noise near borderline items instead of perturbing all items uniformly as randomized response (RR) does. Empirical evaluations on downstream LLM alignment tasks show that our mechanism consistently outperforms existing LDP baselines at matched privacy budgets. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Language Models Learn to Improve Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23344)**  `arXiv:2610.23344`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Minhua Lin, Zijun Wang, Zewen Liu, Zhaotian Weng, Bing He, Zhan Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to automate AI research, including language-model (LM) development, offering a potential path toward recursive self-improvement. However, turning experimental feedback into a learned ability to propose effective improvements across models faces two challenges. First, training such an ability with real feedback is expensive: reinforcement learning (RL) requires evaluating tens of thousands of proposals, each potentially requiring a full model-training run. Even at ten GPU-hours per run, reward collection alone would consume hundreds of thousands of GPU-hours, motivating a cheaper surrogate. Second, surrogate feedback can become unreliable as policy optimization favors overestimated proposals when the proposal distribution shifts. To address these challenges, we propose LM2LM, which trains LLMs with RL to generate effective LM improvement proposals. To provide scalable feedback, we use a research world model (RWM), a frozen LLM that predicts proposal gains from real experimental records provided in context, as the reward model for RL. To maintain feedback reliability, we periodically execute current-policy proposals ranked highly by the RWM and add their measured outcomes to its context. Across eight autoregressive and diffusion LM development environments, real-execution evaluations on held-out tasks show that LM2LM enables Qwen3.8-27B to outperform GPT-6-sol, even when GPT-6-sol is given 300 real experimental records in context, while requiring 33% lower data-generation cost. LM2LM also generalizes to a model family unseen during training.
  </details>

- **[A Proposed Rubric for Evaluating Expressed Clinical Reasoning in Large Language Model Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37788)**  `arXiv:2609.37788`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhangshu Joshua Jiang, Zina Ibrahim, James T. Teo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose a rubric for assessing expressed clinical reasoning in model responses, drawing on three bodies of work: medical education assessment frameworks (ART, SCT, Key Feature Problems and OSCE); clinical LLM benchmarks (MedR-Bench, HealthBench, TIMER-Bench, DR. BENCH, PrIME-LLM and PatientSafeBench); and general LLM reasoning evaluation research, including the Factuality-Validity-Coherence-Utility taxonomy, FaithCoT-Bench and C2-Faith. We use groundedness as a clinically oriented adaptation of the taxonomy's factuality category. The rubric brings these concepts together in a multidimensional framework for scoring free-text responses to gold-standard clinical vignettes. It includes provisional behavioural anchors, applicability rules and a separate flag for case-specific safety-critical errors. General-domain frameworks inform its design but are not treated as validated clinical instruments. The rubric does not replace case-specific reference criteria or the task-specific metrics of existing benchmarks. It has not yet been tested for inter-rater reliability, construct validity or clinical utility. Its immediate purpose is to make evaluation decisions explicit and open to scrutiny before empirical testing.
  </details>

- **[AgentHop: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Agentic Multi-Hop Question Answering over Scientific Citation Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34428)**  `arXiv:2609.34428`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Chanhee Park, Jeongho Yoon, Sungbin Han, Hyeonseok Moon, Heuiseok Lim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic tasks require a large language model to interact with the world, navigating information and gathering evidence across multiple steps with restricted resources. Due to this complexity, agentic task failures arise from various sources, and pinpointing these failure causes is essential to diagnose and improve agentic systems. Existing benchmarks, however, tend to focus on a single leaderboard score, leaving the underlying failure modes opaque. To fill this gap, we introduce AgentHop, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,011 multiple-choice questions paired with a controlled seven-tool sandbox under fixed token, turn, and tool-call constraints. AgentHop reveals model vulnerabilities by dissecting a single accuracy score along four axes of agent operation: retrieval, synthesis, tool-call, and resource management. Across 19 models, we find that behavior clusters by model family, with tool-call signatures revealing distinct family fingerprints: GPT models commit early, Anthropic and GLM checkpoints verify before committing, DeepSeek and Kimi over-search, and Gemini-3 Pro stays balanced. Decomposed axes further expose within-family structure: Claude Opus 4.6 and Sonnet 4.6 land within one accuracy point yet diverge on retrieval-versus-synthesis emphasis, with Opus retrieving more and Sonnet synthesizing better. We release the full benchmark set and the harness to support diagnostic agent benchmarking.
  </details>

- **[Sledgehammer or Scalpel? A Fine-grained Adaptive Framework for Implicit Hate Speech Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27462)**  `arXiv:2608.27462`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Han Wang, Hanqing Tao, Xuesong Wang, Yi Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unlike explicit attacks with obvious profanity, implicit hate speech hides malice within seemingly compliant expressions through metaphors and contextual hints, making its detection in online content review challenging. Many PLM- or LLM-based methods apply the same processing pipeline across samples, which may overlook differences in information requirements and introduce unnecessary computation. We observe that online hate speech is not monolithic but manifests in varied forms. We therefore define three fine-grained categories: Shallow, Targeted, and Context-Dependent. Accordingly, we propose Fine-grained Adaptive Implicit Hate speech Detection (FAID), a novel framework that first performs fine-grained classification and then adapts to specific categories. Specifically, for Shallow samples with surface-identifiable intents, the framework adopts lightweight Prompt-Tuning for rapid classification; for Targeted comments that bind malicious intent to concealed targets, we design knowledge augmentation to iteratively refine the model and reveal hidden targets; for Context-Dependent comments lacking background information, we utilize an agentic framework that automatically generates prompts to evolve context, infer missing background information and identify ambiguous malicious intents. This adaptive architecture focuses computational resources on complex implicit samples while avoiding redundant reasoning for shallow samples. Experiments on four benchmark datasets demonstrate that FAID significantly outperforms SOTA baselines.
  </details>

- **[When Answer Evaluation Misleads Confidence Selection: Fact-Grounded Form Grading for Open-Ended QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25654)**  `arXiv:2608.25654`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhexi Feng, Bingrui Zhang, Wuxi Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many benchmarks now prefer open-ended questions for testing LLM and NLP methods. We find that the choice of a calibration rule can flip when only the correctness labels change: on 141 pilot answers, rules chosen with exact-match labels look 0.239 Brier points better than raw confidence but are 0.103 worse under human labels. How such flips arise remains unclear. We look deeply into their mechanism: reference matching rejects many correct answers (exact match misses 42%), and an exact Brier identity shows these errors favour rules giving such answers lower confidence, which abstain on them at common thresholds. Guided by this, we propose FactForm, a grader that trusts reference matches and otherwise consults evidence retrieved from the question alone. One LLM call per answer fills a fixed-order closed form that a program scores. On 998 fresh EVOUNA answers with 400 fact-based human labels, FactForm reaches a balanced error of 0.077 and an AUROC of 0.985, against 0.112 and 0.930 for the best of eight traditional graders. Its same-model stability is no worse than that of LLM judges (mean absolute run-to-run difference 0.0136 against 0.0159 to 0.0206). We hope this helps benchmarks grade open-ended answers more accurately, with repeatable and discriminative scores.
  </details>

- **[A Systematic Analysis of Linguistic Features in AI-Generated English Text Detection Across Domains and Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04177)**  `arXiv:2606.04177`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yassir El Attar, Esra Dönmez, Maximilian Maurer, Agnieszka Falenska_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpretable linguistic features offer a promising approach for explaining why a given text appears machine-generated, particularly for non-expert users. However, existing findings on which features reliably indicate LLMgenerated text remain fragmented across feature sets, models, and text domains. To address this gap, we conduct a large-scale empirical study assessing the robustness of linguistic signals for characterizing AI-generated text. Our analysis covers 284 interpretable linguistic features across outputs from 30 LLMs and 10 text domains under cross-model and cross-domain generalization settings. We show that classifiers based solely on linguistic features can reliably distinguish AI-generated from human-written text. However, many previously proposed indicators prove strongly context-dependent, with the exception of measures of lexical richness, which remain robust signals across model families and text domains. These results demonstrate which linguistic signals generalize across contexts and provide a foundation for more reliable, interpretable analyses of AI-generated language.
  </details>

- **[IdeaTaste: Do LLMs Share the Research Taste of Human Researchers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03424)**  `arXiv:2611.03424`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shanshan Zhong, Chen Xu, Jingjie Ning, Zhengzhong Liu, Aviral Kumar, Chenyan Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Research taste, the judgment of what to read, which problems to pursue, and which methods to try, increasingly shapes the work of AI scientists, yet it remains far less understood than their ability to execute research. We introduce IdeaTaste, a benchmark that scores an LLM by how closely the research proposals it writes from a literature snapshot frozen at 2025 match the work the same communities published in 2026. Our experiments span six contemporary language models and four research areas from established to emerging fields, covering more than 90{,}000 proposals in total.
