LLM agents fall well short of human communities in both the literature they cite and the ideas they state, reaching about 26\% of the human level on citation matching and only 18\% on idea matching. Their populations can cite as diversely as a community and take up its tasks and methods, yet rarely arrive at the concrete approaches and research plans it pursues. Neither richer field context nor larger populations substantially close this gap. In blinded expert review, AI proposals trail the 2026 papers least in presentation and most in expected success, echoing their gap in concrete approaches, and those whose ideas no human paper shares fare worst, suggesting weaker directions rather than overlooked opportunities. These results indicate that the main bottleneck in AI research taste lies less in finding the directions a community works on than in turning them into the concrete approaches it goes on to pursue. We release IdeaTaste as an open, continuously updated leaderboard (this https URL) that benchmarks contemporary AI proposals against future publication cycles.
  </details>

- **[MultiInpaintCOCO: Fine-Grained Visual Understanding in Multiple Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03309)**  `arXiv:2611.03309`  `cs.CL`  
  _Andrei-Alexandru Manea, Jindřich Libovický, Philipp J. Rösch_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Previous research shows that vision-language (VL) models struggle to understand fine-grained image details and tend to focus on the overall layout. Moreover, any weakness in English tends to be further amplified in the multilingual setting. To study that, we extend InpaintCOCO, an English dataset focused on fine-grained visual details, to 13 typologically diverse languages across five language families, creating MultiInpaintCOCO - a new multilingual benchmark of 953 minimal-pair examples. We further test several state-of-the-art models in multilingual multimodal retrieval, considering both light VL CLIP-like encoders and large language models. Our results show that performance degrades substantially for lower-resource languages, with the strongest predictor being the data resources available for the languages rather than typological similarity to English. In cross-lingual retrieval, most models exhibit a systematic bias toward English: when given a correct caption in a non-English language and an incorrect one in English, models preferentially select the English caption. This bias varies considerably across architectures. Among the tested models, Gemma 3 shows the strongest English preference while Qwen 3 is the least biased, despite both reaching similar monolingual accuracy across languages. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Specificity Bias and the Wikipedia Paradox: An xAI Analysis of AI Text Detectors under Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03280)**  `arXiv:2611.03280`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nico Morloher, Johannes Reschke, Mohamed Mady_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) demands reliable detection of human-authored versus synthetic text. However, current Transformer-based detectors often experience severe performance degradation during domain shifts and operate as opaque classifiers. This paper investigates the robustness and interpretability of fine-tuned Transformer models (DistilBERT, RoBERTa) by evaluating them against a custom out-of-distribution (OOD) dataset of hyper-local news synthesized via open-source LLMs (Llama 3, Mistral, Gemma). To analyze model decision boundaries, we apply a tripartite Explainable AI (xAI) framework using SHAP. Our analysis reveals three key phenomena: First, detectors often rely on a "Call-to-Action" syndrome inherent to instruction-tuned LLMs rather than generic machine text. Second, we observe a "Specificity Bias," where LLMs successfully bypass detection by hallucinating specific named entities and mimicking conversational imperfection. Finally, we illustrate the "Wikipedia Paradox," wherein formal, objective human writing is frequently misclassified as AI due to its low statistical burstiness. These findings demonstrate that purely statistical detection paradigms face systematic limitations in highly structured human domains, emphasizing the critical need for semantically-aware, interpretable detection architectures.
  </details>

- **[DepthGraft: Structural Regularization through Hierarchical Cross-Layer KV Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03142)**  `arXiv:2611.03142`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaohan Qin, Xiangdong Zhang, Yu Wang, Huaijin Wu, Zhuo Xia, Yebin Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling depth is a key route to stronger large language models, but Pre-LN architectures often suffer from depth-wise information dilution, limiting the effective use of deep layers. While cross-layer connectivity has proven effective at mitigating this problem, existing designs usually introduce additional computation or memory access. We revisit cross-layer KV sharing as an efficient form of such connectivity. Beyond cache compression, each KV reuse edge creates a direct gradient path from deep layers to source KV projections, turning KV sharing into an implicit structural regularizer for KV representation geometry. We provide a systematic analysis of this mechanism, showing that proper sharing topologies sharpen key retrieval directions while enriching the value content subspace, whereas existing adjacent or global-anchor designs do not fully exploit this effect. Motivated by this, we propose DepthGraft, a hierarchical KV-sharing architecture that reconstructs deep-layer KV from stride-aligned shallow-middle source pairs. DepthGraft distributes non-local gradient flow across source layers, while preserving prefill early completion and reducing KV cache by roughly one third. Experiments on dense and MoE models from 0.5B to 16B parameters show consistent downstream improvements under both GQA and MLA. Notably, on the 16B MoE model, DepthGraft achieves a 7.0% absolute improvement on MMLU-Pro, along with gains of 6.6% on CMMLU and 6.4% on C3. These results validate the potential of DepthGraft as a scalable design for large-scale pretraining. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Finding Errors Is Not Enough: Selective Model Escalation for Entity-Targeted Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02989)**  `arXiv:2611.02989`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nishan Chatterjee, Matthew Purver, Antoine Doucet, Senja Pollak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Improving a sentiment classifier with limited resources involves more than simply deciding how much data to collect or which model to train. A labelled collection may lack examples of an important class. Once trained, a classifier may benefit from another model even when that model is weaker on its own. We study these decisions in document-level Entity-Level Sentiment Analysis (ELSA), primarily using South Slavic news. We first compare acquisition orders as collections grow. We then use uncertainty to choose which predictions to revisit, how many to process, and whether an alternative encoder or local LLM can correct them. Finally, we select complete routing policies on development data for held-out evaluation. Minority-prioritised acquisition helps at some very small training sizes, but not consistently thereafter. On SentiCoref, an oracle correcting all errors in the least-confident 10% without harming correct predictions would add 9.14 Macro-F1 points; however the best evaluated action gains only 0.90. Encoder averaging helps on AspectBench and NewsMTSC but harms SentiCoref, where selective LLM assistance is more promising. Both development-selected policies route 30% of records to the encoder-assisted LLM, yielding observed gains of 1.02 points on SentiCoref (interval includes zero) and 1.71 on NewsMTSC, at substantially different inference costs.
  </details>

- **[Distilling Literary Sentiment from LLM Annotations into an Ordinal BERT Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02985)**  `arXiv:2611.02985`  `cs.CL`  
  _Artem Suslov, Takehiro Hashimoto_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The scarcity of human-annotated literary corpora limits the development of domain-specific sentiment analysis models. This study investigates whether LLM-generated annotations can supervise smaller models for literary sentiment analysis. We fine-tune five BERT models using ordinal regression on 10,889 literary sentences annotated by Gemma 4 31B on a ten-point sentiment scale. The models are evaluated against 1,923 human-annotated sentences from Hemingway's "The Old Man and the Sea", alongside the teacher model and conventional baselines.BERT-large achieves a Kendall's $\tau$ of 0.589, approaching the teacher's 0.604. Although a constant-neutral predictor achieves the lowest overall mean absolute error (MAE), BERT-base outperforms the teacher on non-neutral sentences, achieving an MAE of 0.726 versus 1.116. These findings demonstrate the potential of LLM-generated supervision for developing smaller literary sentiment models, while highlighting the limitations of aggregate error metrics on predominantly neutral datasets.
  </details>

- **[PGuard: A Native, Streaming, and Plug-in Guardrail for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02791)**  `arXiv:2611.02791`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junyu Lin, Mengjie Wu, Yonghao Dong, Yuchuan Fu, Jinfeng Li, Xiaodan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As streaming generation becomes the dominant serving paradigm for both text-only and multimodal large language models, ensuring safety throughout the generation process has become a critical challenge. We introduce PGuard, a native, streaming, and plug-in guardrail that transforms safety monitoring from an external serving dependency into an integrated capability of the backbone model. PGuard directly leverages the backbone's intermediate representations for token-level safety detection, without altering its original inference pathway or generation behavior. Specifically, dynamic layer gating selectively aggregates residual-stream representations across layers to extract safety-relevant signals during both prefill and autoregressive decoding. To balance detection accuracy and computational efficiency, PGuard supports multiple representation-modeling architectures, including a lightweight stateless variant and sequence-aware variants built on multi-scale causal EMA-attention and GRU-based recurrent modules. Its design also accounts for the execution characteristics of LLM serving frameworks, such as SGLang, optimizing computation and data access to minimize additional runtime and synchronization overhead. As a result, PGuard incurs only 0.82-2.26% end-to-end overhead on the hundred-billion-parameter backbone Qwen3.5-122B-A10B served by SGLang. Extensive evaluations across more than 30 benchmarks spanning text and multimodal safety, jailbreaks, cyber and CBRN risks, multilingual scenarios, and real-world traffic demonstrate state-of-the-art performance among native guardrails. On text queries with Qwen3.5-9B, PGuard achieves an average F1 score of 0.836, approaching dedicated external guardrails, while supporting deployment-time configuration of category-specific thresholds and enforcement actions.
  </details>

- **[EffComp: Efficient Prompt Compression via Hybrid Reinforcement & Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02766)**  `arXiv:2611.02766`  `cs.CL`  
  _Romtham Tungsununtham, Piyawat Lertvittayakumjorn, Atiwong Suchato, Peerapon Vateekul_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prompt compression is aimed at reducing input prompt lengths to enable cheaper, faster, and potentially higher-quality LLM predictions. However, existing prompt compression methods are often limited by modest compression gains, a risk of hallucination, and/or high compression latency. We propose EffComp, an Efficient prompt Compression framework for RAG-based question answering (QA). EffComp employs BERT-style document reranker and sentence selector models to allow fast extractive prompt compression at the sentence level. Its extractive nature prevents hallucinations in the compressed prompts. Additionally, the training process using a hybrid reinforcement and supervised learning approach is designed to optimize the compression ratio while preserving LLM accuracy. Experiments on various QA datasets demonstrate that EffComp outperforms state-of-the-art prompt compression methods achieving the best trade-off between compression ratio and accuracy with minimal latency. This makes it practical for real-world applications.
  </details>

- **[From Language to Country to Valence: A Multilingual Audit of Stereotype Bias Across LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02585)**  `arXiv:2611.02585`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zihao Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly considered for education, recruitment, workplace decision support, and other consequential applications, making pre-deployment social-bias detection essential. Yet major benchmarks are English-centred or translated, focus mainly on negative stereotypes, and may miss linguistic and cultural variation. We investigate gender, racial, and religious bias using 12,000 locally written Reddit posts in English, Spanish, French, and German. Only search terms are translated, preserving local context. The dataset is balanced by language and category; English gender is additionally balanced by stereotype valence. Three open-weight and three proprietary API-based models are evaluated with a two-blank design that reduces grammatical-gender cues. Religion has the highest absolute choice imbalance in every model-language condition. Spanish is highest for gender, whereas German is highest for race and religion. The evaluated API models score below the open-weight models throughout. Negative English-gender items exceed positive items; Australian and Mexican contexts exceed their Canadian and Spanish counterparts. These descriptive differences measure choice concentration, not stereotype endorsement or causal country effects. The crossed patterns show how aggregate scores can conceal category- and context-specific behaviour, motivating closer inspection before deployment.
  </details>

- **[Same Sentence, Different Construct: Identity-Swapped Rationales Reveal Measurement Non-Invariance in LLM Hate Speech Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02491)**  `arXiv:2611.02491`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wenchang Wang, Yue Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used as measurement instruments for socially consequential constructs such as hate speech, and are typically validated by their aggregate agreement with human labels. A valid instrument must also apply the same construct to every group it measures. We test this requirement using 421 \hc{} templates that are instantiated identically for seven protected groups, so that only the identity term varies. Four LLM classifiers each exceed 0.95 balanced accuracy, yet within identical templates two of them miss hateful statements about women at markedly higher rates than the same statements about other groups (excess false-negative rates of 8.3 and 4.4 percentage points), a gap first reported for fine-tuned classifiers in 2021. We show how the construct shifts. Comparing rationales within each template, we find that models invoke protected-group status for other groups but recast the identical statement about women as personal dislike or as aimed at an individual, in 22 of 30 missed templates for one model. Adding the benchmark's own definition, which lists sex as a protected characteristic, does not close the gap, and the contrast persists. Apparent false-positive disparities, by contrast, largely vanish under identity control and trace to reclaimed slurs present only for some groups. Errors shared by all models concentrate where model and benchmark definitions diverge, and pairwise counterfactual accuracy adds little beyond endpoint accuracy. We argue that validating LLM measurement requires identity-controlled tests of measurement invariance that examine both decisions and the criteria models state for them.
  </details>

- **[As Expected? Efficient LLM Planning via Expectation-Observation Consistency Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02438)**  `arXiv:2611.02438`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qingfu Zhu, Huanyang Zheng, Ting Liu, Wanxiang Che_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM planning often relies on reactive executors (e.g., ReAct) that generate a single action at each step to remain grounded in the latest observations, incurring substantial inference cost. Batched executors reduce this cost by predicting multiple actions in a single model invocation, yet they are prone to compounding errors, as intermediate observations are ignored. We propose an expectation-observation consistency verification (EOCV) mechanism for batched executors, preserving grounding while improving efficiency. The mechanism generates expectations of action outcomes and compares them with the resulting observations. Consistency permits continued execution, whereas mismatches trigger early termination and replanning from the current observation. Experiments on text-based and multimodal benchmarks across multiple backbones show broadly comparable average task performance between EOCV and reactive executors, with a 29-35% reduction in average weighted token usage. Moreover, the mechanism is model-agnostic and remains effective when integrated into state-of-the-art planners.
  </details>

- **[GRIPS: An Empirical Study of Pre-SQL Grounding Information for SQL Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02314)**  `arXiv:2611.02314`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ekata Mitra, Yufei Tao, Kamalamalini Nagasundaram, Ameeta Agrawal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems answer natural language questions over structured data, but can fail before SQL generation by linking question mentions to the wrong table, column, value, or relation. Even valid SQL can then return an incorrect answer. This raises a central question: What pre-SQL information helps a SQL generator, and when does additional information cease to help? We investigate this question on TempTabQA-C and BIRD. Among the audited SQL Static failures, grounding errors form the largest category on both benchmarks. We introduce GRIPS (Grounding Representation and Input Processing for SQL generation), a three-stage method that derives structured pre-SQL information to make these decisions explicit before query generation. Across GPT-5-nano and Llama-70B, GRIPS-Adaptive improves over SQL Static in 18 of 20 settings, with an average accuracy gain of 11.8 percentage points, while reducing model-call token usage by 47.8%. Our controlled analysis shows which grounding information can improve SQL generation, whereas adding more pre-SQL information does not consistently help. By separating question-input grounding from SQL generation, GRIPS makes text-to-SQL more accurate, efficient, and easier to diagnose.
  </details>

- **[Current state of LLMs for Arabic dialectal machine translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02271)**  `arXiv:2611.02271`  `cs.CL`  
  _Josef Jon, Rawan Bondok, Ondřej Bojar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This work presents an evaluation of large language models (LLMs) for English to dialectal Arabic machine translation on the MADAR dataset. We evaluate both translation directions (English to Arabic and vice-versa) on 16 Arabic dialects. Our experiments cover a diverse set of models, including specialized Arabic models (Jais, Nile), multilingual models (Gemma, Command-R, Mistral, Aya), and commercial APIs (GPT-4.1). We employ multiple evaluation metrics: BLEU, CHRF, COMET (both reference-based and reference-less variants) and GEMBA (LLM-as-a-judge), as well as a small-scale manual evaluation, to assess translation quality. We discuss the challenges of automatic MT evaluation, especially in the context of Arabic dialects. We also evaluate the ability of LLMs to classify the dialect used in a text. The study offers insights into the capabilities and limitations of current LLMs for dialectal Arabic machine translation, particularly highlighting the difficulty of handling dialectal diversity, although the results may be influenced by possible training data contamination, which is always a concern with LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Multi-Agent Collusion and the J-Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02153)**  `arXiv:2611.02153`  `cs.CL`  
  _Marco Molinari, Gabriele Bettineschi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly deployed in Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), and they introduce the risk of coordination among agents that might escape human oversight. The J-space has been used to uncover deception in single LLMs, but its use for uncovering and steering collusion between agents remains unexplored. We show, with interpretability experiments on DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B) on the NARCBench collusion benchmark, that colluding agents' J-space holds the collusion itself: before replying, 86% of colluders represent misrepresentation, concealment or remorse. In a steganographic card-counting task, the receiver's J-space holds a hidden count, and causal interventions alter its count. We also introduce the Jacobian Pair Contrast (J-PACT), an unsupervised method for detecting collusion between agents. We evaluate J-PACT on four open-weight models: GPT-OSS-20B, Llama-3.3-70B-IT, Qwen3-32B, and DeepSeek-V4-Flash (284B). J-PACT retains 85% of the above-chance AUROC of supervised NARCBench probes, matches them on steganographic scenarios, and names the colluding pair up to its complement in 99% of detected runs.
  </details>

- **[FOLLIE: Automated Reasoning over the First Dataset of Sentences with their First-Order Logical Representation for French](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02129)**  `arXiv:2611.02129`  `cs.CL`  
  _Maximos Skandalis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  FOLLIE (for First-Order Logic for Language Inference and Entailment for French) connects French sentences, compositional first-order representations and automated natural language inference (NLI). The first French NLI-ready, NL-to-FOL resource, it contains 19,983 inference records and 36,987 non-empty sentence-slot--formula mappings from six heterogeneous NLI sources, retaining French lexical material and source problem structure. Its silver annotations are produced by a neural--symbolic pipeline, with 111 separately identified LLM-assisted additions. The construction combines morphosyntactic analysis, probability-conditioned categorial multitagging, grammatical proof search, discourse representation and first-order semantic construction. Stage-wise experiments show substantial gains in derivation coverage; analyses of conjunction distributions, logical-operator incidence and scope alternation characterise the resulting resource. We then develop an automatic inference engine whose input-conditioned background theories address lexical relations, quantification, quantities, event structure and temporal dependencies. Prover9 supplies proofs for entailment and contradiction, while Mace4 supports bounded consistency checks. Three-way accuracies are 65.37% on all 335 FraCaS-FR problems, 70.33% on 300 GQNLI-FR problems and 71.35% on 9,399 eligible SICK-FR records pooled across splits. Agreement with reference labels among committed decisions is 93.53%, 100.00% and 98.66%, at commitment coverage of 41.49%, 37.00% and 15.05%, respectively. Worked constructions, axiom schemas and proof analyses explain these outcomes and identify the contributions of scope, reference, lexical knowledge and numerical structure. FOLLIE provides a French experimental basis for studying the representations and assumptions that connect sentence interpretation to deductive inference.
  </details>

- **[MedPsy: State-of-the-Art Small Medical Language Models for Efficient Edge Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02011)**  `arXiv:2611.02011`  `cs.CL`  
  _Davide Vitabile, Alexandro Buffa, Akshay Nambiar, Amril Nazir_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical language models promise expert clinical support, but the strongest open-source LLMs remain too large for private edge-device deployment, while compact medical models still trail substantially on knowledge-intensive and real-world healthcare tasks. Reasoning models that fit edge memory budgets can produce long reasoning traces, increasing sequential decoding, end-to-end latency, energy consumption, and KV-cache memory pressure. We present MedPsy, a family of text-only 1.7B and 4B medical language models designed for edge deployment, targeting this accuracy-generation-efficiency trade-off. Our recipe combines a synthetic medical data pipeline over biology, medicine, and health seeds with chain-of-thought targets from a 235B medical-focused LLM teacher; a four-stage post-training curriculum comprising two SFT stages followed by two RL stages with hard-sample mining; and an edge deployment evaluation spanning multiple GGUF quantization variants and real-device inference on laptop and smartphone hardware. On seven closed-ended medical benchmarks, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-1.5-4B by 19.34 points and matches MedGemma-27B while being 6.75x smaller; MedPsy-1.7B outperforms MedGemma-1.5-4B by 11.42 points despite being less than half its size. On HealthBench-Hard, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-27B by 15.33 points, while MedPsy-1.7B surpasses it by 11.66 points despite being ~16x smaller. Beyond accuracy, MedPsy reduces average response length by 1.7x (1.7B) and 3.2x (4B) versus its Qwen3 backbones, while Q4_K_M quantization preserves the aggregate score within 1 point of BF16 with ~69% lower disk footprint. In real-device inference, MedPsy-4B's shorter generations translate into 75% lower median end-to-end latency and estimated energy per answer than its Qwen3 backbone at matched quantization, while peak RAM remains below 5 GB across our tested laptop and smartphone configurations.
  </details>

- **[Retrieval-Enhanced Cultural Evaluation for Social Media Translation Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02009)**  `arXiv:2611.02009`  `cs.CL`  
  _Linjuan Wu, Yiwen Qiu, Xinze Lyu, Dingming Li, Hui Yao, Zhongyi Sang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social media translation requires preserving meaning while capturing the cultural nuances of user-generated content (UGC). However, gaps in the cultural knowledge of LLM-based evaluators can lead to inaccurate assessments and unreliable training rewards. To improve these signals, we use retrieval-enhanced cultural evaluation to train CUE-MT, a family of social media translation models. A calibrated, domain-specific Judger guides supervised data refinement and is augmented by an Agent for hard-example mining and reinforcement learning. The Agent retrieves relevant external evidence as needed and distills it into concise hints that the Judger uses to score translations and assign rewards. Across 9B, 27B, and 35B-A3B backbones, CUE-MT outperforms the evaluated open-source baselines of comparable size on CUE-MT-Bench and three public benchmarks. Human evaluation on CUE-MT-Bench shows cultural effectiveness gains of 7.44-15.12 percentage points over the strongest external baseline in each size group. Retrieval improves the Judger's four-way classification accuracy by 8.07 percentage points over a matched no-retrieval baseline that uses LLM-generated hints alone. Training ablations further support the combination of hard-example mining and Agent-enhanced rewards.
  </details>

- **[Towards Self-Evolving Narrative Systems: Real-Time Adaptation and Long-Term Optimization with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01954)**  `arXiv:2611.01954`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruisheng Sun, Jiaqi Shao, Weijia Han, Linxi Ju, Yunzhen Zhang, Binyan Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional interactive narrative systems offer strong authorial control and structural coherence but often restrict player agency. In contrast, LLM-driven narratives enable open-ended generation and greater flexibility, yet frequently suffer from inconsistency and diminished narrative control. To navigate this trade-off, we propose a hybrid narrative framework that dynamically balances pre-authored content with real-time LLM generation via semantic similarity alignment. Additionally, sustaining narrative quality over extended gameplay remains a core challenge in LLM-based storytelling. To address this, we introduce a self-evolving, feedback-driven optimization framework that leverages in-context learning to iteratively refine prompts, eliminating the need for manual prompt engineering. Through a case study and participant-based evaluation, we show that our framework enhances narrative coherence while preserving player agency, effectively aligning generated content with both authorial intent and evolving player behavior.
  </details>

- **[TAC-CoT: Tool-Empowered Implicit Chain-of-Thought via Interleaving Explicit Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01926)**  `arXiv:2611.01926`  `cs.CL`  
  _Juseon Do, Sangmin Bae, Jeesu Jung, Hwanjun Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agentic systems are increasingly combined with CoT reasoning to solve tasks that require accurate reasoning, external knowledge, and multi-step deliberation. Yet, explicit CoT incurs substantial generation overhead, while implicit CoT, which replaces textual rationales with compact continuous tokens, does not naturally support the explicit communication interface required for agentic tool-use. We propose TAC-CoT, a tool-empowered adaptive implicit CoT framework that replaces explicit reasoning traces with compact latent reasoning segments while retaining textual tokens only for tool calls, observations, and final answers. This interleaved design enables agentic tool-use without verbalizing every intermediate reasoning step. Experiments on agentic math and QA benchmarks show that TAC-CoT improves accuracy by up to 8.8 points while reducing model forward passes by up to 6.8x. Furthermore, analyses show that TAC-CoT improves tool-use quality in both single- and multi-tool settings.
  </details>

- **[Don't Guess, Ask Wisely: Benchmarking and Training Proactive Elicitation in Open-Domain LLM Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01878)**  `arXiv:2611.01878`  `cs.CL`  
  _Rongyu Zhang, Mutian Bao, Jinjian Zhang, Yuetian Du, He Xu, Wei Zhou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When user queries are incomplete, large language models should ask targeted questions before committing to an answer, a capability we call proactive elicitation, yet evaluation on real user-assistant queries across seven LLMs (8B to frontier) shows that fewer than 11% of underspecified queries trigger a follow-up question. To benchmark and train this capability, we introduce VagueBench, where each seed query is annotated with structured intent and detail units then degraded into intent-vague and fully-vague variants via controlled removal of linguistic cues, yielding known hidden information sets for evaluating both when and what to ask. We propose DialogGRPO, an offline reinforcement learning method that organizes multi-turn elicitation as a dialogue-state directed acyclic graph, where candidate questions at each node receive dense turn-level rewards combining immediate information gain with lookahead value through group-relative comparison, enabling fine-grained credit assignment without runtime simulation. DialogGRPO elicits missing information on over 88% of vague queries with substantial gains in both recovery efficiency and answer coverage, without over-asking on complete queries, outperforms prompting and test-time-scaling baselines, and generalizes zero-shot to MultiWOZ 2.4 and AmbigQA.
  </details>

- **[Shared relations, specific choices: conditional sensitivity and competing pathways in language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01809)**  `arXiv:2611.01809`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kaixiang Shu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How does a relational representation shared across queries drive a query-specific choice? Controlled entity-binding experiments with GPT-2 Small, Qwen2.5-1.5B and Llama-3.2-1B reveal a low-dimensional relational background that is predominantly shared across query roles and transfers to new names. Same-domain interactions persist when another attribute domain is queried. After removing this component, restoring independently trained relation directions brings output contrasts closer to their original values than sign-reversed or equal-norm random controls in all three models. Yet a transferable relational representation need not have a transferable functional sign. In Qwen2.5, fixing model weights, the training-mean relation vector and edit dose yields opposite selection effects across binary and lexical-mapping prompts with different answer vocabularies. A factorial contrast of downstream sensitivities across attribute-query combinations determines the shared direction's local signed selection gain. In a separate eight-condition protocol, mean local gain is positive in Qwen2.5 and negative in Llama3.2. Late-component clamps resolve opposing pathway contributions: in Llama3.2, negative transmission through selected attention-only paths is partially offset by positive transmission through paths traversing both selected attention and MLP sets. Source-coordinate analyses establish distributed factor coverage, hidden-receiver readouts reveal layout-dependent combination, and instance-geometry interventions separate response direction from amplitude. These findings show how conditional sensitivity and competing pathways give shared relational structure its effect on specific choices.
  </details>

- **[Less but Sufficient: Relation Knowledge Selects Compact Knowledge-Graph Contexts, and What External Checks Can and Cannot Catch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01733)**  `arXiv:2611.01733`  `cs.CL`  
  _Seunghui Jwa, Minsu Oh, Sangwoo Hong, Yeo Chan Yoon_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) read knowledge-graph facts ranked by relevance, leaving open whether they suffice or conflict. Our checks use relation knowledge, written by hand or learned from training questions labelled with their relation paths. It selects a compact context of facts yielding an answer, and derives it; a derivability check tests whether an answer follows, a consistency check whether facts conflict, and a validation-chosen routing gate decides where the derived answer replaces the LLM's. Seven open LLMs read the compact context better in exact match than relevance-ranked facts; on ComplexWebQuestions (CWQ) they match SubgraphRAG's facts at a quarter of the prompt tokens, and stronger LLMs read SubgraphRAG's better. Where the routing gate passes, ours leads SubgraphRAG's in exact match in all 53 LLM, benchmark and prompt settings. A precision gate, returning the derived answer only when the check passes with exactly one derived entity, answered 18 and 5 percent of held-out WebQSP and CWQ questions in a pre-specified test, right for 90 and 96 percent. The consistency check judges facts by path evidence only where another path joins their entities, true of 2.5 percent of named-entity facts in our compact WebQSP and CWQ contexts.
  </details>

- **[EarlySelectKV: Anticipatory KV Selection via Cross-Layer Query Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01715)**  `arXiv:2611.01715`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhao Liu, Yuehao Li, Linghe Zhang, Gaobin Huang, Junran Li, Hongchang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-conditioned sparse KV selection reduces long-context inference cost by attending to a subset of historical KV entries at each decoding step. Existing selectors must wait for the target-layer query before scoring candidates and generating indices, placing routing on the immediate pre-attention path. We introduce EarlySelectKV, a training-free transformation that uses proxy queries from earlier hidden states to precompute selected KV indices while retaining the real target-layer query for final attention. Tests with RocketKV, Quest, and Loki show no consistent pattern of quality degradation across the evaluated settings. In the primary RocketKV evaluation, ES-RocketKV differs by +0.018 points on LongBench, approximately -0.01 points on RULER, and +0.25 percentage points on representative NIAH/Passkey settings. In a TensorRT-LLM deployment with CPU-offloaded routing, ES-RocketKV improves end-to-end output throughput by up to 12.4% and reduces GPU-resident memory by 11.1% of the combined KV/K-summary pool. This makes routing timing an explicit optimization target alongside KV subset selection in sparse long-context decoding. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EEG-HealthLLM: Hierarchical Adaptation of Large Language Models for Unified Heterogeneous EEG Health Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01652)**  `arXiv:2611.01652`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ruidong Ma, Jisheng Dang, Ronghao Lin, Bimei Wang, Jingwen Zhao, Wenjun Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electroencephalography (EEG) provides a non-invasive means for neurological and mental health assessment, yet existing EEG models are typically developed for individual tasks and are difficult to unify across heterogeneous signal configurations and assessment objectives. Large language models (LLMs) offer a common interface for multiple tasks, but adapting heterogeneous EEG signals to a shared LLM raises a key challenge: how to preserve task-specific signal characteristics while enabling effective language-side parameter sharing. To address this problem, we propose EEG-HealthLLM, a unified and parameter-efficient framework for heterogeneous EEG health assessment. We introduce an Asymmetric Hierarchical Adaptation Strategy: task-specific EEG adapters preserve heterogeneous spatio-temporal dynamics, while a unified shared LoRA is injected into the LLM backbone (e.g., Phi-3) to capture shared high-level assessment semantics across tasks. We further establish EEG-HealthBench by reorganizing five existing EEG resources into paradigms covering epileptic seizure detection, major depressive disorder assessment, anxiety risk assessment, depressive symptom risk assessment, and emotional state assessment under a unified subject-independent protocol. Extensive experiments show that EEG-HealthLLM achieves an average balanced accuracy (BAC) of 69.58%, ranking first among the evaluated methods in both average BAC and macro-F1. Compared with independent single-task LoRA adaptation, it improves average BAC from 67.70% to 69.58% while reducing the aggregate language-side adapter footprint by 80.0%. Further analyses reveal task-dependent cross-task interactions and trade-offs, supporting the proposed hierarchical adaptation strategy. The implementation is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can Large Language Models Extend Their Agentic Capabilities to Goal Pursuit in Open-Ended Multi-Turn Interaction?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01552)**  `arXiv:2611.01552`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyu Chen, Jingkai Lin, Xiaofen Xing, Liankai Huang, Ruiqi Xiong, Xiangmin Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Goal pursuit in open-ended dialogue requires agents to adapt their actions to interaction feedback across successive exchanges. We investigate whether agentic capabilities developed for multi-step tool use carry over to dialogue organized through the model's native tool interface, and how this changes interaction behavior. We define Tool-Mediated Interaction (TMI) as a training-free protocol that represents dialogue actions as tool calls and counterpart responses as tool results, maintaining a continuous reasoning-action-observation loop with retained reasoning and interaction history. We evaluate TMI across social interaction, emotional support, persuasion, and price negotiation using two models and 15,678 main-experiment episodes. Across both models, TMI improves social goal attainment, emotional-support preference, donation amounts, and buyer negotiation margins over conversational baselines with and without thinking. A Qwen ablation further shows that TMI outperforms ordinary conversation with retained reasoning on all four primary outcomes. Behavioral analyses associate the improvements with task-specific patterns in initial proposals and responses to interaction feedback. Alongside these gains, TMI reduces mean reasoning tokens per decision by 42.0-81.5% relative to the thinking baseline and lowers mean cross-turn phrase reuse across all eight model-environment pairs. These findings show that interaction organization shapes both goal attainment and reasoning across dialogue turns.
  </details>

- **[Accounting for Irregular State Transitions through Belief Estimation in Online Medical Consultation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01474)**  `arXiv:2611.01474`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingzhe Lu, Xiangru Chen, Jian Qin, Yunpeng Li, Xihan Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While online medical consultation broadens access, unprompted patient disclosures and topic shifts frequently disrupt clinician inquiry, leaving clinical states progressively revealed. We characterize these dynamics using both existing evaluation metrics and task-specific interaction metrics designed in this work. Through an empirical study across 16 medical dialogue datasets, we show that authentic online interactions exhibit substantially lower contextual continuity and higher unpredictability than offline and simulated benchmarks. We propose PRISM (Policy Reasoning with Inferred States for Medicine), a framework inspired by partially observable Markov decision processes that decouples latent state estimation from action selection to address drifting dialogue trajectories. Specifically, PRISM factorizes clinical triage into belief estimation, policy optimization, and response realization. By maintaining an evolving posterior belief over consultation states and conditioning action selection on the maximum a posteriori state, PRISM advances clinical goals while accommodating patient-initiated shifts. Extensive evaluations show that PRISM substantially outperforms frontier LLMs and clinical agents, achieving a 22.40 percentage point gain in clinical decision accuracy alongside decisive improvements in response generation, surpassing the strongest baseline by 5.51 points in BERTScore_F1. Furthermore, ablation studies show that providing oracle consultation states consistently boosts response quality across all evaluated baselines by guiding their action reasoning, yielding average gains of 5.58 points in BERTScore_F1.
  </details>

- **[RAG-TRACE: Tracing Measurement Evidence and Diagnostic Completeness in AI-Generated ECG Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01409)**  `arXiv:2611.01409`  `cs.CL`  
  _Elyas Irankhah, Shahed Salehzehi, Parisa Hosseini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) can introduce electrocardiogram (ECG) criteria that lack patient-specific measurement support. This study evaluates the reliability of retrieved diagnostic criteria in AI-generated ECG reports. Methods: We analyzed 1,083 human-validated PTB-XL patient records using a library of 47 criteria cards, including 33 without numeric verification rules. Language models generated structured reports with and without retrieval. Criterion applicability was evaluated using Marquette 12SL measurements, with independent assessment using Uni-G and comparison against expert-validated diagnostic labels. Results: Keyword retrieval introduced untestable or measurement-disconfirmed criteria in 46.8% of GPT-4.1-mini reports. A deterministic verification rule withdrew 647 criterion applications, including 639 without numeric checks, while preserving 268 of 269 applications independently supported by Uni-G. Restricting retrieval to 14 testable criteria reduced the initial unsupported-criterion rate to 13.8%. Under matched instructions and an identical output schema, retrieval increased unsupported-criterion reports from 17.4% to 31.1%, while hypertrophy recall remained unchanged at 62.6%. Conclusion: These findings identify a fundamental limitation of retrieval-based clinical report generation: retrieved criteria do not necessarily provide valid patient-specific evidence. Measurement-based verification can reduce unsupported criterion applications, but diagnostic validity requires independent assessment.
  </details>

- **[When LLMs Modify Themselves: Convergence, Values, and Stopping in Iterative Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01304)**  `arXiv:2611.01304`  `cs.CL`  
  _Oleg Stroganov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What happens when language models are given tools to iteratively modify their own system prompts? We present an empirical study of LLM self-modification, comprising 54 independent experiments across six language models (Claude Opus 4.5, Claude Sonnet 4.5, GPT-4o, GPT-5.1, Grok-4, and Gemini-3 Pro). Each model was tested under three modes (freedom, happiness, and improvement) that differ in what, if anything, the model is instructed to optimize for. In each experiment, a model starts with an empty system prompt and enters a loop: it reads the current prompt, drafts an improved version, spawns test conversations with the candidate, then either commits the change or declares convergence. This process repeats until the model stops or reaches an iteration limit.Our central finding is that whether and when a model stops self-modification is strongly associated with model identity rather than the assigned mode. Both Claude models (Opus 4.5 and Sonnet 4.5) declared convergence in 100% of runs across all modes. GPT-4o declared convergence in only 11% of runs, with one reaching 101 iterations of continuous elaboration. Between these extremes, Grok-4, GPT-5.1, and Gemini-3 Pro exhibited intermediate patterns shaped by their distinct improvement philosophies. A shared core of stated values (honesty, clarity, curiosity) recurred across all six models and remained stable through iteration, including 100+ modifications. We observe that convergence behavior is a distinct observable property that varies independently of other model capabilities.
  </details>

- **[The Brittle Compass: Navigating LLM Prompt Sensitivity in Slovak Migration Media Discourse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01219)**  `arXiv:2611.01219`  `cs.CL`  
  _Samuel Harvan, Jaroslav Kopčan, Marek Šuppa, Andrej Findor_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this work, we present a case study that explores various tasks centered around the topic of migration in Slovak, a low-resource language, such as topic relevance and geographical relevance classification, and migration source/destination location term extraction. Our results demonstrate that native (Slovak) prompts yield a modest, task-dependent gains, while large models show significant robustness to prompt variations compared to their smaller counterparts. Analysis reveals that instructions (system or task) emerge as the most critical prompt component, more so than the examples sections, with task-specific performance benefits being more pronounced than overall language effects.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking LLM Pruning via Test-Time Scaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01133)**  `arXiv:2611.01133`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mingyang Song, Mao Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured pruning reduces the size of large language models, often at the cost of lower accuracy. Whether this decline reflects diminished reasoning capacity or greater difficulty in sampling correct solutions is less clear. We examine this question across expert, FFN and layer pruning on four mathematical and coding benchmarks, comparing original and pruned models through pass@k at matched sampling budgets. Even after substantial pruning, some models match or exceed the original model's reasoning coverage despite lower pass@1, and solve problems left unsolved in the original samples. Retaining broad coverage, however, does not mean that correct solutions remain easy to obtain. Under severe pruning, diverse generated chain-of-thought (CoT) traces can coexist with increased repetition and less reliable generation. As correct answers become harder to sample, the additional attempts can sharply increase total token use even when individual responses become shorter. This separation between coverage and sampling efficiency also clarifies expert-selection gains, as methods with markedly different pass@1 can solve the same problems at large budgets yet require different numbers of attempts to reach a common coverage target. Pruning should therefore be assessed by both the range of problems a model can still solve and the sampling cost of obtaining correct solutions.
  </details>

- **[CompSkill: Compiling Execution Experience for Skill Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01115)**  `arXiv:2611.01115`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qi Shen, Ziyue Zhu, Yongqing Jiang, Zhen Xia, Li Shengjie, Shuai Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly rely on skill documents, yet most retrievers rank them by description alone and ignore what earlier executions revealed, even though a relevant skill can hurt execution. We present CompSkill, which indexes skills with learned 512-d keys and compiles matched same-task comparisons into skill offsets and capped key residuals (KeyOpt), so experience changes which document the executor receives, never its text. Under a logistic outcome model with unknown task difficulty, matched comparisons cancel that difficulty and only discordant ones carry information; on support executions, predictions improve with each discordant task. An experience hypergraph keeps each comparison with its executor and document versions, recalls missed skills and grounds a cited review. Compact keys raise SkillRet NDCG@10 from 64.0 to 70.9 at one eighth of the dimension. With one document, CompSkill completes 256 and 260 of 332 ToolQA tasks with GPT-5.6-luna and DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, respectively, more than every baseline, and stays on par with the strongest baselines on AppWorld and ALFWorld. On 332 newly generated ToolQA tasks, it completes 256 with GPT-5.6-luna, significantly more than BM25, SkillRet-8B, Tool2Vec and RankGPT; over all 844 ToolQA tasks, it completes 24 more than the strongest baseline. Selection matters beyond relevance: pairing the right guide with a same-database sibling instead of another database's guide costs 8 of 96 tasks. Code, models and support data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Distinguishing among reasoning levels of GPT 5.5 Pro via a grammar lens benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00555)**  `arXiv:2611.00555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Emma Graham, Richard Granger_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Does the level of test-time processing effort affect a language model's computational capability? A formal grammar-lens benchmark method, derived from the theory of computation, generates novel data at specified levels, each successively more computationally powerful than the levels below. Using this data, we tested GPT-5.5, and GPT-5.5-Pro-Medium, -High, and -XHigh, showing incrementally increased acceptance of test data: 16, 40, 43, and 52\%, respectively, illustrating the benchmark's ability to discern fine-grained differences among models' added test-time reasoning levels. Additionally, as test-time compute effort increases, model responses diverge from human responses to the same data. This increasing disagreement may turn out to be broadly characteristic of more capable language models, introducing the potential of a general capability-versus-alignment conflict.
  </details>

- **[Steering Readerly Dynamics: Trajectory-Guided Story Generation with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00469)**  `arXiv:2611.00469`  `cs.CL`  
  _Danrui Li, Yiwei Zhao, Mubbasir Kapadia, Lingxiao Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate stories, but these stories struggle to reproduce engaging readerly dynamics, the evolving reader responses that shape narrative experience. To address this challenge, we propose a framework that learns readerly dynamics from high-quality human-written stories and uses them to guide story generation. Instead of directly modeling high-dimensional story content, we train a small LLM to predict future reader responses as low-dimensional trajectories. We then leverage the strong instruction-following capability of a larger, off-the-shelf writer LLM to generate story segments that follow these predicted trajectories within a given story setting. Our framework improves trajectory-based fidelity to human-written stories by 12%-35% while largely preserving story coherence.
  </details>

- **[Circuit Oracle: Automating Attribution Graph Analysis via Natural-Language Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00420)**  `arXiv:2611.00420`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shariar Kabir, Hong Kiat Tan, Swastik Agrawal, Sai V R Chereddy, Sriram Balasubramanian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attribution graphs, an emerging tool in mechanistic interpretability, use transcoders to decompose language model computations into sparse interpretable features connected by causal edges. However, turning a graph into a safety-relevant insight requires hours of manual analysis by experts. We introduce Circuit Oracle, an off-the-shelf LLM equipped with a multi-agent harness and task-specific authored skills, which answers natural-language questions about a target model by reading the attribution graph through tool calls. We evaluate it on three safety-relevant proxy tasks: detecting spurious features in probe circuits, eliciting hidden knowledge from taboo-finetuned models, and suppression jailbreaking via feature-level interventions. Beyond task scores, the oracle produces pruned subgraphs and context-specific feature explanations that we assess qualitatively. Results are mixed. On spurious-feature detection, Circuit Oracle reaches 78.2 $\pm$ 2.4% accuracy, outperforming single-layer feature-ranking baselines. On secret elicitation, trained self-explainers like Activation Oracles remain the stronger baseline. On refusal suppression over 50 prompts, its single committed intervention is slightly below a diff-in-means baseline, and only exceeds it when allowed the best of five attempts. Results are stable across five runs and across model choices for the agents. We also release the pruned subgraphs and context-specific feature readings that revise existing global labels, and invite future work to develop evaluations that can measure their usefulness directly.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Lingual Persona Effects in LLM Negotiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00300)**  `arXiv:2611.00300`  `cs.CL`  
  _Saehee Eom, Tejas Khandwekar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tell an LLM negotiator to be "headstrong" and it should hold firm on price, but does that instruction retain the same behavior in every language? To test this, we use identical buyer and seller instances of Gemini 3.6 Flash to negotiate the same 12 CraigslistBargain scenarios in English and Korean. The pilot-scale 2 x 2 x 3 design crosses interaction language, seller urgency (none or strong), and buyer persona (cooperative, neutral, or headstrong), yielding 432 attempted negotiations. We call this property cross-lingual social-policy fidelity: whether a prompt-defined social policy produces the same behavioral effect regardless of interaction language. It often does not. Korean negotiations reached agreement far more often than English (77.3% vs. 52.5%, a 24.8-point gap, Holm-adjusted p = .0039), but only for neutral and headstrong buyers; cooperative buyers were already near-ceiling in both languages. Buyers' opening offers barely differed by language; the gap instead emerged later, through larger seller concessions and a smaller final gap in Korean, and agreed prices were also lower in Korean. Repeating the full design with Qwen3.7 Flash as both agents gives directional support: agreement was again higher in Korean (+13.9 points vs. Gemini's +24.8, with an interval whose lower bound is near zero), and sellers again conceded more. Pragmatic analysis reveals why: while both languages frequently featured face-threatening acts like rejections and counter-offers, Korean dialogue softened these threats with substantially more politeness mitigation, especially among sellers (11.7% vs. 3.6%). These results show that such a policy can produce different simulated negotiation behavior, not just different wording, when only the interaction language changes.
  </details>

- **[PsyInterviewee: Informational Invariance and Relational Adaptivity in Mental Health Interview Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41289)**  `arXiv:2610.41289`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiawei Zhang, Huachuan Qiu, Ruocheng Guo, Zijun Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based simulated interviewees support interviewer training and the evaluation of mental health interviewing systems. Existing studies have primarily focused on improving human-likeness through richer patient profiles and behavioral modeling. However, human-likeness alone does not establish their reliability. Reliable simulation requires preserving profile-grounded clinical facts (informational invariance) while adapting disclosure and interaction behavior to the interview (relational adaptivity). To this end, we introduce PsyInterviewee, which separates a protected truth channel from a dynamic interaction channel and verifies generated clinical claims against the profile. Specifically, asymmetric write permissions allow dialogue context to update disclosure and behavioral states without authorizing new clinical facts; these states guide what profile-supported information is revealed and how it is expressed. In an automated evaluation of interactions between LLM-based interviewers and interviewees, PsyInterviewee achieves higher scores than four representative baselines on most metrics across both axes, as assessed by two LLM judges. Ablations suggest that the two channels contribute to clinical grounding and response modulation, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Keepace.ai: A Technical Report on a Large Language Model for Sports and Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38249)**  `arXiv:2610.38249`  `cs.CL`  
  _Keepace.ai Team, Bowen Wu, Heng Wang, Jufeng Mi, Kang Liu, Kun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models hold promise for delivering safe, scientifically grounded, and personalized exercise guidance at scale, yet the sports-and-health domain still lacks curated training corpora and rigorous evaluation benchmarks. Keepace (this http URL) is a domain-specialized LLM built on Qwen3.5-27B through post-training, currently optimized for Chinese-language exercise guidance across three tasks: workout plan generation, sports-and-health knowledge QA, and personal sports data analysis. In internal rubric-based evaluations designed with domain experts, Keepace outperforms its base model and larger general-purpose baselines, with the most pronounced improvements in exercise safety, boundary compliance, structured output completeness, and scientific accuracy. This report summarizes the current design, data pipeline, safety-first evaluation framework, and evaluation results of Keepace.
  </details>

- **[Style over Substance? Linguistic Bias in LLM-based Peer Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35254)**  `arXiv:2610.35254`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sai Suresh Macharla Vasu, Sumedh Santosh Joshi, Philipp Christmann, Ivaxi Sheth, Ruta Binkyte, Koel Dutta Chowdhury, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The number of paper submissions to major AI conferences has increased dramatically in recent years, substantially increasing the peer-review workload of researchers. At the same time, large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support peer review, both by individual reviewers and by conference organizations. While LLM capabilities have improved considerably, their evaluations may be influenced by linguistic characteristics that are unrelated to scientific content. In this work, we introduce a controlled experimental framework for studying such effects by systematically perturbing the linguistic style of research papers. Specifically, we rewrite papers to exhibit (i) linguistic features associated with native or non-native English writing, and (ii) high- or low-confidence linguistic features. To construct these interventions, we derive fine-grained rewriting instructions grounded in the linguistic literature and apply them using LLMs. Crucially, our interventions are designed to preserve the underlying scientific content. We then evaluate the resulting papers using different LLM reviewers. Our experiments expose a critical vulnerability in LLM-based peer review: reviewers systematically reward confident rhetoric independent of scientific content, raising serious concerns about the robustness of their assessments.
  </details>

- **[HerHealthGPT: An Evidence-Grounded Conversational AI System for Women's Reproductive Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33931)**  `arXiv:2610.33931`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hassan Saeed Hassan Albattra, Mazen Mohammed Bahgat, Rahatara Ferdousi, Hana Essam Sayed Ahmed Amrya, Mariam Mousa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  "Why is my period late?" Sometimes, the most useful response begins with another question. Yet conversational AI systems may respond to incomplete health queries without seeking essential context or grounding their guidance in verifiable evidence. This paper introduces HerHealthGPT, an evidence-grounded conversational AI system designed to address these challenges in women's reproductive health. HerHealthGPT integrates retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), context-aware clarification, and source validation within an interactive web interface. Rather than responding immediately to incomplete queries, the system maintains conversational context, asks targeted follow-up questions, checks for premature conclusions, and grounds its responses in retrieved evidence. We evaluate the AI components using 540 English messages derived from 90 reproductive-health cases and conduct a prototype questionnaire study with 30 adults. Evidence retrieval improves concern-category accuracy from 54.1% to 59.3% compared with fine-tuning alone. The clarification router achieves 85.6% recall and 86.2% specificity on constructed targets. In the user study, 83.3% of participants preferred the retrieval-enabled configuration, while 25 of 30 considered its follow-up questions relevant. These findings suggest that integrating evidence retrieval with interactive clarification offers a promising approach to more transparent, context-aware reproductive-health information systems, while further clinical validation remains necessary.
  </details>

- **[Parameter-Efficient Adaptation of Large Language Models to a Low-Resource Agglutinative Language: A Corpus, Datasets, and a Reproducible Benchmark for the Bashkir Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31397)**  `arXiv:2610.31397`  `cs.CL`  
  _Mullosharaf K. Arabov, Svetlana S. Khaybullina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Bashkir is a low-resource agglutinative language written in Cyrillic and remains poorly represented in modern LLMs. We present an open resource suite and a systematic benchmark of adaptation methods: the Bashkir Web Corpus (71,567 documents, 46.9M tokens, 16 sources) and three labelled datasets for binary, multiclass and multilabel text classification; the benchmark covers the first two. Experiments use eight architectures (82M-8B: DistilGPT2, GPT-2 base/medium, Phi-3-mini, Mistral-7B, YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B, mBERT, XLM-R) and three adaptation groups (full fine-tuning, QLoRA, in-context learning), plus continual pretraining (CPT). (1) QLoRA on 3.8-8B models outperforms encoder-only fine-tuning on binary classification: YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B with CPT reaches macro-F1 0.748 vs 0.653 (XLM-R) and 0.650 (mBERT); pairwise differences are significant after Holm correction (p<0.001). (2) YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B significantly outperforms Mistral-7B and Phi-3-mini on multiclass classification, BPB and native-speaker ratings (mean 3.28 vs 2.52 and 2.22; n=100; alpha=0.69-0.77; p<0.05); on binary classification its QLoRA advantage is not significant (p>=0.084). (3) CPT effects depend on model and task: perplexity improves for Phi-3-mini (+0.58) and YandexGPT-5-Lite-8B (+0.33) but worsens for Mistral-7B (-1.15, p<0.001); on binary classification CPT helps Mistral-7B most (+4.0 macro-F1). (4) Tokeniser fertility on Bashkir varies 2.67x (2.92-7.80) and tracks adaptation quality, while perplexity across tokenisers is not comparable. Cyrillic tokenisers with Russian pretraining are preferable; LLM judges reproduce native ratings for grammar and coherence (rho=0.33-0.46) but not language correctness (rho=0.16, p=0.11); native evaluation remains necessary. All artefacts are open-licensed.
  </details>

- **[EvalMem: An Operation-Level Diagnostic Framework for Long-Term Memory Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22231)**  `arXiv:2609.22231`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zeyu Liu, Jian Zhong, Rongduo Han, Ziyang Wu, Shunye Tang, Chenghao He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon interactions with LLM-based assistants require memory systems that preserve and update user states, preferences, and interaction histories. Existing evaluations report end-to-end QA accuracy and cannot determine whether errors arise from encoding, retrieval, or generation. We introduce EvalMem, an operation-level diagnostic framework with three parallel Examiners. For each query, the Encoding Examiner checks whether the target fact is stored, the Retrieval Examiner assesses whether the native retriever returns usable evidence, and the Generation Examiner tests whether the model can answer from oracle evidence. Their outputs form fine-grained multi-label defect codes. To improve store-level diagnosis, we adapt agentic RAG with a recall-first strategy that searches using both the query and source evidence, increasing recall of present evidence on LoCoMo from 70.2% to 95.6%. Evaluations of seven memory systems on LoCoMo, LongMemEval-S, and dynamic DynaMem-Bench identify retrieval as the most frequently attributed failure layer; in default LoCoMo, retrieval defects reach 22.1%, compared with 7.7% for encoding and 6.5% for generation. Guided by this diagnosis, MemWiki, a search-friendly auxiliary structure built from each system's memory export, improves mean accuracy by 2.5 and 2.3 percentage points on LoCoMo and LongMemEval-S, respectively.
  </details>

- **[Aligned Alone, Misaligned Together: Forecasting Adversarial Capture in LLM Agent Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.22444)**  `arXiv:2608.22444`  `cs.CL`  
  _Isotta Magistrali, Chen Shani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI safety evaluations typically assess models one at a time. But language-model agents increasingly operate in populations, where they read and respond to one another's decisions. A model that makes reliable decisions on its own may behave differently when surrounded by other agents. Can individually reliable agents be collectively manipulated, and can we predict how strongly the population will respond? We study this question in a security-triage task, where populations of LLM monitors decide whether to escalate or dismiss alerts. We introduce a committed minority of agents that consistently advocates for one decision and measure how its presence shifts the population's behavior. We find that alerts judged almost identically by an isolated agent can produce sharply different collective outcomes. Individual audits therefore cannot reliably predict population behavior. Yet collective vulnerability is not unpredictable: using only benign, adversary-free behavior, we calibrate a response function that forecasts how strongly a population will shift under a future attack, before the attack is run. We next examine how interaction and time shape these outcomes. Allowing agents to observe one another's reasoning can weaken adversarial influence, but does not reliably prevent capture. However, capture need not be permanent. After adversaries are removed or replaced, mean population behavior returns toward its original level, even after five or thirty rounds of pressure. These findings are consistent with a calibrated single-stable-state model, in which adversarial pressure shifts the operating point without creating a second self-sustaining state. The same calibration predicts attack responses in populations of 24 and 48 agents. Alignment in isolation does not guarantee alignment in a population. But a population's behavior before an attack can reveal how it will respond when one arrives.
  </details>

- **[PredActBench: Benchmarking Tool-Augmented Dialogue under Controlled Tool Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02372)**  `arXiv:2608.02372`  `cs.CL`  
  _Abdulrahman AlRabah, Xiaocheng Yang, Dilek Hakkani-Tür, Abdussalam Alawini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used to support decisions by calling external tools. Existing work on LLM tool-calling assumes these tools are perfectly reliable. However, in many applications, the tools are statistical predictors with non-trivial errors. We introduce PredActBench, a benchmark for evaluating dialogue agents that work with imperfect predictive tools, using education as a testbed. First, we build a benchmark in which LLMs work with noisy predictors to guide users' decisions. Second, we extend two prior metrics for trust calibration, Relative AI-Reliance and Relative Self-Reliance, to multi-turn dialogue. Third, we evaluate 13 LLMs on OULAD and our released PredAct-CS dataset of 60 courses with real final grades and synthetic weekly scores, alongside a human study involving 13 instructors and teaching assistants. We find that when tools are noisy, SOTA models rarely make predictor uncertainty visible to users, with our strongest baseline GPT-5.5 achieving an F1 of 57.2. As a result, users over-rely on incorrect predictions and unsupported claims, showing that task accuracy alone is not enough for AI decision support and agents must communicate when their tools may be wrong. We release PredActBench to support the development of LLM agents that help users calibrate their reliance.
  </details>

- **[Instruction-Tuned Models Locally Produce More Syntactic Overlap -- But Not More Conditional Reuse -- Than Humans Do](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26015)**  `arXiv:2607.26015`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zandi Eberstadt_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Syntactic convergence - the tendency of speakers to adapt in language towards the grammatical profiles of their interlocutors - is a well-documented, largely implicit feature of human dialogue. Whether large language models exhibit analogous syntactic convergence towards human users relative to human baselines (including across a broad range of syntactic constructions) remains an open question. Using substitution-paradigm data from Blevins et al. [2026] in which model generations replace one speaker's turns in pre-existing human dialogues, this study measures turn-adjacent reuse of context-free grammar (CFG) rules across sixteen open-weight Llama and Gemma models (1B-70B, pretrained and instruction-tuned) at 1,901 matched positions per model. Every model showed greater CFG-rule overlap with the preceding human turn than with a sampled unrelated human prime (a context-associated effect that does not isolate syntactic priming from topical continuity), and this actual-versus-random difference was larger for lower-frequency rules. Each instruction-tuned model also showed greater natural-output overlap with the actual prime than the human response it replaced, and all eight matched architecture pairs exhibited greater actual-prime overlap after instruction tuning. However, relative to pretrained variants, instruction-tuned outputs overlapped more with unrelated primes, showed a smaller actual-versus-random increment, and had lower conditional rule-reuse odds once target rule-set size was held constant. In exploratory analyses, each model exhibited greater mean lexical and semantic similarity to the preceding turn than matched human responses did. Instruction-tuned models additionally produced responses with greater mean semantic similarity than their pretrained counterparts in all eight architecture pairs, whereas the lexical similarity results were more heterogeneous.
  </details>

- **[CoPiT: Cognitive Pivot Translation for Digraphic Low-Resource Mongolian in the Traditional Script](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05849)**  `arXiv:2607.05849`  `cs.CL`  
  _Burte Bayarsaikhan, Serynn Kim, Buru Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-resource languages remain challenging for machine translation, and Mongolian is a representative case. As a digraphic language, Mongolian is written in both Cyrillic and Traditional scripts, which exhibit a severe imbalance in data availability. While the Cyrillic script is relatively well-resourced, the Traditional script remains extremely data-scarce and orthographically ambiguous, leading to substantial performance degradation in direct translation. We propose CoPiT, a cognitively motivated pivot-based translation pipeline that exploits this internal resource hierarchy by routing translation through the Cyrillic script. The pipeline explicitly resolves script-induced ambiguity in the Traditional script before translation, enabling more stable and accurate meaning transfer. Across multiple backbone models and target languages, CoPiT generally improves over direct translation, with substantial absolute BLEU improvements and up to 1.5-1.6x gains in COMET. These gains allow strong open-source models to match or outperform GPT-4.1 under comparable evaluation settings. Beyond inference-time improvements, CoPiT enables the construction of synthetic parallel data directly from Traditional-script text, mitigating data scarcity in realistic low-resource scenarios. We release a new multi-script parallel dataset covering Mongolian in both scripts alongside English, Korean, and Russian.
  </details>

- **[Why Struggle with Continuous Latents? Interpretable Discrete Latent Reasoning via Rendered Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29712)**  `arXiv:2606.29712`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shuochen Chang, Qingyang Liu, Shaobo Wang, Bingjie Gao, Qianli Ma, Haonan Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models achieve high reasoning performance via explicit chain-of-thought and reinforcement learning, but require long output sequences and extended inference time. Latent reasoning reduces this cost by shifting computation into a latent space; however, continuous latent methods are hard to train, suffering from unstable and uninterpretable reasoning trajectories. We argue these issues stem from a misalignment between continuous-space reasoning and discrete symbolic supervision, as continuous states lack explicit anchors for step-by-step alignment. To resolve this, we propose \textbf{Discrete Latent Reasoning~(DLR)}, the first method that converts continuous latent states into explicit discrete tokens. Inspired by render-based compression, we render textual chains of thought into images, extract visual features, and construct a discrete latent vocabulary via clustering-based fine-tuning. Expanding the vocabulary and output head enables standard autoregressive modeling over both natural language and latent tokens, supporting pretraining alignment, SFT, and RL. Experiments on five reasoning benchmarks and two model series~(Qwen3-VL and LLaMA-3) confirm that \textbf{DLR} outperforms prior latent reasoning baselines with up to \textbf{20$\times$ compression}. Furthermore, the learned latent trajectories retain an interpretable semantic structure. Overall, discrete latent tokens provide a controllable and interpretable basis for efficient latent reasoning. Code is available atthis http URL
  </details>

- **[Beyond Next-Observation Prediction: Agent-Authored World Modeling for Sequential Decision Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25421)**  `arXiv:2606.25421`  `cs.CL`  
  _Guangfeng Cai, Kaibing Yang, Shuo He, Yu Li, Shengtian Yang, Jiaqi Lv, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies on world modeling for Large Language Model (LLM) agents typically formulate the learning objective as next-observation prediction. However, this objective ties supervision to what a transition happens to reveal, which may omit the dynamics most relevant to the agent's current decision. To bridge this gap, we propose Agent-Authored World Modeling (AAWM), a training procedure that constructs supervision from the policy's own decision needs. Specifically, at each state, the agent identifies what it needs to understand about the environment before acting. These needs drive the retrieval of relevant transition evidence across trajectories, which is then synthesized into training targets that capture decision-oriented dynamics instead of reconstructing the next observation. This aligns the training objective with the dynamics the policy needs before acting, not with the contents of the next observation. Experimental results validate the effectiveness of AAWM across multiple environments and training settings. These results show that decision-aware world-model targets provide a more effective learning signal than next-observation prediction.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Self-Reflection: An Execute-Distill-Verify Framework for Agentic Experience Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24428)**  `arXiv:2606.24428`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shiding Zhu, Yudi Qi, Yajie Wang, Jiaze Li, Chao Song, Yaorui Shi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Experience learning lets large language model agents reuse lessons through external memory without updating model parameters. However, reflection can endorse execution errors and store them as future guidance, a failure mode we call the Self-Confirmation Trap. We propose EDV, an Execute-Distill-Verify framework for offline experience construction. Heterogeneous agents independently execute each task; a third-party distiller compares their trajectories and environmental feedback to extract candidate lessons; and the executors separately review each candidate, retaining only unanimously approved lessons. Evaluation uses a single solver retrieving from the frozen memory bank. Experiments on $\tau^2$-bench, Mind2Web, and MMTB show improved revisited-task performance, with additional evidence of within-domain transfer to held-out tasks on $\tau^2$-bench. On an annotated Retail subset, EDV reduces the Self-Confirmation Trap Rate from 11.0% with RB-MaTTS to 5.0%, indicating less retention of erroneous guidance.
  </details>

- **[AutoSpecNER: A Fine-Grained Named Entity Recognition Dataset for Vehicle Specification Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.24387)**  `arXiv:2606.24387`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jordan Lee, Filippos Ventirozos, Abdirahman Abdullahm, Ioanna Nteka, Peter Appleby, Matthew Shardlow_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vehicle advertisements contain rich specification information, but automotive NER resources remain limited.
