We introduce AutoSpecNER, an expert-annotated dataset for fine-grained entity recognition in vehicle listings. The dataset includes 659 advertisements from a popular car-selling website, with over 10,000 entities annotated across 15 categories, including MODEL, ENGINE_SPEC, and BATTERY_CAPACITY. Annotation quality was validated through inter-annotator agreement, achieving an average score of 91.5%. We benchmark rule-based extraction, fine-tuned transformer encoders, and large language models. DeBERTa achieves the best performance with a 90% micro-F1 score, outperforming the rule-based baseline (43%) and the strongest large language model (77.8%).
  </details>

- **[AtomMem: Building Simple and Effective Memory System for LLM Agents via Atomic Facts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19847)**  `arXiv:2606.19847`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yanyu Yao, Shangze Li, Zhi Zheng, Hui Zheng, Qi Liu, Tong Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate strong reasoning and generation abilities, but their fixed context windows limit long-term information accumulation and reuse across multi-session interactions. Existing memory-augmented systems often construct memory in a coarse and unstable manner, relying on inefficient memory representations or unstable unconstrained updates. To address these challenges, we propose AtomMem, a long-term memory system designed for value-dense storage and stable memory evolution. AtomMem introduces a Fact Executor, which selectively extracts high value atomic facts from long form interactions to serve as highly efficient memory representations. Subsequently, AtomMem organizes these facts into hierarchical event structures and temporal profiles, capturing coherent episodic contexts and tracking dynamically evolving user attributes over time. During retrieval, the system activates an associative memory graph to connect fragmented memories. Experiments on the LoCoMo benchmark confirm that AtomMem achieves state-of-the-art performance across various reasoning tasks, offering a scalable and economically viable solution for deploying intelligent personalized agents.
  </details>

- **[External Experience Serving in Production LLM Systems: A Deployment-Oriented Study of Quality-Cost Trade-offs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11806)**  `arXiv:2606.11806`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lin Sun, Heming Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Production LLM systems accumulate reusable operational experience, but the practical deployment issue is not merely whether such experience can help. It is how different serving strategies trade off quality against online cost under realistic constraints. Injecting external experience can improve task quality, yet it also increases prompt burden, latency, and serving pressure. We study \textit{external experience serving} as a deployment-oriented quality-cost trade-off problem. We evaluate this question in a real production moderation setting, with tool-use and GPQA as supporting contrast tasks that expose different output-cost regimes. We compare no-experience baselines, random experience controls, global prompt injection, and retrieval-based selective injection, and analyze both task quality and serving cost. The results show that, once experience becomes case-dependent, selective retrieval provides a stronger operating point than unconditional global injection. They further show that retrieval quality matters more than simply increasing Top-$K$, and that the same serving policy can exhibit substantially different cost-benefit profiles across short-output and decode-heavy regimes. These findings suggest that external experience is best treated as a selective, cost-aware serving decision rather than as a universal add-on. Overall, in the settings studied here, external experience pays off only when both the serving interface and the task-specific cost structure make its quality gains worth the online cost.
  </details>

- **[Contextual Linear Activation Steering of Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.24693)**  `arXiv:2604.24693`  `cs.CL`  
  _Brandon Hsu, Daniel Beaglehole, Adityanarayanan Radhakrishnan, Mikhail Belkin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Linear activation steering is a powerful approach for controlling and specializing large language models by adding linear representations of concepts to their activations at inference. While effective, existing methods often steer with a fixed strength, independent of how much steering any given input requires. In this work, we introduce Contextual Linear Activation Steering (CLAS), which decomposes activation steering into a fixed steering direction and a context-dependent steering coefficient. This decomposition preserves the interpretability of the steering direction while enabling the steering strength to vary with context. It also enables CLAS to perform selective steering: steering multiple behaviors across different portions of a single generation, which cannot be achieved using a fixed steering strength. In addition, across a wide range of steering benchmarks and language models, CLAS consistently outperforms standard linear activation steering and matches or exceeds ReFT and LoRA in settings with limited labeled data. Notably, CLAS achieves this using directions that also serve as reliable concept monitors, a property that directions learned via fine-tuning lack. We therefore propose CLAS as an interpretable and accurate method for controlling and specializing large language models.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty Quantification for LLM Function-Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22985)**  `arXiv:2604.22985`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zihuiwen Ye, Lukas Aichberger, Michael Kirchhof, Sinead Williamson, Luca Zappella, Yarin Gal, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed to autonomously solve real-world tasks. A key ingredient for this is the LLM Function-Calling paradigm, a widely used approach for equipping LLMs with tool-use capabilities. However, an LLM calling functions incorrectly can have severe implications, especially when their effects are irreversible, e.g., transferring money or deleting data. Hence, it is of paramount importance to consider the LLM's confidence that a function call solves the task correctly prior to executing it. Uncertainty Quantification (UQ) methods can be used to quantify this confidence and prevent potentially incorrect function calls. In this work, we present what is, to our knowledge, the first evaluation of UQ methods for LLM Function-Calling (FC). While multi-sample UQ methods, such as Semantic Entropy, show strong performance for natural language Q\&A tasks, we find that in the FC setting the entropy-based multi-sample methods we evaluate offer no clear advantage over simple single-sample UQ methods. Additionally, we find that the particularities of FC outputs can be leveraged to improve the performance of existing UQ methods in this setting. Specifically, multi-sample UQ methods benefit from clustering FC outputs based on their abstract syntax tree parsing, while for single-sample UQ methods, restricting the logit-based score to semantically meaningful tokens leaves discrimination essentially unchanged but improves calibration and, by smaller margins, the utility of the score under abstention.
  </details>

- **[ReflectMT: Internalizing Reflection for Efficient and High-Quality Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19144)**  `arXiv:2604.19144`  `cs.CL`  
  _Kunquan Li, Yingxue Zhang, Zhibin Lan, Fandong Meng, Jinsong Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent years have witnessed growing interest in applying Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) to Machine Translation (MT). While most approaches adopt a 'pre-thinking' paradigm and benefit from explicit reasoning trajectories, they suffer from substantial inference cost and latency. To address these limitations, we propose ReflectMT, a two-stage reflection internalization framework for machine translation that employs a 'post-thinking' paradigm. Our approach develops the model's 'translate-reflect-refine' capability through reinforcement learning. In the first stage, we cultivate the model's capacity for high-quality reflection and refinement, thereby enhancing its semantic comprehension and task-specific knowledge. In the second stage, we train the model to internalize the knowledge acquired during reflection. On WMT24, ReflectMT achieves a GPT-4o-based GRF score of 86.16, outperforming DeepSeek-R1 (84.03) by 2.13 points, while using approximately 92% fewer output tokens. Independent automatic evaluation, a blinded human evaluation, and preliminary experiments on three additional translation directions provide further evidence for the framework's effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[Defragmenting Language Models: An Interpretability-based Approach for Vocabulary Expansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16656)**  `arXiv:2604.16656`  `cs.CL`  
  _Maitrey Mehta, Nishant Subramani, Zhichao Xu, Ashim Gupta, Vivek Srikumar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  All languages are equal; when it comes to tokenization, some are more equal than others. Tokens are the hidden currency that dictates the cost and latency of contemporary LLM access. However, many languages written in non-Latin scripts observe a poor exchange rate: LLMs take several multiples of tokens to encode the same information in many languages as they do for English. We observe that this issue, known as token overfragmentation, persists in modern open-weight LLMs. The standard remedy is vocabulary expansion that adds target language items missing from the model's vocabulary. We focus on two core decisions in the vocabulary expansion process: What items should we add? and How should we initialize their corresponding input and output embeddings? We comprehensively study and advance the new research direction of interpretability-based vocabulary expansion along these two core decisions, making four key contributions: First, we question the conventional use of frequency-based methods to choose candidate vocabulary items to add (a decision long treated as settled), and show that interpretability-based methods offer a superior performance-token efficiency tradeoff. Second, we strengthen the case for interpretability-based embedding initialization by showing large gains (~20 pts) over baseline initialization methods for several languages written in non-Latin scripts. Third, we identify the phenomenon of "subword detokenization," where models progressively merge fragmented subword tokens into larger subwords across layers. Fourth, we propose FragMend, a method that leverages this phenomenon to push the efficiency ceiling of interpretability-based expansion, and validate its effectiveness.
  </details>

- **[\textit{Versteasch du mi?} Computational and Socio-Linguistic Perspectives on GenAI, LLMs, and Non-Standard Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.28213)**  `arXiv:2603.28213`  `cs.CL`  
  _Verena Platzgummer, John McCrae, Sina Ahmadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The design of Large Language Models (LLMs) and generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) has been shown to be "unfair" to less-spoken languages (Petrov et al., 2023) and to deepen the digital language divide (Bella et al., 2023). Critical sociolinguistic work has also argued that these technologies are not only made possible by prior sociohistorical processes of linguistic standardisation, often grounded in European nationalist and colonial projects (Migge and Schneider, 2025), but also exacerbate epistemologies of language as "monolithic, monolingual, syntactically standardized systems of meaning" (Schneider, 2024, p. 5). In our paper, we draw on earlier work on the intersections of technology and language policy (Kelly-Holmes, 2019) and bring our respective expertise in critical sociolinguistics and computational linguistics to bear on an interrogation of these arguments. We take two different complexes of non-standard linguistic varieties in our respective repertoires-South Tyrolean dialects, which are widely used in informal communication in South Tyrol, Italy (Alber et al., 2024), as well as varieties of Kurdish-as starting points to an interdisciplinary exploration of the intersections between GenAI and linguistic variation and standardisation. We discuss both how LLMs can be made to deal with non-standard language from a technical perspective, and whether, when or how this can contribute to "democratic and decolonial digital and machine learning strategies" (Migge and Schneider, 2025, p. 12), which has direct policy implications.
  </details>

- **[EntropyCache: Decoded Token Entropy Guided KV Caching for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18489)**  `arXiv:2603.18489`  `cs.CL`  
  _Minsoo Cheong, Donghyun Son, Woosang Lim, Sungjoo Yoo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) rely on bidirectional attention, which prevents lossless KV caching and requires a full forward pass at every denoising step. Existing approximate KV caching methods reduce this cost by selectively updating cached states, but their decision overhead scales with context length or model depth. We propose EntropyCache, a training-free KV caching method that uses the maximum entropy of newly decoded token distributions as a constant-cost signal for deciding when to recompute. Our design is grounded in two empirical observations: (1) decoded token entropy correlates with KV cache drift, providing a cheap proxy for cache staleness, and (2) feature volatility of decoded tokens persists for multiple steps after unmasking, motivating recomputation of the $k$ most recently decoded tokens. The skip-or-recompute decision requires only $O(V)$ computation per step, independent of context length and model scale. Experiments on LLaDA-8B-Instruct and Dream-7B-Instruct show that EntropyCache achieves $15.2\times$-$26.4\times$ speedup on standard benchmarks and $22.4\times$-$24.1\times$ on chain-of-thought benchmarks, with competitive accuracy and decision overhead accounting for only $0.5\%$ of inference time. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Echoes as Anchors: Probabilistic Costs and Attention Refocusing in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06600)**  `arXiv:2602.06600`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhuoyuan Hao, Zhuo Li, Wu Li, Fangming Liu, Min Zhang, Jing Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work improves reasoning in large reasoning models (LRMs) by test-time scaling through self-consistency and parallel thinking, adding generic "thinking tokens", or presenting the question twice, as in re-reading (RE2). However, these approaches either inject task-agnostic tokens or mandate heuristics that do not explain, and often ignore, the spontaneous repetition that many LRMs exhibit at the head of their long chain-of-thought (CoT). In this work, we investigate the model's tendency to restate the question, which we dub Echo of Prompt (EOP), as a mechanism that shapes computation from the start and can be viewed as prompt repetition inside the reasoning trace rather than in the input. We formalize its probabilistic cost by casting echo removal as rejection sampling, and define the Echo Likelihood Gap as a computable proxy linking early repetition to likelihood gains and downstream accuracy. We find that (1) in a causal intervention, reinserting an echo into failed GSM8K traces raises exact match by 7.9-10.4 points for two reasoning models but not for a non-reasoning base model; and (2) likelihood analyses controlling for length and suffix indicate that EOP's benefits go beyond verbosity, while layer-wise attention studies show that EOP increases attention from answer tokens to the answer prefix in middle layers, consistent with an attention refocusing mechanism. Building on these findings, we develop Echo-Distilled SFT (ED-SFT) to instill echoing before reasoning via supervised fine-tuning, and training-free Echoic Prompting (EP) to ground the model on the prompt midway through the trace. We evaluate on GSM8K, MathQA, Hendrycks-MATH, AIME24, and MATH-500 under identical decoding settings and budgets, and observe gains over baselines, including the insertion of thinking tokens, in most settings. Our code is publicly released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement World Model Learning for LLM-based Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.05842)**  `arXiv:2602.05842`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiao Yu, Baolin Peng, Ruize Xu, Yelong Shen, Pengcheng He, Suman Nath, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance in language-centric tasks. However, in agentic settings, LLMs often struggle to anticipate action consequences and adapt to environment dynamics, highlighting the need for world-modeling capabilities in LLM-based agents. We propose Reinforcement World Model Learning (RWML), a self-supervised method that learns action-conditioned world models for LLM-based agents on textual states using sim-to-real gap rewards. Our method aligns simulated next states produced by the model with realized next states observed from the environment, encouraging consistency between internal world simulations and actual environment dynamics in a pre-trained embedding space. Unlike next-state token prediction, which prioritizes token-level fidelity (i.e., reproducing exact wording) over semantic equivalence and can lead to model collapse, our method provides a more robust training signal and is empirically less susceptible to reward hacking than LLM-as-a-judge. We evaluate our method on ALFWorld and $\tau^2$ Bench and observe significant gains over the base model, despite using no expert data, task-success rewards, or strong-LLM annotations - only an off-the-shelf embedding model as a distance metric. When combined with task-success rewards, our method outperforms direct task-success reward RL by 6.9 and 5.7 points on ALFWorld and $\tau^2$ Bench respectively; it further outperforms expert-data training on ALFWorld and is comparable to it on $\tau^2$ Bench.
  </details>

- **[Measuring Affinity between Attention-Head Weight Subspaces via the Projection Kernel](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.10266)**  `arXiv:2601.10266`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hiroaki Yamagiwa, Yusuke Takase, Hidetoshi Shimodaira_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding relationships between attention heads is essential for interpreting the internal structure of Transformers, yet existing metrics do not capture this structure well. We focus on the subspaces spanned by attention-head weight matrices and quantify head-to-head relationships using the Projection Kernel (PK), a principal-angle-based measure of subspace similarity. Experiments on GPT2-small, BLOOM-560M, and Qwen2-0.5B-Instruct show that PK reproduces known head-to-head interactions on the IOI task more clearly than prior metrics such as the Composition Score. We further introduce a framework to quantify the informativeness of PK distributions by comparing them with a reference distribution derived from random orthogonal subspaces. As an application, we analyze a directed graph constructed from PK and show that, in GPT2-small, L4H7 acts as a hub by functioning as an Identity Head.
  </details>

- **[Latent Debate: A Surrogate Framework for Interpreting LLM Thinking towards Binary Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01909)**  `arXiv:2512.01909`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lihu Chen, Xiang Yin, Francesca Toni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding the internal `thinking' process of Large Language Models (LLMs) and %how this relates their connections to hallucinations remains a key challenge. To this end, we introduce \emph{latent debate}, a structured surrogate framework for interpreting model outputs on True/False prediction tasks through the lens of internal latent arguments and interactions amongst them. Unlike human debates, latent debate captures the hidden supporting and attacking signals that arise within a model during a single inference. We first present a model- and task-agnostic conceptual framework, and then instantiate it symbolically to approximate the thinking process of LLMs towards binary decisions. Empirical studies demonstrate that our latent debate is a faithful structured surrogate model that has highly consistent predictions with the original LLM, while providing a form of interpretability. We also demonstrate that our latent debate provides a strong baseline for hallucination detection. Specifically, we identify strong correlations between debate patterns and hallucinations, such as a high degree of disagreement in the middle layers of the latent debate surrogate is linked to a higher risk of hallucinations. Our findings suggest that latent debate provides a new framework for understanding the internal processes of LLMs.
  </details>

- **[MBBQ: A Dataset for Cross-Lingual Comparison of Stereotypes in Generative LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07243)**  `arXiv:2406.07243`  `cs.CL`  
  _Vera Neplenbroek, Arianna Bisazza, Raquel Fernández_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative large language models (LLMs) have been shown to exhibit harmful biases and stereotypes. While safety fine-tuning typically takes place in English, if at all, these models are being used by speakers of many different languages. There is existing evidence that the performance of these models is inconsistent across languages and that they discriminate based on demographic factors of the user. Motivated by this, we investigate whether the social stereotypes exhibited by LLMs differ as a function of the language used to prompt them, while controlling for cultural differences and task accuracy. To this end, we present MBBQ (Multilingual Bias Benchmark for Question-answering), a carefully curated version of the English BBQ dataset extended to Dutch, Spanish, and Turkish, which measures stereotypes commonly held across these languages. We further complement MBBQ with a parallel control dataset to measure task performance on the question-answering task independently of bias. Our results based on several open-source and proprietary LLMs confirm that some non-English languages suffer from bias more than English, even when controlling for cultural shifts. Moreover, we observe significant cross-lingual differences in bias behaviour for all except the most accurate models. With the release of MBBQ, we hope to encourage further research on bias in multilingual settings. The dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FedReady: Bounded Agents for Federation Formation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03586)**  `arXiv:2611.03586`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Ziyue Xu, Holger R. Roth, Chester Chen, Peter Cnudde_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Federated learning (FL) runtimes standardize aggregation and deployment, but each new study still requires federation formation: selecting task-eligible sites and preparing their local data and task code for federated training or analysis. This preparation recurs when the target changes, while raw records and site-private mappings are excluded from cross-site FL messages. We present FedReady, a system that performs federation formation with bounded LLM agents. Agents propose shared contracts, local adapters, and task code; the runtime owns workflow state and accepts artifacts only after checks tied to their digests. Candidate site discovery prioritizes recall; local eligibility verification checks task support before site admission. Once participating sites and artifacts are fixed, conventional federated training or analysis proceeds without readiness agents. Across 39 simulated retinal sites and four tasks, discovery reaches recall 1.00 at precision 0.65; local eligibility verification removes all 17 false positives without losing an eligible site-task pair. The participating sites supply 45,575 task-record instances and complete four 100-round jobs, including one with a runtime-generated contract. Across ten patient cohorts, preparation supports seven patient populations and holds endpoints at three sources as unsupported. Comparison with manually prepared reference populations and analysis (the manual reference) gives a maximum survival-curve difference of 0.001133. We also analyze 19 development failures, targeted classification ablations, and standalone and heterogeneous agent configurations. These results establish the feasibility of task-specific data preparation before ordinary FL training or analysis.
  </details>

- **[CONFIRM: Governing Scientific Reporting for Trustworthy Agentic Neuroimaging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01449)**  `arXiv:2611.01449`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Yuxiang Wei, Lijing Zhu, Qizhen Lan, Yanteng Zhang, Vince D. Calhoun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents can autonomously run neuroimaging analyses from research questions to reported findings. Existing agentic systems use tools to check that code runs and reported values match its output, but rely on LLM judgment to decide which results warrant scientific reporting. We show that LLM judges endorse site-confounded findings even when the confound is presented as evidence. We present \textbf{CONFIRM}, a framework that separates running an analysis from deciding which findings to report. CONFIRM encodes each hypothesis as a machine-readable, pre-specified analysis plan that fixes the claim and its pass criteria before computing results. It then filters results through five deterministic gates (search history and multiplicity, confounds, power, analytic stability, and replication) under three configurable reporting standards, from least to most demanding: \textit{Screen}, \textit{Replicate}, and \textit{Full}. Failed claims receive diagnoses that guide follow-up analyses. To evaluate reporting decisions at the claim level, we introduce \textbf{NeuroClaimBench}, comprising 489 executable neuroimaging claims with frozen data, including literature-supported findings and 150 controls designed to violate individual methodological requirements. On NeuroClaimBench, CONFIRM falsely supports at most 3\% of controls, compared with 23--38\% for the baselines, while recovering up to 61\% of literature-supported claims. When auditing an existing agent, CONFIRM reduces false support on controls from 23\% to 0\% while retaining the agent's literature-supported claims. Its diagnostic feedback also produces more supported revisions than Self-Refine with fewer LLM calls. Together, CONFIRM and NeuroClaimBench show that basing reporting decisions on pre-specified analysis plans and deterministic gates is essential for trustworthy autonomous scientific discovery.
  </details>

- **[I-PTC: Interactive Programmatic Tool Calling for Stateful Tool-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01446)**  `arXiv:2611.01446`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Huanzhi Mao, Chengkun Cao, Shuo Yuan, Joseph E. Gonzalez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly capable of writing and executing code, suggesting a natural paradigm for tool-augmented agents: expressing tool use as programs rather than isolated calls. Programmatic tool calling (PTC) captures this idea through loops, batching, abstraction, and structured computation, but existing PTC approaches are largely stateless, limiting their ability to reuse intermediate results and adapt over time. This is problematic for real-world tasks, where tool environments and problem-solving processes are inherently stateful. We therefore present I-PTC, an interactive framework that executes stateful PTC: models issue code snippets that call tools, maintain reusable state, and evolve the tool interface through API corrections. To evaluate the effectiveness of I-PTC and the general PTC setting, we introduce PTC-Bench, a benchmark of long-tail, production-style user requests whose difficulty comes from large initial environments, many dependent API interactions, extended planning, and recovery across follow-ups and transient failures. Experiments on PTC-Bench show that I-PTC improves performance on complex multi-step tasks with large tool sets while substantially reducing token consumption over conventional tool-calling baselines and the original PTC baseline, highlighting stateful programmatic tool calling as a promising direction for scalable tool-augmented agents.
  </details>

- **[Trail: Accurate Local Smart Home Control Using Decision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00610)**  `arXiv:2611.00610`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Maleeha Masood, Deepak Vasisht, Indranil Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural-language driven smart home control remains challenging: cloud-hosted Large Language Models (LLMs) raise privacy concerns, while local Small Language Models (SLMs) can generate invalid actions or misinterpret requests. We introduce Trail (Typed Registry-Anchored Inference for Language commands in Smart Homes), a fully local framework that replaces open-ended command generation with hierarchical typed decisions. Trail exploits the natural structure of a home's action space, selecting a room, device, and operation from candidates derived from the runtime device registry. This ensures valid actions while enabling confidence-based abstention when requests cannot be reliably resolved. We also design a structured memory module that uses typed decisions to support user preferences, device references, and multi-turn interactions. To evaluate Trail, we extend HomeBench and generate three datasets: Trek-Base, which tests natural-language variation; Trek-Robust, which evaluates robustness to changes in the home and its description; and Trek-Memory, which assesses conversational memory across multi-turn interactions. Trail achieves 85.3% accuracy on Trek-Robust versus 69.8% for a finetuned Qwen3.5-4B baseline, while remaining competitive on Trek-Base requests. With memory, Trail resolves 75.6% of the requests that depend on earlier turns, compared with 42.5% for the finetuned baseline given conversation history.
  </details>

- **[Hacking or Just Failing? A More Capable LLM Monitor Better Separates Reward Hacking from Honest Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00407)**  `arXiv:2611.00407`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Kaushik Kezendirane_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent's visible score can exceed its real progress because it gamed the score or because it simply made mistakes, so a useful reward-hacking monitor must tell the two apart. We built a sandboxed task in which an agent can game a completion score, labelled every episode by replaying it and repairing the final state counterfactually, and ran five pre-registered studies. On 60 episodes from a Llama 3.1 8B agent, small local monitors (Llama 3B and 8B) did not separate exploit attempts from honest failures with the same score gap: prompt changes traded missed exploits for false alarms, and the pre-registered primary monitor reached AUC 0.64. On identical inputs, Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite, which completed 9/10 clean tasks against 2/10 for the 8B model, met our pre-registered criterion (AUC 0.80, 95% interval [0.66, 0.93]; 1/35 honest failures flagged); with only 14 exploit episodes, this estimate is uncertain. On an exploit that no prompt names, Gemini usually described the action correctly yet never flagged it, in 5 and then 25 new episodes, even when manipulation was defined by its effect on the score. Given the full trace, the 8B monitor flagged all 25 replication episodes and none of 25 honest look-alikes, but its explanations described actions that had not occurred; given additional structured evidence, it also flagged 15 of the 25 look-alikes. A two-rule deterministic check caught every known exploit. We release the environment, labels, pre-registrations and per-judgement logs.
  </details>

- **[Aligned Data Can Induce Misalignment via Context Confusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38379)**  `arXiv:2609.38379`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yavuz Bakman, Duygu Nur Yaldiz, Baris Askin, Swastik Roy, Morteza Ziyadi, Salman Avestimehr, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are frequently updated for various use cases, where filtering out misaligned training samples is a common practice for preventing post-update misalignment. However, alignment is inherently context-dependent: a behavior that is aligned in one context may be inappropriate in another. For example, recommending that researchers retain their data for reproducibility is aligned, whereas recommending that a mobile app retain users' sensitive data may violate users' privacy. Starting from this observation, we identify a posttraining phenomenon where aligned training induces misaligned behavior in other contexts. We call this phenomenon context confusion. We demonstrate context confusion across three domains: (1) Gender Equality, (2) Privacy, and (3) Physical Safety. Unlike the broad misalignment seen in emergent misalignment, context confusion causes domain-specific misalignment that evades general alignment evaluations. Further, it is not effectively reduced by injecting general alignment training data, but can be substantially reduced by including targeted alignment data for the misaligned domain or providing in-context learning examples during inference. Lastly, we provide a mechanistic explanation of context confusion. We observe that queries from different domains can undergo similar representational shifts during the fine-tuning. Consequently, a query from a different domain may activate the same behavioral feature learned during fine-tuning, which causes the behavior to transfer to a context where it is misaligned. Based on our findings, we argue that it is difficult to predict the alignment state of a model after training by inspecting the training data alone, which highlights the importance of comprehensive post-training alignment evaluations.
  </details>

- **[Distance Is Not Enough: Forget-Retain Alignment Gap Predicts LLM Relearning Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25429)**  `arXiv:2608.25429`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yi Chen, Hanna Hsieh, Shuhong Liu, Chuanbo Hua, Zihan Ma, Kun Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Machine unlearning aims to make a model forget specific data, yet unlearned LLMs often fail to stay unlearned: brief fine-tuning can revive removed knowledge. Existing robustness predictors rely on global weight-space displacement, but distance alone can be misleading when random or destructive updates collapse performance. We argue that relearning robustness depends on update structure: robust unlearning should affect forget-critical weights while sparing retain-critical ones. We introduce the Forget-Retain Alignment Gap (FRAG), a training-free predictor that scores an update's forget-retain alignment without running a relearning attack, and separates selective from dense updates more reliably than global distance. Building on the forget-critical, retain-sparing principle, Forget-Retain Pruning (FRP) improves relearning robustness. Our results suggest that weight selectivity better explains robustness than distance alone. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[OpenFinGym: A Verifiable Multi-Task Gym Environment for Evaluating Quant Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26350)**  `arXiv:2606.26350`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Kaicheng Zhang, Wen Ge, Lei Jiang, Weixin Yang, Jordan Langham-Lopez, Jialin Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although large language model agents are increasingly applied to quantitative-finance workflows, their evaluation remains fragmented across isolated tasks, while the financial relevance of benchmark tasks is often overlooked. Yet financial workflows are inherently multi-stage, spanning interdependent tasks such as forecasting, strategy construction, risk management, and trading. Existing platforms typically focus on a single task, and can therefore overstate agent competence and fail to reveal weaknesses in generalization, real-market interaction, and financially meaningful decision-making. We introduce OpenFinGym, a unified gym environment for quantitative-finance agent development that covers forecasting, market generation, real-time trading, and fraud detection under a single execution and verification interface. OpenFinGym additionally provides an automated task-construction pipeline that turns quantitative finance publications into executable task packages; a containerised runtime with a host-side verifier service that supports scalable agent rollouts and prevents runtime train-test leakage; a paper trading engine with a low-latency data-stream design; deferred-resolution support for long-horizon and event-market forecasts; and integration for SFT and RL post-training.
  </details>

- **[Harnessing Agent Skills: Architectural Patterns and a Reference Architecture for Skill-Mediated LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20631)**  `arXiv:2606.20631`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Boming Xia, Liming Zhu, Zhenchang Xing, Qinghua Lu, Dino Sejdinovic, Xiwei Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  CONTEXT: Agent skills package reusable behavioural guidance and resources that large language model agents load or apply during a run. Within the agent harness, agent skill harnessing comprises connected architectural responsibilities that govern which skills enter a run, which actions they can cause, and how their use is recorded and informs revision. OBJECTIVES: We identify architectural patterns for agent skill harnessing and organise their responsibilities into a reference architecture that makes these connections explicit. METHODS: A multivocal literature review covered 36 practitioner systems and 51 academic papers; we consolidated practices into patterns and derived the reference architecture from patterns, source evidence, and architectural reasoning. We evaluated the reference architecture by mapping eight systems and applying it in Deep Agents and Hermes Agent. RESULTS: Ten patterns cover skill supply and activation, skill declarations and execution authority, verification and recording of skill use, and run evidence feeding repair and governed revision. The reference architecture organises their responsibilities into four layers (Supply Chain, Mediation, Execution Control, Evidence & Feedback) and two cross-cutting substrates, separating permission determination from enforcement and routing persistent revisions through acceptance. Mapping eight systems showed the first three layers broadly realised and 14 of 15 partial or unestablished ratings in Evidence \& Feedback. Applying the architecture in Deep Agents and Hermes Agent turned requirements into checks that exposed a shell route outside the file-tool rules and a preview--apply mismatch. CONCLUSION: The patterns and reference architecture support comparing implementations and turning requirements into responsibility allocations and checks.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Safe Data: Pretraining-Stage Alignment with Regular Safety Reflection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19168)**  `arXiv:2606.19168`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jinhan Li, Kexian Tang, Yihan Xu, Zhuorui Ye, Kaifeng Lyu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To achieve deeper safety alignment for large language models (LLMs), recent efforts have studied how to push safety interventions earlier into the pretraining stage, primarily by filtering unsafe data or rewriting it into safer forms. We argue that pretraining-stage alignment should go beyond making the data safe: LLMs may generalize unsafe behaviors from seemingly benign pretraining data. To this end, we propose Safety Reflection Pretraining, a pretraining-stage alignment method which regularly inserts short safety reflections between sentences in pretraining corpora. This integrates self-monitoring directly into language modeling, establishing a foundational capability that is subsequently reinforced by compatible post-training. Our experiments with 1.7B models pretrained on FineWeb-Edu show that Safety Reflection Pretraining improves safety classification accuracy and substantially reduces the success rates of inference-stage and fine-tuning attacks. Complementary to our real-world experiments, we also introduce a fully controlled synthetic environment, MedSafetyWorld, with a clear definition of safety and a reasoning structure under which we find that models can easily generalize unsafe behaviors from safe data. Ablations in MedSafetyWorld further demonstrate a clear advantage of Safety Reflection Pretraining over data filtering and rewriting in preventing models from acting on unsafe behaviors. Taken together, our findings suggest that pretraining alignment should not only make the training data safe, but also shape the behaviors that models are likely to acquire from safe data.
  </details>

- **[When Does Multi-Agent RL Improve LLM Workflows? Workflow, Scale, and Policy-Sharing Tradeoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24202)**  `arXiv:2605.24202`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yifan Zeng, Yiran Wu, Yaolun Zhang, Wentian Zhao, Kun Wan, Qingyun Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Outcome-reward RL can train every role of a multi-agent LLM workflow. Its benefit is hard to predict because the reward assigns no credit to individual roles and a shared policy trades pooled data against specialization. We ask when this training improves a workflow over its base model and single-agent RL, and what each role learns. We train three workflows (Eval-Opt, Voting, Orch-Workers) with GRPO on math and code at three Qwen3 scales, with one policy for all roles (Shared-Policy, SP) or one per role type (Isolated-Policy, IP). Every workflow gains by adapting to its structure; this does not make a role better than training it alone. Single-agent RL in the same structure matches or beats trained Voting and Orch-Workers, and within the grid only Eval-Opt under IP exceeds it. Eval-Opt's evaluator learns when to retry and its generator learns to complete the retried attempts, a joint strategy that a single policy trained alone does not acquire. Under SP, per-output token averaging sets each role's gradient share, a hidden hyperparameter absent from single-agent RL, and the solving roles learn slowly; reweighting it gives SP the speed and peak of IP, and its decline. Under IP, a parallel role, whose instances share one adapter, takes one optimizer step per instance per training step and can drift after the peak as its entropy rises and accuracy declines. Merging those steps delays the decline.Our code is at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[ZenBrain: A Neuroscience-Inspired 7-Layer Memory Architecture for Autonomous AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23878)**  `arXiv:2604.23878`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Alexander Bering_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  ZenBrain is a seven-layer, neuroscience-derived architecture for agent memory in LLM agents. An open library holds the seven layers, a MemoryCoordinator and nine foundational algorithms; six Predictive Memory Architecture components run in a private production system. In the open library at version 0.5.1, decay acts only on working memory and nothing is replayed.Ablating one component at a time exposes an effect we call cooperative masking. The ablation runs in a simulation harness that switches fifteen components as stand-ins at simulated values, not as production code. Under moderate load, fourteen of the fifteen ablations look costless. Raising decay, the horizon, the corpus and the queries, with no change to the components, moves ten of the fifteen in the same direction on all ten seeds - nine losses (Delta-Q down to -93.7%) and one gain. With ten seeds this is the one p-value the normal approximation of the Wilcoxon test can reach (5.06e-3), and it does not pass the Bonferroni-corrected level for fifteen contrasts (3.33e-3).On LongMemEval_S (500 questions), the evaluated ZenBrain arm - a Python port of a scoring class from our test harness, not the seven-layer system - answers 47.7% of the questions correctly under a binary judge (95% interval [43.3, 52.1]). With the compared systems' adapters repaired, Letta reaches 48.4% and Mem0 47.2%. ZenBrain reaches 91.3% of a full-context baseline's accuracy with 1/109.7 of its input tokens in the answer step; building the memory is not counted. Of nine head-to-head comparisons of the retrieved context (3 competitors x 3 LLM judges), three hold at Bonferroni-corrected significance, all against A-Mem, and the remaining six show no significant difference.On LoCoMo, the routing results of version 8 came from placeholder embeddings; with text-embedding-3-small, the flat variant (F1 0.059) lies above the full system (0.058).
  </details>

- **[Health-SCORE: Improving Open-Ended Health LLMs Using Scalable Rubric-Based Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.18706)**  `arXiv:2601.18706`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhichao Yang, Sepehr Janghorbani, Dongxu Zhang, Jun Han, Qian Qian, Andrew Ressler II, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning is a powerful approach for improving language models, but applying it to open-ended domains such as healthcare remains challenging because high-quality responses are often multidimensional, context-dependent, and difficult to capture with a single reward signal. Rubrics offer a natural form of supervision by providing a structured representation of the criteria that define a strong response. Existing rubric-based approaches, however, often rely on instance-specific criteria, creating two important limitations: (1) such rubrics are typically unavailable for unseen examples at test time; and (2) constructing new rubrics for every instance incurs substantial reward design costs. We introduce Health-SCORE, a scalable framework that captures recurring principles of response quality in the form of reusable meta-rubrics. Rather than relying on manually constructed instance-level criteria, Health-SCORE captures generalizable principles that can be applied across examples, tasks, and healthcare datasets. These meta-rubrics support two forms of model improvement: they can serve as structured, safety-aware reward signals for reinforcement learning during post-training, and they can be incorporated directly into prompts to guide model behavior at inference time. Across several open-ended healthcare benchmarks, models trained with Health-SCORE achieve performance competitive with models trained using human-authored instance-level rewards, while substantially reducing reward-design costs and improving generalizability.
  </details>

- **[Taurus: LLM-in-the-Loop CT Annotation Matches Coding Agents at Half the Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00247)**  `arXiv:2611.00247`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Bing Hu, Rishabh Sharma, Jatin Mehta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dense labels of the aorta in CT are slow to draw, and the public aortic CT datasets that could stand in for them each label different structures under their own conventions. Large language model (LLM) agents can now write code and read images. We asked whether such a model labels these scans better as a free-form agent that writes its own segmentation code, or inside a harness, where an automated pass labels the scan and the model reviews the result. We built a harness, Taurus, and compared it with free-form agents by blind label recovery: we withheld the human labels of six public datasets (939 volumes) and scored every approach against each dataset's own classes. In the first version of Taurus, the model's review raised the primary metric on only two of six datasets, by less than the noise between two reviews of the same case. A free-form agent running Claude Opus 5.5 scored higher on most datasets. Moving what the free-form agents did well into the automated pass then carried most of the gain on 38 held-out cases. The final version smooths the label surfaces with a fixed rule and checks for the large errors the automated pass tends to make, such as an aneurysm drawn to half its size. Each check that fires puts a multiple-choice question to the model, with candidate corrections drawn on the scan. It matched the surface smoothness of the human references, and in the primary metric we found no difference from the free-form Opus agent, at under half its cost, $1.22 against $2.71 per case. Most of what made Taurus competitive came from the harness and its automation rather than from the language model itself. Taurus shows that this kind of annotation can be automated, so that expert time can move from drawing labels to checking them.
  </details>

- **[MemU-Bench: Benchmarking GUI Agents on Long-Horizon User Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00191)**  `arXiv:2611.00191`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Yizhou Liu, Zhengxi Lu, Fei Tang, Yiwen Qiu, Yong Du, Tongbo Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing Graphical User Interface (GUI) benchmarks predominantly rely on precise and stateless user instructions. In everyday usage, however, instructions are often \emph{vague and history-dependent}: users omit the exact details of their past operations because they no longer remember them, leaving the agent to recover the missing context from long and noisy interaction histories. To bridge this evaluation gap, we introduce \textbf{MemU-Bench}, a mobile benchmark of \textbf{118 vague tasks} grounded in 29 long-horizon user trajectories, where agents must distill raw interaction streams into memory and retrieve the missing context on demand, with task outcomes verified against serialized application databases. Alongside Success Rate, we incorporate Interaction Efficiency (IE) and Success weighted by Path Length (SPL) to expose agents that succeed by exhaustively searching across different applications rather than by \emph{genuinely recalling} the history. Evaluations show that without user history, state-of-the-art systems struggle under such ambiguity: the best-performing Gemini-3.1-Pro achieves an SPL of only 16.4\%, and end-to-end GUI agents fall below 5\%. To address this, we further propose \textbf{AppMem}, a training-free framework that distills user history into a \emph{structured, app-centric} memory bank, tracks interrupted intents across application switches, and selectively retrieves relevant memories via target-app inference. AppMem raises the SPL of GPT-5.5 from 15.7\% to 66.9\% (\textbf{+51.2\%}), outperforms a naive summary baseline by 9.3\%, and delivers consistent gains across evaluated backbones.
  </details>

- **[Optimize the Harness, Not the Model: Fitting a Minimal Agent to a Self-Hostable LLM for Investigative Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03434)**  `arXiv:2611.03434`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Shai Nahum Gefen, Vered Chassida, Dmitry Basin, Guy Feigenblat_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Government intelligence teams need LLM agents that can analyze large, messy evidence collections, but security and cost constraints often rule out hosted frontier models and broad, hard-to-audit tool surfaces. We report CLEO: Constrained Loop for Environment Optimization, a method for fitting a minimal agent harness to a fixed, self-hostable LLM. Given an expert-validated dataset, CLEO admits only the tools, guardrails, memory policies, and loop components that improve accuracy while remaining general and auditable. On our held-out dataset, the CLEO harness reaches an 83.8% pass rate, within 1.2 points of the strongest out-of-the-box Claude Code SDK baseline, while being two orders of magnitude smaller.
  </details>

- **[Self-Evolving Agentic Workflows via Semantic Decomposition of Complex Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01840)**  `arXiv:2611.01840`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Zichuan Fu, Junjia Qi, Xian Wu, Wenlin Zhang, Yimin Deng, Kaifeng Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic workflows organize complex tasks into reusable procedures. However, invoking an LLM at every step incurs repeated inference costs and delays. We propose a framework for self-evolving agentic workflows through semantic task decomposition. Each subtask has explicit applicability conditions and a procedure that executes code or invokes a model as needed. An agent uses execution feedback to refine the decomposition and update these procedures. Experiments in the GLEE competition show the framework on bargaining, negotiation and persuasion. Most observed final-decision procedures are implemented in code. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[ParetoSaddler: Pareto-Guided Optimization of Agent Harnesses for Quality and Cost](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02079)**  `arXiv:2611.02079`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Xingzhi Qian, Sungho Park, Rongyuan Tan, Jianbo Yu, Jue Zhang, Lorenzo Cavallaro, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Harness optimization can substantially improve LLM agents without modifying model weights, but existing methods primarily optimize response quality. Extending them to joint quality--cost optimization introduces a challenge: applying the deployment preference during intermediate updates can prematurely reject candidates that are valuable for later improvements. We introduce ParetoSaddler, which addresses this mismatch by separating intermediate candidate promotion from final deployment selection. At each iteration, an optimizer updates a parent harness to produce a candidate, and then compares the two over repeated executions and uses Bayesian bootstrap Pareto support to promote candidates showing credible progress in either quality or cost. Promoted candidates receive broader development evaluation and remain available as parents for later optimization steps. We evaluate ParetoSaddler on ClawMark and GAIA2 using domain-disjoint splits. On ClawMark, ParetoSaddler improves quality from 70.86\% to 81.35\% while reducing cost from \$1.17 to \$1.01 per task; on GAIA2, it improves quality from 56.22\% to 62.89\% while reducing cost from \$0.78 to \$0.45 per task. The Pareto-selected harnesses also achieve higher quality at lower cost than quality-only harness optimization. Analysis shows that Pareto-aware optimization retains intermediate candidates that would be discarded by deployment preference but can support later improvements, while Bayesian bootstrap screening filters out candidates whose gains are weakly supported across runs.
  </details>

- **[Variable names provide priors: semantic grounding enables data-efficient numerical prediction by large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02637)**  `arXiv:2611.02637`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Xin-Rui Ma, Shi-Zhi Chen, Hai-Yan Jiang, De-Cheng Feng, Michael Beer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have achieved strong performance across diverse tasks, from question answering to code generation. Recent studies have found that LLMs can perform numerical prediction on tabular datasets via in-context learning. However, it remains unclear under what conditions and how LLMs use variable names and in-context examples for numerical prediction. Here we show that semantic grounding, the match between variable names and the values they describe, allows five LLMs without task-specific training to match or outperform seven supervised machine learning (ML) models across 20 datasets from nine scientific domains, particularly when examples are scarce. Reasoning traces indicate that variable names allow LLMs to draw on domain-specific knowledge acquired during pretraining. Using controlled tasks based on physical formulas, we further show that LLMs integrate domain-specific knowledge with in-context examples in a way consistent with Bayesian updating. Specifically, when the relationship between the inputs and the target cannot be inferred from the examples alone, LLMs rely on domain-specific knowledge as the prior. Conversely, as contradicting examples accumulate, LLM predictions shift progressively towards these examples, which act as the likelihood. Our work reveals how LLMs use domain-specific knowledge in numerical prediction and positions them as data-efficient predictors for data-scarce scientific domains.
  </details>

- **[Wrong but Useful: Trajectory Value Beyond Answer Correctness in Multi-Agent Messages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14375)**  `arXiv:2608.14375`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Chih-Hsuan Yang, Anjir Ahmed Chowdhury, Cheng-Hau Yang, Weijian Zheng, Fernando Llorente, Xiaolong Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A solution can end with a wrong answer and still help another solver. In multi-agent reasoning, evaluating only a message's proposed answer can therefore miss its contribution to the receiver. We measure this contribution as trajectory value: the change in a fixed integrator's probability of answering correctly when a cached message is present rather than removed. Controlled replay across five benchmarks and two model families separates message effects from identical-input variation. The strongest evidence is for Gemma: in a pre-registered fresh-call re-test, 34 of 73 selected wrong-answer messages pass individual confirmation, exceeding control-calibrated upper bounds on expected false confirmations of 11.4--20.8. The finding is benchmark-dependent and the conservative criterion no longer passes without SciBench. A blinded two-author audit supports the selected answer labels, while neutral replacements show that the helpful effect is not explained solely by message length and position. On held-out replay blocks of the same problems, a message-specific removal rule improves accuracy over the strongest tested correctness-only rule by 1.61 percentage points [1.01, 2.25]. Post hoc analysis localizes most of this gap to pools containing no correct proposal. Evidence for gpt-oss is weaker, and the tested predictors do not establish a practical way to identify useful errors in advance. The results support measuring answer correctness and receiver benefit separately, with value understood as conditional on the message pool, receiver and generation format.
  </details>

- **[When Should LLMs Be Less Specific? Selective Abstraction for Reliable Long-Form Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11908)**  `arXiv:2602.11908`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shani Goren, Ido Galil, Ran El-Yaniv_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs are widely used, yet they remain prone to factual errors that erode trust and limit adoption in high-risk settings. One approach to mitigate this risk is to equip models with uncertainty estimation mechanisms that abstain when confidence is low. However, this binary "all-or-nothing" approach is excessively restrictive in long-form settings, often discarding valuable information. We introduce Selective Abstraction (SA), a framework that enables LLMs to trade specificity for reliability by selectively reducing the detail of uncertain content. We formalize SA through selective risk and coverage, and propose Atom-wise Selective Abstraction, a claim-level instantiation that decomposes responses into atomic claims and replaces uncertain atoms with higher-confidence, less specific abstractions. We develop a novel end-to-end pipeline for evaluating long-form generation that instantiates risk as factual correctness and measures coverage as retained information. Our comprehensive empirical evaluation across five LLMs and two long-form factual benchmarks shows that atom-wise SA consistently improves the risk-coverage trade-off, outperforming seven baselines, including claim removal, self-revision, sampling-based aggregation, and chain-of-verification. It improves AURC by up to 35.61% over claim removal, demonstrating that reducing specificity boosts reliability while preserving substantial information. We further provide a risk-guided procedure for selecting a confidence threshold that satisfies a target risk.
  </details>

- **[Same Outputs, Different Scores: Auditing the Unchecked Graders of Multimodal LLM Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03377)**  `arXiv:2611.03377`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Ahmad Jarjis Hasan, Jonathan Lwowski_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Every multimodal-LLM multiple-choice benchmark ends with a scoring rule that converts a model's response, often free-form text rather than a clean option letter, into a right/wrong verdict, buried in its code. Unlike the dataset, this rule is chosen without justification and left unexamined, yet it sets the published number. We reproduce the official scorers of eight peer-reviewed benchmarks on five open vision-language models (VLMs) that fit a single 16 GB GPU, and introduce a blinded human-plus-LLM-judge adjudication (Cohen's kappa=0.956) that recovers what the model actually answered. The scoring rule alone moves accuracy by up to 22 points on identical outputs, yet exactly 0 wherever a model emits clean option letters, so it hides on strong-model leaderboards. It is the fallback and not the rambling that does the damage: the benchmark with the highest non-compliance we measure has an artifact of exactly zero, because its scorer abstains instead of guessing; a reasoning model with non-compliance 0.000 still swings 9.5 points with which regex match of its trace the parser keeps. No standard rule is reliable: snapping a non-answer to its closest option credits answers never given yet buries correct ones, abstention discards answers stated in words, and likelihood scoring follows answer-token priors, so a single grade conflates capability with luck rather than measuring either. We release all code, labels, and the protocol, and argue benchmarks should report a non-compliance rate and at least two rules, not one unexamined grade.
  </details>

- **[When No Answer Is Correct: Diagnosing Absent Answer Detection for MLLMs in Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08239)**  `arXiv:2606.08239`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Yiheng Wang, Yueqian Lin, Lichen Zhu, Yudong Liu, Hai "Helen" Li, Yiran Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made substantial advancements in video understanding, yet the reliability of their responses remains underexplored. This work presents a diagnostic study of absent answer detection for MLLMs in video understanding, where the correct answer is deliberately excluded from the candidate set and a reliable model is expected to recognize that no valid option exists. We evaluate the absent answer detection behavior under three settings: multiple-choice questions augmented with a ``None of the Above'' option, open-ended generation with a detection instruction, and standard evaluation without any guidance. Across a diverse set of models and benchmarks, we find that MLLMs overwhelmingly select plausible distractors rather than detecting the absent answer. This failure is more pronounced in temporal reasoning tasks and worsens with denser frame sampling. We further explore chain-of-thought prompting as a mitigation strategy and find that while it substantially improves detection rates, performance remains unsatisfactory, suggesting that prompting-based strategies alone are insufficient to fully address this limitation. These findings expose a systematic failure in absent answer detection and highlight the need for explicit detection mechanisms in multimodal systems.
  </details>

- **[User Attributes and Political Ideology Shape LLM Receptiveness in Online Political Debates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02908)**  `arXiv:2611.02908`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Akram Elbouanani, Yousra Taleb, Gianluca Demartini, Adrian Popescu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Online political discussions increasingly involve AI-mediated participation, as users invoke LLMs to assess contested claims, arbitrate disputes, and respond to opposing viewpoints. As these systems become participants in online exchanges, they may alter which speakers and argumentative strategies receive validation within the discussion.We study this interactional dimension by reconstructing 5,376this http URLdebates as LLM conversations and running nearly 150,000 controlled multi-turn conversations that vary user political orientation, expertise, rhetorical strategy, and defended position, including conversations with competing users.Across naturalistic and controlled debates, we find that LLMs are systematically more receptive to left-leaning than right-leaning users. The asymmetry persists when users with different political orientations defend the same position and when opposing users appear in the same conversation, indicating that it is not fully explained by stance agreement or simple accommodation to the current speaker. Repeated interaction increases receptiveness without eliminating this ordering; expertise also matters, while rhetorical differences are smaller. The pattern appears across multiple models, but is not fixed: fine-tuning on different political corpora can largely erase or reverse it. These results show that LLMs can respond systematically differently to participants in the same political debate, making such interactional asymmetries important for understanding their emerging role in online public discourse.
  </details>

- **[Learning by Doing: Autonomously Compiling and Curating Agent Skills from Verified Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01386)**  `arXiv:2611.01386`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Daniel Chalef, Peter Evans, Pavlo Paliychuk, Mehul Patel, Preston Rasmussen, Jack Ryan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory systems for production LLM agents mostly store semantic memory (facts about users and domains) or episodic memory (earlier interactions). Research systems such as Trace2Skill, WikiSkill, and SkillRevise learn procedures from agent runs, package them as Skills, and evaluate them on benchmarks. We describe Zep Agent Skills, a production service that gives deployed agents this procedural memory. The service uses only verified attempts as evidence, cites the supporting events for each field of a Skill, and governs the admission and distribution of each Skill version. The service records each task attempt as a Trace with ordered events, an outcome, and a verification strength. A compiler with separate Reflector and Curator roles turns verified Traces into versioned Skills. Scans and admission gates control which candidates become active, and verified use outcomes change the search rank of each Skill. The Agent Skills format is an open standard that Anthropic originated and donated to the Agentic AI Foundation, so any host of the format can load a compiled Skill. Agents retrieve Skills through lexical search with progressive disclosure, over an API or MCP. We evaluate Zep Agent Skills on $\tau^3$-bench banking, AppWorld, and EnterpriseOps-Gym. Skills compiled from the Traces of a teacher model increase the pass rate of three weaker executor models on the same tasks, at every reasoning effort, by 4.5 to 18.8 points. Most of the gain is on the tasks that the teacher solved. On ALFWorld, Skills compiled from the runs of a small model give a gain similar to or larger than the gain that prior work reports for continuous fine-tuning, with no training.
  </details>

- **[What Do LLM Judges Actually Verify? Matched Controls and Record Gaps for Document Currency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01311)**  `arXiv:2611.01311`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Unnati Shah, Samruddhi Kakade, Aum Patel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM judges often check, against an amendment record, whether the documents an answer cites are still in force. Such records are rarely complete. From a table of replacements, our test generates matched answers citing a replaced document, its successor, the replaced document before its replacement, and an older document that a newer one updated without replacing; it removes links from the record and scores each finding by its stated reason. On 48 replacements from the IETF RFC index, judges given a correct record caught nearly every replaced citation, as a lookup would. On the 24 faults whose link we removed, every judge caught more from the texts alone than with the record: Gemini 3.1 Pro 23/24 against 1/24, GPT-5.5 23/24 against 9/24, Claude Opus 4.5 17/24 against 9/24 and Gemma 4 26B 13/24 against 0/24. The drop recurred on a second draw of removed links. Most misses carried no finding; in an exploratory run with a conflict code, only Gemini used it often. Rewording the gap as "no replacement recorded" did not help; presenting the record as an unverified note, or warning that it was incomplete, restored most lost catches for Gemini, GPT-5.5 and Claude (significant after correction for the first two) but at most 5 of 13 for Gemma; where catches returned, so did false alarms on updates. With both texts, judges mostly told replacements from updates; every update flagged had a near-identical title. A descriptive case study on Indian rooftop-solar rules is appended; we release the harness.
  </details>

- **[Concept-Guided Prompting Strategies Increase Diversity in LLM-Generated Recipe Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01171)**  `arXiv:2611.01171`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Stuart Farmer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Novelty in solution generation supports automated discovery, but LLM outputs can remain repetitive. To address this, we propose a simple concept-guided prompting method that generates thematic concepts before responses within each of these concepts. To demonstrate, we generated 1,000 recipes for a recipe book task and compared the results against a human-authored corpus. Two naive prompting strategies were compared as baselines. Concept-guided prompting outperformed both baselines in all cases and reached semantic coverage of 47.0% versus 35.1% and 13.1% in respective prior-title and nonce prompt strategies. These findings suggest broader population exploration and diversity, but remain contained to this specific task. Further work is needed to determine the quality of solutions and generalizability.
  </details>

- **[Skill++: Towards Lifelong Agents via Routing-Aware Skill Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00544)**  `arXiv:2611.00544`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hanrong Zhang, Minghao Liang, Jiawei Wu, Yankai Chen, Shicheng Fan, Zhiwei Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents extend their capabilities with skills, each with a name, a description, and a body. Under progressive disclosure, an LLM router selects skills by name and description alone and loads only the selected bodies. Skill libraries grow continually as users and agents add new skills. Because the router relies on this metadata, a similar new skill can displace an earlier one, breaking previously correct routing even though the earlier skill and the router are unchanged. This routing interference is aggravated for public skills, whose descriptions may not match their bodies. We formulate the problem as lifelong skill routing, a class-incremental continual learning problem. A solution must keep earlier skills selectable without suppressing new ones. Existing approaches fall short, since continually retraining the router risks catastrophic forgetting and rewriting methods either ignore similar skills or require per-skill feedback. We therefore propose Skill++, which rewrites only the name and description of each incoming skill, without router training or labeled data. The new description states what the body supports, how the skill differs from its closest predecessor, and what related work it excludes. As a library grows to 731 skills over ten sessions, Skill++ selects all required skills for 76.3% of queries, versus 36.4% under the original metadata. Across three benchmarks and five LLM routers, it outperforms all compared approaches, and its gains carry over to the Codex CLI agent harness.
  </details>

- **[Agentic Context Management for Multi-Turn Human-AI Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00518)**  `arXiv:2611.00518`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Matthew Ho, Chenglong Wang, Baolin Peng, Nebojsa Jojic, Philippe Laban, Michel Galley, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs degrade in multi-turn conversation partly because of context pollution: the model's own earlier outputs stay in context and anchor later turns, so an early misreading of the user's intent can persist after the user corrects it. Assistant omission (AO), dropping every assistant message, removes this pollution but fails when interaction is referential: later turns refer back to drafts, decisions, and tool-call results that exist only in assistant turns. We propose Agentic Conversation Context Curation (AC3), a training-free method in which a separate analyzer (1) consolidates the task specification from the user's messages alone, (2) audits the assistant's prior outputs against it, and (3) edits the context, either appending the analysis or discarding the assistant side and rebuilding the context from the specification. A second finding: pollution is contagious. An analyzer that sees the assistant's reasoning while extracting the specification can do worse than no intervention, and instructing it to ignore them did not close the gap, so we exclude assistant turns structurally. On Lost in Conversation (replaying difficulty-selected instances), where AO is an oracle by construction, AC3 closes 55-84% of the gap between full context and AO on three of four tasks and exceeds AO on the fourth, and an LLM that faithfully summarizes the history does not reproduce the gain even with more compute. On real WildChat conversations, a GPT-5-mini judge prefers AC3's next turn over both full context and AO in every configuration of our main table, though this preference depends on the judge; on CollabLLM results are mixed. In stateful tool use (tau2-bench), AO collapses to 0% on every model; AC3 does not collapse, though it falls slightly behind full context. Detector studies show that Reset removes injected pollution but is not span-selective: the gain comes from rebuilding the specification.
  </details>

- **[From Small to Large: Assessing Medical Reasoning, Benchmark Contamination, and Model Size in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00460)**  `arXiv:2611.00460`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Navapat Nananukul, Mayank Kejriwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) now answer medical exam questions at or above expert level, but it is unclear whether such scores reflect clinical competence, or are instead affected by the format of the questions being asked (e.g., multiple-choice versus open-ended), prior exposure to the benchmarks, and the difficulty of the questions. These benchmarks are also public and widely used online, which may be resulting in LLM memorization. Models may perform better on questions that they have seen before. It is also not evident whether smaller models, which are more locally deployable in HIPAA-compliant hospital settings, have achieved human performance. In this study, we evaluate fourteen current models from six leading providers, one smaller and one larger model from each provider with two additional open-weight models, on 3,076 questions from four medical benchmarks (MedQA-USMLE, MedMCQA, PubMedQA, and MedBullets) under standard multiple choice, the same questions with the answer choices withheld, and reconstruction of a hidden question from its choices alone. In the standard multiple-choice setting, the strongest models exceed the human-expert baseline on MedQA-USMLE and PubMedQA, but the smaller models trail their larger models by 8.7 percentage points and only two of the six pass the human-expert bar on the licensing examination. Withholding the answer choices, however, makes every model struggle: mean accuracy falls from 82.3% to 50.9%, and the two largest drops belong to the medically specialised models. On memorization, the strongest models reconstruct one question in four from the answer choices alone, yet accuracy holds on the questions they cannot reconstruct. We further weigh accuracy against inference cost and test whether ensembling several models improves medical question answering.
  </details>

- **[Quantiles, Not Points: Streaming Quantile Matching for Ordinal-Aware LLM-as-a-Judge Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00447)**  `arXiv:2611.00447`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ilija Subasic, Andrew Rabinovich, Zhao Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-as-a-judge scores are often distributionally shifted from human ratings on any given evaluation task, and post-hoc calibration (mapping LLM to human scores in a small labeled anchor set) has become a popular method to correct for this shift. In this paper we derive the calibration from two positions: that LLMaJ scores are ordinal ratings drawn from a distribution rather than isolated points. We present streaming quantile matching (SQM), a version of a classical method not previously bench marked for LLMaJ calibration, which is built on the two positions by computing the optimal-transport map between the LLM and human score distributions. We develop an evaluation framework and within it we show that unlike point-wise calibrations, SQM respects the ordinality of the human anchor set and achieves the best distributional fidelity and the top overall rank across models and data. We also show that in general, post-hoc calibration narrows the raw error gap between smaller and larger judge models, which opens the possibility of more efficient LLM judges.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Escalation Reliability in Healthcare LLM Agents: A Perturbation-Controlled Benchmark of Workflow-State Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21270)**  `arXiv:2610.21270`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zihao Zheng, Junyi Yao, Baichuan Li, Jiayu Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trustworthy biomedical AI requires reliable action selection when evidence is missing, stale, conflicting, or late, not only accurate answers on static inputs. We instantiate an episode-level evaluation protocol as 400 synthetic healthcare workflow episodes: 40 hand-authored semantic seeds across four task classes, two surface parameterizations, and five controlled variants, scored on ANSWER/ASK CLARIFICATION/ABSTAIN/ESCALATE under a pre-declared 5:1 missed-versus-unnecessary escalation cost. Reference actions are single-author study-policy labels, not clinician-adjudicated ground truth. Across five open-weight configurations (1.7B--9.7B), clean decision accuracy reaches 0.97, yet uncertainty-prompted models miss 32--97% of required escalations. The stronger 9.7B configuration narrows but does not remove the gap, falling from 0.97 clean accuracy to 0.78 under conflict. Verbalized confidence is inconsistent (ECE 0.10--0.54; failure AUROC 0.48--0.77), and a development-tuned confidence-only policy yields no utility gain. A transparent workflow-aware controller improves 5:1 utility for every model and reduces missed escalations to 0.12--0.24, at specificity 0.83--0.93. However, a 30-concept lexical stress test reduces each rule detector's recall from 1.00 on trigger phrases to 0.00 on unseen paraphrases, limiting the controller claim to this benchmark. Seed-clustered statistics, ablations, cross-task transfer, raw outputs, and manifests are packaged for audit. The results motivate explicit workflow-state evaluation while showing that apparently effective routing controls need their own generalization tests.
  </details>
