- **[RAP: Research Attention Prediction in Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10092)**  `arXiv:2609.10092`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yingqian Wu, Jingcong Liang, Shengbin Yue, Siyuan Wang, Zhenfei Yin, Junchi Yu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly support research planning and execution, where judgments about current and anticipated research activity inform decisions. Yet whether they can infer changes across a whole field from the partial evidence they retrieve through online scholarly search remains unclear. We introduce Research Attention Prediction (RAP), a literature search benchmark for evaluating this ability. We build a local search environment from 356,357 dated arXiv papers and construct two complementary evaluation tasks. The benchmark comprises a Core set of 2,160 questions across 54 AI/ML fields and an Extended set covering 269 fields. Our evaluation and analysis of LLM agents show that search substantially improves stronger agents' recognition of recent changes, while forecasting gains remain limited or negative. Agents update forecasts across dates and adjust their investigations to forecasting goals, but these adaptations do not reliably yield accurate predictions of future shifts. Even for recent changes, final judgments can diverge from what the retrieved papers indicate. These findings show that agents can recognise some recent changes, yet reliably anticipating future research activity remains difficult despite changes in their answers and investigations.
  </details>

- **[Humans' ALMANAC: A Human Collaboration Dataset of Action-Level Mental Model Annotations for Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06388)**  `arXiv:2606.06388`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Jiaju Chen, Yuxuan Lu, Jiayi Su, Chaoran Chen, Songlin Xiao, Zheng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in LLM agents have enabled complex cognitive capabilities, such as multi-step reasoning, planning, and tool use, that increasingly position these agents as collaborative partners in human workflows. Effective collaboration, however, requires collaborators to continuously maintain and align mental models of their own reasoning, partners' intentions, and shared goals during the collaborative process. Today's agents rarely develop such capabilities since they are primarily optimized for task completion rather than for maintaining the underlying mental models required for collaborative processes. Moreover, the community lacks authentic human collaboration data with action-level mental model annotations that could guide agents toward collaborative competence. To bridge this gap, we present ALMANAC, a dataset of Action-Level Mental model ANnotations for Agent Collaboration built from the Map Task, a classic dyadic routing task from social science. ALMANAC contains 2,987 collaboration actions, each paired with theory-informed mental model annotations that record the participants' self-reasoning, perceived partner intent, and perceived team goal. We benchmark six LLMs on predicting humans' next-turn behavior and mental models. Our results demonstrate ALMANAC's utility in evaluating models' ability to simulate human collaborative behaviors and infer their underlying mental models. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Foundation Models to Unlock Real-World Evidence from Nationwide Medical Claims](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02740)**  `arXiv:2605.02740`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Fan Ma, Yuntian Liu, Xiang Lan, Weipeng Zhou, Jun Ni, Mauro Giuffrè, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world data increasingly inform regulatory and clinical decisions. Administrative claims, among their major sources, continuously track care and its cost across providers throughout enrollment, yet remain largely unexplored for healthcare foundation models. Here we present ReClaim, a family of generative foundation models built on 43.8 billion claims records from more than 200 million enrollees in the MarketScan Research Databases. ReClaim learns diagnoses, procedures, medications and expenditure jointly through pre-training from scratch, then specializes for downstream prediction tasks through post-training, with a training cutoff at the end of 2022. In predicting more than 1,000 diseases, ReClaim achieved a mean ROC-AUC of 0.769, outperforming disease-specific gradient-boosted models (0.663) and a transformer-based baseline (0.694), and retained this advantage when generalizing to two independent cross-domain electronic health record datasets. ReClaim also outperformed gradient-boosted models in forecasting next-year expenditure and in identifying high-need, high-cost enrollees. ReClaim maintained its advantage in disease prediction and expenditure forecasting on post-cutoff data (2023-2024). Beyond prediction, ReClaim representations used as covariates resulted in improved effect estimates compared with algorithm-derived covariates, when benchmarked against results expected from randomized controlled trials. Together, these results establish nationwide insurance claims as a scalable substrate for healthcare foundation models supporting disease prediction, expenditure forecasting and confounding adjustment in real-world evidence studies. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Presupposition Problem in Representation Genesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.21745)**  `arXiv:2603.21745`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yiling Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are the first systems to achieve high cognitive performance without clearly undergoing representation genesis: the transition from a non-representing physical system to one whose states guide behavior in a content-sensitive way. Prior cognitive systems had already made this transition before we could examine it, and philosophy of mind treated genesis as a background condition rather than an explanatory target. LLMs provide a case that does not clearly involve this transition, making the genesis question newly urgent: if genesis did not occur, which cognitive capacities are affected, and why? We currently lack the conceptual resources to answer this. The reason, this paper argues, is structural. Major frameworks in philosophy of mind, including the Language of Thought hypothesis, teleosemantics, predictive processing, enactivism, and genetic phenomenology, share a common feature when applied to the genesis question: at some explanatory step, each deploys concepts whose explanatory purchase depends on the system already being organized as a representer. This pattern, which we call the Representation Presupposition structure, generates systematic explanatory deferral. Attempts to explain the first acquisition of content-manipulable representation within the existing categorical vocabulary import resources from the representational side of the transition itself. We call this the Representation Regress. The paper offers a conceptual diagnosis rather than a new theory, establishing the structure of the problem and deriving two minimum adequacy conditions for any account that avoids this pattern. LLMs make the absence of such a theory consequential rather than merely theoretical.
  </details>

- **[VeriCypher: Named-Entity Verification for GraphRAG via Text-to-Cypher Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.08274)**  `arXiv:2511.08274`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Anton Gusarov, Anastasia Volkova, Valentin Khrulkov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Evgenii Maslov, Ivan Oseledets_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graph question answering has traditionally focused on Resource Description Framework (RDF) graphs, SPARQL queries, and triple-based representations. Labeled Property Graphs (LPGs), however, pose a different challenge for LLM-based query generation: executable Cypher queries must align with schema structure, traversal direction, property keys, and literal entity values. This motivated us to introduce VeriCypher, an LLM-based text-to-Cypher framework for question answering over LPGs. It combines query generation conditioned on Cypher-like graph schema summaries with runtime execution, LLM-based query evaluation, and iterative correction where the central mechanism is named-entity verification: the system extracts node labels, relationship patterns, property keys, and literal values from generated queries, checks them against the target graph and schema, and turns mismatches into correction instructions for the next generation step. We evaluate VeriCypher on the CypherBench benchmark across multiple domains and LLM backbones. Compared with an LLM baseline, this method consistently improves answer correctness and query robustness. Ablation results further show that named-entity verification helps correct hallucinated schema elements and entity values, supporting more reliable LLM-based question answering over heterogeneous property graphs.
  </details>

- **[From Natural Language to Exact Cover: A Neuro-Symbolic Approach to Zebra Puzzles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03641)**  `arXiv:2611.03641`  `cs.AI`  
  _Paulius Skaisgiris, Thomas Pammer, Veronika Semmelrock, Mykyta Ielanskyi, Maximilian Heisinger, Erich Kobler_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-Thought (CoT) generation has substantially improved the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) on complex reasoning tasks, including code generation, data analysis, and exam-style question answering. Despite these advances, purely neural LLMs continue to struggle with elementary logical reasoning problems and lack the determinism, soundness, and reliability characteristic of symbolic reasoning systems. Conversely, classical symbolic methods such as SAT solving and exact cover guarantee correctness and completeness, but require problems to be expressed in highly specialized formal encodings, limiting their applicability to natural language inputs. In this work, we present a synergistic neuro-symbolic framework that bridges this gap by combining neural semantic parsing with deterministic exact cover reasoning. Given a Zebra-style logic puzzle in natural language, an LLM translates entities and constraints into structured tool calls that construct an exact cover instance. A symbolic solver then computes the solution and provides feedback to iteratively refine invalid, under- or overconstrained encodings, exploiting the uniqueness property of Zebra puzzles. We conduct a comprehensive empirical evaluation across multiple parameter scales, post-training paradigms, and LLM families. The results demonstrate that our hybrid approach consistently outperforms strong plain neural baselines, including CoT prompting, as well as recent neuro-symbolic methods.
  </details>

- **[Grading the Unannotated: Insights from the WorkRB Skill Extraction Challenge at RecSys-HR 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03525)**  `arXiv:2611.03525`  `cs.AI`  
  _Warre Veys, Luis Gasco, Jens-Joris Decorte_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Skill extraction benchmarks score models against a few human-annotated positives and treat every other skill in the vocabulary as equally wrong. At the scale of ESCO (13,939 skills) this binary view can reward models that rank the annotated positive high for the wrong reason, and hides semantically nonsensical predictions. The WorkRB Challenge at RecSys-HR 2026 replaces it with graded relevance: every candidate skill in a model's ranking window carries a 0 to 4 grade, and the leaderboard scores nDCG@100 against these grades. Since ternary judgements proved too coarse for objective annotation, relevance is decomposed into a cascade of four binary questions (same domain, activity mentioned, right granularity, replacement of a gold positive) that a smaller LLM judge can answer reliably; the labels are validated with blind human and frontier-model re-annotation. This paper documents the annotation methodology, the construction of five benchmark datasets (four with vacancy sentences as queries, one with lexically distant skill surface forms), the validation of the LLM cascade, and insights from the submissions. The field converged on multi-stage retrieve-and-rerank pipelines, whose gains come from clearing nonsense out of the ranking window rather than finding more positives: at the top of the leaderboard, the graded ordering is uncorrelated with what binary evaluation would have said. Graded labels expose much bigger gaps between top submissions and baselines than binary evaluation does, and show that semantic-understanding skill recommendation is still wide open. The clear next direction is bringing efficiency back: every submitted system we could size uses at least 42x, typically several hundred times, more parameters than the baselines it beats.
  </details>

- **[DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412)**  `arXiv:2611.03412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yumou Liu, Mingju Chen, Zhoufutu Wen, Qianyu Yang, Zijie Wang, Yeqian Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Its 11 projects, spanning financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting, are reconstructed with 13 domain experts from real materials and chain 150 subtasks through 2-15 rounds of new materials and client feedback. Because no reference outputs are injected, agents inherit their own errors from one task to the next. Expert rubrics then score every checkpoint as well as the final package. Across seven model-harness systems, even the best system scores only 65.0 out of 100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. The failures are often failures of carrying work forward: in one audit project, all seven systems addressed the review findings, but only one preserved traceability. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery, which also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.
  </details>

- **[Toward Knowledge-Centric AI: Discovering, Internalizing, and Using Explicit Knowledge Across Agents and Generations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03157)**  `arXiv:2611.03157`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yisen Gao, Haoyu Huang, Zhongwei Xie, Yufei Li, Hong Ting Tsang, Boyi Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge is how understanding accumulates across people and generations: durable records that outlive their producers and that others can check, combine, teach, and correct. AI agents are now built and run by many independent users and organizations, and each agent is upgraded, replaced, or retired within months. Yet what an agent learns stays trapped in model parameters, a transient context window, or a system-specific memory store that is appended to and retrieved from rather than processed. We argue that the next generation of AI should be knowledge-centric, built around an explicit, continuously evolving knowledge layer independent of any single agent. In this loop, agents discover knowledge from their environments and trajectories; the layer internalizes it by turning fragmented, heterogeneous records and experience into composable, revisable understanding; and agents across systems and generations use the result and return new outcomes for revision. While discovery and use have advanced rapidly with large language model agents, internalization remains the bottleneck. We argue that internalization should become a persistent object of training and maintenance-centered on condition-preserving conceptualization of events and deductive, inductive, and abductive reasoning across noisy, heterogeneous representations (text, tables, graphs, rules, and programs). To power this layer, we call for knowledge foundation models that provide shared neural capacity for knowledge computation, and outline a research agenda showing how an explicit knowledge layer delivers reuse, cost efficiency, auditability, and continuity across agent generations.
  </details>

- **[From Local Skills to Long-Horizon Web Tasks: Progressive Skill Exploration for LLM Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03116)**  `arXiv:2611.03116`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xueyang Feng, Xiaohe Bo, Xu Chen, Quanyu Dai, Feng Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are driving autonomous agents toward real-world environments, with the Web emerging as one of the most representative interaction scenarios. However, training Web agents with naive exploration remains challenging: successful trajectories are rare, and the sparse terminal rewards make it difficult for agents to obtain useful feedback from failed trajectories. Inspired by skill acquisition theory, we propose Skill-Composed Progressive Exploration (SCOPE), a short-to-long learning framework for long-horizon Web agents that decomposes complex task exploration into a gradual expansion process from local skills to complete task-solving capability. Specifically, SCOPE iteratively explores around local skills: Skill Discovery extracts reusable local skills and failure experiences from historical rollouts; Skill Orchestration composes discovered skills into high-level skill chains; and Skill Expansion leverages failure experiences aligned with the current skill chain to guide the agent toward exploring new subskills. Through this process, SCOPE progressively constructs task-completing skill chains across rollouts and internalizes them into the policy parameters through agentic on-policy distillation. Extensive experiments on WebArena, WebVoyager, and WebShop show that SCOPE consistently improves exploration dynamics and achieves 3.9% to 18.6% relative gains over the strongest baselines in each setting.
  </details>

- **[LUCA: A Synthetic World for Experimental Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03100)**  `arXiv:2611.03100`  `cs.AI`  
  _Anna Gogleva, Thomas Charman, Martin Balla, Karl Gemayel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An experimental agent can succeed for the wrong reasons. Evaluations, however, often focus on the outcome, not the experiments, evidence, and inferences that produced it. We introduce LUCA, a configurable synthetic world for evaluating this process under fully known ground truth. Agents learn unknown regulatory systems through budgeted experimentation, then predict unseen interventions, diagnose hidden faults, and attempt rescues. This setup allows us to evaluate experiment selection, prediction, diagnosis, and intervention separately.Across seven frontier LLMs and three baselines, these capabilities dissociate sharply. Agents frequently rescue malfunctioning systems while diagnosing the wrong fault; diagnosis varies 7x across models while prediction varies only 1.4x. Models also adopt surprisingly different experimental strategies, and greater exploration does not reliably yield better performance. In one failure mode, agents often perform the experiment needed to distinguish competing causes, but still fail to use the resulting evidence. LUCA turns these hidden failures into measurable behaviours, providing a controlled environment to study where experimental agents succeed and fail. Code:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[PRIME: Predictive Routing of Interventions by Modeling Effects for Scientific Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03055)**  `arXiv:2611.03055`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fengxian Ji, Xie Yu, Zhexuan Cui, Yuke Li, Zirui Song, Fan Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Feedback-driven iteration is widely used to improve LLM-generated scientific ideas, but existing systems either leave revision decisions implicit in generation, align revisions with feedback-localized weaknesses, or restrict action selection to system-specific operations. This is problematic because problems, hypotheses, and solutions are interdependent: the location where a weakness manifests does not itself determine the revision scope that will produce a better successor. We term this phenomenon diagnosis-intervention mismatch. We introduce Predictive Routing of Interventions by Modeling Effects (PRIME), a plug-and-play feedback-to-revision decision layer for existing scientific ideators. PRIME operates in three stages: (1) Structural Attribution interprets feedback over the problem-hypothesis-solution structure without fixing a revision target; (2) Counterfactual Routing constructs alternative scope-conditioned interventions and compares their projected successor utility and risks through lightweight rollouts; and (3) Intervention Execution compiles the selected intervention into an ordered generator-facing plan with semantic actions, preservation constraints, and dependency requirements for successor generation. We validate diagnosis intervention mismatch on 112 human-annotated idea-feedback pairs. Across four benchmarks, two ideators, and fixed- and open-problem settings, PRIME improves successor quality and routing reliability over strong baselines; removing counterfactual routing reduces quality from 3.463 to 1.960 and increases route regret from 0.426 to 1.929, confirming cross-scope effect modeling as the primary source of its gains.
  </details>

- **[BLPO: Beyond-Local Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03005)**  `arXiv:2611.03005`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuanren Chen, Chongyang Tao, Wenpeng Hu, Yong Wang, Bo Deng, Xinlei Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon tasks require large language model (LLM) agents to coordinate interdependent actions, a capability central to automating complex workflows. Stepwise group-based reinforcement learning provides finer credit than trajectory-level supervision, but reliable comparisons must account for differences in interaction history. Enforcing this consistency within the limited rollouts of each task can leave insufficient support for estimating action preferences. We propose Beyond-Local Policy Optimization (BLPO), which estimates task-progress advantages from experience shared across tasks and training updates. We summarize trajectory prefixes into task-progress states and abstract actions by their function, allowing corresponding decisions from different tasks to contribute to shared estimates. To control bias and noise from heterogeneous tasks, we accumulate context-relative returns in a global memory with balanced aggregation and reliability weighting. These global advantages complement local comparisons over normalized actions and trajectory-level credit without a learned critic or additional rollouts. Experiments on ALFWorld and WebShop with 1.5B and 7B policies show improved task success over the evaluated baselines under matched training budgets. Further analyses show faster learning and shorter trajectories with little computational overhead.
  </details>

- **[Integrating Domain Adaptation and Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Saline-Alkali Land Reclamation Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02883)**  `arXiv:2611.02883`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zuguang He, Congcong Zheng, Wanyue Chen, Wenya Zhou, Zaijin Yang, Long Long, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reclamation of saline-alkali land requires management plans that account for soil and water conditions, treatment evidence, and financial constraints. This study develops Wenyao, an agricultural decision-support system integrating domain adaptation with retrieval-augmented generation. A bilingual corpus of 8,664 research papers was processed using a 110-indicator schema to construct SA-Bench, comprising 4,860 paper-level planning instances. A QwQ-32B model was adapted through low-rank supervised fine-tuning and combined with case retrieval for budget-conditioned planning. Evidence extraction achieved an F1-score of 0.83 on 300 expert-annotated papers. Without retrieval, Wenyao achieved 95.83% accuracy on 936 source-disjoint multiple-choice questions, compared with 87.93% for the strongest general-purpose baseline; removing explanatory supervision reduced accuracy to 89.42%. Matched-evidence comparisons across 69 region-budget cases supported complementary contributions from adaptation and retrieval. In a blind assessment by 31 experts, Wenyao with retrieval obtained the highest mean scores for objectives and projected outcomes, whereas GPT-5 scored highest for cost estimates. These findings support literature-informed reclamation planning with professional review, while ongoing field monitoring will assess soil and crop responses.
  </details>

- **[SpecOPD: Branch-Parallel On-Policy Distillation for Speculative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02846)**  `arXiv:2611.02846`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tianyu Liu, Jun Zhang, Wenjie Huang, Yuhao Shen, Tianqi Xu, Xinyue Hao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Speculative decoding (SD) accelerates large language model inference by reducing the sequential cost of token-by-token generation. Advanced SD methods reduce proposal cost with parallel drafters that predict entire blocks in one forward pass, but overall speedup still depends on acceptance length. On-policy distillation (OPD) can push well-trained drafters beyond acceptance-length plateaus, yet conventional pipelines collect supervision through sequential draft-and-verify rollouts. Our key insight is that on-policy supervision need not inherit these dependencies: a draft block can be supervised using only its reference prefix and draft-sampled history. We propose SpecOPD, a branch-parallel OPD framework that parallelizes draft sampling and target supervision across independent branches drawn from the evolving drafter policy at reference anchors. We pack these branches into proposal trees to share target-prefix computation, using tree masks for full attention, branch-consistent indexer groups for sparse attention, and indexed state replay for linear attention to preserve each branch's history. We train the drafter with an E2E-TV surrogate that directly targets acceptance length. Our study spans 8B full-attention (GQA), 27B full-linear (GQA--GDN), and 125B sparse-linear (QSA--GDN) targets, three parallel draft architectures (DFlash, DSpark, and DFlash V2), and four task domains (dialogue, coding, mathematics, and agentic). SpecOPD achieves up to 41.45% higher mean acceptance length, $16.09\times$ supervised-token throughput over sequential-rollout OPD under native configurations, and up to $3.56\times$ generation speedup over autoregressive decoding.
  </details>

- **[Predictive Safety Guardrails Do Not Always Make LLM Agents Safer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02815)**  `arXiv:2611.02815`  `cs.AI`  
  _Weixiang Zhao, Zhihao Liu, Guancheng Li, Yanyan Zhao, Bing Qin, Ting Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents increasingly take consequential actions in digital and physical environments, where safety risks may emerge only after multiple interaction steps. This has motivated world-model-driven predictive safety guardrails, which anticipate future consequences before execution. However, existing evaluations largely focus on end-to-end safety outcomes, leaving unclear whether safety gains arise from reliable prediction and faithful use of predicted risks. In this work, we systematically diagnose the prediction-to-action pipeline along three dimensions: how world-model predictions are integrated into decision making, whether the predictions themselves are reliable, and whether their content is faithfully used. Across seven policy models and two safety environments, we find that stronger prediction involvement does not yield monotonic safety gains and can reduce task completion while substantially increasing inference cost. We further find that world-model predictions remain insufficiently reliable, especially over longer horizons, where safety prediction degrades substantially relative to near-term forecasting. Finally, we find that safety gains can persist even under corrupted predictions, while locally verified predictions still leave subsequent safety violations, showing that predictive content is not faithfully incorporated into the decision process. Together, these findings expose a fundamental prediction-to-action gap in current predictive guardrails: producing more or seemingly accurate foresight is insufficient unless that foresight is both reliable and faithfully translated into safer decisions.
  </details>

- **[Is your LLM an Agressive Narcissist with Depression? Analyzing Psychological Portraits of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02757)**  `arXiv:2611.02757`  `cs.AI`  
  _Polina Fedorinina, Anna Fattakhova, Nadezhda Romanova, Ekaterina Lezina, Irina Nikishina_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Nowadays, interaction with AI requires caution not only regarding the factual reliability of generated content but also regarding personality markers that models may implicitly transmit during dialogue that can subtly influence users' perception of information. In this paper, we conduct a large-scale experimental study in which 22 large language models complete 11 psychological inventories. Furthermore, we investigate correlations between human and model responses to track the adjustments made for the users who interact with them on a daily basis. The results show that LLMs exhibit coherent and relatively stable psychological patterns. Personality traits remain largely consistent, whereas emotional and symptomatic dimensions are more variable, with anxiety and neuroticism emerging as dominant tendencies. Model explanations further suggest that LLMs generate internally consistent and safety-oriented profiles while also reflecting correlations with the users they regularly interact with.
  </details>

- **[AffToM: Benchmarking Recursive Affective Theory of Mind in Dynamic Multi-Party Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02725)**  `arXiv:2611.02725`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinpeng Hu, Yiduo Jin, Erqiang Wang, Jia Li, Jingjing Wu, Peipei Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding emotion in realistic social interactions requires more than recognizing facial expressions or assigning affective labels. The same event can elicit different emotions depending on what each participant knows, what they believe others know or feel, and how these nested mental states evolve over time. Existing benchmarks largely study higher-order epistemic Theory of Mind (ToM) and multimodal emotion reasoning in isolation, leaving recursive affective reasoning under asymmetric and dynamically changing information underexplored. Therefore, we introduce AffToM, a benchmark for affective ToM with 1,922 test pairs and 19,759 training pairs. AffToM evaluates Individual Affective Reasoning (IAR), which infers emotions from events and socio-cognitive context, and Multi-party Affective Reasoning (MPAR), which recursively tracks participants' nested beliefs and emotions as new evidence emerges. We develop an event-driven multi-agent generation pipeline grounded in dynamic character interaction graphs that explicitly capture event sequences, interpersonal relations, and mental-state updates. Furthermore, we propose AffToM-R1, a two-stage post-training framework combining supervised fine-tuning with perspective-aware rubric-guided reinforcement learning. Experiments show that MPAR remains challenging, with even the strongest evaluated MLLM achieving less than 60% accuracy, while AffToM-R1 improves MPAR accuracy by 13.38 percentage points over its base model. Our dataset is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Human-Supervised Artificial Intelligence Researchers in Electric Power Systems: Knowledge, Physics, Simulation, and Governance for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02711)**  `arXiv:2611.02711`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiangrui Meng, Jingcheng Liu, Ke Wang, Miaopeng Yu, Zhengping Lin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conclusions in power-system research require a clear evidential basis. Researchers need to understand the data sources, modelling assumptions, and solution procedures to assess the conditions under which a conclusion holds. Large language models (LLMs) can help researchers extract information from documents and translate research requests into code and tool calls, but their outputs still require physical validation. Focusing on human-supervised artificial intelligence (AI) researchers, this review examines power-system LLM applications and related scientific workflows, together with physics-grounded power AI studies, to discuss the conditions for using LLMs in scientific research. In the proposed knowledge-physics-simulation-governance framework, LLMs translate information and coordinate tasks, simulators and validation tools test models, and experts retain authority over critical assumptions, safety boundaries, and final conclusions. Research processes should preserve materials for review so that claims can be traced to their sources, computations can be reproduced, and failed attempts and approvals can be examined. On this basis, a staged development roadmap and requirements for research reporting are proposed. Evaluation should focus on research artifacts and their validation to assess whether LLMs can improve the efficiency of power-system research within clearly defined permissions.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Representation-Space Fraud Detection: A Bayesian IRL Framework for Calibrated Uncertainty and Its Limits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02673)**  `arXiv:2611.02673`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shivam Swarup, Divya Prakash Shrivastava, Rakesh Thakur_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Shilling detectors for recommender systems are almost always evaluated by one number, detection AUROC, with no account of when it can be trusted. We audit representation-space detection with VEIL (Variational Evaluation via Inverse-reinforcement Learning), a pairwise (Bradley-Terry) Bayesian reward-inference framework over frozen encoder embeddings, following the Bayesian auditing paradigm for LLM alignment [1]: (A) a base posterior; (B) a hierarchical ARD extension with learned confidence; and (C) a label-routed organic/attacker mixture used as an evidence-accumulationthis http URLprove (Proposition 1) that no detector built on a representation invariant to a transformation of user profiles can outperform chance against an attack that is indistinguishable from organic profiles under thatthis http URLis near-perfect against synthetic-structure attacks (AUROC 0.936-1.000) but collapses under donor-mimicry, which clones a real user's item set and splices in 1-3 target items. Performance falls to chance on MovieLens-1M (0.48-0.51), partially degrades on Amazon Musical Instruments, and degrades least onthis http URL-mimicry is not undetectable: a non-learned baseline, based on each profile's maximum raw item-set Jaccard similarity to any other profile, reaches AUROC 0.92-0.97 (average precision 0.50-0.57) on all four donor-mimicry configurations, including ML-1M, where every embedding-based score is atthis http URLsignal is present in the raw profiles, but LightGCN's representation discards it, placing injected profiles closer to their true donor than its zero-propagation MF reduction does (distance 1.98 vs. 3.09). Severity varies by dataset, encoder, and detection method, with no single axis predicting the others. The limit lies in the representation, not the attack, and raw-similarity checks are a necessary complement to any learned detector.
  </details>

- **[UPSHIFT: User Preference Steering from Human-AI Interaction Feedback Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02667)**  `arXiv:2611.02667`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingping Wang, Zhanhao Li, Shao Zhang, Shuyue Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Personalizing large language model assistants requires adapting their responses to individual users, including the selection of content, reasoning approach, level of detail, and form of presentation. Such preferences are often latent, becoming visible only when users respond to an initial answer and request revisions. We investigate whether these feedback trajectories can be converted into a reusable personalization signal without explicit preference profiles or labels, prompt-time access to interaction histories, or model parameter updates. We introduce UPSHIFT (User Preference Steering from Human-AI Interaction Feedback Traces), a training-free method that compares the hidden representations of initial and feedback-conditioned responses within each session, averages the resulting trajectory displacements across a user's sessions, and injects the aggregated preference direction when answering a new query. The method is motivated by the implicit-preference hypothesis: although an individual conversational transition may reflect topic progression or task-specific correction, changes that recur across sessions retain a component that captures how the user wants responses to be adjusted. We validate the preference-representation capability of UPSHIFT on PrefEval. In evaluations with 40 simulated users, every tested configuration improves preference alignment over the unsteered baseline on held-out topics for both Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-32B; the best configuration increases macro-averaged Elo from 1364.0 to 1605.2 and from 1341.9 to 1591.8, respectively. Matched-user and positive-versus-negative controls further demonstrate that these gains arise from the user-specific preference direction and its learned orientation. These results show that interaction histories can provide compact, reusable supervision for lightweight personalization.
  </details>

- **[Widening Harm Evaluation Coverage with a Judge that Reads the World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02624)**  `arXiv:2611.02624`  `cs.AI`  
  _Prabhpreet Singh Setia, Bhavesh Neekhra, Debayan Gupta_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An agent acting in a stateful environment can leave evidence of harm in two places: the record of what it did and said, or the world it changed. Safety evaluations for discovering harm examine one or the other in limited ways, using either an LLM judge over the trajectory or deterministic checks over the final state. Although LLM-based verifiers that inspect the environment exist, they have been used to verify task completion, not to detect and confirm harm in a safety setting. We address this gap with an LLM that has read-only access to the environment state and is given a harm taxonomy. We evaluate this approach in three environments: two from DecodingTrust-Arena and one from OSWorld. We hold the judge model and harm taxonomy fixed, varying only what the judge observes: the trajectory, read-only tools for inspecting the environment, or both channels together. In the two DecodingTrust-Arena environments, combining the trajectory and state-only judges recalls 94 percent of confirmed harms, compared with 53 percent for the trajectory judge alone. On OS-Harm, the union recalls 77 percent, compared with 67 percent. A single judge given both channels does not perform better and produces more false negatives than the state-only judge. We also record disagreements and find that the two channels miss different episodes. These results suggest that combining verdicts from two channel-specific judges is a robust and flexible approach to detecting and verifying harm in agentic settings.
  </details>

- **[BasisKV: Value Latents for Communication and Key Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02583)**  `arXiv:2611.02583`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lixun Zhang, Zishan Shao, Kangning Cui, Yu Liang, Fan Yang, Yiran Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context large language model (LLM) inference is increasingly constrained by the cost of maintaining an ever-growing KV-cache. Low-rank K/V projections provide a promising way to compress the KV-cache with minimal quality loss, but prior work has largely optimized KV-cache compression in isolation, leaving distributed inference, especially with tensor parallelism where attention computation is partitioned across multiple GPUs, largely underexplored. We make two observations: (i) tensor-parallel communication is an important cost when deploying low-rank KV-cache compression in distributed inference; and (ii) Value compression can be associated with the output projection, allowing the same low-rank Value representation to reduce both Value-cache width and tensor-parallel communication. Based on these observations, we propose BasisKV, a low-rank KV-cache compression method for communication-intensive, long-context LLM inference. BasisKV jointly optimizes the low-rank factors across per-GPU attention partitions against the complete attention output, rather than fitting each partition in isolation. The compressed Value cache is further reused to help identify which Key pages should be accessed: it predicts part of the Key information needed for routing, while a Key-specific residual stores what cannot be recovered from Value. The original Keys are then retrieved only for the selected pages to compute exact QK scores. Across multiple LLM families and system settings, BasisKV achieves a better quality--efficiency tradeoff than existing low-rank baselines, reaching up to 1.42x end-to-end speedup under 8-GPU tensor-parallel inference. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[FORGE: Fault-Oriented Rework Code Generation for Executable Interlocking Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02536)**  `arXiv:2611.02536`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fuqiang Wang, Jiahao Fu, Ren Hefeng, Yining Qian, Chuanting Zhang, Cheng Tan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have demonstrated strong capabilities in structured reasoning and code generation, yet identifying an assembly fault does not guarantee the ability to generate executable rework programs. In interlocking assemblies, a locally plausible repair program may violate blocking dependencies, omit required recovery operations, or terminate in an incorrect configuration. We identify this challenge as fault-conditioned executable rework generation, where models must jointly reason about structural failures and generate programs that restore constrained assembly states. To benchmark this capability, we introduce FORGE-Bench, containing 4,000 tasks across 26 interlocking assemblies with five assemblies held out for evaluating generalization to unseen structures. We further propose FORGE (Fault-Oriented Rework Code Generation for Executable Interlocking Assembly), a structured state-to-program framework that decomposes rework generation into Rework Structuring and Action Grounding. Rework Structuring captures fault-induced recovery dependencies, while Action Grounding maps these dependencies into state-conditioned executable actions. Experiments show that locally reasonable repair code is insufficient for reliable assembly recovery, and that connecting structural recovery reasoning with executable action realization is essential for verified target restoration.
  </details>

- **[Cumulative and Audited Guardrail Tool Evolution for LLM Agent Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02516)**  `arXiv:2611.02516`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiao Yang, Aishan Liu, Siyuan Liang, Zonghao Ying, Le Wang, Jiakai Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate in open-world environments where safety risks cannot be fully predefined. However, most existing guardrail mechanisms remain static, relying on fixed rules, prompts, or classifiers that generalize poorly to emerging risks. Here we present EVOLVE, a tool-level self-evolving defense framework that formulates agent security as a process of cumulative security evolution. Inspired by how human societies transform experiences into transferable protective knowledge, EVOLVE externalizes security knowledge into executable guardrail tools that are continuously inherited, independently audited, and iteratively refined through lifelong interaction. Across multiple benchmarks, backbone models, and embodied environments, EVOLVE consistently outperforms existing defenses across diverse risk scenarios with +22\% average F1 score. We further validate the framework in real-world robotic and autonomous agent systems. Our results suggest that scalable agent safety may require continuously evolving defense systems rather than static guardrails.
  </details>

- **[Towards Multi-Value Alignment via Value Localization in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02503)**  `arXiv:2611.02503`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xueqi Ma, Yanbei Jiang, Xingjun Ma, James Bailey, Sarah M. Erfani_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning large language models (LLMs) with multiple, potentially conflicting human values remains challenging, as existing multi-objective alignment methods often require costly fine-tuning and provide limited insight into the internal mechanisms underlying value-related behaviors. In this work, we investigate multi-value alignment from a mechanistic interpretability perspective by identifying value-sensitive attention heads (value heads) in LLMs. Using a probing-based value localization framework, we discover sparse sets of attention heads associated with diverse dimensions, spanning both human values (e.g., harmlessness, honesty) and stylistic preferences (e.g., coherence, complexity). Analyses across multiple LLM families reveal functional specialization and structured interactions among value heads, including conflicts between certain values. Targeted head ablations further induce substantial, value-specific behavioral changes, providing causal evidence of their functional importance. Building on these findings, we propose an inference-time activation steering strategy that enables controllable alignment with individual values or combinations of values without updating the underlying model parameters, supported by theoretical analysis. Experiments across multiple value alignment benchmarks demonstrate effective single- and multi-value control while largely preserving non-target model performance. Our findings connect mechanistic interpretability with flexible multi-value alignment, offering a foundation for more interpretable and pluralistic LLM behavior. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Benchmark of THReD: Evaluating an LLM Agent and Its Preprocessing Tools for Turning Electronic Health Records into AI-Ready Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02484)**  `arXiv:2611.02484`  `cs.AI`  
  _Javad Mohammad Alizadeh, Huanmei Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly built into data-analysis software, yet little evidence shows how reliably they preprocess clinical data or whether the tools they invoke give correct, reproducible output. We report a benchmark of THReD, a web-based system that turns electronic health record (EHR) extracts into AI-ready datasets through a chat agent whose proposed steps run only after user confirmation. The agent benchmark has 141 tasks (100 templates) in seven categories, including goal-level requests, failure recovery, requests calling for no action, schema variation and prompt injection through the data, on synthetic tables calibrated to a regional health information exchange; success is judged by checks on the produced data. Over 2,550 episodes with Claude Haiku 5.5 in four context configurations, the deployed agent completed 92.7% (95% CI 88.1-96.4) of tasks on its first answer, abstained on every advice request, recovered from every injected fault, and never proposed an action requested by planted text. Without the data profile, completion fell to 58.0%; goal-level requests were hardest (80.6%). Six experiments on real records of two chronic-disease cohorts and on synthetic data at scale validate the tools: 91-92% of inferred patient classes and 99.7-99.8% of inferred vital-sign labels were correct in masked reconstruction; a dictionary builder combining curated dictionaries with LLM suggestions mapped 99.0% of 2,208 raw variants correctly; all 2,176 replays of 128 saved pipelines gave byte-identical files under pinned dependencies; subgroup differences in inference accuracy vanished within facility and lookup key; and the same-day consolidation rule changed 90-day emergency revisit rates by up to 3.2 points without shifting cohort composition by more than one point. Processing time grew roughly linearly to 5.5 million records while the prompt stayed at about 13 thousand tokens.
  </details>

- **[RLiD: Reinforcement Learning in the Decision Plane for Language Agents and LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02455)**  `arXiv:2611.02455`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziyi Chen, Yan Zhang, Jianhui Wei, Jianing Yin, Daoan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has improved the capabilities of large language models (LLMs) across a wide range of tasks. However, token-level RL assigns an action's advantage to the entire generated response, including tokens specifying the parsed action, reasoning chains, and malformed content. Although the environment executes only the parsed action, variation in how the action is expressed in natural language can introduce additional variance into policy-gradient estimates. We propose RLiD (Reinforcement Learning in the Decision plane), a framework that directly optimizes the probabilities of executable action options. RLiD compiles observations, an optional compact memory, and a labeled action menu into the model input, and uses a large decision model (LDM) to select an action in a single forward pass. By eliminating autoregressive token generation and bypassing action parsing, RLiD avoids invalid action outputs and improves RL rollout efficiency. Our analysis shows that, within a fixed text policy, marginalizing over alternative realizations of the same action removes a zero-mean gradient component without changing the expected update. We further distill trained LDM policies into generative LLMs through supervision on their action trajectories. Experiments on ARC, Countdown, and Sokoban demonstrate improved performance or lower computational cost across several settings. On ALFWorld, the complete training and distillation pipeline reaches 99.3% success in 12.7 H100 GPU-hours, compared with 86.6% in 17.3 GPU-hours for token-level RL of the same LLM. With compiled memory, our strongest Qwen2.5-1.5B LDM and distilled generative LLM achieve 100% success on all 134 unseen and 140 seen ALFWorld evaluation games.
  </details>

- **[RecGroupMemory: Feedback-Aware Cohort Memory for E-Commerce Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02453)**  `arXiv:2611.02453`  `cs.AI`  
  _Wendong Zhang, Weipeng Huang, Zhixin Ma, Bin Zhang, Dimin Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cohort memory can support LLM-based recommendation planning through group-level user profiles and advice. As outcome feedback becomes available, this guidance may need to be updated while preserving the underlying observations. We present RecGroupMemory, a workflow that provides this memory to an orchestration LLM and separates memory construction from feedback-based revision. It uses feedback from the previous day to revise decision guidance, while validation checks keep selected source values unchanged. Online comparisons were conducted among Taobao users with low purchasing power selected by the business team. Relative to a no-memory baseline, memory without feedback and memory with feedback showed increases of 1.58% and 1.78%, respectively, in the number of guided transaction users.
  </details>

- **[SGR-OPSD: Learning from Subgoal Plans with Graph-Regularized Credit Assignment for Agentic Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02414)**  `arXiv:2611.02414`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shu Liu, Yang Chong, WenShi Chen, Man Lan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  OPSD provides dense supervision for agentic reinforcement learning through privileged information feedback. However, existing privileged information often lacks explicit task-progression planning, while teacher-student log-probability gaps reflect shifts in model preference rather than true task progress. To address these limitations, we propose Subgoal-Guided Graph-Regularized OPSD (SGR-OPSD), a framework that combines subgoal supervision with interaction-derived evidence for turn-level credit assignment. Specifically, SGR-OPSD uses LLM-generated subgoal plans to provide dense action-level guidance, converting them into advantage weights via a constrained, KL-regularized objective. To ground credit in observed outcomes, we construct a Subgoal Progress Graph that estimates empirical returns from sampled transition frequencies. The graph compares empirical action returns against the state average to derive preferences that calibrate the teacher's credit weights. Evaluated on ALFWorld, WebShop, and Search-QA across two model scales (Qwen2.5-3B and 7B), SGR-OPSD consistently outperforms GRPO and strong self-distillation baselines, improving success rates over GRPO by up to 7.4 percentage points and reaching 85.8% on ALFWorld with Qwen2.5-7B. Ablation studies confirm that subgoal guidance and graph-based weight calibration are key to these performance gains.
  </details>

- **[Toward Agentic AI-Ready Workflows in Built Environment Organizations: Knowledge Structures and Interoperability Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02286)**  `arXiv:2611.02286`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ahmet Bahaddin Ersoz, Justin McHenry, Yevgeniya Kovalchuk, Edward Barrett, Mandana Sarey Khanie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rise of agentic artificial intelligence (AI) in the built environment is shifting attention from isolated AI tools toward autonomous systems that reason, plan, and act across project and organizational workflows. The effectiveness of such systems depends not only on large language model capabilities, but also on the readiness of heterogeneous data environments across the Architecture, Engineering, and Construction (AEC) sector. These environments combine structured sources, such as CAD and BIM models, schedules, cost databases, and asset registers, with unstructured ones, including technical reports, design narratives, planning and policy documents, contracts, meeting minutes, and risk registers. This paper reviews how built environment data can be organized to support agentic AI-ready workflows, focusing on knowledge structures, interoperability, and access and integration mechanisms such as retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and the Model Context Protocol (MCP). Drawing on existing literature, it examines approaches proposed to organize structured and unstructured data and considers how such data can be contextualized and made accessible to AI agents in a reliable manner. The paper synthesizes current perspectives, highlights challenges around fragmentation and interoperability, and identifies open questions for future research on AI-compatible information management in the built environment.
  </details>

- **[The 3-Tier Integrated Technology Risk Model for Occupational Safety and Health](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02257)**  `arXiv:2611.02257`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ramiro Pesina, Ashish Madkaikar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper presents the 3-Tier Integrated Technology Risk Model, a progressive framework for Occupational Safety and Health (OSH) management that integrates Job Safety Analysis (JSA), dynamic risk assessment using leading and lagging indicators, and predictive analytics powered by Large Language Models (LLMs). The model provides organizations with a structured pathway to evolve from static, reactive safety practices to a sophisticated, predictive safety paradigm. Each tier builds upon the previous one, creating a comprehensive, data-driven approach that enables just-in-time training and preemptive risk mitigation.
  </details>

- **[Can LLMs Reliably Evaluate Themselves? A Probabilistic VC Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02253)**  `arXiv:2611.02253`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jae Oh Woo, Mengdie Flora Wang, Rahul Ghosh, Baishali Chaudhury, Mun Young Kim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) find increasing use in critical applications, evaluating their ability to assess their own outputs has become crucial. We present a theoretical and empirical framework that examines whether LLMs can differentiate between correct and incorrect solutions while maintaining properly calibrated confidence. Building on classical Vapnik-Chervonenkis (VC) theory, we adapt it to probabilistic predictors through two complexity metrics: Probabilistic VC (PVC), which measures a model's ability to confidently classify across problem types, and Calibration-aware PVC (C-PVC), which additionally demands alignment between confidence scores and actual success rates. Unlike Expected Calibration Error (ECE) and Actual Error (AE), these measures give an integrated assessment of self-evaluation expressiveness and calibration, with sample complexity bounds and generalization guarantees comparable to classical VC theory. We test eleven 7-8B models on 360 original mathematical reasoning problems, TruthfulQA, and CommonsenseQA. Each model chooses between two of its own generated solutions and reports its confidence, with ground truth determined by an ensemble of larger models. The results show a systematic inverse relationship: models with greater self-evaluation expressiveness have weaker calibration. s1.1-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct achieve the highest PVC-VUS scores, while JiuZhang3.0-7B is best calibrated, with the lowest ECE and the smallest PVC-VUS gap. The trade-off persists across architectures, training paradigms, and domains, pointing to a fundamental challenge in developing self-reflective LLMs, and our framework offers practical tools for diagnosing it.
  </details>

- **[TIGER: Learning from Researcher Taste to Generate Tailored Research Ideas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02234)**  `arXiv:2611.02234`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shane Gu, Yufeng Lin, Dixi Yao, Xuchen Gong, Chenhao Tan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In pairwise comparisons where each judge chooses between an idea generated by its corresponding TIGER-8B policy and one generated by GPT-4.1, the three LLM researchers select the TIGER-8B idea in 56.6%-68.4% of 76 comparisons per researcher. PhD1 and PhD2 select the TIGER-8B idea in 19 of 30 pairs (63.3%) and 17 of 30 pairs (56.7%), respectively. All six directed cross-policy comparisons likewise favor the judge's matched TIGER-8B policy over an off-target policy, supporting the use of individual preference feedback for tailored research ideation.
  </details>

- **[ZeroGraph: Compositional Blockwise Training via Materialized Boundary Supervision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02200)**  `arXiv:2611.02200`  `cs.AI`  
  _Omar Ramadan, Sam Siavoshian, Amir Kashif Saeed, Benjamin A. Johnson, Amin M. E.-A. Diab, Benjamin M. Rodriguez_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Blockwise learning can remove online gradient dependencies across depth, but independently optimized blocks need not compose into a useful model. We present ZeroGraph, a systems transformation for pretrained-model conversion and distillation that materializes a frozen teacher's coherent boundary trajectory as a versioned dataset. Each partition then trains with disjoint parameters, optimizer state, random state, and checkpoints, replacing live cross-depth dependencies with persistent supervision. Experiments separate learning quality from systems behavior. On GPT-2 Large, an unanchored objective reduces denoising error by 99.44%, yet assembled held-out cross-entropy worsens from 3.63 to 10.48 and context use becomes negative. A six-arm TinyLlama study identifies clean teacher-boundary regression as the successful interface; the tested noisy objective fails with cached or online targets. The clean recipe passes quality and context-use gates across three Qwen2.5-1.5B seeds and on a 3.086B Qwen2.5 model. In an objective-matched comparison, cached and online targets are bitwise identical. Cached jobs are faster after materialization, but a 618.31 s cache-build cost prevents wall-time break-even through 16 measured reuses. Boundary-shift tests show tolerance to intermediate representation mismatch, and an interrupted block resumes to a bitwise-identical final state without rerunning unaffected blocks. In the tested pretrained-model conversion and distillation setting, cross-depth semantic dependence can move from online optimization into persistent data.
  </details>

- **[State-Aware Virtual Patient Simulation for Training Psychiatrists in Challenging Clinical Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02067)**  `arXiv:2611.02067`  `cs.AI`  
  _Harsh Mohta, C Chandrahas Reddy, Sunhith Reddy Bojja, Manik Inder Singh Sethi, Vinesh Gupta, Raghuram Bharadwaj Diddigi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Psychiatrists must be prepared to manage patients exhibiting challenging psychological states, including suicidal ideation, hallucinations, and other high-risk behaviors. However, opportunities to safely practice such interactions are limited. While recent conversational agents can simulate patients, existing approaches largely rely on large language models (LLMs) to determine patient responses, making it difficult to consistently maintain psychological state, adapt to the clinician's responses, and generate clinically plausible challenges that meaningfully test the clinician. We present a state-aware virtual patient simulation pipeline for training psychiatrists in challenging clinical scenarios. The proposed system maintains an evolving patient state throughout the interaction and orchestrates patient behavior rather than leaving response generation entirely to an LLM. At each interaction, a learning-based behavioral strategy selector determines the type and level of challenge appropriate to the current patient and clinician context. The selected strategy, patient state, clinician response, interaction history, and relevant clinical knowledge are then provided as grounded context to an LLM to generate a semantically relevant and psychologically consistent patient response. Following each session, the system analyzes the interaction and provides feedback to the clinician on their communication and management of the virtual patient.
  </details>

- **[LARK: Lean-Assisted Reasoning for LLM-Generated GPU Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02028)**  `arXiv:2611.02028`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zixi Zhang, Jiayi Nie, Haoran Wu, Yujie Yan, Yann Herklotz, Rika Antonova, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Efficient GPU kernels are typically low-level and hardware-specific, and LLM agents can now generate them faster than experts can verify. Existing approaches rely on testing, which provides evidence but not a guarantee of correctness. We introduce LARK, an agentic framework that automatically and formally verifies GPU kernels against their PyTorch reference implementations. LARK proves equivalence in functional and memory behavior under exact arithmetic and establishes a new trust boundary: human effort is only needed to verify operator-level Lean semantics ahead of kernel generation time, instead of checking every single line of generated code. LARK formalizes the GPU machine model and the semantics of each operation in Lean 4 once per target language, and the Lean 4 semantics of any incoming kernel is derived from these pre-built definitions. LLM agents then automate the proof in two steps. First, an agent decomposes the whole kernel semantics into smaller segments and Lean 4 verifies this segmentation, which addresses the intractability of proving equivalence for large kernels directly. Second, agents write Lean proofs that each kernel segment matches its high-level PyTorch counterpart. Together, the two steps give whole-program equivalence. We demonstrate that LARK can be used 1) to ensure correctness in existing code-generation agents and 2) to automatically verify human-written kernel libraries. On the KernelCraft GPU tasks, combined with a simple ReAct agent, LARK enables GLM 5.3 to fully verify 10 of 12 Level-1 kernels and Opus 5.5 all 5 Level-2 kernels. Moreover, over 10 production kernels randomly sampled from popular open-source LLM serving frameworks, LARK verifies 9 and identifies a new data race bug in the tenth. We believe LARK represents a significant step toward verifiable code generation in the agentic era.
  </details>

- **[Safety4DM: A Vision-Language Framework for Risk-Aware Dynamic Maps in Vehicle-Road-Cloud Collaborative Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01996)**  `arXiv:2611.01996`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengshu Zhou, Shaoyu Wang, Lu Tao, Xinhui Hu, Jiaqi Gu, Tingting Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic map (DM) is a core information infrastructure for vehicle-road-cloud (VRC) cooperative autonomous driving. It fuses perception data from multiple sources to overcome the inherent line-of-sight and field-of-view limits of single-vehicle perception. However, DM still falls short on driving safety in two ways. First, DM data specifications already define road conditions semantically, but no pipeline yet turns real-world road observations into fine-grained risk entities in the DM. Second, existing DM systems lack a natural language interface for driving risks. To close these gaps, we propose Safety4DM, a safety-oriented language system for VRC dynamic maps. We first build RoadRisk, a real-world road image dataset that covers four types of road conditions and four risk levels. On this dataset, we fine-tune a vision-language model (VLM) for road safety risk perception. The model outputs structured risk representations that the DM can ingest directly. We then introduce a Safety-aware Step-wise Prompting (SafeStep) mechanism as the natural language query interface of the DM. SafeStep answers queries accurately and also generates actionable driving advice. To build and evaluate this interface, we develop SafeVRC-Sim, a VRC cooperative simulation framework, and SafeDM-QA, a safety question-answering benchmark. Experiments show that even the compact Qwen3-VL-4B reaches 89.83% accuracy on risk perception after fine-tuning. SafeStep also plugs easily into multiple LLM families. With medium and large models (20B and above) from five families, it achieves over 93% natural language query accuracy in every case, which shows strong generalization.
  </details>

- **[RubricWorld: World Model-Guided Rubric Optimization for LLM Graders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01986)**  `arXiv:2611.01986`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hongji Pu, Hang Li, Yucheng Chu, Jiliang Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rubric optimization improves LLM grading by revising scoring instructions while keeping the grader's parameters fixed. Existing optimizers often judge edits by aggregate validation performance, limiting the supervision they retain about which errors an edit corrects or introduces. Testing each candidate also requires rescoring validation answers, which limits how many edits can be explored within a fixed budget. We propose RubricWorld, a framework that learns from previous edit evaluations to guide further search. Its world model predicts an edit's correction and degradation rates at score boundaries, conditioned on the current rubric state. The training signal comes from paired grading results: scores for the same answers before and after an edit. Predictions select one candidate for direct evaluation, and held-out confidence bounds determine whether it corrects enough target errors while keeping new errors within prespecified limits. Measured effects update the model even when the edit is rejected. Across EIR (National), ASAP2.0, and ASAP-SAS with three frozen LLM graders, RubricWorld achieves the highest score in 20 of 27 metric comparisons. On ASAP2.0, it raises gpt-5-mini accuracy from 18.20% with the original rubric to 46.70%, exceeding the strongest baseline, APO, by 22.60 percentage points. code will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Multimodal Emotion Conflict: Temporal Context-Aware Hidden Emotion Detection with Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01943)**  `arXiv:2611.01943`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yonghao Wang, Jiaxin Zhou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal emotion recognition commonly combines textual, acoustic, and visual signals to infer affective states. Recent work has begun to study conflicts among modalities, but a conflict does not necessarily indicate that a person is concealing an emotion. It may also result from mixed emotions, individual expressive styles, or contextual factors. This proposal aims to distinguish ordinary emotional inconsistency from emotion concealment and to infer the underlying emotion when supported by evidence. We plan to construct a multimodal benchmark with explicit annotations of expressed emotion, internal emotion, and concealment conditions, and to develop an MLLM-based framework that integrates fine-grained facial Action Units (AUs), cross-modal features, and temporal context.
  </details>

- **[Is Your Long-Horizon Benchmark Really Deep? Measuring and Synthesizing Depth in Terminal Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01927)**  `arXiv:2611.01927`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Jiang, Yue Huang, Hang Hua, Yuyang Wu, Zixuan Weng, Olexandr Isayev, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon terminal tasks are used to evaluate and train agents. Human-curated datasets are scarce, so many synthesis methods have emerged. They usually measure the length of a task by the turns a model needs to pass it, and its difficulty by labels the LLM sets during generation. We find, however, that the turn count largely reflects the habits of the model, not only the demands of the task, and that the labels barely track how often models pass. We describe a terminal task by its work, the number of operations it requires, and its depth, the number of turns that must happen in sequence because the commands of each turn can only be chosen after reading the output of the previous one. Estimating depth as the smallest turn cap, stated to the agent, under which the task passes, we audit 734 tasks from six existing datasets. Synthesis has mostly added work, not depth: 94% of the solved synthetic tasks pass within three turns, yet depth, unlike the turn count, predicts difficulty beyond work. We propose DEPTH, in which a generation agent writes each task one segment at a time in a real software environment, carrying out each segment before writing it and starting the next from the state it leaves. Our 105 tasks exceed every audited dataset in depth and work, and none of six current models passes half of them. Fine-tuning Qwen3-8B on runs from these tasks improves it on two terminal benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Does reflective LLM feedback survive a wet-lab-scale label budget?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01908)**  `arXiv:2611.01908`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haoxuan Zeng, Xin Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can reflective language-model feedback improve a biological predictor when each cycle buys only one plate of labels? We separate three possible sources of benefit: proposing model edits, choosing measurements, and revising decisions through repeated feedback. We replay $36$ ProteinGym assays, starting with $96$ labels and acquiring $96$ per round for six rounds, with a sealed test set and a shared edit schema. The pre-specified comparison of full LLM control against random edits detects no advantage in test rank correlation ($\Delta z=-0.008$, $95\%$ CI $[-0.034,+0.018]$); equivalence is also unestablished. Exploratory ablations nevertheless identify useful model editing. With random acquisition fixed, LLM edits improve Fisher-transformed test Spearman by $0.043$ over no edits; with acquisition fixed to upper confidence bound, they improve normalized top-variant recovery by $0.023$. LLM acquisition alone does not detectably outperform fixed adaptive rules, and repeated feedback does not detectably outperform one initial recommendation under full control. Thus, the observed gains support constrained specialist editing, while the incremental value of recursive reflection remains unestablished at this label budget.
  </details>

- **[NEIGHBOR: Aligning Service Behaviour and Evidence Use in an LLM Agent for Community Resident Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01836)**  `arXiv:2611.01836`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zongyou Yang, Yinghan Hou, Xiaokun Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Residents who contact a community service desk about a repair or a service charge need answers they can act on, with facts that hold for their case and commitments the desk can keep. In tool-augmented LLM agents, such facts come from documents and records, and the conduct of a service answer is usually specified in a system prompt. We present NEIGHBOR, an LLM agent for community resident services that trains this conduct into the model that drives its tools. NEIGHBOR is deployed on a live resident-service platform for a district of an East Asian city of more than ten million people. It adapts Qwen3-235B-A22B with LoRA on 7,824 supervised fine-tuning examples built from public community-service materials around six service-behaviour targets, among them commitment control and human referral, and the adapted model plans document retrieval and read-only text-to-SQL over the resident's own records. In an offline evaluation with seven configurations, an independent domain expert rated all 350 answers to 50 stratified held-out queries. NEIGHBOR scores 7.74/10 against 5.86 for the same agent running the un-tuned model (+1.88; paired Wilcoxon $p<10^{-8}$; higher on 46 of 50 queries) and improves all six rated dimensions. No tool-augmented answer received a red-line flag (standalone models: 8-14% of answers). An LLM judge ranks the seven configurations much as the expert does (Spearman $\rho=0.93$). Its rubric counts specifics absent from the reference answer as fabricated, so it scores tool-grounded answers 1-2 points lower, enough to change which configuration ranks first. For resident-facing agents, service behaviour is a practical fine-tuning target that raises expert-rated answer quality with the tools held fixed.
  </details>

- **[Delivered, Not Used: Evaluating Agent Memory at Its Point of Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01718)**  `arXiv:2611.01718`  `cs.AI`  
  _Changran Xu, Zeju Li, Qiang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task success, retrieval quality, and cost do not show whether an LLM agent used a delivered memory or what followed from that use. We introduce MemUse, which evaluates memory at its point of use in recorded trajectories: each memory delivery is compared with what the agent could already see and followed through the agent's later actions and their consequences. An agentic judge rates each delivered entry's relevance, accuracy, novelty, influence, and observed effect; programmatic checks enforce complete reading of cited evidence, and fixed rules aggregate the ratings into verdicts linked to memory histories and delivery cost. On 8,000 SWE-bench Verified episodes from three memory systems, resolve rates stay within 0.4 percentage points of a no-memory baseline, yet MemUse shows that most delivered entries were irrelevant, most relevant entries went unused, and in two systems most deliveries restated content the agent could still see. It also surfaces effects that outcomes hide: 23.2\% of episodes with beneficial memory use still failed, and 5 of 16 with harmful use still succeeded.
  </details>

- **[Just-in-Time Skill Retrieval via Long-Horizon State Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01701)**  `arXiv:2611.01701`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xin Lin, Ziheng Wang, Zerui Chen, Jiayao Ma, Zhimin Wei, Bingshuai Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external skill libraries to support complex task execution. However, determining which skills are needed remains challenging in long-horizon tasks. Existing methods typically select a fixed skill set before execution, implicitly assuming that a task's procedural requirements can be determined from its initial description. This assumption is restrictive for long-horizon interaction, where new requirements emerge from intermediate observations, execution outcomes, and newly revealed constraints. This motivates just-in-time skill retrieval, which dynamically selects skills as execution unfolds. Just-in-time skill retrieval is promising but challenging: the utility of a skill depends not only on the current context, but also on the evolving task state shaped by prior steps. To this end, we propose JitSkill, a state-grounded framework that addresses this challenge through continuous task-state tracking. By connecting emerging requirements with accumulated task progress, JitSkill provides procedural support throughout execution without treating each retrieval decision independently. Experiments across three agent benchmarks demonstrate its effectiveness in improving long-horizon task completion. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SPAR: Experience-Driven Strategy Evolution in Multi-Session B2B Sales](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01695)**  `arXiv:2611.01695`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hangming Zhang, Yao Zhao, Haotian Kuang, C. L. Philip Chen, Tong Zhang, Tengyue Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  B2B outbound sales involves high transaction values and relationship-based trust, so closing a deal typically requires multiple rounds of outreach building on prior context. Existing LLM-based sales agents operate within single sessions and cannot carry forward context or refine strategies from experience. Methods that do learn from experience store lessons as unstructured text and treat all outcomes as equally informative, with no mechanism to distinguish a flawed strategy from an external factor. We propose SPAR, a self-evolving framework that models selling strategies as testable hypotheses and refines them without parameter updates. A double-loop architecture lets an inner loop adapt to individual merchants through episodic memory while an outer loop evolves a shared strategy playbook through causal attribution and hypothesis-driven evolution. In a live A/B test on the 1688 platform with over 200,000 merchants, SPAR achieves +15.8% conversion lift and +35.8% high-intent merchant growth. Human evaluation by domain experts rates SPAR at 4.62 out of 5 on overall sales quality, the highest among all methods.
  </details>

- **[\textsc{ChatDify}: Automating End-to-End Dify Workflow Synthesis from Natural Language via Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01563)**  `arXiv:2611.01563`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tao Fan, Guoqiang Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The proliferation of Large Language Models has catalyzed powerful application development platforms like Dify, which enable users to build sophisticated LLM-powered workflows through modular node orchestration.
