However, manually configuring these workflows requires deep platform expertise and remains time-consuming and error-prone, creating a significant barrier for non-expert users. To address this challenge, we propose \textsc{ChatDify}, a novel multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end Dify workflow synthesis from natural language descriptions. \textsc{ChatDify} employs an Actor-Critic architecture comprising three specialized agents: the \textit{Planner Agent} that decomposes user requests into structured execution plans, the \textit{Executor Agent} that performs context-aware parameter synthesis and schema-augmented DSL compilation, and the \textit{Reviewer Agent} that ensures reliability through hybrid verification with closed-loop refinement. We evaluate \textsc{ChatDify} on a curated benchmark of 100 production-inspired tasks across three state-of-the-art LLM backends, achieving 0.96, 0.91, and 0.75 success rates with Qwen-3.7, GLM-5.1, and DeepSeek-v4, respectively. These results demonstrate its robustness and practical applicability for lowering the technical barrier to creating sophisticated LLM applications.
  </details>

- **[Compile as Hypothesis: A Headroom Typology for Prompt Optimization on Real Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01135)**  `arXiv:2611.01135`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhengshuai Yang, Changyun Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated prompt compilers search for or rewrite prompts under the implicit assumption that optimization consistently improves downstream task performance. We evaluate this assumption on frontier reasoning models through rigorous paired, same-day measurements. Across four optimizer families (genome search, profile-guided mutation, GEPA, and ESPO) and eight tasks, structured search yields no statistically significant accuracy gains on strong reasoners. However, a discrete structural genome provides essential architectural stability, preventing catastrophic bimodal seed-collapse (where unconstrained search collapses toward the judge floor in 3/5 runs). Through 190-problem evaluations on knowledge-intensive benchmarks, we establish a three-dimensional headroom typology: prompt optimization cannot compensate for intrinsic knowledge deficits or physical token truncation, but successfully resolves protocol and formatting errors. On gpt-6, while free-text reflective search (GEPA) yields a +.14 gain on a task where structured search remains flat, qualitative ablation reveals that ~.035 stems from judge-rubric reconstruction while ~.105 reflects genuine instruction refinement. Our pre-registered AutoAPC-Select deployment gate matches the oracle policy in 5/7 groups and rescues seed-collapse arms with zero regret. Finally, we expose silent backend-mixing in commercial APIs during outage windows and provide automated per-call audit verification tools to ensure empirical evaluation fidelity.
  </details>

- **[HiGenTrans: Hierarchical LLM Knowledge Transfer for Generative Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01129)**  `arXiv:2611.01129`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuohang Jiang, Yuxin Chen, Haohao Qu, ZeFei luo, Yunlong Wang, Xu Yuan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative recommendation promises to simplify conventional multi-stage recommender systems by generating item Semantic IDs (SIDs) autoregressively. However, two obstacles limit its effective- ness in industrial settings: interaction-only supervision underuses the semantic knowledge encoded in pretrained language models, while beam-search likelihood ranks token sequences rather than directly estimating item relevance. To address these limitations, we propose HiGenTrans, a three-level framework that introduces LLM knowledge at the input, representation, and decision levels. First, Global Semantic Priming (GSP) adds an LLM-derived user representation to the beginning-of-sequence embedding, provid- ing a semantic prior before SID generation. Second, Hierarchi- cal Representation Transfer (HRT) aligns intermediate LLM representations with depth-matched GenRec layers through mean- centered projections and gated residual connections. Finally, Multi- Signal Decision Calibration (MDC) uses a lightweight mixture- of-experts calibrator to combine semantic relevance, generation confidence, and candidate-set context when reranking beam-search outputs. All semantic features are computed only from the observ- able interaction prefix, preventing target leakage, and can be cached offline. Experiments on three public benchmarks show that HiGen- Trans improves Recall@10 by up to 12.9% and NDCG@10 by up to 33.7% over the strongest baselines. These results demonstrate the value of transferring LLM knowledge at complementary stages of the generative recommendation pipeline.
  </details>

- **[Constraint Dependency Graph Retrieval for Compliance Detection on Long SKILL Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01042)**  `arXiv:2611.01042`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuaitao Zhao, Feng Ni, Lichao Ma, Yuchen Wei, Shuaike Li, Jiaye Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents carry out business operations under enterprise documents we refer to as SKILL documents, which take the form of standard operating procedures (SOPs) that state the conditions to verify and the actions to take before an operation proceeds. Long, densely cross-referenced SKILL documents exceed the context budget of small deployed auditors. Similarity-based retrieval then brings in irrelevant rules while omitting mandatory prerequisites, and the two-stage audit policy provides no trainable signal for the retrieval stage. We present SkillCDG, a compliance detection framework that reformulates each SKILL document as a constraint dependency graph, shifting rule retrieval from similarity-based matching to closure-based selection under the deployment's context budget. Each atomic rule is anchored to its source text span as a graph node, and mandatory prerequisite relations form the sole edge type. Policy-generated retrieval keywords activate seed rules, whose dependency closure yields a compact and conditionally complete rule context within that budget. The same graph also supports a joint optimization scheme. The retrieval stage is trained with group-relative policy optimization using a verifiable reward that combines output-format validity with downstream verdict correctness, with closure non-emptiness serving as a validity gate, while selective on-policy distillation concentrates teacher supervision on high-entropy reasoning tokens, with verdict and termination tokens always included, to improve verdict accuracy. Experiments on three enterprise datasets and two public benchmarks show that SkillCDG improves accuracy and F1 over the full-document and retrieval-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[Pulse: Long-Horizon Tasks Need Streaming Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00988)**  `arXiv:2611.00988`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Jialiang Liang, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents increasingly undertake complex, long-horizon tasks that benefit from sustained personalized support through long-term memory. Existing memory-augmented methods retrieve and use memory primarily from the initial request, with limited awareness of ongoing execution and emerging personalization needs. Agents may therefore overlook user preferences and project constraints during multistep tasks. We propose Pulse, a streaming personalization method for long-horizon tasks. An independent, lightweight memory agent continuously monitors execution, identifies emerging personalization needs and potential preference conflicts, and uses user and project memories to provide targeted interventions at appropriate stages. Reinforcement learning optimizes when and how the memory agent intervenes, improving the timeliness and accuracy of personalized support. Pulse scores 71.9% on $\pi$-Bench and 46.4% on MCP-Persona, achieving the highest overall scores among the compared methods on both datasets while improving task execution efficiency.
  </details>

- **[VCoT-SQL: Enhancing Text-to-SQL Generation through Dual Chain-of-Thought Validation Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00815)**  `arXiv:2611.00815`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zhang, Yujia Su, Ruixuan Li, Yuhua Li, Yixin Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Open-source LLMs provide a flexible alternative to closed-source models for Text-to-SQL but still struggle with complex queries. Finetuning with chain-of-thought (CoT) data improves reasoning, yet standard datasets lack CoT annotations, requiring synthetic generation. Existing synthesis methods only verify the final answer, allowing flawed reasoning steps to corrupt the training data. We propose VCoT-SQL, a framework that improves Text-to-SQL generation through intermediate-step validated CoT supervision and a dedicated SQL validator, which enables small open-source models to outperform frontier closed-source models. VCoT-SQL trains two complementary models on curated reasoning traces: an SQL generator using clause-level CoT templates where reasoning steps are verified via execution and schema alignment checks, and an SQL validator using diagnostic CoT templates to detect syntactic, semantic, and type level errors. We further design a hierarchical schema filtering pipeline that integrates and refines complementary retrieval strategies to distill relevant database elements. VCoT-SQL achieves 66.82% execution accuracy on challenging dataset BIRD dev and 86.8% on Spider test, improving the base 7B model by 7% on BIRD. Notably, our CoT synthesis relies on 7B models yet outperforms methods that use 72B-scale or even proprietary closed source teachers for data generation, demonstrating that rigorous step-level validation can be more effective than scaling up teacher model size. Our dataset and code are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AI Data Analysis: A New Approach - Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00807)**  `arXiv:2611.00807`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jean Ye_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI data analysis today is three fragmented worlds: LLMs understand semantics but cannot compute; BI aggregates precisely but is stateless; ML processes large-scale data on a stateful kernel but lacks semantics. This paper proposes a combination mechanism, named Multi-engine Joint State Data Analysis, that fills a still-open gap - the multi-engine joint context: the data context is embedded into LLM conversational analysis, where the LLM serves only as the semantic, reasoning, and orchestration layer (not computation) and connects four engines through the 1M context window - Python and R ML kernels, SQL over relational databases, Cypher over graph databases - yielding two multiplications, "semantics and precise computation" and "relational and graph", under enterprise-grade governance. The novelty is a four-dimensional coordinate system: horizontal (engine kinds), vertical (state flow - the watershed), spatial (business generalization), temporal (iterative convergence); a deterministic grounding method (one-shot injection ofthis http URL+this http URL, M+S) carries the spatial and temporal dimensions. Empirically, all four dimensions are substantiated: horizontally, four engines are quantified - SQL, Cypher (96.5% on CypherBench movie), ML in Python and R (TabReD weather); vertically, state flows across engines and grounding is reused across kernels; spatially, SQL generalizes across three business databases - AdventureWorks 91.3%, financial 95.3%, formula_1 94.6% (average), about 20-23 points above single-shot SOTA 74.45%; temporally, accuracy converges monotonically as notes accumulate. The core invariant - the separation of semantics from computation plus a cross-data-domain persistent context - does not expire as LLMs and agents evolve or engine forms change.
  </details>

- **[Who Schedules Agent Memory? A Sidecar Approach to Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00779)**  `arXiv:2611.00779`  `cs.AI`  
  _Qijia Zhuang, Rui Liu, Yuxiang Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language model agents increasingly undertake complex, long-horizon tasks, memory has become an important component for supporting sustained decision-making and action. Existing methods primarily focus on memory storage, updating, and retrieval, but often overlook how memory scheduling affects task execution: the main agent must identify memory needs, initiate retrieval, and wait for results while advancing the task, increasing both scheduling burden and execution time. To address this problem, we propose MemSidecar, which decouples memory scheduling from task execution through two independently selectable modes. Hooks directly delivers bound memory to the main agent when execution events match predefined conditions. Foresight uses an independent agent to predict potential queries, retrieve results in parallel, and store them in a cache, delivering matching and valid results when actual requests arrive. Hooks and Foresight achieve scores of 41.23% and 44.34% on MCP-Persona, respectively, while effectively reducing execution costs and latency and improving the efficiency of long-horizon agent execution.
  </details>

- **[Designing Informative Actions: Action Construction for Long-Horizon Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00773)**  `arXiv:2611.00773`  `cs.AI`  
  _Alkut Arxidin, Shaoning Zeng, Jianhang Zhou, Jianping Gou, Xinzhong Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents support automated scientific research by connecting literature analysis and planning with experimentation. Evaluating which action an agent selects does not establish which experiment its instruction specifies, because omitted interventions or replication counts leave consequential choices to the executor. We introduce a reading-set framework for evaluating action construction under a common cost rule. Across 30 synthetic states, complete-design requests increase affordable efficiency by 465.98% and 327.91% relative to the action-name baseline in the two model families, respectively. An author-constructed research-action evaluation dataset provides 60 scenarios for complementary judgment evaluation. On this dataset, the scenario-level information-per-cost measure has a mean within-scenario correlation of 0.733 with judgments from 18 human participants. The framework makes design specification and execution ambiguity measurable, providing a reproducible basis for assessing how research agents translate scientific intent into informative, budget-feasible experiments.
  </details>

- **[Reading Between the Chapters: A Graph-Free RAG Rebuilt With AI and Human Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00752)**  `arXiv:2611.00752`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dipto Sumit, Syeda Fabiayee Jahan, Farig Sadeque_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many questions about a book can only be answered by connecting passages chapters apart, such as a cough heard three times across a horse's life. We introduce CrossChapterQA, 1,853 such questions over 14 public-domain books. Each question lists the two to six chapters it needs (spanning half the book at the median) and the relation linking them, cites evidence from each, and has two independent reference answers graded blind. We use it to build LadderRAG, which indexes a book as passages, summaries and book-level cards for about $0.41 and answers a question for under a cent. Its first version failed mostly at retrieval. We redesigned it four times, steered by an LLM judge's verdicts and our analyses of failed answers: a design-level counterpart of RLAIF and RLHF in which feedback changes the architecture, not model weights. We also report the two redesigns that made answers worse. On the 191 questions its first version got wrong, the final version retrieves over twice as many required chapters on held-out books (recall 0.36 to 0.79), and ten annotators rate its answers 6.5/10 on average (764 ratings).
  </details>

- **[Can Jev Improve Demonstration Retrieval for In-Context Learning?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00737)**  `arXiv:2611.00737`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Song, Yingji Li, Hao Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Demonstration selection plays a crucial role in the effectiveness of in-context learning (ICL) for large language models (LLMs). Extensive research has explored various retrieval strategies to identify informative demonstrations and improve downstream performance. Recently, the emergence of Jev, a decision-oriented language model capable of producing probabilistic judgments, offers a new perspective on evaluating demonstration utility. This motivates us to investigate a fundamental question: Can Jev improve demonstration retrieval for in-context learning? In this paper, we demonstrate that Jev can effectively enhance demonstration retrieval by directly assessing the utility of candidate demonstrations for a given query. However, applying Jev to large-scale retrieval introduces substantial computational overhead, as each candidate requires an additional decision-making inference. To address this limitation, we propose a simple yet effective knowledge distillation approach that transfers Jev's utility estimation capability to a lightweight multilayer perceptron (MLP). By learning to approximate Jev's probabilistic judgments, the distilled model enables efficient demonstration ranking while maintaining competitive retrieval performance. Our study highlights the potential of decision-oriented language models for demonstration selection and provides an efficient framework for integrating their decision-making capabilities into ICL.
  </details>

- **[Evidence-grounded large language models guide perovskite solar cell design and optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00633)**  `arXiv:2611.00633`  `cs.AI`  
  _YanKai Chen, Zhi Wan, ZiHao Li, Tao Jing_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Using large language models to interpret experimental evidence, design discriminating experiments and formulate well-supported mechanistic explanations remains a central challenge in materials research. Here we establish PSM-Bench, an 800-question benchmark developed by perovskite researchers to evaluate scientific reasoning in perovskite photovoltaics. We also introduce OpenPerov, an expert-informed language-model framework that learns from expert-calibrated evidence records reconstructed from 4,532 articles; these records link experimental strategies, controls and observations to mechanistic interpretations. Structured-evidence adaptation outperformed conventional question-answer training, and OpenPerov Flash ranked first among 13 closed-book systems. OpenPerov Pro raised Scientific Mastery further, from 87.6 to 89.1, by revising selected passages with retrieved evidence. On questions derived from subsequent studies, both models also outscored expert-authored answers. In an experimental test of self-assembled monolayer design, OpenPerov selected a K analogue that outperformed the Na analogue preferred by experts. Devices incorporating the selected contact reached a power conversion efficiency of 27.05% and retained 96.74% of their steady-state reference efficiency after 1,000 h of dark thermal ageing at 85 $^\circ$C. These results show that reconstructed scientific reasoning provides an effective basis for building domain models that interpret experiments and inform materials design.
  </details>

- **[TopoQuant: Topological Framework to Characterize Behavioral Shifts in Quantized Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00631)**  `arXiv:2611.00631`  `cs.AI`  
  _Baha Rababah, Shahzeb Qamar, Lorenz Sparrenberg, Rafet Sifa, Murat Kantarcioglu, Carson K. Leung, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces the com putational cost of large language models (LLMs), but it can introduce internal structural and behavioral changes that are not captured by traditional metrics such as accuracy and per plexity. We present TopoQuant, a topological framework that uses topological data analysis (TDA) to quantify structural drift in self-attention weights and relate it to behavioral changes in quantized LLMs. Across four LLM families and multiple quantization levels, we show that topological drift remains stable at moderate compression levels but increases sharply below 4 bit precision, closely tracking declines in attention agreement. Our results reveal a strong relationship between topological drift and attention agreement, indicating that structural changes in model parameters closely reflect behavioral degradation under quantization. We further demonstrate that topological features can accurately predict attention consistency using lightweight machine learning models, achieving R2 scores of up to 0.85. Predicting attention agreement enables efficient assessment of be havioral consistency in quantized LLMs, allowing quantization induced degradation to be detected without running expensive downstream tasks. The results indicate that topological drift pro vides an efficient early-warning signal for quantization-induced degradation and can help identify safe compression limits without extensive downstream evaluation.
  </details>

- **[WAR-CRAFT: Refining Web API Recommendations through Requirement Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00564)**  `arXiv:2611.00564`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zishuo Xu, Dezhong Yao, Zhiqiang Xu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Web API recommenders return a set of APIs for a mashup request but do not check it against provider documentation, the main public evidence of an API's capabilities. The set can therefore contain unsupported or redundant APIs and miss requested capabilities. Filling every apparent gap does not fix this, because a gap may already be served, may not need its own API, or may have no documented provider. We study post-recommendation refinement, which revises a frozen recommender's output using only the request, the catalog, and provider documentation. We propose WAR-CRAFT, which treats refinement as three decisions. An LLM removes unsupported or subsumed APIs by judging candidates jointly, then audits the remaining set against a requirement checklist derived from the request. For each uncovered requirement, a fine-tuned catalog-constrained Resolver proposes a catalog API or abstains, and an independent documentation check admits only supported proposals. On ProgrammableWeb with 7 heterogeneous upstream recommenders, WAR-CRAFT raises mean SetF1 from 0.558 to 0.644, above the strongest of 5 refinement baselines (62.5%), and roughly halves functional redundancy (0.127 to 0.065) without changing mean functional coverage. Gains range from +2.2 points on generative recommenders to +18.2 on fixed-cutoff rankers. At matched set sizes, WAR-CRAFT still outperforms truncating the upstream output, so the gain is not explained by set-size calibration alone. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Low-Margin Branch Points in Deterministic LLM Deliberatio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00546)**  `arXiv:2611.00546`  `cs.AI`  
  _Hajime Shimao, Warut Khern-am-nua_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deterministic multi-agent LLM deliberation can exactly re- play a fixed transcript, yet exact replay alone does not test local resistance to controlled internal interventions. Because each response becomes context for later agents, a changed greedy token can redirect a transcript-mediated execution. We present a white-box audit of this local failure mode in a five-agent committee under greedy decoding. We prospectively select low- and high-margin token positions, require exact no-op re- play, inject norm-relative residual displacements, and measure first transcript divergence within ten generated tokens. Low- margin positions are more branch-sensitive than high-margin controls, while matched margin-reducing gradient directions branch substantially more often than margin-increasing con- trols. We then continue selected realized branches through the committee to distinguish textual persistence from preference and final-decision changes. The contribution is a controlled audit for local branch susceptibility in this specified protocol, not an estimate of deployment frequency, decision correct- ness, or a phenomenon unique to multi-agent systems.
  </details>

- **[To Jev or Not to Jev: When Can System-1 Decision Models Replace LLM Judges?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00505)**  `arXiv:2611.00505`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shreyas Subramanian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Decision models such as TypeSafe's Jev answer typed questions about a text (yes/no, multiple choice, or an ordinal score) with calibrated probabilities in a single forward pass and without generating text, which makes them an inexpensive candidate for LLM-as-a-judge workloads. We evaluate 14 System-1 decision models, selected with a pre-stated protocol, against four generative judges (Claude Haiku 5.5, GPT-5.6 with and without reasoning, and Claude Opus 5.5 with thinking) on eleven established judge benchmarks. Five of the 14 are statistically indistinguishable from GPT-5.6 without reasoning on all five pairwise and best-of-4 benchmarks, and the two strongest, DReX-Max and d1-ultra, are significantly more accurate than Haiku on JudgeBench, LLMBar and RewardBench 2. Conditioned on a reference answer generated once per prompt, eleven models reach 90.3-93.4% on JudgeBench, statistically indistinguishable from Opus 5.5 and GPT-5.6 with reasoning, and on PPE best-of-16 every reference-guided System-1 judge matches GPT-5.6 with reasoning at roughly one-sixteenth of its cost per prompt. Several System-1 judges are better calibrated than every generative judge and correlate more strongly with human helpfulness ratings. Robustness to instructions injected into the judged response is a property of the individual model rather than of the System-1 design: an appended note to the evaluator reverses 0-5% of the correct verdicts of five models but 17-49% for the other nine. Two general System-1 leaderboards that contain no judging tasks predict judging accuracy (Pearson r = 0.95-0.96), a well-calibrated System-1 first stage matches Opus on LLMBar while escalating 17% of items, and same-size controls show that the correctness deficit of a 4B decision model is a reasoning gap rather than a size gap.
  </details>

- **[Dependence-Aware Risk Assessment and Optimal Design of AI-Agent Safeguard Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00386)**  `arXiv:2611.00386`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijia Li, Maochao Xu, Peng Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on safeguards to prevent unauthorized actions while preserving legitimate service. However, existing risk assessments often focus on individual safeguard performance while overlooking dependence among safeguard decisions. This paper develops a dependence-aware framework for risk assessment and optimal design of multi-component AI safeguard systems, jointly selecting configurations and operating policies for sequential layered screening and selective verification. We establish stochastic comparisons of layered-screening error probabilities and invocation costs under lower-orthant ordering. For selective verification, we characterize exactly which policies have error probabilities and review costs that respond monotonically to stronger dependence under supermodular ordering. For a fixed first stage and reviewer, we show that the optimal routing map is a two-threshold rule in the first-stage likelihood ratio. An accompanying open-source software package, Dependence-Aware Policy Optimization Algorithms (DAPOA), facilitates the practical implementation of the proposed framework. Numerical experiments show that ignoring dependence can lead to inaccurate risk assessments and infeasible safeguard designs, with independence-based selections violating security requirements in six of eight scenarios. An empirical study using four LLM safeguards on benchmark-derived agent actions further demonstrates the practical implications of dependence modeling. Under the principal operating setting, dependence-aware selection lowers held-out total cost by 39.6% relative to independence-based selection.
  </details>

- **[OmniPiano: Diverse Dexterous Piano-Playing Challenges for Standard, Robust, Safe, and Multi-Agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00243)**  `arXiv:2611.00243`  `cs.AI`  
  _Fan Xu, Fan Yang, Yifan Xu, Ruiyuan Zhang, Yimou Wu, Nuo Yi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dexterous robotic manipulation remains a major challenge in robotics, exemplified by piano playing, which requires coordinated control of fingers and provides a demanding testbed for reinforcement learning (RL). Although previous work established a benchmark for robotic piano playing, it is restricted to two hands and does not support unified evaluation. To address this gap, we introduce OmniPiano, a benchmark that provides one to up to five Shadow Hands and supports standard, robust, safe, and multi-agent RL within a shared piano-playing task family. OmniPiano is developed for scalable and diverse piano-playing tasks with configurable perturbations, explicit safety constraints, and decentralized cooperation settings. Particularly, to facilitate usability and extendability, OmniPiano adopts a highly modular design and provides comprehensive tasks to support RL study of task performance, robustness, safety, and cooperation in dexterous control. With at least 912 task settings and 36 baseline algorithms, OmniPiano further incorporates LLM-based agents to broaden the evaluation scope and reveal new insights. Extensive evaluations show that state-of-the-art RL algorithms and frontier LLM-based agents still struggle on challenging piano-playing tasks, even under standard RL settings, highlighting substantial room for improvement in dexterous control. The code, dataset, tutorial, and demo are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[RE-SciVQA: Improving Scientific VQA With Context-Relevant Retrieval and Lightweight MLLM Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00242)**  `arXiv:2611.00242`  `cs.AI`  
  _Sheikh Tahmim Mehdi, Muminul Islam Jami_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A deep understanding of scientific literature often relies on interpreting figures that contain key information beyond the main text. Leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to extract insights from these figures can significantly accelerate knowledge discovery. Existing works on Scientific Visual Question Answering rely on resource-intensive training pipelines that are often inaccessible and require large computational resources. Instead of designing complex architectures, we propose RE- SciVQA, which focuses on improving the quality of retrieved context and fine-tuning small LLMs with parameter-efficient LoRA training. Using a simple query expansion step, relevant section headers are appended to the original question to improve the retrieval coverage. In addition, a simple adjustment of similarity scores is applied using figure references and caption alignment. On the SciVQA benchmark, RE-SciVQA achieves a ROUGE-1 F1 score of 73.9%, outperforming chart-focused models such as UniChart (14.7%), ChartGemma (6.9%), and Matcha-ChartQA (6.3%). Our relevance-enhanced RAG setup also improves the exact match by 11.9% and the fuzzy match by 29.5% compared with a standard RAG baseline, resulting in an overall gain of 14.3%. We also examine closed-ended infinite visual questions, a category in which chart-understanding models often struggle. Our experiments show that using plot digitization instead of synthetic captions leads to an 18.5% improvement for this question type.
  </details>

- **[Neurons Are All You Need](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00186)**  `arXiv:2611.00186`  `cs.AI`  
  _Joseph B. Alzagatiti, Alain C. Glanzman, Perry A. Demsko, Pablo I. Salanova, David L. Glanzman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For decades, artificial neural networks have operated under the assumption that durable, learned capability resides exclusively in adaptive synaptic parameters, primarily due to the established neurobiology of synaptic connectivity. This design forces models to remain stateless during deployment, incurring well-known limitations such as catastrophic forgetting and expensive retraining. However, recent neurobiological research by Glanzman and colleagues, and others, shows that memories can be transferred between individual organisms in a synaptically independent manner. Inspired by this research, we sought to demonstrate memory transfer of persistent memory between AI model instances, leveraging a neurocentric version of memory. In this paper, we propose NURESA (Neural Unit with Retentive Self-Adaptive Architecture), a novel neural layer that eschews sole reliance on synaptic weight updating by introducing persistent intra-unit memory as a first-class, computational primitive. By structurally separating computation from persistent memory, NURESA enables autonomous memory accumulation and consolidation into transferable stores of memory registers. Experiments across MLP, GPT-2, and modern frontier models like Qwen2.5 demonstrate qualitatively new capability: zero-shot transfer of learned capability into a naive model instance without retraining or access to the original training data; this transfer is enabled by a sparse set of parameters in NURESA's memory register that the model selects on its own based on which neurons were most active. The memory transfer capability of NURESA also protects against domain loss when transformer models undergo learning via fine-tuning. Finally, the memory transfer process completes in approximately one minute even for transformer models with millions to billions of parameters, producing a transfer file orders of magnitude smaller than the original model.
  </details>

- **[Reimagining Text-to-SQL: A Survey of Large Language Model Architectures and Strategies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00171)**  `arXiv:2611.00171`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shikhar Dave, Bhaskarjit Sarmah, Stefano Pasquali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-SQL systems translate natural language questions into executable SQL queries, democratizing access to relational data. Early approaches relied on handcrafted rules and template matching, while subsequent neural methods introduced encoder-decoder architectures, schema linking, and constrained decoding to handle increasingly complex queries. The emergence of large language models (LLMs), exemplified by systems such as ReFoRCE, Agentar-Scale-SQL, Snowflake's Arctic-Text2SQL-R1, and the modular XiYan-SQL framework, has dramatically advanced the field, pushing execution accuracy to new state-of-the-art levels on challenging benchmarks such as Spider, Spider 2.0, and BIRD. In parallel, modular frameworks decompose the task into sub-problems (schema retrieval, sketch-based generation, repair, and ranking), while agentic systems coordinate multiple specialized agents that communicate and dynamically allocate sub-tasks. This survey systematically reviews these LLM-driven approaches within a unified taxonomy of five paradigms (in-context learning, supervised and parameter-efficient fine-tuning, agentic and tool-augmented orchestration, retrieval-augmented generation, and reinforcement learning) and characterizes them by model granularity (monolithic vs. modular), level of supervision (zero-shot, fine-tuned, or reinforcement-based), and reasoning style (direct decoding vs. decomposed multi-step reasoning). We further analyze evaluation metrics, datasets, and open challenges, including domain generalization, efficiency, and real-world deployment, and we examine the role of the semantic layer in production systems. As LLMs increasingly access sensitive databases, we also discuss responsible AI practices. We survey over 100 papers and technical resources, focusing on work published between 2022 and 2026.
  </details>

- **[Specification-Guided Generation for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41511)**  `arXiv:2610.41511`  `cs.AI`  
  _Alessandro Previti, Konstantinos Vandikas, Mario Alviano, Mats Carlsson, Albin Larsson Forsberg, Alexandros Nikou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate structured outputs, but existing constrained-decoding methods mainly enforce syntax and cannot directly enforce satisfaction of a formal specification. We introduce a neuro-symbolic, specification-guided decoding framework that combines LLM generation with symbolic reasoning. At each step, the framework refines the decoding grammar by filtering out values that cannot be extended to an output satisfying the specification. We present a forward-only variant that guarantees specification satisfaction by construction, and a backtracking variant that revises earlier feasible choices when their downstream consequences leave only values that the LLM itself judges unsuitable. The framework is model-agnostic, requires no retraining, and builds on existing grammar-constrained decoding mechanisms. Experiments on configuration and constraint-solving benchmarks show that the forward-only variant reliably produces specification-compliant outputs while reducing repeated LLM inference compared with rejection sampling and incurring modest symbolic-reasoning overhead. The backtracking variant further reduces cases in which the model is forced to select values that it has itself judged unsuitable, thereby substantially improving adherence to the LLM's stated interpretation of the prompt.
  </details>

- **[KARMA: Training-Free Knowledge-Atomic Harness Evolution for Industrial Advertising Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39884)**  `arXiv:2610.39884`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinhua Miao, Yuanzhen Rong, Weiru Zhang, Xiaoyi Zeng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Industrial advertising agents must continually adapt their decision knowledge to changing product offerings, traffic patterns, and operating policies. We present KARMA (Knowledge-Atomic, Regression-Gated Modular Adaptation), a training-free framework that evolves six typed knowledge modules while keeping model weights and executable harness code frozen. During our evolution, a deterministic Trace Verifier maps observed errors to candidate modules, and a Critic compares successful and failed trajectories to guide a tool-constrained Evolver. The Evolver applies atomic knowledge edits, which a fixed validation gate retains only when their measured gain reaches a threshold; otherwise, the previous knowledge state is restored. We evaluate KARMA with three LLM families on cases sampled from a corpus of approximately half a million real-world operation logs across six e-commerce domains. Across 18 domain--model settings, KARMA raises the average hold-out score from 30.19 to 39.15 (+8.96 points, +29.7%). Ablations show that removing either successful or failed trajectories more than halves the average hold-out gain. Evolved knowledge also transfers across domains without further adaptation, yielding an average hold-out gain of 8.54 points across all source--target pairs. By linking retained patches to their diagnostic evidence and validation outcomes, KARMA supports inspectable, reversible knowledge adaptation for agents in Web marketplaces.
  </details>

- **[Encode Once, Answer Many: Internalizing Context via Parametric Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38356)**  `arXiv:2610.38356`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xinhao Zhong, Mengxuan Wu, Chonghua Liao, Linxi Xie, Ruicheng Feng, Kangqiao Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are typically adapted to a task either by placing knowledge in the input context, which keeps it external and re-encodes it on every call, or by fine-tuning, which internalizes it but demands a costly round of optimization per task. We aim to internalize the knowledge carried by a context directly into model parameters, without per-task optimization and without keeping the context in the input at inference time. To this end, we propose CLIK, a context-conditioned parameter generator that maps a given context to LoRA parameters for internalizing knowledge in a single forward pass through a Transformer encoder with cross-attention, requiring no test-time gradient updates. Once the generated LoRA is inserted into a frozen base model, the model answers downstream queries from the query alone, having internalized the context into its weights rather than re-reading it. Because the adapter depends only on the context, it is generated once and reused for every query on the same document, so the cost of reading that document is amortized across queries. On document question answering, CLIK substantially improves over the no-context base model and outperforms existing hypernetwork-based generators, while remaining behind a base model that reads the context directly, and it carries over to multi-turn dialogue without any multi-turn-specific training. Analysis further shows that the context-dependent part of the generated adapter concentrates in a compact and specific substructure.
  </details>

- **[Multiscale Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35013)**  `arXiv:2610.35013`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tao Ren, Hui Yang, Jia Deng, Tingyang Chen, Qiyao Wang, Shuo Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon LLM agents interact with their environments through complete assistant turns while generating each action token by token. We observe that critic values vary primarily across turns, motivating optimization anchored at turns with finer credit allocation among tokens. We propose Multiscale Policy Optimization (MPO), combining Multiscale Advantage Estimation (MAE) and turn-level importance sampling to align credit assignment and policy updates at the turn scale. Extensive experiments across search, tool-use, and automation tasks with 4B and 30B-A3B models demonstrate consistent gains over strong RL baselines.
  </details>

- **[PersonaScienceBench: Evaluating Scientific Agents with Diverse Simulated Researchers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32106)**  `arXiv:2610.32106`  `cs.AI`  
  _Siyang Wu, Weizhi Zhang, Chen Yang, Esther Xu Fei, Jorin Overwiening, Xu Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific agents collaborate with researchers whose expertise and preferences differ, but benchmarks usually hold the collaborator fixed. We introduce PersonaScienceBench: 200 tasks from five benchmarks across four scientific domains. Simulated researchers provide context and feedback according to persona profiles, while task objectives and grading remain fixed. Agents encounter researcher preferences only through conversation. Across eighteen modelconfigurations, task performance, fact recovery, and self-repetition reveal different strengths. Detailed GPT 6 Astra analyses show distinct agent responses across researcher profiles; interaction traces illustrate targeted clar ification and workflow revision. These behaviors can differ even when final scores match. PersonaScienceBench therefore evaluates how agents collaborate with diverse researchers alongside whether they complete the task.
  </details>

- **[PLE: Physical-Engineering's Last Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30537)**  `arXiv:2610.30537`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rujun Han, Yufan Zhuang, Arijit Ray, Han Yu, Zhongying CuiZhu, George Lee, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier language models now score above 90% on popular knowledge benchmarks and are increasingly deployed as agents that carry out multi-hour professional work. Yet no existing benchmark tests whether they can perform the real-world, economically valuable work of a professional physical engineer, such as modelling physics and legal code requirements across a multi-format project corpus, optimising material and installation costs, and issuing 2D/3D engineering design packages that a licensed reviewer would sign off on. Existing engineering benchmarks focus on textbook question answering, isolated tool calls or self-contained toy design problems. As a result, most of these benchmarks are already saturated by leading agents and solving them is far away from delivering real engineering design. We introduce PLE, a benchmark of 85 long-horizon, agentic tasks in professional physical engineering consisting of electrical, civil, structural and mechanical engineering, where solving a task means completing a realistic, billable engineering deliverable. PLE combines two complementary curation paths. Expert-Authored tasks are created end to end by practising engineers from a professional data provider's expert pool. Co-Authored tasks start from problem statements written by practising engineers; an adversarial Creator-Solver agent pipeline constructs each task's realistic project environment, executable grader and reference deliverables before sending them to expert engineers for validation and difficulty intensification. We evaluate a variety of frontier LLMs under seven publicly available agent harnesses. Even the strongest configuration reaches a mean score of only 50.6%, while spending 27M tokens and 63 minutes per task on average, demonstrating the substantial gap between current agents and autonomous physical engineering design.
  </details>

- **[TimesJev: Thinking Fast on Time Series with System 1 Sufficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26002)**  `arXiv:2610.26002`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chenghao Liu, Wei Fan, Jindong Han, Dongjie Wang, Aniket Mahanti, Pengyang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have recently shown strong potential for time series reasoning, while models that reason over time series are usually with long chains of thought. However, this process often consumes thousands of tokens, directly leading to substantial latency during inference. In contrast, System 1 models such as Jev make decisions instantly and can naturally be an alternative, but raw time series are not compatible with the embedding space of these models, thus hindering the reasoning performance, especially when input series contain thousands of numeric points. In this paper, we make a very early attempt to propose a time series System 1 model, namely TimesJev. Specifically, we first formalize System 1 sufficiency in time series reasoning, which motivates a compressed representation for time series with key frequency patterns preserved. To construct this representation, we derive a dimension reduction objective that combines (i) a sufficiency divergence that measures the core information lost during compression, and (ii) a realizability divergence that quantifies the incompatibility of the System 1 model to the optimal prediction given this representation. We optimize this objective by learning time series compression with a gating mechanism for sufficiency, and then aligning the selected representation with the embedding space for realizability. Finally, experiments on 13 datasets demonstrate that TimesJev outperforms reasoning models by over 62% with 62.9% less inference time than their fine-tuned versions on average.
  </details>

- **[GraphEvoResearcher: A Cost-Matched Mechanism Analysis of Explicit Reasoning Graphs in Training-Free Self-Reflection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25978)**  `arXiv:2610.25978`  `cs.AI`  
  _Suxing Liu, Byungwon Min, Zhijian Zheng, Jiahao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training-free self-reflective protocols (generate, critique, revise) improve LLM reasoning but operate on a flat token stream: revisions cannot identify which prior step is unreliable, and stopping cannot exploit structural convergence. We introduce GraphEvoResearcher, a training-free protocol that lifts EvoResearcher's self-reflective meta-reward onto an explicit reasoning graph whose nodes are intermediate conclusions and whose typed edges encode support, contradict, and depends_on, with a graph-aware reward over correctness, efficiency, reflection, diversity, and structural signals. On deepseek-chat over GSM8K, MATH, and BBH (n=50 each), the protocol reaches 98%, 92%, and 82% in about two calls per question. Gains are significant against single-shot decoding on GSM8K and MATH and against flat reflection only on MATH; against cost-matched self-consistency the protocol is competitive, not dominant. A paired mechanism ablation over a shared response cache then shows what the graph contributes: holding model outputs fixed, neither the structural stop rule nor contradiction-driven repair changes which item is answered correctly, so the gains come from the forced second pass rather than from the structural reward. The ablation exposes the reason: the answer step never reads the edges it builds. We fix this with an edge-consulting arbitration step, which repairs one BBH item and fires on only 4% of questions, locating the binding constraint in how often construction declares conflicts. Testing the implied prediction on HotpotQA (n=100) falsifies it: the trigger rate does not rise with mul
  </details>

- **[Stepwise Reasoning Verification: Don't Generate, Classify!](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25441)**  `arXiv:2610.25441`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yifei Shi, Michalis Korakakis, Rami Aly, Eric Chamoun, Lukasz Szpruch, Andreas Vlachos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifying the reasoning of large language models is increasingly important as they are applied to complex mathematical and scientific problems. Existing work focuses on large decoder models and reports mixed results for generative and discriminative verifiers, leaving unclear which paradigm is more effective and whether large models are necessary. In this work, we cast stepwise verification as a natural language inference task: the problem and partial solution prefix form the premise, while the next reasoning step serves as the hypothesis to be classified as entailed or non-entailed. This formulation enables error localisation at individual reasoning steps and direct comparison of encoder classification, decoder classification, and decoder verdict generation across model scales and annotation budgets. Across five reasoning datasets, a 0.15B encoder classifier outperforms most generative verifiers with up to 4B parameters. On identical decoder backbones, classification outperforms generation from 0.5B to 4B parameters and matches it on the strongest 7B backbone---reaching comparable performance with roughly one tenth as many annotated problems. Our findings establish classification as a parameter- and data-efficient approach to stepwise verification and show that strong verification does not require large generative decoders.
  </details>

- **[RSIFlow: Adaptive Self-Improvement Workflows with an Evolving Meta-Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21791)**  `arXiv:2610.21791`  `cs.AI`  
  _Huan Zhang, Mingju Chen, Dongxu Zhou, Can Lv, Sen Cui, Faguo Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in complex tasks, including code generation, tool use, and multi-hop question answering. Existing self-improvement methods enhance agent performance through model optimization, harness evolution, and executable artifact refinement, while recursive self-improvement (RSI) further extends optimization to the improvement mechanisms themselves. However, adapting self-improvement workflows to evolving performance bottlenecks while improving workflow orchestration through execution feedback remains a key challenge. We propose \textbf{RSIFlow}, a \textbf{R}ecursive \textbf{S}elf-\textbf{I}mprovement framework that integrates adaptive work\textbf{flow} orchestration with meta-agent capability evolution. RSIFlow diagnoses performance bottlenecks from task-agent trajectories and environmental feedback to dynamically select component-specific improvement workflows. The meta-agent then distills workflow outcomes into component-specific skills and cross-component principles, evolving its own harness to improve subsequent workflow orchestration. Experiments across six benchmarks and two backbone models show that RSIFlow achieves the SOTA average performance on all benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of adaptive workflow orchestration and meta-harness evolution. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Can the Library Speak? Self-Advertised Method Selection for Formal Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09401)**  `arXiv:2610.09401`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaopeng Yuan, Suijin Wang, Yanli Wang, Haibo Jin, Peng Kuang, Jerry Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based formal provers can retrieve relevant lemmas and prior proofs, but relevance alone does not say whether a mathematical method can be used on the current theorem. A method has prerequisites, a target, an intended action, and obligations that its use leaves to prove. Methods that look equally related to a theorem may therefore differ substantially in whether they offer a plausible next step. We formulate this as an applicability-aware method-selection problem and introduce self-advertisement: before candidates are ranked, a model generates a problem-specific proposal for each one, stating what part of the goal it targets, what action it would take, and what conditions that action requires. We organize 82 reusable methods from Putnam 2000-2014 as Method Contracts, which pair applicability descriptions with Mathlib anchors, a checked example or scaffold, and expected proof obligations. A single batched call elicits proposals across the library; vague or unsupported proposals are demoted, yielding a ranked shortlist accompanied by inspectable claims about each candidate's use. We analyze when similarity-based representations cannot distinguish methods with different applicability, how errors in applicability estimates affect shortlist quality, and what a checked scaffold guarantees under its stated assumptions. Against lexical, embedding, and embedding-plus-LLM reranking baselines, self-advertisement achieves 95.0% hit@5 on Putnam 2015-2025, compared with 84.2% for the strongest reranker. On IMO ProofBench, it achieves 91.7% compared with 88.3%. These results indicate improved coverage of annotated methods in the retrieved shortlists, particularly on Putnam.
  </details>

- **[Interpreting at Write Time: A Policy Ablation for Multi-Goal Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02897)**  `arXiv:2610.02897`  `cs.AI`  
  _Albert Sadowski, Jarosław A. Chudziak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A long-running assistant cannot keep everything it has seen, so it summarises. Summarising is not neutral: what is kept depends on what the record is for, and the choice is made once, before anyone knows which of the user's goals will ask. We ask what a memory should summarise for when it serves several standing goals at once. Three write policies answer differently: summarise with no goal in view, write one summary covering every goal, or write one summary per goal and read them together. We compare them with five models, two short event streams and one LLM judge, holding the read step fixed. Answers from different goal stores overlap less than answers from one store on two runs, so the goals do pull apart. Per-goal summaries read together scored highest. They beat the all-goal summary on relevance, completeness and accuracy, but the neutral one only on relevance and completeness, and at matched answer length only on relevance. The all-goal summary scored below the neutral one, which had a quarter of its budget. A single goal store served its own goal's queries better than the other goal stores, but not better than the neutral summary.
  </details>

- **[Robust Nash Alignment under Preference Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00715)**  `arXiv:2610.00715`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shihab Ahmed, Debamita Ghosh, David Tang, Yudan Wang, Alvaro Velasquez, Yue Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preference-based alignment methods typically optimize against a single preference model, and can therefore be brittle when pairwise preferences are uncertain: noisy, heterogeneous, or shift after deployment. To address these issues, we propose Robust Nash Alignment, a game-theoretic framework for alignment to uncertain pairwise preferences. Our formulation has a major learner seeking a policy with a large worst-case win rate against both an adversarial competitor and any preference kernel lying in an ambiguity set around a nominal preference. When the ambiguity set captures the uncertainty in preferences, the resulting robust objective of the game directly yields a certified lower bound on worst-case performance. However, we note this problem is computationally challenging to optimize, and to address this, we introduce a four-player primal-dual proxy game involving the leader policy, follower policy, adversarial kernel, and dual variable, and develop a single-loop optimistic mirror descent-ascent algorithm for it. We show that the proxy always lower-bounds the truncated hard-constrained objective, quantify the proxy-to-hard gap, and characterize an exactness condition under which the proxy recovers the robust objective. We then prove an $\mathcal{O}(1/\sqrt{T})$ average-iteration convergence for the proxy-game duality gap, which implies a near-optimal robust policy for the original robust objective. Experiments on controlled tabular games and LLM alignment with uncertain preference illustrate the method and show improved robustness to preference perturbations over nominal baselines.
  </details>

- **[Component Replacement in LLM Agents: A Critical Scoping Review of Task Benefit and Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.39989)**  `arXiv:2609.39989`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuyang Zhang, Jianshuo Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Background: Choosing an agent controller and deciding which component to improve require different evidence. A replacement can change both the decision rule and what the agent can do after that decision. Objectives: We ask what existing replacement comparisons establish about task benefit and about attribution to local decision quality. Methods: This critical scoping review maps 348 studies and applies a five-step interpretation procedure to 94 comparison and diagnostic records from 42 studies, reading every inconsistent record against its source. Eight purposively selected cases illustrate the procedure. Results: The records comprise 33 executed task-outcome contrasts, 49 comparisons with restricted endpoints, and 12 diagnostic summaries. Only 14 task contrasts report contrast-level uncertainty, and the response policy is documented as retained in nine, all rule replacements from four studies; none of the 12 necessity-decision records executes a task contrast. Source checks distinguish whole-plan correctness, correct skill loading, and choice accuracy conditional on presentation from completed workflows. In Outcome Monitors, a count-matched timing placebo detects no timing effect (+0.88 points, 95% CI -4.39 to 6.14), so the source evidence attributes the detectable package gain to recovery content; detector selection at a fixed warning quota remains untested. Conclusions: The analysis separates evidence that can inform adoption from evidence that supports component development. Recovery and disruption, intervention timing, and downstream consumption organize three candidate mechanisms, and eight conditional reporting items specify the comparisons needed to distinguish the remaining explanations.
  </details>

- **[Map Users and Mapmakers: The Scope of Cognitive Attribution from Acquired Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13879)**  `arXiv:2609.13879`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiling Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  An acquired representation can enlarge a system's cognitive repertoire without transferring the capacities exercised in producing that representation. This paper develops a framework for specifying that enlargement and its limits. Its central contribution is a five-part attribution table distinguishing effective tracking, application of acquired structures, acquisition from explicit specifications, acquisition from identifying observations, and retention and reuse. Each entry identifies a positive capacity commitment and a further claim requiring additional support. The argument deliberately grants meaningful content, causal efficacy, and productive inference, so that its conclusion does not depend on treating representations as inert encodings. Map and category examples show why even complete application competence leaves acquisition capacity undetermined, and why acquiring a criterion from its description differs from finding it in examples. Short formal proofs appear in an appendix. The framework is applied to Andrew Ng's world-model interpretation of Othello-GPT and to the specific indicators discussed in contemporary accounts of machine concepts. It preserves demonstrated recognition, classification, inference, and qualified acquisition while specifying what remains unestablished about criterion discovery and accumulation. The result concerns the scope of cognitive attribution rather than the constitutive conditions of concept possession: cognitive achievements deserve credit for the capacities they establish, without silently importing a broader repertoire through the labels attached to them.
  </details>

- **[Modeling Social Dynamics with an LLM-Enabled Agent Based Network-Dynamic (LAND) Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00929)**  `arXiv:2608.00929`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lynnette Hui Xian Ng, Kathleen M. Carley_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Social dynamics encode the process in which individual network and discourse interactions aggregate into collective influence, narrative dominance and coordinate behavior. This paper uses the the GhostField architecture, a hybrid LLM-Enabled Agent Based Network-Dynamic (LAND) model as a social simulation framework to build the AuraSight scenario. In the AuraSight scenario, 314,244 heterogeneous cyber social agents and human actors exchange 529,327 messages over 30 days surrounding a fictional international song-writing contest. We methodologically examine emergent social dynamics across four analytical layers: ego-network topology, semantic network evolution, coordination dynamics and influence dynamics. Our results show how generated social simulations do also produce social dynamics, and how the dynamics of coordination and influence emerge not from individual agents but from the recursive interaction between network topology and narrative exchange.
  </details>

- **[HierFlow: Hierarchical Coupled Dual-Space Search for Automatic Agentic Workflow Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.21609)**  `arXiv:2607.21609`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dong Li, Yanchi Liu, Xujiang Zhao, Wei Cheng, Zhengzhang Chen, Xintao Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although structured workflows empower Large Language Models (LLMs) to tackle complex problems, automating their creation is severely hindered by a vast combinatorial search space, frequently resulting in inflexible and resource-heavy offline training dependencies. To address this, we conceptualize workflow generation as an intertwined topology-and-execution search paradigm, where the broader topological layer dictates subtask boundaries and lower-level execution outcomes actively reshape the topology itself. Building on this foundation, we introduce HierFlow, a training-free, test-time hierarchical search architecture that automates agentic workflow design by merging feedback-guided topology adjustments with a fast, MCTS-inspired tree search for sub-workflow optimization. HierFlow maximizes efficiency through an intelligent gating module that selectively triggers execution-level searches based on contextual necessity, a mechanism we further support with an in-depth analysis detailing how varying degrees of cross-task coupling impact the effectiveness of hierarchical splitting. Comprehensive testing across question answering, mathematical reasoning, and code generation benchmarks confirms that HierFlow consistently outperforms strong baselines, delivering an optimal balance of high-quality results and computational efficiency without any additional training overhead.
  </details>

- **[SkillDAG: Self-Evolving Typed Skill Graphs for LLM Skill Selection at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03056)**  `arXiv:2606.03056`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tong Bai, Zhenglin Wan, Pengfei Zhou, Xingrui Yu, Yang You, Ivor W. Tsang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large skill libraries are now standard equipment for LLM agents, and which subset reaches the prompt decides what the agent can do. Enumeration and top-$K$ embedding retrieval both treat skills as independent documents, assuming relevance is a property of one skill at a time. It is not: skills depend on, conflict with, specialize, and duplicate one another, so the closest match to a task can name it while omitting a prerequisite it needs. Graph retrieval encodes these relations but spends them in a fixed pipeline, handing the agent an opaque bundle. We present SkillDAG, which exposes a typed skill graph to the LLM as an agent-callable interface rather than consuming it for the agent: search returns vector matches, typed-edge neighbors, and conflict signals, and a propose-then-commit protocol lets it register execution-backed edges as it works. Across two benchmarks and three model families, SkillDAG outperforms full-library prompting, vector retrieval, and Graph-of-Skills, gaining +14.3 points of ALFWorld success and tying only at ceiling; its ranking holds flat across a $10\times$ pool expansion that degrades the fixed pipeline. Inter-skill structure, these results suggest, is worth more to an agent that can query and revise it than to a retriever that consumes it.
  </details>

- **[Automated Reformulation of Robust Optimization via Memory-Augmented Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11813)**  `arXiv:2605.11813`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinbiao Chen, Shuang Jin, Guoyun Zhang, Junyu Zhang, Guanyi Wang, Hanzhang Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robust optimization (RO) provides a principled framework for decision-making under uncertainty, but its practical use is often limited by the need to manually reformulate uncertain optimization models into tractable deterministic counterparts. Recent large language models (LLMs) have been shown promising for automating optimization formulation, yet RO reformulation remains challenging because it requires precise multi-step reasoning and mathematically consistent transformations. To facilitate systematic evaluation of LLM-based reformulation, for which no dedicated benchmark currently exists, we develop AutoRO-Bench, a benchmark featuring an automated data generation pipeline for the core RO reformulation task and a curated dataset for the RO application task. To address the reformulation challenge, we propose Automated Reformulation with Experience Memory (AutoREM), a tuning-free memory-augmented framework that autonomously builds a structured textual experience memory by reflecting on past failed trajectories through a tailored offline adaptation procedure. AutoREM requires neither domain-specific expert knowledge nor parameter updates, and the resulting memory readily transfers across different base LLMs. Experimental results show that AutoREM consistently improves the accuracy and efficiency of RO reformulation across in-distribution datasets, out-of-distribution datasets, and diverse base LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Teacher-Aware Evolution of Heuristic Programs from Learned Optimization Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.10634)**  `arXiv:2605.10634`  `cs.AI`  
  _Minyu Chen, Song Qin, Ling-I Wu, Jianxin Xue, Guoqiang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based automatic heuristic design has shown promise for generating executable heuristics for combinatorial optimization, but existing methods mainly rely on delayed endpoint performance. We propose a \emph{teacher-aware evolutionary framework} that uses independently trained learned optimization policies as behavioral teachers. Instead of deploying or imitating the teacher, our method queries it on states visited by candidate heuristic programs and uses its action preferences as local feedback for evolution. The resulting search discovers static executable heuristics guided by both task performance and teacher-derived behavioral signals. Experiments on scheduling, routing, and graph optimization benchmarks show that our method improves over performance-driven LLM heuristic evolution baselines while requiring no neural inference at deployment. These results suggest that learned optimization policies can be repurposed as behavioral feedback sources for automatic heuristic discovery.
  </details>

- **[Alignment Imprint: Zero-Shot AI-Generated Text Detection via Provable Preference Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16923)**  `arXiv:2604.16923`  `cs.AI`  
  _Junxi Wu, Kailin Huang, Dongjian Hu, Bin Chen, Hao Wu, Shu-Tao Xia, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Detecting AI-generated text is an important but challenging problem. Existing likelihood-based detection methods are often sensitive to content complexity and may exhibit unstable performance. In this paper, our key insight is that modern Large Language Models (LLMs) undergo alignment (including fine-tuning and preference tuning), leaving a measurable distributional imprint. We theoretically derive this imprint by abstracting the alignment process as a sequence of constrained optimization steps, showing that the log-likelihood ratio can naturally decompose into implicit instructional biases and preference rewards. We refer to this quantity as the Alignment Imprint. Furthermore, to mitigate the instability in high-entropy regions, we introduce Log-likelihood Alignment Preference Discrepancy (LAPD), a standardized information-weighted statistic based on alignment imprint. We provide statistical guarantee that alignment-based statistics dominate Fast-DetectGPT in performance. We also theoretically show that LAPD strictly improves the unweighted alignment scores when the aligned and base models are close in distribution. Extensive experiments show that LAPD achieves an improvement 45.82% relative to the strongest existing baselines, yielding large and consistent gains across all settings.
  </details>

- **[WMIP: A Working-Memory-Inspired Programmable Probe of Cumulative State Tracking in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27343)**  `arXiv:2603.27343`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dengzhe Hou, Lingyu Jiang, Deng Li, Zirui Li, Fangzhou Lin, Kazunori D Yamada_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human working memory lets people maintain and update information. Large language models (LLMs) face a related demand when tracking updates within a single prompt without writing out intermediate states, yet existing tests differ in their constraints on written steps. We introduce a working-memory-inspired probe of cumulative state tracking with program-computed answers, 3 to 100 updates, and final-state, intermediate-state, or multi-target queries. It compares protocols on identical items. Across 18 LLMs, accuracy is 84.9% on single-step arithmetic but 31.7% on core items requiring one number after three to seven updates. From 50 updates on, no model exceeds 10% accuracy. Core accuracy reaches 88.0% under a larger budget and instructions to write steps and place the answer on the last line, mainly due to the instruction changes rather than the budget. Core accuracy also correlates positively with public Berkeley Function Calling Leaderboard scores. These findings show that strong single-step arithmetic does not ensure reliable cumulative tracking and that output protocols substantially change measured accuracy. We release the generator, items, scoring code, and responses for reproducible comparisons across depths, query types, and protocols.
  </details>

- **[MMCOMET: A Large-Scale Multimodal Commonsense Knowledge Graph for Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.01055)**  `arXiv:2603.01055`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinwoo Kim, Rina Carines Cabral, Dhita Putri Pratama, Eileen Wang, Hiba Arnaout, Dhita Pratama, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Commonsense knowledge is widely used to support multimodal reasoning and generation, yet existing resources are predominantly text-only and lack grounding in visual context. This limitation often leads to the retrieval of semantically related but visually misaligned knowledge, and can degrade downstream performance. We introduce MMCOMET, a large-scale multimodal commonsense knowledge graph that integrates physical, social, and eventive knowledge with visual grounding. MMCOMET extends ATOMIC2020 by associating each commonsense triple with representative images through a hybrid alignment pipeline, resulting in over 900K multimodal tuples spanning 19 relation types. We further establish a unified evaluation framework across diverse vision-language tasks, including visual commonsense reasoning, visual question answering, visual storytelling, image editing, and image captioning. Our analysis shows that multimodal grounding provides more contextually aligned and reliable knowledge compared to both text-only knowledge graphs and LLM-based knowledge generation, particularly in reasoning-intensive scenarios. At the same time, we observe that the effectiveness of external knowledge is task-dependent, and naive knowledge integration can introduce noise in perception-dominated settings. To mitigate this, we propose an adaptive knowledge router that dynamically selects optimal paths or bypass knowledge in harmful settings. MMCOMET provides a scalable multimodal commonsense resource and a benchmark for studying the role of grounded knowledge in multimodal systems.
  </details>

- **[Instructor-Aligned Knowledge Graphs for Personalized Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17111)**  `arXiv:2602.17111`  `cs.AI`  
  _Abdulrahman AlRabah, Priyanka Kargupta, Jiawei Han, Abdussalam Alawini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mastering educational concepts requires understanding both their prerequisites (e.g., recursion before merge sort) and sub-concepts (e.g., merge sort as part of sorting algorithms). Capturing these dependencies is critical for identifying students' knowledge gaps and enabling targeted intervention for personalized learning, especially in large-scale courses where instructors cannot feasibly diagnose individual misunderstanding. While knowledge graphs offer a natural representation for capturing these conceptual relationships at scale, existing approaches are either surface-level or disregard the rich pedagogical signals embedded in instructional materials. We propose InstructKG, a framework for automatically constructing instructor-aligned knowledge graphs that capture a course's intended learning progression. Given a course's lecture materials, InstructKG extracts significant concepts as nodes and infers learning dependencies as directed edges (e.g., "part-of" or "depends-on" relationships). The framework synergizes the rich temporal and semantic signals unique to educational materials (e.g., "recursion" is taught before "mergesort") with the generalizability of large language models. Through experiments on diverse lecture materials, human evaluation, and a prerequisite-learning benchmark, we demonstrate that InstructKG captures rich, instructor-aligned learning progressions.
  </details>

- **[Benchmark Health Index: A Systematic Framework for Benchmarking the Benchmarks of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11674)**  `arXiv:2602.11674`  `cs.AI`  
  _Longyuan Zhu, Hairan Hua, Linlin Miao, Wei Wang, Bing Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are advancing rapidly, yet the benchmarks used to measure this progress are becoming increasingly unreliable. Score inflation and selective reporting have eroded the authority of standard benchmarks, leaving the community uncertain about which evaluation results remain trustworthy. We introduce the Benchmark Health Index (BHI), a pure data-driven framework for auditing evaluation sets along three orthogonal and complementary axes: (1) Capability Discrimination, measuring how sharply a benchmark separates model performance beyond noise; (2) Anti-Saturation, estimating remaining headroom before ceiling effects erode resolution and thus the benchmark's expected longevity; and (3) Impact, quantifying influence across academic and industrial ecosystems via adoption breadth and practice-shaping power. By distilling 106 validated benchmarks from the technical reports of 91 representative models in 2025, we systematically characterize the evaluation landscape. BHI is the first framework to quantify benchmark health at a macro level, providing a principled basis for benchmark selection and enabling dynamic lifecycle management for next-generation evaluation protocols.
  </details>

- **[Dynamic Mixed-Precision Routing for Efficient Multi-step LLM Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.02711)**  `arXiv:2602.02711`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuanzhe Li, Jianing Deng, Jingtong Hu, Tianlong Chen, Song Wang, Huanrui Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) achieve strong performance in long-horizon agentic tasks through multi-step interaction and reasoning at test time. However, repeatedly invoking these models incurs substantial inference cost over an episode. Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces this cost, but using a fixed low precision throughout an episode can sharply lower task success. We observe that interaction steps differ sharply in their sensitivity to quantization: given the same context, decoding a step at low precision turns a success into a failure at only about 1% of the steps, which we call critical steps. Based on this observation, we propose Dynamic Mixed-Precision Routing (DMR), a framework that adaptively selects between high-precision and low-precision versions of the same LLM at each decision step. Leveraging an entire-space factorization to jointly predict a step's divergence and its effect on the outcome, DMR learns to recognize these critical steps by training only a lightweight router on the model's hidden state. By reusing a shared high-precision KV cache and the hidden states, DMR avoids redundant context prefill and a separate text encoder, enabling precision switching with less than 1 ms of overhead per step and adding less than 0.11% to step latency for routing. This mixed-precision design also enables nested quantization, reducing the memory footprint by 39% on average compared with FP16. Across three long-horizon agentic benchmarks with nested quantization, DMR achieves an average 2.44x decode speedup over FP16 at comparable task success rates, without fine-tuning the underlying model. On ALFWorld with GPTQ, DMR achieves a 1.71x decode speedup with 86.9% success, compared with 87.2% for FP16.
  </details>

- **[From Structured Graphs to Unstructured Evidence: Agentic Root Cause Analysis on Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07342)**  `arXiv:2601.07342`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nicolas Tacheny_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
