  Root cause analysis over telecom and datacenter infrastructures is commonly addressed by methods that consume structured data, from graph traversal and rule-based correlation to probabilistic and learned models. Yet a digital twin of such an infrastructure cannot model everything at a reasonable cost. Operators therefore record much of what changes as unstructured data attached to the graph, where the discriminating evidence often sits. Methods restricted to structured attributes cannot use it. A diagnosis needs a layer that interprets this data. An agentic layer is a natural candidate, since which fragment matters depends on hypotheses that emerge during the investigation. We demonstrate its feasibility with ReAct, the most widely used agentic architecture. A single LLM interprets the descriptions and explores the graph through a typed tool interface exposed via the Model Context Protocol (MCP). On a benchmark of nine synthetic scenarios covering single-node, multi-node and distractor-heavy evidence (360 runs across four LLMs), a frontier-tier model names the root cause and its type on every run. It reaches 96.7% end-to-end diagnostic accuracy, which also requires the exact supporting evidence. A mid-tier model reaches 86.7%. Accuracy drops for every model when the evidence must be combined across several resources.
  </details>

- **[SynBench: A Benchmark for Differentially Private Text Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.14594)**  `arXiv:2509.14594`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yidan Sun, Viktor Schlegel, Srinivasan Nandakumar, Iqra Zahid, Yuping Wu, Yulong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic text generation with Differential Privacy (DP) guarantees emerges as a principled approach that can enable the sharing of sensitive datasets across institutional and regulatory boundaries, while bounding the risks of re-identification and membership inference. LLM-based methods deliver promising results; however, comparisons are exacerbated by differing evaluation setups and "private" datasets, potential pre-training contamination is not considered and guarantees are not verified with DP audits. To advance this field, we introduce a unified evaluation framework with standardised utility and fidelity metrics and privacy audits, encompassing nine curated datasets that capture domain-specific complexities such as technical jargon, long-context dependencies, and specialised document structures. In a large-scale empirical study, we benchmark LLM-based state-of-the-art DP text generators of varying sizes (between 1--8B). Our results indicate that DP synthetic text generation remains an unsolved challenge, with quality deteriorating more as the private datasets deviate further from the generators' pre-training corpora. Our analyses suggest that synthetic data quality may be overestimated when LLMs have been pre-trained---without DP---on portions of the "private" data to be generated, and our membership inference (MIA) auditing setup for synthetic text reveals measurable membership signals even under tight privacy budgets. Finally, we note that under the "public pre-training, private generation" paradigm, the nominal $(\varepsilon,\delta)$ guarantee does not account for exposure during pre-training, motivating contamination-aware evaluation of DP synthetic text generation. Code and data splits:this https URL
  </details>

- **[A Comprehensive Review of Multimodal Large Language Models: Performance and Challenges Across Different Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.01319)**  `arXiv:2408.01319`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaqi Wang, Hanqi Jiang, Yiheng Liu, Chong Ma, Xu Zhang, Yi Pan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In an era defined by the explosive growth of data and rapid technological advancements, Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) stand at the forefront of artificial intelligence (AI) systems. Designed to seamlessly integrate diverse data types-including text, images, videos, audio, and physiological sequences-MLLMs address the complexities of real-world applications far beyond the capabilities of single-modality systems. In this paper, we systematically sort out the applications of MLLM in multimodal tasks such as natural language, vision, and audio. We also provide a comparative analysis of the focus of different MLLMs in the tasks, and provide insights into the shortcomings of current MLLMs, and suggest potential directions for future research. Through these discussions, this paper hopes to provide valuable insights for the further development and application of MLLM.
  </details>
